# Preface.

---

The concept of a "primer" <note>original: *Vorschule*, literally a "pre-school" or "preparatory school," often used in 19th-century German literature to mean an introductory or foundational text</note> of aesthetics is a rather vague one, as is evident from a comparison of the works published under this title to date, such as those by **Jean Paul** (1st ed. 1804, 2nd ed. 1813, etc.), by **Ruge** (2nd ed. 1837), by **Eckardt** (1863–64), and by **Egger** (1872). Without making an effort to clarify or fix this vagueness, I am using it here simply to give this writing a short, appealing title with the following meaning:

This work will offer, in two parts, a series of essays on aesthetic topics. These are presented without a systematic sequence and with a freer treatment than would be appropriate for a formal system of aesthetics; nonetheless, they should be well-suited to foster a more general interest in this field of study. Thus, while they will indeed address very general questions, they will do so with constant application to specific circumstances—even delving into these in dedicated sections—while everywhere maintaining the intention of being easily understood.

In the first two sections, which serve as an introduction, I explain the principles that underlie the entire contents of this work. To summarize them briefly beforehand: this work abandons the attempt to conceptually define the objective nature of beauty <term>objective nature of beauty: the philosophical idea that beauty exists as a fixed, inherent property within an object, independent of the observer's feelings</term> and to develop a system of aesthetics from that starting point.

<summary>Gustav Fechner introduces his book as an accessible "primer" rather than a rigid academic system, explaining that he will focus on specific examples and easy-to-understand essays rather than abstract definitions of beauty.</summary>
<keywords>Gustav Theodor Fechner, aesthetics, Preface, Jean Paul, philosophy of art, objective beauty, introductory text</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface, specifically defining his methodological shift from a philosophical (top-down) to an empirical (bottom-up) approach to aesthetics.</meta>

...instead, it contents itself with using the concept of the beautiful as an auxiliary concept in the sense of common linguistic usage. It serves as a brief designation for that which unites the predominant conditions of immediate pleasure. This approach seeks to investigate the empirical conditions of this pleasure, thus placing the primary weight on the laws of pleasure <note>original: *Gesetze des Gefallens*. Fechner is pivoting away from abstract definitions of "Beauty" toward the psychological laws that govern why we like things.</note> rather than on conceptual developments derived from a definition of the beautiful. Furthermore, it replaces (as discussed on pages 16 and 256) the concept of the so-called **objectively beautiful** with the concept of that which, in consideration of its relation to the good, *ought* to please immediately.

The question will certainly arise whether I will find the inclination in others to follow this path as steadily as it is pursued here—a path which, contrary to the otherwise prevailing direction, leads from the **bottom up** rather than from the **top down**, and more into the **clear** than into the **sublime** <note>original: *ins Hohe*, literally "into the heights." Fechner is contrasting his empirical, "lowly" scientific method with the "high" metaphysical systems of philosophers like Hegel or Schelling.</note>. I admit that one cannot achieve everything with this method that might be desired from a system of aesthetics; however, I seek to prove through the following pages that one can achieve many things with it that an aesthetics of a "higher style" leaves to be desired in its opposing path. If nothing else, one may view what follows as a supplement to such works, and consider that it is no fault of a piece of writing to lack what can be found in others.

Although the following essays are intended to supplement one another, their contents do overlap here and there. This fact, along with the fact that they were partly written independently of one another, has resulted in some repetitions. I hope these will not be found too burdensome; I chose not to eliminate them all through cross-references so as not to break the flow of the presentation.

This first part of the work, as shown in the table of contents, deals with the more general conceptual and lawful relationships of the aesthetic field. This includes, specifically, the elaboration and application of two principles which—

<summary>Fechner defines his "aesthetics from below," favoring empirical observation of pleasure over metaphysical definitions of beauty, and positions his work as a clear, accessible supplement to traditional aesthetic systems.</summary>
<keywords>empirical aesthetics, laws of pleasure, objective beauty, aesthetics from below, methodology, psychological aesthetics</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s introduction to his "bottom-up" approach to aesthetics, shifting from the theoretical framework to his specific publication history and the structure of the upcoming book.</meta>

...are discussed particularly in sections 6 and 9, along with the general principles of taste; the second part will explore more general reflections on art, various controversies concerning art, a further series of aesthetic laws, and several specific subjects.

Some who have only taken notice of my writings in other fields may find it strange that, after a career devoted for so many years to other disciplines, I have finally begun to concern myself with aesthetics. After all, old age usually makes one less suited for any new pursuit the more mature that age itself becomes. However, this book has grown out of the end, rather than the beginning, of my engagement with aesthetic matters—an engagement that was not always merely a side pursuit. To provide evidence of this—to present, so to speak, my "aesthetic service record" <note>original: *ästhetisches Dienstbuch*. A service record or workbook used by soldiers or servants to prove their employment history; Fechner uses this humorously to prove his "credentials" in the arts.</note>—I will briefly list here what I have previously published in this field, though these isolated works have not found a wide audience.

In the year 1839, I published a small book under a pseudonym (as **Mises** <note>Fechner used the pen name "Dr. Mises" for his more satirical, humorous, and creative writing to keep it separate from his serious scientific work.</note>), written in the same style as my other "Mises-writings," titled *On some paintings from the second Leipzig Art Exhibition* (Leipzig: Voss). It was mainly directed against a false trend of idealization and was included in the recently published (1875) collection of the *Shorter Writings* by Mises. — Against the exaggeration of the principle of the **golden section** <term>Golden Section: A mathematical ratio (roughly 1:1.618) believed to create the most pleasing proportions in art and architecture.</term>, I presented several experimental facts in the treatise *On the Question of the Golden Section* in Weigel’s Archive, 1865, page 100. — In a more general way, I championed the idea of an **experimental aesthetics** <term>Experimental Aesthetics: A field Fechner founded that uses scientific experiments and data to understand why we find certain things beautiful, rather than relying on abstract philosophy.</term> in the work *Toward Experimental Aesthetics* (Leipzig: Hirzel, 1871), which was included in the proceedings of the Royal Saxon Society of Sciences, and to which a continuation is yet to be provided. In the present work, a sample of how this is carried out is given in Section 14. — *On the Aesthetic Principle of Association* is a treatise of mine in Lützow's Journal, 1866...

<summary>Fechner defends his transition into the field of aesthetics in his later years, arguing that it is the culmination of decades of research rather than a new whim. He provides a bibliography of his earlier aesthetic works, including his critiques of art exhibitions and his foundational work on the Golden Section.</summary>
<keywords>Gustav Theodor Fechner, Dr. Mises, Golden Section, Experimental Aesthetics, Leipzig Art Exhibition, Aesthetic Association, psychological laws, history of science</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s bibliographic "service record," transitioning from his early satirical work to his serious scientific and experimental contributions to art history and aesthetic theory, specifically regarding the "Holbein controversy."</meta>

contained, which one will find in a somewhat expanded form in the 9th section of this writing. — I participated in the disputes—primarily historical, though they also involve aesthetic aspects—regarding the two versions of the work simply known as the **Holbein Madonna** <note>This refers to a famous 19th-century art controversy over two versions of Hans Holbein the Younger’s *Darmstadt Madonna*. Fechner was interested in why people preferred one over the other regardless of which was the "original."</note> in the treatise "The dispute over the two Madonnas by Holbein" in the *Grenzboten* <note>original: *Grenzb.* A prominent German weekly journal for politics, literature, and art.</note> 1870. II, in the short pamphlet "On the question of the authenticity of the Holbein Madonna" (Leipzig: Breitkopf & Härtel, 1871), and in several articles in Weigel's *Archive* (1866 to 1869). — A public aesthetic experiment involving the comparison of these versions was conducted by me on the occasion of the Holbein Exhibition in the year 1871, which was reported in the small publication "Report on the album displayed at the Dresden Holbein Exhibition" (Leipzig: Breitkopf & Härtel, 1872). *) — Finally, over various years, I have given lectures at the Leipzig Art Society <note>original: *Kunstverein*. These were influential local associations of art enthusiasts that played a major role in 19th-century cultural life.</note> on specific aesthetic questions and at the university on general aesthetics.

---

*) In several public reviews of the aforementioned experiment—partly due to the carelessness of the reviewers, partly because one copied from the other—the experiment was, in direct contradiction to its stated purpose, related more to the question of authenticity <note>original: *Aechtheitsfrage*.</note> than to the aesthetic question. I hereby once again lodge a protest against this, as those reviews are quite capable of calling my own judgment into question and are likely more widespread than the aforementioned little pamphlet, which in fact became very little known, but which explains the actual facts of the experiment.

->## I. Aesthetics from Above and from Below.<-

The dual manner in which human knowledge <term>Knowledge (Erkenntniss): In this context, it refers to the process of cognitive understanding or rational inquiry.</term> strives to ground and develop itself is also evident in aesthetics—the study of pleasure and displeasure, or according to others, the study of the beautiful. To use a brief expression, one treats it **from the top down**, by starting from the most general ideas and concepts and descending to the particular; or **from the bottom up**, by ascending from the particular to the general. In the former approach, the field of aesthetic experience is merely categorized and subordinated within an ideal framework constructed from the highest perspectives; in the latter, the entirety of aesthetics is built from the ground up based on aesthetic facts and laws. In the "from above" approach, it is a matter, in the first and highest instance, of the ideas and concepts of beauty, art, and style—their position within the system of most general concepts, and especially their relationship to the **True** and the **Good** <note>Fechner is referencing the classical philosophical triad where Truth, Beauty, and Goodness are seen as inextricably linked.</note>; and from there, one gladly ascends to the Absolute, the Divine, the divine ideas, and divine creative activity. From the pure heights of such generalities, one then descends into the earthly-empirical realm of the particular—of that which is beautiful in a specific time and place—and measures every individual instance against the yardstick of the general. In the "from below" approach, one starts from experiences of what pleases and displeases, basing all concepts and laws that have a place in aesthetics upon this foundation. One seeks to generalize these further and further—while taking into account the general laws of what "ought to be" <note>original: *Sollen*. This refers to normative or ethical laws that dictate how we should act or judge, as opposed to purely descriptive laws.</note>, to which those of pleasure must always remain subordinate—and thereby arrive at a system of the most general possible concepts and laws.

Fechner, *Introduction to Aesthetics* <note>original: *Vorschule d. Aesthetik*. The word *Vorschule* literally means "pre-school" but here serves as a title for a foundational or introductory text.</note>.

<summary>This page introduces the two primary methods of aesthetic inquiry: the "top-down" philosophical/idealist approach and the "bottom-up" empirical/experimental approach favored by Fechner.</summary>
<keywords>aesthetics, bottom-up aesthetics, top-down aesthetics, Gustav Fechner, empiricism, philosophy of beauty, pleasure and displeasure</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the methodology of aesthetics, specifically contrasting the empirical "Way from Below" with the speculative "Way from Above." The previous text likely discussed the pitfalls of starting with abstract definitions.</meta>

...and has tried to lead, has been confused and mistaken. He who first seeks light—and the **Way from Below** <note>The "Way from Below" refers to an empirical approach that starts with observations and data rather than abstract theories.</note> is a path of such seeking—cannot expect to illuminate this path with a light that is already finished.

In my opinion, the essential tasks of a general aesthetics are to be described as follows: the clarification of the **concepts** under which aesthetic facts and relationships are categorized, and the establishment of the **laws** which they obey, of which the theory of art <term>Kunstlehre|The practical study of the rules and techniques for creating art.</term> contains the most important applications. However, the approaches of the **Aesthetics from Above** <note>The "Way from Above" refers to a philosophical approach that begins with high-level metaphysical ideas about "Beauty" or "The Ideal."</note> have primarily kept only the first task in view, in that they seek to replace the explanation of aesthetic facts through laws with an explanation through concepts or ideas, rather than using them to complement one another.

Indeed, if one looks at most of our textbooks and general treatises on aesthetics—most of which pursue the *Way from Above*—one finds that the main content consists of discussions and disputes over the correct definitions of:
*   **Beauty**
*   **Sublimity**
*   **Ugliness**
*   **The Pleasant**
*   **Gracefulness**
*   **The Comic**
*   **The Tragic**
*   **The Ridiculous**
*   **Humor, Style, and Mannerism**
*   **Art, Beauty in Art, and Beauty in Nature**

The focus is on categorizing individual instances under these concepts and dividing the entire aesthetic field from their perspective. But this does not exhaust the task of aesthetics. For in everything that concerns us aesthetically, the question will not merely be: to which concept does it belong, or what place does it occupy in our system of concepts? One must certainly ask this, as it belongs to a clear orientation within our field of knowledge; however, the most interesting and important question will always remain: *why* does it please or displease, and to what extent does it have the *right* to please or displease? 

And this can only be answered through **laws of liking and disliking** <note>Fechner is advocating for psychological laws that explain the human response to stimuli.</note> while drawing upon the **laws of what ought to be** <note>original: "Gesetze des Sollens"; these are normative laws or standards of "correct" taste and ethics.</note>. It is just like the question: "Why does a body move in such and such a way, and how are we to move it?" This cannot be answered with a definition and a classification of different types of motion, but only with the **laws of motion** and a consideration of the purposes toward which they are directed. And so long as the conceptual ex—

<summary>Fechner argues that aesthetics must move beyond mere definitions of beauty and sublimity to discover the psychological laws governing why we feel pleasure or displeasure. He critiques the "Way from Above" for focusing on abstract categorization rather than the functional "laws of liking."</summary>

<keywords>aesthetics, Way from Below, Way from Above, laws of pleasure, beauty, sublimity, empirical psychology, Kunstlehre, Gesetze des Sollens</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from a discussion regarding the history of aesthetics. The previous page likely concluded a segment on historical bibliographies or specific authors.</meta>

...is not the intention here; regarding the main historical relationships, one should refer to the historical accounts of aesthetics by <note>Rudolf Hermann Lotze (1817–1881), a German philosopher who sought to reconcile science and religion.</note> Lotze and by <note>Robert von Zimmermann (1824–1898), known for his formalist approach to aesthetics.</note> Zimmermann.

## II. Preliminary Concepts.

As we have stated, we do not intend to begin with the difficult philosophical and theological preliminary concepts in which the <term>Aesthetics from Above<note>A theoretical approach that starts from metaphysical principles and universal ideals to define beauty, rather than starting from human experience.</note></term> seeks its foundation, nor will our subsequent discussion lead us there. Whatever explanations we require for our own purposes in the following pages will be provided as they arise. In the meantime, there are many concepts—or words used to designate concepts—without which one cannot take a single step in discussing aesthetic relationships, nor even clarify the concept of aesthetics itself. It will be beneficial to offer some explanations of these beforehand, as the way these terms are used is not entirely fixed in either common life or science. In any case, it is necessary to state how we ourselves intend to use them. The close connection, however, in which basic aesthetic concepts stand with practical concepts—including ethical ones—will naturally lead us to combine explanations of the former with the latter, and to delve into the relationship of ethics to aesthetics itself within certain limits.

### 1) Liking and Disliking, Pleasure and Displeasure.

Generally speaking, we say that something pleases or displeases us depending on whether, when presented to our contemplation or imagination, it imparts a character of <term>pleasure<note>German: *Lust*. In this context, it refers to a broad psychological state of satisfaction or positive affect.</note></term> or <term>displeasure<note>German: *Unlust*. Often translated as "unpleasure" or "pain" in psychological texts, it denotes the opposite of pleasure.</note></term> to it. The pleasure that we feel immediately in the delicious taste of a food, or the feeling of pleasure in strength and health, is not yet "liking" it; rather, liking is the pleasure found in the *mental representation*—that we are tasting, have tasted, or will taste something pleasant—just as it is the representation that we are healthy and...

<summary>This page introduces the foundational terminology for the author’s empirical approach to aesthetics, specifically defining "liking" as a pleasure derived from mental representation rather than just raw sensory input.</summary>
<keywords>Aesthetics from Above, Lotze, Zimmermann, Ethics, Pleasure, Displeasure, Liking, Mental Representation</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous section regarding the definitions of Liking and Disliking, specifically addressing how these feelings relate to internal states and external objects.</meta>

...are in good strength. In these cases, it is the pleasure of the objective contemplation <note>The German *gegenständliche Vorstellung* refers to the act of the mind placing a concept or image before itself as an "object" of study or awareness.</note> of internal states that determines the concept of liking—and in any case, common usage permits the concept of liking to be applied to this as well. In other cases, the objective representation upon which the pleasure of liking depends can be directly awakened by external reality itself, as in the liking of a painting or a piece of music.

Accordingly, the concept of liking and disliking depends essentially on the concepts of pleasure and displeasure; the investigation into the conditions of liking and disliking partly coincides directly with those of pleasure and displeasure, and partly leads back to them.

<note>Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841) was a German philosopher and founder of pedagogy who believed aesthetics should be based on the formal relationships between ideas rather than emotional content.</note> Herbart (*Textbook for the Introduction to Philosophy* § 82, *Collected Works* Vol. I, 122) avoids providing an explanation of the concepts of liking and disliking by attributing to them an original self-evidence <note>In this context, "evidence" (*Evidenz*) refers to a truth that is so fundamental it requires no further proof or explanation.</note>. This does not seem valid to me, insofar as those concepts still allow for a reduction to other concepts, which are the ones that should truly be granted such self-evidence. Furthermore, he excludes internal states from the domain to which the concept of liking is applicable, expressing himself in this regard as follows:

> "Common usage becomes confused when someone says: the scent of the hyacinth pleases me more than the scent of the lily. For in the expression *it pleases*, something that pleases is presupposed as something that can be clearly placed before the eyes. No one, however, can communicate the scent of a flower—which is a sensation within them—to others, nor point to it as an object of contemplation."

With this, however, it seems to me that common usage is merely restricted without motive or effect, rather than being clarified. Indisputably, there is something common to the character of pleasure that can accompany the contemplation of an internal state as well as an external object; this commonality demands a common designation. Since common usage has introduced the expression "liking" for this, there is no reason to restrict it to only one side. Moreover, Herbart will not be able to prevent people from continuing to find liking not only in the scent of a flower or the taste of a dish, but also in the indulgence of any sort of pleasurable representation.

Pleasure and displeasure themselves, when grasped purely and abstractly from all secondary conditions, are simple, non-analyzable determinations of our soul. However, they do not occur in reality as abstractly as they can be grasped through our capacity for abstraction. Instead, they occur only as co-determinations or resultants—functions, if you will—of other mental determinations to which they impart a character...

<summary>This page argues that "liking" applies to both internal sensations and external objects, contrary to Herbart's formalist view. The author defines pleasure and displeasure as fundamental, yet inseparable, components of more complex mental states.</summary>

<keywords>liking and disliking, pleasure and displeasure, Johann Friedrich Herbart, aesthetics, internal states, objective representation, mental determinations</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of "Liking" and "Disliking" by transitioning into a deeper psychological analysis of "Pleasure" and "Displeasure." The author is arguing that while we experience many different types of pleasure, there is a core, identical essence to the feeling itself.</meta>

and through which they receive a specific character. Depending on the nature of their determining factors or causal moments <note>The author uses "moments" (*Momente*) in the philosophical sense to mean a constituent element or factor of a complex process, rather than a point in time.</note>, we then distinguish between different types of pleasure and displeasure: the pleasure in the pleasant taste of a food is, in this respect, different from that of a pleasant smell; the pleasure in contemplating a beautiful painting is different from listening to beautiful music; the pleasure of the feeling of knowing oneself loved is different from knowing oneself honored; the pleasure in any active occupation is different from that in any receptive impression. In itself, pleasure remains pleasure, just as gold remains gold; however, like gold, it can enter into the most diverse compounds and be conceptually separated out from those various compounds.

Indeed, were it not so—though here and there it has been denied that there is an essence of pleasure that is identical everywhere—where would the need for a common designation for it in all those cases come from, if there were nothing identical to designate? Indeed, if one simply holds the previous and any other instances of the occurrence of pleasure against the equally frequent occurrences of displeasure, one will feel that, despite all the differences existing on each side for themselves, each side retains something in common against the other, which we can then abstract as pleasure and displeasure and contrast with one another.

The breadth of its applicability is connected to the simplicity and purity with which one grasps the concept of pleasure. In this respect, it is like a pure distillate <note>A "distillate" refers to the pure substance left over after a process like evaporation and condensation; the author is suggesting we strip away the "impurities" of specific contexts to find the core definition of pleasure.</note>. Everything that the distillate carries with it from the start limits its applicability, even though it only becomes enjoyable and useful in its applications. Therefore, one should, so to speak, purely distill the concept of pleasure from the start—for the sake of its most general applicability—from everything into which it enters; grasp it generally and purely, detached from every special or secondary relationship regarding cause, consequence, type, level, strength, or quality. Distinctions and specifications of the concept will be found naturally as its determining factors and relationships are considered, and thus as the discussion turns toward concrete types or cases of pleasure or displeasure.

What pleasure and displeasure are in their pure form in themselves cannot be explained through any description at all, but only through...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of "Liking" and "Disliking" from page 9, moving from the definition of pleasure as a "gold-like" essence to how it is clarified through internal experience and categorized by "height" versus "intensity."</meta>

...make them clear through internal demonstration. Feel them, and then you know them; there is nothing more that can be said for their final clarification; this is due to their simple nature. On the other hand, much can be said about their causes, consequences, and relationships, and explanations can be provided accordingly. Yet, their ultimate clarity is always obtained only through an internal pointing toward that which we feel identically as <term>pleasure and displeasure</term><note>In German, *Lust* and *Unlust*. These are the foundational terms in 19th-century German psychology for the entire spectrum of positive and negative affect, ranging from sensory "fun" to "sublime joy."</note> emerging from all concrete occurrences. The fact that such a demonstration is possible—either in something internally clear or in a way that is easily made clear—gives a clear core to all concepts that are dependent upon them.

We describe pleasure and displeasure—and thus the liking and disliking into which they enter—as being of a higher nature, or attribute to them a higher character, the higher the spiritual or intellectual realm in which they take place, or the higher the connections, relations, and circumstances to which they are tied; the lowest are those tied to simple sensory impressions. Thus, the pleasure (and consequently the liking) of a **harmonic chord** is of a higher nature than that of a single, pure tone; it is higher for a **musical movement** than for a single chord, and higher for the **harmonious unity** of an entire musical piece than for a single movement.

In everyday life, one easily confuses the **height** with the **intensity** of pleasure. One is inclined to perceive pleasure only in a lower sense, as having a certain strength or liveliness, and to keep in view only the concrete types of pleasure as they present themselves in life. However, "higher" pleasure in the sense described above is not always the more intense or greater; for one person may take greater pleasure in a simple sensory enjoyment than in a correct <term>insight</term><note>German: *Erkenntniss*. This refers to the pleasure of intellectual discovery or understanding.</note>. But the joy in a correct insight is just as much "pleasure" as the pleasure in the most sensory enjoyment; and the weakest feeling of satisfaction or comfort can be brought under the concept of pleasure just as well as the strongest, if one wishes to have a common concept for what is shared across all of them—a concept which is indeed necessary. And if, in common life, the need to go beyond concrete versions of pleasure and displeasure is not great, one still cannot entirely escape it; even more so...

<summary>This page argues that pleasure and displeasure are irreducible internal experiences that gain "higher" status based on the complexity of the intellectual or artistic context they inhabit. <meta>This page continues the discussion of how "pleasure" and "displeasure" are categorized. The previous page established that while we can distinguish between "high" and "low" forms of pleasure based on their intellectual or spiritual complexity, they all share a core "gold-like" essence. Page 11 now addresses the linguistic challenges of using the word "Pleasure" to describe both a simple physical sensation and a sublime spiritual experience.</meta>

...science has been even less able to avoid this approach, according to which the **concept of pleasure** in psychology is unhesitatingly used in that full breadth and generality which depends on its "abstractability" in its purest form. In this view, even the highest forms of pleasure are subordinated alongside the lowest, because such a framework is necessary to establish the most general scientific perspectives—a level of abstraction that the needs of ordinary life do not require.

To escape the restrictive secondary meanings that common usage often attaches to the word **pleasure** <note>original: *Lust*</note>, some have suggested or preferred other words for this general use, such as **well-being**, **a sense of well-being**, **happiness**, or **bliss** <note>original: *Wohl, Wohlgefühl, Glück, Glückseligkeit*</note>. This changes nothing in the substance of the matter; however, these words do not fit linguistic application as well as "pleasure" does, and without explicit explanation, they can serve even less—or fundamentally much less—to designate the most universally applicable concept. This does not prevent us from using them where it is linguistically appropriate, either in place of it or in dependence upon it, as we shall do often enough, since they stand in a dependent relationship to the concept of pleasure in any case.

The fact that we are predominantly inclined to use "pleasure" in a lower sense is evident, for example, in words such as *merry*, *festivity*, *lewdness*, *cravings*, and *voluptuousness* <note>original: *lustig, Lustbarkeit, Lüsternheit, Lüste, Wollust*</note>. This inclination certainly represents an unmistakable and not-to-be-underestimated disadvantage for using the word **pleasure** in that broadest sense—which encompasses the lowest pleasure alongside pleasure of the highest character—since the narrower and lower meaning easily and involuntarily creeps in. If only the language offered a sufficient substitute in its vocabulary! But as it stands, the expression **pleasure** does not directly resist that broadest interpretation; even in ordinary life, one can still speak of a **pleasure in divine things**, a **pleasure in researching the truth**, in **doing good**, and so on. Yet, how could one speak of a "sense of well-being" or a "bliss" in those same things? This linguistic awkwardness in using any substitutes for the expression "pleasure," combined with its already accepted use in psychology in its greatest breadth, leads me to prefer it over other expressions in the field of **aesthetics** as well, without entirely excluding the use of the others.

Insofar as something identical can be abstracted as "pleasure" or "displeasure" from all the various types of pleasure and displeasure, we can assume that in all the diverse causes of pleasure and displeasure, there is also something identical to be found as the ultimate, general, and essential ground of pleasure or displeasure...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 12 regarding the psychological and psychophysical search for the "ultimate ground" of pleasure and displeasure. Given the great interest that the <unclear>yield</unclear> <note>original: *Ertrag*. Fechner uses this economic term to describe the "return" or "output" of pleasure one gets from an experience.</note> of pleasure and displeasure from things and circumstances holds for him, he also finds occasion to form concepts and expressions in relation to them.

Now, there are many concepts, and therefore expressions, which are applied to things and circumstances according to the degree to which they grant a present or immediate <unclear>yield</unclear> of pleasure or displeasure. On the side of pleasure, these include: pleasant, graceful, appealing, lovely, charming, dainty, pretty, beautiful, etc., to which just as many correspond on the side of displeasure. We group both together as **aesthetic categories** and distinguish them as positive and negative.

There are others that refer to the <unclear>yield</unclear> of pleasure and displeasure of things and circumstances with regard to their context and their consequences, insofar as these in turn can bear a character of pleasure or displeasure—the consideration of the present yield not being excluded. Thus, on the side of pleasure: advantageous, useful, purposeful, prosperous, wholesome, beneficial, valuable, good, etc., to which, as positives, no fewer negatives correspond on the side of displeasure. We group both together as **practical categories**, insofar as they are primarily of importance for the direction of our actions.

From the start, even without having the previous definitions of the two main categories in mind, one might find something mysterious in their relationship. Certainly, from a preliminary view, the positive aesthetic categories appear more related to the positive than to the negative practical categories; the same applies when swapping positive and negative. One would rather place "pleasant" and "beautiful" on the same side as "useful" and "good," rather than with "harmful" and "bad," and yet something pleasant can be very bad, and something unpleasant can be very good. How does that reconcile? Quite simply, if one goes back to the definitions above. The present <unclear>yield</unclear> of pleasure can, after all, be outweighed by a greater <unclear>yield</unclear> of displeasure in the consequences, and the present <unclear>yield</unclear> of displeasure by a greater <unclear>yield</unclear> of pleasure. The common relationship of both kinds of categories to...

<summary>Fechner distinguishes between aesthetic categories, which concern immediate pleasure (like beauty), and practical categories, which concern long-term utility and consequences (like goodness). He explains that while we naturally associate the beautiful with the good, they are distinct because immediate pleasure can lead to future suffering.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical derivation of the "practical categories" (such as the good and the useful) versus "aesthetic categories" (such as the beautiful). The author is justifying why he bases these values on the psychological experience of pleasure rather than simply on the human will or "striving."</meta>

Pleasure and displeasure reveal themselves from the outset by the fact that both present a corresponding contrast of the positive and the negative, more so than belongs to pleasure and displeasure themselves; however, this is fully clarified by the aforementioned definitions. Thus, we have good reason to trust this definition of concepts from the most general point of view.

Indisputably, the **practical categories** <note>These are categories related to action and utility, such as "useful" or "beneficial."</note> can be explained through their relationship to our conscious **drives and counter-drives**—or, as also occurs with the same result, through a relationship to a concept of **love** extended beyond the scope of ordinary linguistic usage—rather than through their relationship to pleasure and displeasure. In this view, they are grasped as that which is "striven for" and "worthy of striving," or "awakening love" and "deserving of love." According to the fundamental psychological relationship between pleasure and displeasure on one hand, and conscious drives and counter-drives on the other (about which a few more words will be said under section 4), both explanations actually merge into one another. They will always allow for a translation into one another, though some mistakenly believe that one explanation excludes the other. 

On our part, however, there was a double reason to prefer the relationship of practical categories to **pleasure and displeasure** over the relationship to **striving and counter-striving** in our foundational explanation. First, it was necessary to clearly and immediately highlight the relationship between these categories and the **aesthetic categories** <note>Categories related to immediate sensory or formal appreciation, like "pretty" or "dainty."</note>. This could only happen through a common intermediate concept—namely, through pleasure and displeasure—insofar as these already formed the core of the aesthetic categories. Secondly, however, it seems to me that the general consciousness of language and concepts does, in fact, grasp the practical categories in a more direct relationship to pleasure and displeasure than to striving and counter-striving. 

For one does not find something **advantageous** or **good** simply because one strives for it or should strive for it; rather, one strives for it, or should strive for it, because one finds it advantageous or good. So that this does not result in an identical sentence <note>In modern terms, a "tautology" or circular reasoning.</note>, one must think of "advantageous" and "good" as being determined by a concept other than "striving"; and it is merely a matter of clear analysis to recognize the concept of pleasure (in our sense) within it. If I have therefore admitted above that the practical categories can be explained just as well by their relationship to striving and counter-striving as to pleasure and displeasure, this only holds as long as one considers these categories in isolation. I cannot, however, admit that a system of concepts constructed with the help of the first method of explanation is as easily accessible to general understanding, or as free from hidden or open circular reasoning, as one based on the latter method of explanation.

Among the aesthetic categories, the concept of the ***beautiful***, and among the practical categories, the concept of the ***good***, appears—depending on a wider or narrower interpretation—either as the ***most general*** (i.e., encompassing the others under itself) or as the ***highest*** (i.e., in a certain...)

He positions "beauty" and "goodness" as the ultimate presiding concepts of aesthetics and ethics respectively.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the transition from defining general psychological categories to establishing "the Beautiful" as the primary focus of aesthetics. The author is moving from the general theory of pleasure to the specific definition of aesthetic value.</meta>

...[it arises as] the preferred meaning above the others, at any rate as the **main concept**. We are essentially concerned here only with the first meaning, without being able to completely set aside the second's relationship to it.

To grasp the concept of the beautiful as the **main concept** of <term>Aesthetics</term><note>The philosophical study of beauty, art, and taste.</note> corresponds to general agreement; by some, this doctrine is even explained simply as the "doctrine of the beautiful." The **Beautiful** itself, however, is explained in various ways according to its origin (from God, imagination, inspiration), its essence (the sensory appearance of the Idea, the perfection of sensory appearance, unity in diversity, etc. etc.), or its effect (in gratification, pleasure). On our part, we are bound to the latter mode of explanation—not only by our principle of always proceeding conceptually from the clarification of common linguistic usage, but also by the logical consistency of our general definitions of aesthetic categories, which for their part return to such clarification in a more general way.

<margin>?</margin>
According to this, "beautiful" in the **broadest** sense—which is also the most common—refers to everything that possesses the quality of awakening pleasure **immediately**, rather than through reflection or its consequences. This is especially true if it possesses this quality to a significant degree and relatively purely. Meanwhile, for a lesser or only relative degree of pleasure, we prefer expressions such as **pleasant** (often with a sensory connotation), appealing, or pretty; we designate various shades of pleasure through other expressions such as **graceful**, **dainty**, **sublime**, **splendid**, and so on. In that broadest sense, something can taste just as "beautiful" as it can look "beautiful"; there are "beautiful" souls just as there are "beautiful" bodies, "beautiful" ideas just as there are "beautiful" statues. Common usage indeed not only tolerates all of this, but it is also

good that it does so, for otherwise we would have no common designation for all these things, which we nevertheless require. In the **narrower** sense of aesthetics and the study of art, however, something is called beautiful only insofar as it is capable of letting us draw higher-than-merely <term>sensory pleasure</term> **immediately** from the sensory. <meta>This page continues the author's hierarchical definition of beauty. Having previously defined beauty in its broadest sense as anything providing immediate pleasure, the author now transitions into the "narrower" and "narrowest" definitions, specifically addressing the distinction between natural and artistic beauty, and the intersection of beauty with morality (the Good).</meta>

...to enter into this, there will be ample opportunity. Even in this **narrower sense**, however, the term "beautiful" is used more readily for an object the fuller and purer its impression of pleasure is; the various shades of this impression are covered by specific **aesthetic categories**, the discussion of which aesthetics textbooks usually consider one of their primary tasks. If, however, some wish to see the term "beautiful" in this narrower sense applied only to **works of art** (as creations of the <term>mind</term><note>The German *Geist* can mean mind, spirit, or intellect depending on context; here it refers to human creative agency.</note>), this is an arbitrary restriction which general educated usage does not share, and against which the beauty of a living human being or a landscape may rightly defend itself. This does not prevent us from recognizing the differences between **natural beauty** and **artistic beauty**; we have both terms precisely to distinguish between the two. What is certain is only that the concept of beauty in the narrower sense is more often fulfilled by the artistically beautiful than by the naturally beautiful, the closer consideration of which belongs elsewhere.

However, one must still speak of a concept of the beautiful in the **narrowest sense**. With the previous definitions, we do not move beyond the **subjectivity** of the beautiful; according to those, one person might still find beautiful what another excludes from that domain. Now, however, not everything that happens to please *should* please. There are not only laws according to which pleasure and displeasure actually occur—which will be discussed later—but also <term>prescriptive laws</term><note>original: *Forderungsgesetze*, literally "laws of demand" or "requirement." These are normative rules about what *ought* to be found pleasing.</note> of pleasure and displeasure. These involve the rules of **good taste** and the dependent rules of the **education of taste**, which do not stand in contradiction to the former laws, but rather utilize them in the right direction. To define the concept of the beautiful in the narrowest sense—the **truly beautiful**, the **authentic beauty**—which not only pleases from a higher point of view but also has a *right* to please, one must also consider the **value** of the pleasure that enters into the liking. Accordingly, the concept of authentic beauty is subject to an essential co-determination by the concept of the **Good**, upon which the concept of value depends (in a manner to be considered later). In short, one could say: in the concept of the beautiful in the narrowest sense, the general concepts of the **Beautiful** and the **Good** intersect, whereas otherwise they reach beyond one another.

<summary>The author distinguishes between beauty in a narrow sense (often associated with art but including nature) and beauty in the narrowest sense (the "truly beautiful"). He argues that true beauty is not merely subjective but is defined by its intersection with "the Good" and normative rules of taste.</summary>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s transition from a purely subjective definition of beauty to a more objective one. Having previously argued that beauty is that which provides pleasure, he now begins to distinguish between mere pleasure and the "truly beautiful," which carries moral or divine weight, while reiterating his preference for an "Aesthetics from Below" (empirical) over an "Aesthetics from Above" (metaphysical).</meta>

Even in this **narrowest** formulation, the concept of the **beautiful**—unlike the concept of the **good** in itself—always essentially retains the ability to immediately arouse favor and, with it, **pleasure**. However, not every instance of favor or every pleasure is of equal value, and thus equally "good," when one considers its consequences and interconnections. This will be discussed in greater detail in the subsequent section regarding **taste**.

Accordingly, nothing prevents us from deriving the **truly beautiful**—that which is worthy of arousing pleasure—in the highest instance from **God**, from whom everything must ultimately be derived, and in whom everything must finally conclude and reach its summit. This allows us to relate beauty to the most valuable, highest **Ideas**, explaining it as their expression within the **earthly** and **sensory** realms. However, according to our method of working **"from below,"** we cannot begin with such explanations; instead, for the sake of brevity, we must make use of the word "beautiful" from the very start. We use it to point toward a specific achievement <note>The "achievement" Fechner refers to is the production of pleasure or aesthetic effect.</note> that everyone, even outside the fields of **aesthetics** and **art theory**, is accustomed to finding indicated by that word.

There is no single, simple characteristic that makes things "beautiful" in either the widest or narrowest sense. There are, however, many attempts to capture the essence or core of beauty from one perspective or another through a simple phrase. The systems of **"Aesthetics from Above"** <note>This refers to philosophical systems that start with abstract metaphysical principles (like "The Absolute") and try to deduce what beauty is.</note> usually start with such an attempt, outdoing or contesting one another, and never reaching an end. In contrast, **"Aesthetics from Below"**—given what was said earlier about its character—initially offers only explanations to clarify how language is used. This is done so that we may speak clearly about the **laws** according to which something pleases and *ought* to please, and which claim to remain correct regardless of how one defines the concept of the beautiful.

It is certain that, however broadly living language and conceptual usage may grasp the concept of beauty, it refers not to the origin or essential nature of the beautiful, but rather to its **effect** in producing pleasure. In this regard, it is illustrative to compare the concept of beauty with that of **wholesomeness** <note>original: *Heilsamkeit*; this term implies both healthiness and a more general sense of being "good for the soul."</note>. This concept, too, has formed only in relation to a certain effect of a means, and it can only be established clearly and factually in relation to this effect.

->Fechner, *Propaedeutic of Aesthetics*<- <note>original: *Vorschule d. Aesthetik*</note>

<summary>Fechner argues that while beauty is inherently linked to pleasure, the "true" beautiful can be linked to divine Ideas. He contrasts his empirical "Aesthetics from Below" with metaphysical systems, emphasizing that beauty should be defined by its effects rather than its abstract essence.</summary>
<keywords>Aesthetics from Below, Aesthetics from Above, God, pleasure, taste, wholesomeness, Ideas, Gustav Fechner</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page where Fechner proposed an empirical approach to aesthetics. He is now using a medical analogy to explain why we should define beauty by its "achievement" (pleasure) rather than its metaphysical "essence" or "origin."</meta>

...which consists in making people healthy. If one wanted to define the concept of the **healing power** of remedies in relation to a certain general quality or origin of those means, and make **medical science** dependent on this, it would be just as invalid as if one sought to define the concept of **beauty** accordingly and make **aesthetics** dependent on it. Rather, once the concept of **healing power** has been established in relation to the **achievement** <note>Fechner uses the term "Leistung" (achievement/performance) to describe the functional effect an object has on a person—in this case, the effect of health or pleasure.</note> to be carried out by those means, the question of *how* things become healing is merely a question of the **laws of health**, which is impossible to answer within the definition itself. 

In the same way, once the concept of **beauty** has been established in relation to the **achievement** to be carried out by the beautiful object, the question of *how* things become beautiful is merely a question of the **laws of beauty** or of **favor** <note>The term "Gefallen" refers to the immediate mental approval or "liking" one feels when perceiving something beautiful.</note>. It is equally impossible to answer this sufficiently within the definition itself, as we know as little of a general property that makes things **pleasure-giving** as we do of one that makes them **healing**. Only when we have succeeded in describing the ground of this "achievement" of the beautiful as clearly and simply as the achievement itself could a fundamental explanation of beauty be based upon it.

Nevertheless, failing to recognize the impossibility of this, the explanations of beauty placed at the head of aesthetics until now have primarily clung to references regarding **origin** or **essence**; for this very reason, they have remained unsatisfactory for a successful development of aesthetics. It is not that all aestheticians have not conceded—or even demanded—that the achievement of the beautiful for those capable of recognizing it lies in a **higher pleasure**; rather, the fact that all of them, despite other occurring deviations, agree on this point proves that this achievement is essential to the concept of beauty. 

However, it was believed that this was not enough for a definition of beauty, and seekers tried to replace it with a definition that, instead of stating the achievement, immediately included the **condition** or **principle** of that achievement. Meanwhile, they treated the achievement of **pleasure** as something non-essential to the definition, considering it only incidentally or secondarily. But all such explanations are harmful, as they do not provide what is necessary to clarify general language and conceptual usage.

<summary>Fechner argues that beauty should be defined by its functional effect—producing pleasure—rather than its metaphysical essence, comparing aesthetics to medicine, where "healing" is defined by the result of health rather than the origin of the medicine.</summary>
<keywords>Aesthetics from below, healing power, achievement, pleasure, laws of beauty, definition of beauty, favor, medical analogy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s critique of "top-down" aesthetics. He previously argued that defining beauty by its "essence" is less productive than defining it by its "achievement" (the pleasure it produces). He now points out how even famous philosophers have been distracted by formal definitions before transitioning to a comparison between the Beautiful and the Good.</meta>

...actually allows for use as a starting point and introduction to the factual investigation; instead, they seemingly provide that which cannot be given through any general explanation in a simple sentence, and thereby lead away from the correct path of its investigation.

Now, there are certainly also aestheticians—such as **Kant**, **Bouterweck**, and **Fries** <note>Immanuel Kant, Friedrich Bouterweck, and Jakob Friedrich Fries were influential German philosophers who grappled with the subjective nature of taste and beauty.</note>—who, in determining the **Beautiful**, proceed from its achievement in **pleasure** or its property of **pleasing**. However, instead of taking the path from there toward investigating the laws of **pleasure** and **displeasure**, they remain stuck in formal determinations about the nature of pleasure in the beautiful, or they fall back into the habit of trying to establish the origin or ground of the property of pleasing sufficiently within the definition itself.

But let us turn from the primary concept of **aesthetic categories** to that of **practical categories**, in order to clearly perceive its relationships of difference and kinship.

The concept of **the Good** is explained—just like that of **the Beautiful**—according to its origin, essence, or achievement. And once again, for our part, we are bound to the relationship of its **achievement** <note>Fechner uses "Leistung" (achievement/performance) to emphasize the functional effect an object or action has on the world.</note>, no less to align ourselves with the most common usage of the concept than to maintain its relationship to the Beautiful, as established in the general relationship between aesthetic and practical categories. According to this, in the broadest sense—which is also the most common—everything is called **good** insofar as it is a presumptive condition for more **pleasure** <note>Lust</note> than **pain** <note>Unlust</note>, or a means to prevent or erase more pain than it creates, with regard to a considered or unspecified circle of contexts and consequences. According to this, one can speak just as well of **good weather** or a **good harvest** as of a **good person** or a **good state institution**. 

Conversely, **good** in the narrower sense of **ethics** and **religion** is applied only insofar as the concept is focused on the **disposition** <note>Gesinnung</note>, action, thoughts, and strivings <note>original: "Dichten und Trachten," a traditional German idiom referring to the inner inclinations and habitual aims of a person.</note> of rational beings—and in the highest instance, the **Divine Being**. According to this, a person is only to be called good insofar as they act out of a certain disposition and according to rules by which, presumably, **happiness** rather than unhappiness—and thus more pleasure than pain—is promoted in the world. Even **God** is only called good insofar as one presupposes that He makes arrangements for...

...the welfare <note>The original German "Heile" implies both healing and salvation/well-being.</note> of humanity, that is, its happiness, from the highest and ultimate points of view, indeed, even turning disaster itself toward this end.*)

**Advantageous**, **useful**, **expedient**, and other **practical categories** are subordinated to the broadest concept of the **Good**. They are distinguished by being applied only with regard to a more or less specific and limited circle of connections and consequences, and primarily in relation to external things and circumstances. This is in contrast to those things which fall into the circle of the **Good** in the narrower sense—the **ethically** or **morally Good**. Conversely, the **ethical categories**, such as honest, lawful, faithful, conscientious, charitable, magnanimous, noble, and so on—in short, all **virtue designations**—apply to the specific qualities of the latter.

If the **morally** and **divinely Good** is brought under a common category with so many other types of "Good," and thus appears merely subordinate within this entire commonality, this conceptual subordination detracts nothing from its objective height. An objectively highest level always remains the highest, despite the fact that it conceptually enters into a common category with lower levels; indeed, without this, it could not occupy the rank of a highest level at all.

If one wishes to explain the **ethically Good** as that which, in the disposition and will of man, is in accordance with the **divine will**, this explanation does not objectively contradict the one above; however, it can only be appropriate within the context of **religion**. Even then, one would still have to ask: what then is in the sense of the divine will? And even if one attempts to answer this question through the **Ten Commandments** and the word of the **Bible**: "Love God above all things and your neighbor—

<margin>*) To be sure, one falls into insoluble **antinomies** <note>An antinomy is a logical contradiction between two principles that both seem equally true. Here, Fechner refers to the "Problem of Evil": how can God be all-powerful and all-good if evil exists?</note> with the way theology attempts to reconcile the **omnipotence** and **goodness of God**, both at once, with the existence of **evil** in the world. For my part, I believe that evil exists in the world neither through the will nor through the permission of God, but through a **metaphysical necessity** of existence. However, I also believe that there exists a just as necessary and related **tendency** in the world to constantly lift, improve, and reconcile this evil. Above all individual human conscious tendencies in this direction, there exists the more general, higher, infinitely reaching divine tendency, which is precisely where the goodness of God resides. This is not the place to further elaborate or substantiate this, as the goal here is not to prove the matter of goodness, but to explain its concept in contrast to that of **beauty**. Yet even the most hideous thing can be found to be good, such as a bad-tasting and bad-looking **medicine**, provided that its immediate yield of displeasure is outweighed by the removal of greater subsequent displeasure.

Insofar as, according to the foregoing, both the **Beautiful** and the **Good** can be used in very different breadths, the rule of usage for us will be that we grasp them more broadly or narrowly as the circle of consideration expands or contracts; thus, we grasp them in the broadest sense as long as limiting determinations do not assert themselves automatically or are not explicitly asserted.

The common man generally uses only the concept **beautiful** out of all **aesthetic categories**, as he feels no need in his little-developed conceptual system to engage in finer distinctions of what is immediately pleasing; thus, **beautiful** in its broadest sense represents all other aesthetic categories for him. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the definitions of "Beautiful" and "Good." ...pleasant, pleasing, graceful, delicate, neat; he says everywhere only: that is beautiful.

But even the more educated, who are familiar with finer distinctions, often use the expression "beautiful" in its broadest sense when they are not explicitly concerned with asserting those distinctions. Consequently, they say without hesitation: "that tastes beautiful," or "smells beautiful"; they speak of a "beautiful tone," "beautiful weather," a "beautiful idea," or a "beautiful proof." None of this fits the narrower conceptual definition of beauty upheld by **aesthetics of the higher style** <term>Aesthetik höheren Stils: a formal, academic approach to the study of art and beauty that seeks to separate "pure" beauty from mere physical sensation</term>. According to that stricter definition, neither that which is merely sensory-pleasing nor that which falls entirely into the internal spiritual domain is categorized under the concept of "beautiful."

Exactly the same as with the "Beautiful" occurs in these relations with the **Good** <note>original: "G u t"</note>. Expressions like "useful," "advantageous," "purposeful," "valuable," or "wholesome" are rarely heard from the common man; for all practical categories, he has only the same expression, **good**, just as he uses the expression **beautiful** for all aesthetic categories. He might use "blessing" <note>original: "Segen"</note> to describe a great abundance of the good. In both cases, the most general meaning is also the most common. Educated speech and conceptual usage distinguish practical and aesthetic categories in the same way, yet often they cannot escape the broadest definition of the concept **Good** any more than that of **Beautiful**. This is because a general term for that which provides **pleasure** <term>Lust: in this philosophical context, it refers to any positive sensation, joy, or satisfaction</term>—while taking contexts and consequences into account—is often just as necessary as the distinction of sub-concepts and nuances is, at times, of no interest.

Something similar to what was said about the relationship of the main categories, **Beautiful** and **Good**, to their subordinate categories can be said about the relationship between these two main categories themselves. In daily life, they are distinguished exactly in the sense described above.

Thus, in daily conversation, one says to another: "it is beautiful that you are coming," when one wants to express the immediate pleasure that the other's arrival awakens; "it is good that you are coming," when one thinks of the consequences of his arrival in terms of pleasure or the prevention of **displeasure** <term>Unlust: the philosophical opposite of 'Lust'; it encompasses pain, discomfort, or dissatisfaction</term>. One speaks of "beautiful weather" or "good weather," depending on whether one refers to the immediately pleasing impression of it or the pleasing...

<meta>This page continues the linguistic and psychological distinction between the "Beautiful" and the "Good." This page focuses on the role of "consequences" and "utility" in defining the Good versus the Beautiful.</meta>

...beneficial consequences it promises. — Regarding the same painting, one person might say it is a beautiful one, while another says it is a good painting. In essence, they likely mean to express the same thing, but the first person views the painting in terms of how it actually brings **pleasure** <note>original: "Lust"; in this context, it refers to psychological pleasure or aesthetic satisfaction</note> through its presence; whereas the second views it as possessing such qualities that it *can* bring pleasure under the necessary circumstances, without implying anything about the painting's current effect of pleasure in their expression. — One calls a house **beautifully built** if it is constructed in such proportions and so decorated that it grants immediate pleasure through its appearance. Yet such a house could be built in a way that it would collapse over our heads sooner or later, or bring about inconveniences during its use that would be greater than the pleasure its sight now grants us. In that case, we could not call it **well-built** <note>original: "gut gebaut" (literally: good-built)</note>; but we would also no longer find it beautiful if its poor construction made itself so apparent in the immediate impression that the **displeasure** <note>original: "Unlust"; the psychological opposite of "Lust," often translated as pain or discomfort</note> of the consequences was transferred onto it through association. — I heard someone say: »If you **girdle** <note>original: "ringelt"; a gardening technique where a strip of bark is removed from a branch to force more nutrients into the fruit</note> the grapevine, the grapes ripen earlier and grow larger.« »That is certainly quite beautiful,« another replied; »but I still do not consider it **good**; it will suffer from this unnatural treatment and, on the whole, one will lose more than gain from it.« With the expression "beautiful," he referred here to the immediate gain in pleasure; with the expression "good," to the total gain, including the consequences. — When a matter that has caused us difficulties for a long time is finally brought to a proper conclusion, or an evil that has plagued us for a long time is finally lifted, one will—despite the immediate pleasure one takes in this—not say: »now it's beautiful,« but rather »now it's good«; this is because the connection between the success and the passing of those lifted and henceforth eliminated difficulties or grievances appears even more vividly before our eyes than the present joyful success itself.

Since, in the case of **goodness**, immediate effects of pleasure—where they exist—are always also taken into consideration, they will naturally be the *only* thing considered when they are the only thing present, or when there is no specific reason to think of consequences in terms of pleasure or displeasure beyond them. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the definition of "value" as a mathematical or psychological standard of goodness. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page's discussion on the difficulty of calculating these values with absolute precision.</meta>

...[calculate] values within certain limits of certainty, and we must be satisfied with this, insofar as we are unable to push that certainty any further. Daily, hourly, however, man practices looking at everything he encounters in terms of its relative contribution to the increase, preservation, or reduction of human **happiness**, in short, to its **pleasure- and displeasure-yield**. Without being aware of it, pleasure and displeasure results calculate themselves in his feelings as a total outcome, such that he arrives at **determinations of value** for things—he himself knows not how, and often without reason appearing to have played any part in it; although reason is neither entirely idle in this process nor should it be. Meanwhile, the means available to the individual for obtaining correct value-determinations do not go very far, and so he rests primarily on value-determinations that have been established through the experiences and reflections of a collective throughout the course of history; to which he himself can nevertheless contribute something, in order to fix or alter them.

Whether one chooses to relate the **concept of value** to the *conditions* of pleasure or to the *pleasure* itself is materially indifferent, provided that one estimates those conditions solely according to the measure of their **pleasure-yield** <note>original: "Lustertrag"; the net gain of satisfaction or positive experience produced by an object or act</note>. The **value** or **lack of value** <note>original: "Unwerth"; literally "un-value" or worthlessness, describing something with a negative impact</note> of a pleasure, however—according to which it deserves to be pursued or not—is to be measured, according to the general **principle of goodness**, not merely by its own magnitude, but also by the magnitude of the pleasure or displeasure of which it can be seen as the source. We say that a pleasure is a source of pleasure or displeasure insofar as its existence depends on conditions, or is connected with such, which result in pleasure or displeasure. For example:

*   The pleasure in **doing good** <note>original: "Wohlthun"; benevolent or charitable acts</note> is connected with impulses that are suited to increase the pleasure in the world.
*   The pleasure in **cruelty** is connected with impulses suited to diminish it.
*   The pleasure in a **moderate indulgence** is connected with such a preservation of the person that makes him capable of enjoying and creating pleasure in the future.
*   The pleasure in an **immoderate indulgence** is connected with such a disruption of health that much greater **displeasure** <note>original: "Unlust"</note> is brought about as a result.

According to this, a pleasure is generally to be declared bad, and therefore of negative value, insofar as the assumption exists that, according to the conditions upon which...

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page regarding the calculation of "value." It moves from the individual's subjective experience to a broader social and moral framework, asserting that the true value of pleasure or displeasure is measured by its impact on the "total state of humanity."</meta>

...circumstances in which it exists, according to the context into which it enters, whether it generates greater **displeasure** in its consequences or prevents greater **pleasure** from arising than it contains within itself. If **value** is to be understood in its most general sense, these consequences must not only relate to the individual’s own state of pleasure, but to the collective state of pleasure of all humanity. Accordingly, **displeasure** <note>original: "Unlust"; the opposite of pleasure, encompassing pain, sorrow, or dissatisfaction</note> can even possess a higher value than pleasure if it is capable of being outweighed by greater subsequent pleasure or of preventing greater subsequent displeasure. And although, as admitted, a precise estimation of this is not possible, the estimation of value must fundamentally be placed upon this viewpoint, because any other estimation would be subject to even greater lack of clarity while being at least as impossible to calculate accurately.

The pleasure of the wicked and pleasure *in* what is evil do not, according to this, have the same value as the pleasure of the good and pleasure *in* what is good—even if they are of equal magnitude—insofar as that pleasure, by the very nature of good and evil, is linked to a preponderance of displeasure-consequences, while the latter is linked to a preponderance of pleasure-consequences. The happy state of the wicked person maintains him in his evil inclinations, strengthens his capacity for evil, and thus maintains and strengthens a source of general displeasure. In contrast, the punishment of the wicked—whether divine or human—gains value, even though it causes direct displeasure. This value is not based on the empty principle of **retaliation** <note>original: "Retaliation"; the concept of "an eye for an eye" or lex talionis</note> or the dogmatic principle of **atonement** <note>original: "Sühne"; the idea of making amends for a sin or crime to satisfy a religious or legal requirement</note>, where the question of "Why?" always remains unanswered, but rather insofar as it improves, restrains, or deters the wicked; in short, it checks evil as a source of displeasure. The more these conditions are met, the greater the value the punishment will have. *)

<margin>Even **higher pleasure** (pleasure of a higher character) has greater value than lower pleasure only insofar as it is simultaneously a source of more pleasure. The pleasure of a child in their innocent play, or the pleasure of a diligent worker in their simple meal, although "lower," is nonetheless more valuable than pleasure derived from a wicked intrigue or an immoral novel.</margin>

---
*) I believe that only when we learn to grasp the value of punishment from the above viewpoint will we move beyond the one-sidedness that still dominates the understanding of its principles.

<summary>The author argues that the moral value of an action is determined by its total contribution to human happiness, justifying punishment as a tool for reform and deterrence rather than mere revenge.</summary>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into how we calculate "value." The author shifts from the immediate, subjective experience of pleasure to a broader ethical framework, distinguishing between the "pleasant," the "beautiful," and the "useful," ultimately arguing that moral goodness represents the highest form of value because it sustains society as a whole.</meta>

In general, the breadth of the **concept of value** follows the various ways in which the concept of the **good** can be understood, and vice-versa; according to which **value** is often measured only by a limited circle of contexts and consequences—those currently before one's eyes—including the immediate yield of pleasure. However, if one does not grasp **pleasure** and **displeasure** <note>original: "Lust und Unlust"; terms referring to the basic spectrum of positive and negative sensory and emotional experience</note> merely in a lower, common sense—if one does not estimate the conditions of pleasure and displeasure merely by their presumed yield in individual, egoistic, momentary pleasure and displeasure, but rather by the presumed yield for the whole for the sake of the whole—then one will thereby possess the true and full value of these conditions from the highest, most general perspective. An absolute estimation of the true value of things and circumstances is, of course, an ideal; yet it can simply be said that **virtue** is truly more valuable than **vice**, and in general, relative judgments in this regard are easier to make than absolute ones.

To the **pleasant** and the **beautiful** in the narrower sense, we can under certain circumstances attribute greater value than to that which is only **useful** according to its consequences. This is true, first, because the immediate effect of pleasure from the pleasant and the beautiful can outweigh the total effect of pleasure from the useful (which, by its very definition, is only considered within a limited scope). Secondly, it is because the concept of the **beautiful** in the narrowest sense—the **truly beautiful**—includes a consideration of pleasure in its consequences as a secondary characteristic. The truly beautiful can, through the inspirations it grants in a good sense, be more useful than what is merely called "useful." In contrast, the **good** in the narrower and higher sense—the **morally and divinely good**—seems to humans to be that which, among all things, confers and possesses the highest value. This is because within it lie the most general and firm conditions for the preservation of a flourishing state for humanity in general. Without rational reflection, humans sense within the disposition and conduct of the moral person a feeling of security in this regard, insofar as it depends on human will and action; and likewise, they sense the opposite in the immoral.

We see, for example, someone who is witty, spirited, graceful in behavior, clever, and beautiful; who would not want to be like this person? Who does not envy them for all the pleasure they effortlessly scatter around themselves and gather in?
