<meta>This page continues the author’s exploration of how we determine the "value" of a person. Having previously established that value is linked to the total yield of "pleasure" (well-being) for the whole of society, the author now applies this calculation to specific human characters—contrasting the charming but dishonest man with the dull but dutiful one, and discussing why we value aesthetic genius like Goethe's even alongside moral failings.</meta>

But now it is said: he is a bad person, dissolute, harsh toward his family, dishonest; and he is lost in our opinion and respect; even while he entertains us or hosts us, an unsettling feeling creeps over us. We feel clearly that all the pleasure <note>original: "Lust"; in this philosophical context, "Lust" refers to the broad spectrum of positive experience, from simple enjoyment to deep satisfaction, rather than specifically sexual desire</note> that his wit, his spirit, and his clever behavior immediately bring to him and others does not weigh as much as the displeasure <note>original: "Unlust"; the opposite of "Lust," encompassing pain, dissatisfaction, or social harm</note> which his dissoluteness will bring to himself through its consequences, or the sad hours he causes his wife and household, or the misfortune he brings upon others through his dishonesty. All that pleasure appears to us now only like the white foam over a dark pool of displeasure. We do not, of course, tell ourselves this in detail; but our feeling—educated by countless experiences and lessons—has the power to unite everything that the intellect could say individually into a single resultant force.

Let us now contrast him with the dry, steady, even pedantic man of unassuming appearance, who does not know how to entertain anyone well, but who does his duty, manages his office in good order, promotes the community and useful institutions to the best of his ability, lives in peace with his wife, and raises his children well; who contributes to the pleasure of others as much as he can, not through intellectual means which are not at his disposal, but through material ones. We would, of course, not like to be as dry and pedantic as he is; but in comparing his value with the former, we will not hesitate for a moment to place him above the other. We will, as we express it, respect him more highly than the former; but to respect <note>original: "achten"</note> is, after all, nothing other than to estimate value—as we feel clearly how much more pleasure flows on the whole from his way of acting than from that of the former.

Yet we do not value qualities in a person merely insofar as they can be brought under the concept of the moral; rather, everything that proceeds from a person and brings a wealth of pleasure of a higher character into the world is held in high regard by the world; only a correct feeling knows how to place the "higher" even above the "high." How highly Goethe <note>Johann Wolfgang von Goethe (1749–1832), the preeminent German poet and polymath, often scrutinized for his personal life</note> is esteemed, despite the fact that he was morally no greater than so many insignificant spirits. How high in value stands a female singer if she is beautiful and sings beautifully, even if one knows nothing of her morality. And even if one knows that she is somewhat flighty, one forgives her much for the sake of her beauty and her beautiful singing, and would still rather be this flighty—though not bad—being, than a so-called "moral silly goose" <note>original: "moralische dumme Gans"; a disparaging term for a person who is virtuous but dull, narrow-minded, or lacks any spark of life</note>. Why? Because the former is a living spring of pleasure, and the latter is a parched puddle. The measure of pleasure cuts through everything. But if that same singer, who carries us away with her song and grace, also appears modest and noble in her character, how unspeakably higher do we then place her than both the flighty one who throws herself away and the "silly goose." We feel that the world as a whole gains infinitely more pleasure from this than through the occasional indiscretions of the one or the well-meaning but dull will of the other.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of philosophical value systems, specifically transitioning from the concept of "the interesting" into the "trinity" of the Beautiful, the Good, and the True. It picks up from the previous discussion on Lotze’s definitions of pleasure and value.</meta>

even the charm of novelty can make a thing *interesting*, so long as it is new to us; such was the case with the ugly Pastrana <note>Julia Pastrana (1834–1860) was a world-famous performer with hypertrichosis who was exhibited as a "curiosity." In 19th-century aesthetics, she was often used as an example of how something traditionally considered "ugly" could still be "interesting" through its uniqueness.</note>. But also the utility or harm that a thing performs or promises from any point of view can draw our *interest* toward it; and in phrases such as: that someone "has their *interest* in view," the concept of *interest* even coincides with that of utility or advantage itself.

With the concepts of the *Beautiful* and the *Good*, the concept of the *True* is everywhere grouped into a kind of *trinity* <note>The "Transcendentals" or the "Great Triad" of classical philosophy: Truth, Beauty, and Goodness.</note>. Let us here, with the greatest possible brevity, examine its position relative to those other concepts.

A representation is *simply*, *absolutely*, or *objectively true* when it exists without contradiction alongside every other real or possibly conceivable representation that is itself free of contradiction; or, when it belongs to the *total circle* of representations that exist harmoniously together. A representation is called *certain* when there is a consciousness that the conditions of *truth* have been fulfilled. However, to the extent that this concept of the simply *True* and *Certain* suffers restrictions or is only grasped conditionally—for instance, when applied only to certain fields of thought or specific beings, or when the conditions of *truth* or *certainty* are thought to be fulfilled more or less incompletely—more or less relatively valid categories take the place of the absolute categories of *truth* and *certainty*. These can be grouped with the absolutes as *theoretical* categories, such as: *internally true*, *externally true*, *subjectively certain*, *correct*, *exact*, *apt*, *convincing*, *reliable*, *doubtless*, *credible*, *probable*, and so on. These possess a positive character, and there are just as many corresponding terms with a negative character.

At first, one misses a relationship between the *True* and the *Beautiful* and *Good* in these definitions; but even if such a relationship does not immediately appear in the abstract definition, it appears as a fundamental one in actual fact. If we were able to define the *Beautiful* and the *Good* in relation to the unknown ultimate general ground of pleasure <term>Lust: a broad philosophical term for satisfaction or positive affect</term>, then the conceptual relationship to the *True* might also be found here. Indeed, an innate feeling of pleasure is not only linked directly to the recognition of truth and the finding of truths—which acts as a driving force in science and in...

<meta>This page continues the definition of "Aesthetics," picking up from the incomplete sentence at the end of page 31/32 which introduced the term as a philosophical concept.</meta>

...to which many later thinkers have followed, although the actual practice of aesthetics has never truly followed suit. Indeed, how far would aesthetics have to reach in one direction and how narrowly would it have to contract in another, if it were to fulfill this definition without exceeding it? The entire range of relationships within sensory perception, including its barely separable connection to physiological and physical conditions, would belong within it; yet, of Goethe’s *Faust* and the Sistine Madonna <note>A famous masterpiece by the Renaissance painter Raphael, often used in the 19th century as the ultimate example of artistic beauty.</note>, nothing would be subject to aesthetic consideration except that which purely touches the senses. 

Aesthetics has never been grasped so broadly in one direction and so narrowly in the other, not even by Baumgarten <note>Alexander Gottlieb Baumgarten (1714–1762), the German philosopher who coined the term "Aesthetics" to mean the "science of sensory knowledge."</note> himself; rather, by elevating "the Beautiful" as the "perfection of sensory perception" <note>original: "Vollkommene der sinnlichen Wahrnehmung"</note> to the main object of study, and by drawing in perspectives that reach beyond purely sensory relationships, he transitioned into the definition of aesthetics that is now traditional. From this, one can assert that from the very beginning—as is still the case today in the usage of the term <term>aesthetic</term> <note>original: "ästhetisch"</note> and in its practical application, if not always in its formal definition—the doctrine's relationship to pleasure and displeasure has essentially asserted itself.

Therefore, one now generally understands <term>aesthetic</term> to refer to everything that relates to the conditions of immediate pleasure and displeasure in that which enters into us through the senses. This is done without focusing solely on the purely sensory side; rather, it includes the relationships of the sensory (as in music), "associative ideas" <note>original: "Associationsvorstellungen"; the mental process where one thought or sensation triggers another based on past experience.</note> that merge immediately with the sensory (as with words in poetry and forms in the visual arts), and finally the relationships of these ideas, insofar as pleasure or displeasure is attached to all of them. 

Indeed, according to a narrower usage of the aesthetic, one even excludes that which is able to awaken pleasure or displeasure merely through its sensory effect (or an effect only slightly beyond it), in order to include only that which is immediately pleasing or displeasing from higher viewpoints and according to higher relationships. Thus, for example, one considers the pleasing impression made by a pure, full tone, a deep saturated color, or the pleasant fragrance of a...

Now, one must admit that this restriction of the aesthetic corresponds not only to common usage in daily life, but also to the generally prevailing scientific usage; from the latter side, this restriction is often even emphatically insisted upon. Yet not every scientific treatment of aesthetics has adhered to this, and with a somewhat more general conception of the field, it is simply impossible to stop there, for the twofold reason that there are enough viewpoints which span across both lower and higher pleasure, and that both can (according to Section V) combine uniformly into a larger and higher product. Let us therefore also follow the narrower usage in the following only to the extent that the circle of consideration narrows accordingly, without fundamentally limiting ourselves to it; which, moreover, is neither intended to reform the usage of everyday life, nor to prevent others from using the narrower term for a circle of consideration that was more narrowly conceived from the start.

Admittedly, even today aesthetics is not everywhere explicitly explained in relation to pleasure and displeasure, <term>pleasure and unpleasure</term> <note>original: "Lust und Unlust," standard psychological terms of the 19th century referring to the basic spectrum of positive and negative affect.</note>; insofar as it is explained as a doctrine of the Beautiful, but makes the concept of the Beautiful dependent on other concepts, such as Idea, perfection, and so on, of which we spoke briefly above. <meta>This page continues the argument that aesthetics must be grounded in the experience of pleasure and displeasure, contrasting the author’s empirical "Aesthetics from Below" with the abstract "Aesthetics from Above."</meta>

...to include the definition of these concepts, in order to clearly designate the direction of its task from the very beginning. And if this is not in the prevailing sense of the **Aesthetics from Above** <note>A term coined by the author, Gustav Fechner, to describe philosophical systems that start with abstract ideals of "Beauty" rather than observed human experience.</note>, I find the reason—based on a previously made observation—in the fact that such an approach fails to "hit the nail on the head" with its opening explanations. Consequently, it takes a more or less skewed direction; that is, one does not actually learn what really matters regarding the **pleasure** and **displeasure** <note>original: "Gefallen und Missfallen"</note> derived from things, but only to what extent something subordinates itself to the ideal viewpoints placed at the top, for which the concept of pleasure and displeasure is merely incidental.

From a certain point of view, it would certainly be desirable to be able to use the term *aesthetic* in a different sense than the one it has acquired through its relationship to pleasure and displeasure—if only this relationship were not already so firmly established in prevailing language and conceptual usage, and if another term were available as a substitute. Every object with which we interact has, through that very interaction, acquired for us a **significance** <note>original: "Bedeutung"</note> that reaches beyond its sensory impression; this significance asserts itself simultaneously with that impression, as will be discussed in detail in our Section 9. For example, in a crown we see not just a yellow band with several elevations, but also an object intended to cover the head of a king. Undeniably, one might wish to designate such impressions—which are composed of a sensory element and an associated meaning—with a specific word; however, no such word exists unless one wishes to use *aesthetic* for it. Yet, doing so would cause the relationship to **pleasantness** and **unpleasantness** to drop away as an essential feature; for among such impressions, there are plenty that are indifferent. The pleasant and unpleasant ones would then form only a special category, and could certainly be treated as being of primary interest in a special section of an aesthetics based on the previous general concept.

This is essentially the conception of the aesthetic and of aesthetics advocated by **C. Hermann** in his *Outline of General Aesthetics*, 1857 (published by Fr. Fleischer) and his *Aesthetic Color Theory*, 1876 (published by M. Schäfer); and I do not see what could be objected to in principle against the establishment of such a doctrine, of which our aesthetics would, in a certain sense, form only that special department—insofar as one does not wish to posit purely direct impressions without associated meaning. However, Hermann relies only on the result of the **Principle of Association** <term>Principle of Association</term> <note>The psychological theory that our enjoyment of an object comes from the memories and ideas we associate with it, rather than just its physical form.</note>, without going into the development of the principle itself, and on the whole follows the path **from Above** rather than **from Below**, so that our agreement with him remains only partial. Furthermore, a doctrine which, like ours, places the viewpoint of pleasure and displeasure at the top and treats associated meanings only

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s argument from page 35. He is explaining why his "Aesthetics from Below" focuses on the immediate human experience of pleasure and displeasure rather than abstract philosophical definitions of beauty.</meta>

...taking into account their participation in **pleasure** and **displeasure** <note>original: "Gefallen und Mißfallen"</note>, necessarily takes a somewhat different turn than an approach which prioritizes the way sensory impressions are co-determined by a "significance" or meaning, and considers agreeableness and disagreeableness only in a subordinate way.

Undeniably, while maintaining this relationship to pleasure and displeasure, one could still imagine a great generalization of the concept of **aesthetic**. Specifically, regardless of whether impressions originate from the external world or occur directly, one could understand **aesthetic** to mean anything generally related to conditions of pleasure and displeasure. **Aesthetics** would then be a general doctrine that tracks the total relations of pleasure and displeasure in the world—both internal and external—according to their conceptual and lawful relationships, their chains of cause and effect, and their ways of emerging and intervening. And since a concept can certainly be grasped in such a way, and the idea of such a comprehensive doctrine of pleasure and displeasure can be established, a scientific need may also arise to use those terms in this broadest sense, should no others be found for it. However, the term **aesthetic** has never been used with such breadth; for that general doctrine, however, to my knowledge, the term **Hedonics** <note>From the Greek 'hedone,' meaning pleasure; the branch of psychology or philosophy dedicated to the study of pleasure.</note> has already been proposed. *) In any case, we are not concerned with such a general doctrine here, and so we will only use the term **aesthetic** in this broadest sense when the conceptual context exceptionally leads to it and makes it self-evident.

<note>*) The main features of such a doctrine (without using the word **Hedonics** for it) can be found in Hartsen’s *Basic Features of the Science of Happiness* (Halle, Pfeffer, 1869) and his *Beginnings of Life-Wisdom* (Leipzig, Thomas, 1874), short works with whose pure "from below" approach and **eudaimonistic** tendency <note>An ethical theory that views happiness or well-being as the highest good.</note> I find myself in full agreement.</note>

Among the many ways to categorize the human inner life, there are two that intertwine, which can be briefly distinguished as division by **sides** and by **stages**. According to the first, there is a side of **sensing and representing** <note>original: "Empfinden und Vorstellen"; the basic mental processes of receiving sensory data and forming mental images or ideas.</note>—along with everything that grows out of it in memories, concepts, etc.—a side of **drive and will**, and a side of **feeling pleasure and displeasure**. The latter is rooted in the first side (or enters into it as a co-determining factor), and in the second side, it sets impulses and acts as a mediating...

Herbart <note>Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841), a German philosopher and educator who argued that ethics is actually a branch of aesthetics because moral judgments are based on a kind of "taste" or immediate liking.</note> includes **Ethics** within **Aesthetics**, and if one wishes to elevate the

secondary notions, awakens an immediate **delight** <note>original: "Wohlgefallen"</note>, and
the same is true of the **Beautiful**. But apart from the fact that the morally good
is a purely internal matter—which the Beautiful, in the narrower sense, is not—
we do not call the Good "good" because it awakens an immediate delight
when correctly viewed; that is a secondary matter, external to it, so to speak;
rather, it is good because, regardless of how it appears to an observer, it is—
in the sense indicated on page 19—a source of **prosperous consequences**. 
From this, and not from the viewpoint of immediate delight in it, the 
moral laws and demands are to be derived, taking into account the 
empirical nature of humans and things. In doing so, one will certainly 
find Herbart’s **ethical model concepts** <note>original: "ethische Musterbegriffe"; Herbart’s five basic moral ideas, such as Inner Freedom and Justice</note> 
again, yet find no reason to enclose the developments within their framework, 
or to enter into their discussion based on Herbart’s fundamental viewpoints.

generalizing and heightening the fundamental attributes of our own 
spirit, to think of the attributes of the **divine spirit**—and we have 
no other point of reference for imagining it or basis for drawing a 
conclusion—then one would also have to think of a side of **pleasure** 
and **displeasure** in Him, and of what pleases and displeases Him 
in His world. Also...

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of metaphysical aesthetics. In such a system, the Beautiful would appear in a clearly traceable relationship to the Divine, a connection so often attributed to it. But since even the viewpoint <note>original: "Gesichtspunct"</note> of such a way of grounding an **Aesthetics from Above** has not yet been granted or clarified, all talk of grounding Beauty in God remains a mere well-sounding phrase.

### 4) The Eudaimonistic Principle.

Our relation of aesthetic categories to ethical ones—and consequently of **aesthetics** to **ethics**—has occurred from a **eudaimonistic** <note>from the Greek "eudaimonia," relating to the pursuit of happiness or "flourishing" as the ultimate goal of life</note> viewpoint (one that sets happiness and pleasure as the goal), and I do not know how it could be done more clearly or appropriately. However, the prejudice against subordinating ethics to a eudaimonistic viewpoint in general is so widespread and, on the part of some, so strong, that it could easily stand in the way of the accessibility of the entire conceptual system described above; which is why I seek to contribute something here, by way of an appendix, toward the clarification and support of this viewpoint as we understand it, by clarifying several points that are not universally understood.

For the most part, of course, that prejudice persists only because people do not properly distinguish between **subjective (egoistic) eudaimonism**, which is rightly rejected, and **objective (universal) eudaimonism**, which is the only thing at issue here. It is also partly because the pivot <note>original: "Angelpunct"</note> of the entire eudaimonistic system—the **concept of pleasure** <note>original: "Lustbegriff"</note>—is grasped too narrowly and lowly; but psychological obscurities also contribute to this. Regarding this, first the following:

<summary>The author critiques vague metaphysical attempts to link Beauty to God, proposing instead a rigorous "Aesthetics from Above," and begins a defense of "Universal Eudaimonism" as the logical link between ethics and aesthetics.</summary>
<keywords>Aesthetics from Above, Anthropomorphism, Eudaimonism, Ethics, Pleasure and Displeasure, Universal vs Subjective Eudaimonism</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Eudaimonistic Principle," specifically exploring how human will and conscience are driven by the fundamental forces of pleasure and displeasure.</meta>

Our idea of an action to be performed (or, conversely, omitted) can be tinged with the character of **pleasure** or **displeasure** <note>original: "Lust oder Unlust." These terms are foundational to 19th-century German psychology, referring not just to physical joy or pain, but to any positive or negative mental state.</note>. Every conscious **impulse** and **counter-impulse** <note>original: "Antrieb und Gegenantrieb"</note> toward an action is determined and directed by this character; the more conscious the impulse, the more decisively it is directed. Because of this, we often simply refer to conscious impulses and counter-impulses toward an action as "pleasure" or "displeasure" for that action.

If conscience can lead us to do something against our immediate pleasure—that is, to act even though the idea of performing that action is tinged with displeasure from some perspective—it is only because the idea of *failing* to do the action is tinged with even more displeasure from the side of conscience. Countless similar conflicts occur in other areas of life.

In very many cases, the pleasure and displeasure that determine the conscious impulses and counter-impulses toward our actions depend on the *idea* of the pleasure and displeasure that will result from that action for us. However, we must carefully distinguish between this pleasure or displeasure which is merely an **object** <note>something we are thinking about in the future</note> of our mental representation, and the pleasure or displeasure which is a **feeling-element** <note>original: "Gefühlsmoment," an immediate emotional quality</note> of the mental representation itself. This distinction is not always made clearly.

After all, we can imagine a pleasure that we are unable to reach with a feeling of displeasure, or imagine a displeasure that we hope to escape with a feeling of pleasure. Fundamentally—that is, necessarily and immediately—it is always only the **feeling-element** of pleasure and displeasure that determines the impulse and counter-impulse toward action. While this feeling-element can be determined by the imagined successful outcome (pleasure or displeasure) of the action, it can also be influenced by other factors—even in opposition to the outcome, or entirely on its own.

For instance, we may be instinctively and innately reluctant to do or omit something without thinking about the consequences of pleasure or displeasure at all. In fact, a psychological after-effect, gathered from past experiences of pleasure and displeasure, plays an important role in determining our present impulses, even without a conscious memory or mental reflection of those past experiences. Furthermore, and perhaps also for instinctive reasons, the power of **example** intervenes: all other things being equal, we love to do what we see others doing. Within these factors that determine our impulses lie the means for educating them. How much in many impulses, especially those...

<summary>The author explains that all human actions are driven by the balance of pleasure and displeasure, even when acting out of conscience. He distinguishes between the "imagined" pleasure of a future result and the "immediate feeling" that actually triggers our impulses.</summary>

<keywords>pleasure and displeasure, psychology of will, conscience, impulse and counter-impulse, mental representation, eudaimonism, behavioral conditioning, power of example</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Eudaimonistic Principle," specifically building on the distinction between immediate feelings of pleasure and the long-term goals of an action. ...of conscience, whether it may be innate or acquired through upbringing, may be a matter of dispute; in any case, education has played a role in it everywhere. No objection can likely be raised against the psychological validity of the preceding definitions. Now, the **eudaimonistic principle** <note>original: "eudämonistische Princip." From the Greek 'eudaimonia,' this refers to an ethical system where the goal of actions is to produce happiness or well-being.</note> represented here rests on nothing other than this: it takes that same element which necessarily determines the direction of everyone's conscious **impulses** <note>original: "Antriebe"</note> and presents it also as the goal of these impulses in relation to the **Whole** <note>the community or collective good</note>; it commands that the education of everyone's impulses be directed toward the greatest possible fulfillment of this goal. This occurs by asserting a sense of solidarity, in which the well-being of the individual reveals itself to be linked with the well-being of the whole—the more completely the principle is fulfilled and the further it is pursued to its logical consequences, the clearer this link becomes.

Just as it is not the intention of this principle to favor one's own well-being over the well-being of others, neither does it intend the sacrifice of one's own well-being for that of others. For one's own well-being itself forms a constituent part of the **general well-being**; thus, in order not to diminish the well-being of the whole, everyone may and should strive for their own well-being to the extent that no greater disadvantage arises for others than the advantage they gain for themselves. In certain respects, every person can care for themselves better than they can let others care for them; conversely, in other respects, they can care for others better than those others can care for themselves. Now, the **Law**—with regard to historical, national, and even more specific circumstances—and **Ethics**—from broader, more general perspectives—must weigh rights and duties in this regard and establish laws. By governing and binding the judgment of the individual, these laws maintain the actions of all in a cohesive direction toward what is best. Even in the mere commonality of following a law, there is something good; for it is better if everyone belonging to a certain circle follows an existing law together and consistently—even if it were not the best law, provided it is not the worst—than if everyone acts without law according to their own personal view of what is best.

Now, it cannot be denied that human impulses are, from the outset, directed much more toward one's own and immediate well-being than toward that of the whole (or toward the distant recovery of one's own well-being derived from the whole); thus, they do not function in the sense of the previously mentioned...

<summary>The author argues that the eudaimonistic principle balances individual well-being with the common good, suggesting that social laws and ethics are necessary to coordinate these impulses. He emphasizes that following a collective law, even an imperfect one, is more beneficial for the "Whole" than individuals acting solely on their own judgment.</summary>
<keywords>conscience, eudaimonistic principle, impulses, solidarity, individual well-being, general well-being, ethics, social law, common good</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "eudaimonistic principle," moving from its theoretical definition to its practical application in education and the necessity of religious motivation to ensure ethical behavior.</meta>

...are destined for the Principle. To educate them in this sense, however, the same means are at one's disposal—to be directed precisely in the sense of the Principle—that have been in use everywhere and from time immemorial wherever education is concerned: example, praise, blame, reward, punishment, reference to the wrath and favor of God, threats and promises regarding what lies beyond this world; to which must be added an awakened insight into the nature, demands, and consequences of the Principle. 

The highest goal of this education, however, will not be that which is prescribed by an impractical, doctrinaire rigorism <note>original: "doctrinären Rigorismus." This refers to an extreme moral strictness—often associated with certain interpretations of Kantian ethics—that demands duty be performed without any regard for personal happiness.</note>—something that can be set down on paper but cannot be fulfilled in human nature—namely, that man should completely banish consideration for his own advantage from his motives. Rather, the goal is that he should not separate the regard for his own well-being from the regard for the well-being of the whole at all, neither in immediate feeling nor in view of the consequences. 

To this end, on the one hand, it is necessary that, through the feeling of love for his neighbor, he finds his own happiness in working for the happiness of others; and beyond that, that he feels the higher feeling of the satisfaction of conscience <term>satisfaction of conscience (Gewissensbefriedigung): the internal peace or moral fulfillment derived from acting rightly.</term>—a satisfaction which, in the feeling of also satisfying God, can be elevated to a motive surpassing every other in strength and height. 

On the other hand, this requires the empirical <note>original: "erfahrungsmässige." Based on observation and practical experience rather than pure theory.</note> view that even here in this life, the good and bad consequences of one's actions strike back at the individual all the more surely the longer they run; this is supplemented by the faith that the principle of this retribution reaches over from this world <note>original: "Diesseits." Literally "this side"; the earthly life.</note> into the afterlife <note>original: "Jenseits." Literally "the other side"; the world to come.</note> and finds its completion there. For this, it is certainly also necessary to awaken and strengthen faith in God and the afterlife in the right sense; however, among the principles of right faith itself, it must be counted that it should both satisfy people most and guide them best.

In fact, it is an empty delusion to believe that one can correctly and fully educate—whether it be the common people, or people of higher education, or oneself—in the spirit of the Principle without the inclusion of religious motives. Without them, there remains an uncovered remainder in relation to the highest and ultimate concerns, which cannot be covered by all the preaching of "humanity" <note>original: "Humanität." Here used to describe secular or purely philosophical moral systems.</note>; for what has ever been achieved of significance by such means? If the Principle is to gain practical validity, it can only be in connection with those motives which tower above all others, and which ultimately alone...

<summary>This page argues that moral education must bridge the gap between self-interest and the common good using traditional means like rewards and punishments, but insists that true ethical behavior is impossible without religious foundations and the belief in divine retribution.</summary>

## III. Aesthetic Laws or Principles in General.

In the interest of a unified character for the entirety of aesthetics, it would be desirable if all laws of pleasure and displeasure <note>original: "Gefallens und Missfallens." In this context, these terms refer to the basic aesthetic reactions of "liking" or "disliking" an object or experience.</note> to be discussed could be presented as special cases of one most general law. However, even if such a law exists in itself, it remains as much in the dark for us as the most general and ultimate ground of all pleasure and pain <term>pleasure and pain (Lust und Unlust): the fundamental psychological states of satisfaction or dissatisfaction that underpin all aesthetic experience.</term>, with which it is naturally connected. 

It is true that the well-known principle of the **unified connection of the manifold** <note>This principle, often called "unity in diversity," suggests that beauty arises when many different elements are brought together into a single, coherent whole.</note>—which nothing prevents us from formulating as a law—has been placed at the head of all aesthetics; and it is certainly one of the most important principles; we shall speak of it later; but I would not know how to get by with it alone. How, for example, could it explain why the pleasure we find in the resolution of a dissonance into a consonance does not 
<margin>—</margin>
remain the same if we reverse the sequence of the chords; that we can grow accustomed to the hideous and become weary of the most beautiful; that everywhere there is a "too much" and a "too little" which displeases us, and so on.

Zimmermann <note>Robert von Zimmermann (1824–1898), a prominent philosopher known for "formal aesthetics," which focused on the relationships between forms rather than the emotional or spiritual content of art.</note>, one of the primary spokesmen of contemporary aesthetics, author of a *History* and a *System of Aesthetics*, active and powerful in aesthetic criticism, has proposed—in place of this single law—two as fundamental for the whole of aesthetics...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of fundamental aesthetic laws, moving from the "unity in diversity" principle to specific laws proposed by the Herbartian school, which the author evaluates critically against the views of Edmund Burke.</meta>

set forth, one as authoritative in a quantitative sense, the other in a qualitative relationship; they read as follows:

1) (The so-called **Principle of Perfection**): "The stronger representation pleases alongside the weaker representation, while the weaker displeases alongside the stronger." <term>representation (Vorstellung): in 19th-century German psychology, this refers to any mental image, idea, or perception that occupies the mind.</term>

2) "The predominant identity of formal elements pleases; their predominant contrast displeases unconditionally."

I would not know how to get by in aesthetics with these two laws; in fact, I cannot even quite reconcile myself with them, undoubtedly because I am unable to reconcile myself with the Herbartian philosophy <note>original: "Herbartschen Philosophie." Referring to Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841), whose influential philosophy focused on the mathematical and formal relationships between ideas.</note> in which they are rooted—though this is naturally not the place for such a dispute. I wish only to mention one curiosity <note>original: "Curiosum."</note> that I encountered regarding the first law, in order to attach a few remarks to it that will save us the trouble later on.

A main consequence of this law is the principle already voiced by Herbart and accepted by Zimmermann <note>Robert von Zimmermann (1824–1898), a prominent follower of Herbart who developed a system of "formal aesthetics" that prioritized the structure of an object over its content.</note>: "**The large pleases next to the small, the small displeases next to the large.**" Against this, Burke <note>Edmund Burke (1729–1797), the British philosopher whose 1757 treatise *A Philosophical Enquiry into the Origin of Our Ideas of the Sublime and Beautiful* heavily influenced aesthetic theory.</note>—who, to be sure, could not yet have studied Herbart—begins his enumeration of the qualities by which something becomes beautiful in his *Treatise on the Sublime and Beautiful* with the sentence: "**The beautiful must first be comparatively small**," and even has an entire chapter titled "**Beautiful objects are small**." In this chapter, among other points he raises, he notes: "I have been assured that in most languages, things that one loves are designated with diminutive epithets. At least it is so with all the languages I know."

Now, one could indeed—according to an auxiliary principle <term>auxiliary principle (Hülfsprincip): a secondary rule used to explain exceptions to a primary law.</term> occasionally brought in by Zimmermann—trace the pleasure in the smaller back to the pleasure in the larger by arguing that the smaller possesses the quality of "smallness" to a stronger degree *) or deviates more strongly from the mean than the "less small" does; in short, it is something "greater" in a negative sense. However, it would be better for clarity and 

*) Indeed, this corresponds to Zimmermann’s explanation in his *Textbook* <note>original: "Lehrb." abbreviation for *Lehrbuch*.</note> p. 39, as to why, in apparent contradiction to his proclaimed aesthetic "right of the stronger," a greater mildness can nevertheless please us more than a lesser mildness.

<summary>This page critiques the Herbartian aesthetic laws of perfection and formal identity, highlighting a contradiction between the Herbartian preference for "the large" and Edmund Burke’s assertion that beauty is found in "the small."</summary>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of the Herbartian principle that "large pleases next to small," which the previous page introduced. The author is now evaluating whether the "middle ground" or the "extremes" of size hold more aesthetic value, contrasting the theories of Burke and Zimmermann.</meta>

In order to avoid the accusation of moving within contradictory representations, it might be advisable to simply declare that which deviates most strongly from the average toward one side or the other as the more pleasing; the facts asserted by <term>Burke</term> <note>Edmund Burke (1729–1797), who argued that smallness is a characteristic of beauty while vastness belongs to the sublime.</note> and <term>Zimmermann</term> <note>Robert von Zimmermann (1824–1898), who followed the formalist view that size relations determine pleasure.</note> do indeed appear equally striking from their different perspectives. But of course, a third person might then come along and, despite <term>Zimmermann</term> and <term>Burke</term>, declare a "right middle" between the large and the small to be the most pleasing, and succeed in bringing forth facts for it that seem no less striking.

In days of old, <term>Venus</term> <note>The Roman goddess of love and beauty.</note> fought with <term>Pallas</term> <note>Pallas Athena, the Greek goddess of wisdom.</note> and <term>Juno</term> <note>The Roman queen of the gods.</note> for the "apple of beauty" based on the beauty of their form; one sees that with the preceding argument, she is now tasked with fighting giants and dwarfs for the prize based on the beauty of size. Should I now be chosen as the <term>Paris</term> <note>The Trojan prince tasked with judging which goddess was the most beautiful.</note> to decide this, I would undoubtedly only need to follow a very general consensus to award the prize immediately to her, who stands in the middle between the two. Yet I hesitate to do it quite so simply, as I remember that I might go into a sideshow <note>original: "Schaubude" — a booth at a fair or circus exhibiting curiosities.</note> to see a dwarf or a giant, but not to see a person of ordinary size; must I not, therefore, find more pleasure in seeing the former than in seeing the latter? Meanwhile, I also remember that in ordinary life, I prefer to see people of ordinary size around me and to interact with them, rather than seeking out dwarfs or giants. In short: I prefer the exceptional in exceptional cases, and the habitual for the usual; indeed, I do this not merely regarding the impression of size, but in general. Thus, a very general aesthetic principle could be made of this, though not one so universal that pleasure and displeasure depend on it alone; it is merely one factor that co-determines the effect everywhere, just as it is subject to other co-determining factors.

Even for the enjoyment of <term>the Sublime</term> <note>original: "das Erhabene." A key concept in 18th and 19th-century aesthetics referring to an aesthetic quality of greatness, whether physical, moral, or intellectual, that can inspire awe or even a degree of fear.</note>, it is necessary that it be not merely something large, but also something exceptional; often, other co-determining factors are also required. If its greatness grants us more points of connection for pleasurable engagement, we will certainly prefer it to the small, which in its smallness can grant less of it; but conversely, we prefer the small if the large is a richer source of displeasure <note>original: "Unlust."</note> than of pleasure.

## IV. The Principle of the Aesthetic Threshold.

There is much that leaves us indifferent, even though, by its own nature and our own, it would be well-suited to awaken pleasure or displeasure, and indeed does so at other times. Generally speaking, this depends on whether the strength of the objective influence, the degree of our receptivity to it, or our attention to it, exceeds the so-called *threshold* <note>The "threshold" (German: *Schwelle*) is a central concept in Fechner’s psychophysics, referring to the minimum intensity a stimulus must reach to be consciously perceived.</note>—that is, the level at which the influence first becomes perceptible to our consciousness. For it is a general law—valid not only for the sensation of pleasure and displeasure, but for all sensations—that a certain degree of those factors upon which sensation depends, both externally and internally, is required for it to enter consciousness; the quality of the condition is not enough; it must be supplemented by the required quantity, the required degree. As long as this degree is not reached, we say of the conditions of pleasure and displeasure—as well as of the pleasure and displeasure themselves and the resulting liking or disliking—that they remain below the *threshold*.

In fact, just as we can be certain that countless foul odors float in the air, we generally smell nothing of them because of their dilution. Even the worst-tasting medicine does not taste bad to us in homeopathic dilution <note>Fechner refers here to the practice of extreme dilution used in 19th-century homeopathy to illustrate how a substance can be present but undetectable to the senses.</note>. For many things that gave us pleasure when our receptivity was fresh, our receptivity becomes dulled without being entirely extinguished; the stimulus of pleasure must simply be strengthened to provide pleasure once again; and how many things strike our senses but receive too little of our attention, and therefore leave us indifferent.

Depending on whether one considers the external or internal conditions of liking or disliking, one can speak of an external or internal threshold which must be crossed if liking or disliking is to enter consciousness with a real value of pleasure or displeasure <note>Fechner uses the terms *Lust* (pleasure/enjoyment) and *Unlust* (displeasure/pain) as the two fundamental poles of human feeling.</note>. However, both thresholds are not independent of one another. For every specific degree of receptivity and attention, there will be a specific degree of external influence that must be exceeded, and thus a corresponding specific external threshold; but as those internal conditions change, a greater...

<summary>Fechner introduces the "aesthetic threshold," explaining that stimuli only trigger feelings of pleasure or displeasure once they reach a certain intensity relative to our attention and receptivity. This concept links his psychological theories of perception directly to the study of art and beauty.</summary>

<keywords>aesthetic threshold, Gustav Theodor Fechner, pleasure and displeasure, consciousness, psychophysics, receptivity, sensory perception, 19th-century psychology</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter IV's discussion on the "threshold" of perception and introduces Chapter V, which defines the "Principle of Aesthetic Assistance."</meta>

...or a lesser external influence becomes necessary for this to occur; consequently, the external <term>threshold (Schwelle)</term><note>In Fechner’s psychophysics, the "threshold" is the point where a stimulus becomes strong enough to be consciously felt.</note> rises or falls, and the same happens in reverse with the internal threshold when the degree of external influence changes. Now, if the threshold of a sensation is to be crossed at all, it must always be the internal and external thresholds simultaneously; however, this can occur more through an increase in conditions from the inside or the outside.

Regarding conditions that are capable of awakening <term>pleasure or displeasure (Lust or Unlust)</term> by crossing a threshold, we say generally that they are "in the sense of" pleasure or displeasure, without them actually awakening such feelings as long as they remain below the threshold.

Although conditions for pleasure or displeasure below the threshold are, by definition, insufficient to let that pleasure or displeasure be felt, it is still not the same as if they were entirely absent. Rather, their insufficient presence can become important from one of the following two perspectives:

**First.** The closer the internal or external conditions are to the threshold of pleasure or displeasure, the smaller the additional increase in their degree or strength required to make them cross the threshold; thus, the circumstances are more favorable for the actual emergence of pleasure or displeasure.

**Second.** A condition of pleasure or displeasure that is or would be below the threshold if it remained on its own, can—when combined with different types of conditions for pleasure or displeasure which themselves would be below the threshold—yield a result of pleasure or displeasure that exceeds the threshold. The <term>Principle of Aesthetic Assistance</term><note>Fechner’s term "ästhetische Hülfe" refers to the idea that multiple small aesthetic factors, which might not be pleasing on their own, can "assist" one another to produce a combined effect that is consciously pleasurable.</note>, which we shall consider immediately, depends in part on this.

---

## V. Principle of Aesthetic Assistance or Enhancement.

Before stating the law specifically, we shall illustrate it with a few special cases.

A poem, heard in a foreign language, still provides the full impression of meter, rhythm, and rhyme, but without...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the end of the previous page regarding how individual conditions for pleasure, even if they are below the conscious "threshold," can combine to create a significant aesthetic experience. This is the core of what Fechner calls the "Principle of Aesthetic Assistance" or "Reinforcement."</meta>

the connected meaning. This impression is more pleasant than that of a lawless **gibberish** of words, but this pleasantness is in itself so slight that one would hardly attribute any significant **aesthetic value** to it without the meaning; in fact, on its own, it does not easily cross the <term>threshold of pleasure</term><note>The "threshold" (Schwelle) is a central concept in Fechner's work, representing the point at which a physical stimulus or psychological condition becomes strong enough to be consciously felt as pleasure or pain.</note>. Yet the most beautiful poems lose all or nearly all of their charm when their content is rendered in **prosaic speech**, because the meaning without meter, rhythm, and rhyme likewise does not cross the threshold of pleasure. One might think, for instance, of: "You fill bush and vale again," or: "The bright day has passed," and so on. <note>These are references to famous German lyrics: "Fülleft wieder Busch und Thal" is the opening line of Goethe’s poem *An den Mond* (To the Moon); "Vergangen ist der lichte Tag" is a common opening for evening hymns and poems.</note> However, because both factors of pleasantness assist each other in crossing the threshold—or in rising above it—a positive **result of pleasure** is produced, which is incomparable in magnitude to the aesthetic effect of the individual factors.

Corresponding assistance is provided in the pure field of direct impressions by **euphony**, and the melody and harmony of tones. The sensory euphony of pure, full tones has very little aesthetic value in itself, and yet, how much it contributes to the beauty of a **song**. Of course, if pure, full tones were not already more pleasant in themselves than impure, harsh tones, then no increase exceeding the sum of their individual effects would arise from the interaction of these elements. Generally, the **principle** can now be stated as follows:

**From the non-contradictory coincidence of conditions for pleasure, which individually achieve little, a greater—often much greater—result of pleasure emerges than corresponds to the pleasure-value of the individual conditions alone; a result greater than could be explained as the mere sum of the individual effects. Indeed, through a coincidence of this kind, a positive result of pleasure can be achieved and the threshold of pleasure crossed where the individual factors were too weak to do so; provided only that they, in comparison with others, allow a perceptible advantage in pleasantness to be felt.** Cases of "non-contradictory coincidence" are specifically those where one condition serves simultaneously as a **prerequisite** or **foundation** for the realization of the others; whereas

<summary>Fechner defines the "Principle of Aesthetic Assistance," arguing that aesthetic pleasure is not just the sum of its parts (like rhythm and meaning), but a greater result produced when these elements reinforce one another.</summary>
<keywords>Aesthetic Assistance, threshold of pleasure, euphony, rhythm, poetic meaning, psychophysics, harmony, Goethe</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Fechner's "Principle of Aesthetic Assistance," where individual pleasant factors combine to create a total effect greater than the sum of its parts. It specifically addresses how direct sensory impressions and mental associations work together.</meta>

Cases where one factor hinders the realization of another do not belong under this principle. In particular, the principle includes cases where a **directly pleasant impression** is simultaneously the occasion for **pleasant associative ideas**, as well as those where a lower pleasant impression serves as the foundation for the realization of a higher one. The examples provided are chosen from these two classes; the following sections will provide plenty of other examples.

A consequence of the **aesthetic principle of assistance** is that the loss of one element of pleasantness from a conflict-free union of such moments does incomparably greater damage to beauty than the presence of that single element could achieve on its own. All of the preceding points, however, agree that one must not draw a conclusion against the important contribution of a single moment to the **beauty of a whole** simply because that moment has an insignificant effect by itself.

To the pleasant impression of a **work of art** as well as a **work of nature**, which we call beautiful, various moments generally contribute, which can be separated through analysis; it is not easy for one of these alone to achieve a significant **aesthetic effect**. To account for the magnitude of the **total impression**, we must therefore generally call upon the **principle of mutual assistance**. For it to appear in full force, all moments must act in perfect unison—as they say, harmoniously—toward the sense of **pleasure** <term>Lust</term><note>Fechner uses "Lust" and "Unlust" as technical terms for the basic spectrum of human experience, from aesthetic pleasure to sensory discomfort.</note>. Where this is not the case—and conflicts occur all too frequently in both art and nature—the principle’s performance suffers deductions, which may in turn be surpassed by reconciling effects; however, the rules for this must be sought elsewhere.

The previous principle can also be transferred from conditions of pleasure to conditions of **unpleasure** <term>Unlust</term>. If a speech, which does not please us because of its content, is also delivered in an unpleasant voice, it becomes utterly unbearable. However, cases of this kind do not present themselves as easily or strikingly as those regarding pleasure, because one removes, avoids, or seeks to escape them as much as possible by turning one's attention away.

The principle of assistance is expressly [focused] on a conflict-free combination—

<meta>This page concludes the discussion of the "Principle of Aesthetic Assistance" (also known as the Principle of Aesthetic Augmentation). It transitions from explaining how small pleasures combine into a larger whole to discussing how these interactions are limited by the biological laws of sensation. ...coincidence of conditions has been referred to, which by themselves achieve little aesthetically. Should **conditions of pleasure** <term>Lustbedingungen</term><note>The specific aesthetic or sensory factors that generate a positive response in the observer.</note> coincide that already achieve much on their own, one would indeed expect an increase beyond the performance of each individual part; however, this increase would be less than the sum of the individuals. This is to be expected if the **psychophysical laws**, under which this "Principle of Assistance" is categorized, are still applicable here. According to these laws, when a stimulus first crosses the **threshold** <term>Schwelle</term><note>A fundamental concept in Fechner's psychophysics, referring to the point where a stimulus becomes strong enough to be consciously perceived.</note>, the sensation increases at a much faster rate than the stimulus that triggers it. However, from a certain point of increase (the **cardinal point** <term>Cardinalpuncte</term><note>In Fechner's law, the point at which the intensity of sensation begins to increase at a slower rate than the physical stimulus.</note>) onward, it increases at a slower rate. This may be briefly called the **growth law of sensation**. Thus, it is to be expected that when the coincidence of strong conditions of pleasure causes pleasure to rise higher, this will also occur in a lesser proportion than the sum of those conditions. It must be admitted, however, that direct evidence for this is not as decisive as it is for the laws of the threshold and assistance.

### VI. Principle of the Unitary Connection of the Manifold.

#### 1) Statement of the Principle.

This is an important principle we are about to address; while its statement is simple enough, its consideration offers many sides and viewpoints, and its application offers many difficulties.

By innate disposition, in order to feel well while actively or receptively engaged with an object, a person requires a certain change of moments of activity or impressions; for this, the object must provide the opportunity through a **manifold** <note>A "manifold" (Mannichfaltigkeit) refers to a variety or a collection of different parts and features within a single work.</note> of points of engagement. If the required opportunity in this regard is lacking, the object makes the displeasing impression of **monotony, uniformity, boredom, emptiness, bareness, poverty**, and thereby drives one to transition to other objects. By an equally innate disposition, however, a person requires—in order to feel well—that for the entire duration of the engagement with an object, all moments of the engagement following each other in time and space [be linked] by points of...

<summary>Fechner explains that while small pleasures add up, intense pleasures follow a "growth law" of diminishing returns. He then introduces the requirement for "Manifoldness" (variety) to prevent boredom, balanced by "Unitary Connection" (unity).</summary>
<keywords>psychophysical laws, threshold, cardinal point, growth law of sensation, Principle of the Manifold, monotony, aesthetic pleasure, unity in variety</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the physical laws of sensation (the Principle of Aesthetic Assistance) to a fundamental philosophical concept in the author's theory: the "Principle of the Unified Manifold." It explores why humans seek a balance between variety and order in art and thought.</meta>

...commonality of connection or, as one says briefly, are **unifiedly linked**; otherwise, there arises the displeasing feeling of distraction, fragmentation, lack of connection, or even contradiction, which also drives the transition to other objects. Wherever the need for a change of occupation occurs, be it for this reason or that, one uses the expression of being weary of or fatigued by the previous occupation depending on circumstances.

It is strange that language does not offer equally well-descriptive and distinctive expressions for the two sides of pleasure, which meet in the satisfaction of our principle, as for those of displeasure, which arise through the violation of the same. A work of art can please us because we bring to consciousness the connection of everything in it through a **unified idea**, but also because our contemplation indulges in the variety of the parts and moments so connected. These are actually different sides of pleasure, which must coincide for full satisfaction; but how to distinguish them linguistically? At most, it can be said that one finds oneself in a "unified mood" from the first side, and "entertained" by the second.

In short, according to what has preceded, the **aesthetic principle** <term>ästhetische Princip</term><note>The foundational rule governing how we perceive beauty or interest.</note> at issue here summarizes as follows: that man, in order to find pleasure in the **receptive engagement** <term>receptive Beschäftigung</term><note>The act of observing, listening, or perceiving art, rather than the "active" process of creating it.</note> with an object—for aesthetics essentially does not deal with active engagement—must find offered therein a **unifiedly connected manifold** <term>einheitlich verknüpfte Mannichfaltigkeit</term><note>A classic aesthetic concept (often called "Unity in Variety") which suggests that beauty arises when many different parts are held together by a single organizing theme.</note>.

What we call a unifiedly connected **manifold** <term>Mannichfaltigkeit</term><note>A philosophical term for a collection of many different things or parts.</note> translates, upon closer inspection, into an **agreement** <term>Uebereinstimmung</term><note>Concordance or harmony between different elements.</note> of the many in certain relationships despite deviation in others. This agreement does not need to consist of qualitative equality, but can also lie in the agreement of the parts of a whole toward a certain purpose, a certain idea, or in the **causal connection** <term>Causalverknüpfung</term><note>The logical link where one event or part leads to another through cause and effect.</note> of the moments of an event (which always presupposes a dependence on the same law), and can take place from a lower or higher **viewpoint** <term>Gesichtspuncte</term><note>The level of intellectual or sensory perspective from which we judge a work of art.</note>, as will be discussed further and illustrated by examples.

<summary>This page defines the "Principle of the Unified Manifold," arguing that aesthetic pleasure requires a balance between diversity (variety) and a central organizing idea (unity). The author notes that while we have many words for the discomfort of distraction, we lack precise terms for the distinct pleasures of "mood" and "entertainment" found in unified works.</summary>
<keywords>aesthetic principle, manifold, variety, unity, receptive engagement, causal connection, boredom, agreement, artistic purpose</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the fundamental aesthetic requirements for pleasure. While the previous page introduced the need for "unified linking," this page explores the necessary tension between unity and variety (the "Manifold"), specifically how they function across time and space.</meta>

In itself, a unified connection cannot exist without variety <term>Mannichfaltigkeit</term><note>Often translated as "the manifold," this refers to a collection of diverse or different parts within a single whole.</note>, for without such variety, we would have nothing but simple identity. For an engagement of short duration, however, even a very small degree of variety is sufficient to allow the mind to find satisfaction and even positive pleasure, provided that unity is not lacking. Conversely, a variety that asserts no relation to unity *) is not only repulsive the longer it forces itself upon us, but is so almost from the very beginning. And when we, driven by the need for change, move on to engage with something new, we do not wish to move toward a fragmented variety, but only toward something else that is, once again, unifiedly linked. 

In this respect, more weight seems to be placed on the perspective of unity than on that of variety; yet one should not say that pleasure essentially depends on a preponderance of unity over variety—that is, where the identical outweighs the non-identical—lest one consider a white sheet of paper or a purely sustained musical tone to be the most beautiful thing in the world. For any larger whole intended to occupy us for a certain duration, we will instead demand a great deal of diversity <term>Ungleichheit</term>, which must only be somehow unifiedly mediated and bound together in order for us to find ourselves captivated by it.

Temporal and spatial variety fall under the same perspective insofar as spatial variety—though perceivable at once up to certain limits—must nevertheless be followed sequentially with our attention to be clearly grasped; meanwhile, in temporal variety, the lingering effect of earlier impressions into later ones necessitates a certain simultaneity of those impressions.

They fall under different perspectives, however, because in spatial variety, the direction in which we follow the parts is more or less arbitrary, whereas in temporal variety (insofar as it is not also spatial), the direction is prescribed by the given sequence itself.

*) <note>original: "Wenn die Glieder einer solchen Mannichfaltigkeit uns jedes für sich annehmmlich sind..."</note> If the individual members of such a variety are each pleasing to us on their own, a conflict arises with the unpleasantness that stems from the lack of a unifying connection between them. We will speak of conflicts later; for now, they are to be set aside.

<summary>This page argues that beauty requires a balance: unity prevents fragmentation, while variety prevents the boredom of "simple identity" (like a blank page). --- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of how "unity" and "variety" interact. Having established that pleasure requires both, the author now examines how the "height" or complexity of unified relations is determined by the relationships between parts rather than just the parts themselves.</meta>

The fact that unified relations <term>Einheitsbezüge</term><note>The way different parts are linked together into a single, cohesive whole.</note> can rise to varying heights is explained as follows: within a manifold <term>Mannichfaltigkeit</term> of distinguishable parts, elements, moments—in short, members—one can find not only the members themselves to be more or less similar or dissimilar, but also the various differences or ratios occurring between those members. Through the equality of differences or ratios between specific members of a whole, a higher level of unified relation is established among these members than would belong to the individual members on their own (by virtue of their own sub-divisions) or to the whole if all differences between the members were removed. However, instead of a simple equality of differences or ratios, there can also be a "harmony" or "concordance" of the members toward something equal, mediated by those very differences.

->For example:<-

The unified relation that links the parts of a circle is higher than that which links the parts of a straight line; and the unified relation between the parts of an ellipse is higher than between the parts of a circle. In the case of a straight line, the unified relation is based directly on the identical direction of all its elements. In a circle, every identical element deviates from the next in its direction, but it deviates by an equal amount; in short, the differences—and thus simultaneously the ratios—of these directions to one another are equal for elements that are adjacent or separated by equal distances. In an ellipse, even these differences are unequal; every element deviates from the next (or from one at an equal distance) by a different angle. However, the differences between these differences—so-called differences of a higher order <term>Unterschiede höherer Ordnung</term>—are linked by a common rule in which they harmonize, and which the mathematician is able to express in a formula. — If a uniform surface is striped uniformly, the rule of this striping establishes a higher unified relation than the uniformity belonging to each stripe individually, or that which would belong to an entirely uniform surface; this is because, instead of a simple equality of all parts, the differences introduced into the surface by the uniform striping are balanced out across the entire surface at equal intervals.

All of a person's actions can be unifiedly linked through their relationship to his own well-being; they can also be unifiedly linked with the actions of other people through their relationship to the greatest possible well-being of all <term>Wohl Aller</term><note>This refers to the "common good" or the utilitarian principle of the greatest happiness for the greatest number.</note>. This latter relationship is higher than the former, as the differences between the actions of individuals must harmonize in this shared sense.

<summary>This page explains that the "height" of unity increases with complexity; for instance, an ellipse has a higher order of unity than a circle because its changing parts follow a consistent mathematical rule. This principle is extended from geometric shapes to human ethics, where the "common good" represents a higher unity than individual self-interest.</summary>
<keywords>unified relations, manifold, higher-order differences, geometry, circle, ellipse, aesthetics, common good, ethics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of "unified relations" by applying geometric analogies (circles and ellipses) to aesthetic perception. When considering a painting that depicts a battle scene, the various moments of behavior of each individual fighter are linked uniformly through the mental representation of their goal to overwhelm the opponent; however, the very diverse behaviors of everyone involved are linked in a higher sense through the motive of what the battle itself is about for all of them.

In general, with the emergence of higher <term>unified relations</term><note>Einheitsbezüge: The way different parts are linked together into a single, cohesive whole.</note>, the possibility of multiple viewpoints regarding that unity is simultaneously granted. For instance, in the uniform change of direction of all parts of a circle, there is also the equal distance of those parts from a given point—which one might call a **composite** or **multiple** unified relation. In an ellipse, alongside the higher unified relation that links the elements of the curve, there is also the relation that links the <term>radius vectors</term><note>original Latin: "radii vectores"; lines connecting the foci of an ellipse to any point on its perimeter.</note>, insofar as the sum of any two radius vectors drawn from the <term>foci</term><note>Brennpuncte; the two points that define the shape of an ellipse.</note> to the circumference is equal.

Where we do not consider the relationships of parts to the whole (which includes the parts themselves)—as is the case with the <term>Golden Section</term><note>goldener Schnitt: a mathematical proportion where the ratio of the whole to the larger part is the same as the ratio of the larger part to the smaller.</note>—but only the relationships of the parts to one another, a greater "height" of unified relation can only exist on the basis of an increased number of differences (a larger multiple <term>manifold</term><note>Mannichfaltigkeit: A collection of diverse or varied elements.</note>); conversely, an increased number of differences does not necessarily carry with it a higher unified relation.

Before proceeding to a further and more deeply-reaching discussion, we shall explain the <term>principle</term><note>Princip; here referring to the "Principle of the Unitary Connection of the Manifold."</note> through a series of examples which, though seemingly very different in nature, are collectively subordinate to it, thereby proving its great scope. However, in order not to be misled by apparent contradictions, three points must be kept in mind—points which, incidentally, do not apply only to this principle, but are no less transferable to other <term>aesthetic principles</term>, and have essentially already been provided for by earlier, more general remarks.

**First**, from the objective side, unity and variety matter essentially only insofar as they are actually perceived as such by us, thereby translating themselves into a subjective experience. Fundamentally, nothing in the world is so disparate that it is not linked by some points of commonality, and nothing is so identical that it does not deviate in some points; but insofar as we are unable to perceive these points, they do not exist for the purpose of the principle. — **Secondly**, while the unified linkage of the manifold is always a condition for <term>pleasure</term><note>Lust: in this context, the psychological or aesthetic gratification derived from an object.</note> (cf. p. 50), it is by no means always sufficient on its own to push that liking above the threshold. While we...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the subjective perception of "unified viewpoints." ...a unified viewpoint that we are unable to grasp does not affect us aesthetically from the outset; conversely, one that has become completely familiar to us—as is the case with countless things in our life and surroundings—ceases to touch us aesthetically and can no longer capture our attention because we have become desensitized to it. On the other hand, many a viewpoint loses its power because we are distracted by other things. —

Thirdly, since a unified connection is not the only condition for <term>pleasure</term><note>Lust: In this psychological context, it refers to the positive aesthetic response or "hedonic tone" experienced by the observer.</note>, and since there are also conditions that work in the opposite direction, the effect of unity can be supported and heightened by the addition of similar conditions, just as it can be outweighed by opposing ones. From this latter perspective, despite the full satisfaction of the principle, displeasure may arise—or, if the principle is not satisfied, pleasure may still occur—though in both cases to a lesser degree than would be the case without the conflict. Whenever the principle seems to fail, the reason can always be found in one of these viewpoints.

### 2) Examples.

In our principle, we distinguished two sides—a side of **Unity** and a side of **Variety**<note>Mannichfaltigkeit: Often translated as "multiplicity" or "diversity"; the presence of different elements that the mind must organize into a whole.</note>—which must work together to create aesthetic appeal. Let us first highlight examples in which the first side, Unity, is particularly conspicuous.

The simplest illustration of the principle in this regard is found in the pleasure we take in the uniform purity of a colored surface, the pure stroke of a line, a purely sustained musical tone, or the clean smoothness of a surface as the eye or finger moves across it. Because of the sensory equality of the sensation, which links all points in space and time, the easy accessibility of this <term>unified relation</term><note>Einheitsbezug: The mental connection made between different parts of an object that allows us to see it as a single thing.</note> satisfies the Principle of Unity to the highest degree. Meanwhile, variety is reduced here to the lowest possible level; it is not entirely absent only insofar as it can still be found in the different spatial and temporal positions of the individual points.

Indeed, even the uniformly colored surface or the purely sustained tone can possess a certain variety from this latter view-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of aesthetic unity and variety. The previous page ended by stating that even in simple sensory experiences, individual points in space or moments in time contribute to a unified whole. This page explores how purity and uniformity create a basic sense of pleasure, and how small "impurities" can disrupt that unity.</meta>

...points cannot be denied. Consider, for example, the swarm of stars in the sky or the pips on a die, or listen to the beats of a <term>metronome</term><note>original: *Tactmesser*; a mechanical device used to mark time in music.</note>; one cannot consider the difference in spatial and temporal positions to be entirely indifferent, though their distinctness certainly diminishes greatly as they flow by. Yet it remains a different thing for the eye to wander through the <term>multiplicity</term><note>original: *Mannichfaltigkeit*; the variety of different elements that the mind must organize into a whole.</note> of points on a uniform surface than to constantly fixate on a single point. Here we have the simplest and clearest **unified connection** alongside the smallest possible, most indistinct variety.

All such things, of course, soon become boring to us if they are to occupy our attention for a long time. But even the most beautiful **work of art** becomes boring if we stay with it too long; it is just that the need for change arises more quickly with pure uniformity—or uniform purity—than with a work of art, which, due to its greater internal variety, makes the need for external change felt less quickly. 

In general, the eye enjoys lingering for a while on a pure **color panel**, especially if one becomes conscious of its purity. One can certainly find pleasure in the pure stroke of a line or a purely sustained tone if attention is focused on it. In contrast, every spot, every tiny splinter, every irregular curve, thickening, or thinning of an otherwise pure and straight line, every noise mixed into a tone, every unmotivated fluctuation in pitch, every roughness encountered on an otherwise smooth surface, diminishes the pleasurability or arouses **displeasure**. This happens because the <term>unified connection</term><note>original: *Einheitsbezug*; the way various parts of an object relate back to a single theme or structure.</note> between the disturbing spot and every other spot is lost; thus, the unity of the whole suffers a rupture.

One may notice that the increase in displeasure at an impurity does not keep pace with the increase of the impurity itself. A small smudge on an otherwise completely clean surface disturbs us exceptionally; if a second one is added, the displeasure usually grows, but in a much smaller proportion, and under some circumstances, hardly at all. Many a woman is beside herself over the first stain made on her white dress or tablecloth; if a second one is added, she thinks there was nothing left to lose. In this, of course, ethical considerations also come into play...

<summary>This page discusses how perfect uniformity in colors, lines, and tones provides a basic aesthetic pleasure through "unity," and explains why even a single small defect can disproportionately ruin the experience of an otherwise perfect object.</summary>
<keywords>aesthetic unity, multiplicity, uniformity, displeasure, sensory perception, purity, metronome</keywords>
