<meta>This page continues the discussion of how small irregularities or "spots" disrupt the perception of a pure form. The previous page ended by describing how any noise mixed into a pure tone or any irregular thickening of a line creates a sense of displeasure.</meta>

...however, these go hand in hand with aesthetic ones, and the same principle applies to moral stains as it does to physical ones. There is a twofold reason why the feeling of displeasure often lags behind the actual cause of it. First, according to a **psychophysical** law (already touched upon on page 53) that can be applied to **aesthetics** through its relationship to <term>pleasurable and unpleasurable stimuli</term><note>original: *Lust- und Unlustreize*; sensory inputs that trigger positive or negative feelings.</note>, a sensation generally grows more slowly than the stimulus itself once it passes a certain intensity, or it may eventually not be noticeable at all. A light brought into a nearly dark room adds an extraordinary amount of brightness; a second identical light added thereafter increases the brightness only in a disproportionately smaller ratio. Second, when a disturbing spot is doubled, the disturbance itself is not quite doubled, insofar as the disturbing spots themselves and the ways they cause disturbance offer something identical. Both reasons should generally be considered together.

As little as uniform purity is able to captivate us for long on its own, the purity of **contours** and colors in the individual parts of a **work of art** is generally welcome to us. This is because each part, by itself, demands only a brief observation, beyond which it would begin to become boring. We soon pass from one part to another in order to perceive the <term>higher unified connection</term><note>original: *höheren Einheitsbezug*; the overarching relationship that binds diverse elements into a single meaningful whole.</note> existing between them; now, the perception of the lower order can combine with the uniformity of the parts in a beneficial way. Indisputably, we can also demand purity of contours because the object being represented is thereby brought more sharply into focus, but these two ideas do not contradict each other; rather, they assist one another. Otherwise, a cleanly drawn line could not please us more than an uncleanly drawn one even when it is not part of a larger drawing.

Naturally, the strongest disturbance to the uniformity of an impression is experienced through its complete interruption. One can say that such an interruption is inherently always in the sense of displeasure, except that the threshold of displeasure is not exceeded everywhere, especially by individual interruptions. Furthermore, the regularity of interruptions can bring about a compensation, provided that a higher **unified connection** occurs that is able to compensate for the break in the lower one, as is certainly the case with <term>meter</term><note>original: *Tact*; refers to rhythmic pulse, beat, or measure in music and poetry.</note> in...

<summary>The author explains the psychophysical reasons why sensory disturbances are not always proportional to their physical intensity and argues that while uniform purity is briefly pleasing, its main role is to facilitate the perception of a "higher unity" across the different parts of a work of art.</summary>
<keywords>psychophysics, sensory threshold, aesthetic purity, contours, unified connection, rhythm, perception</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the perception of sensory input, moving from how we perceive individual parts of a work of art to the general aesthetic preference for continuity and smoothness over abruptness.</meta>

applies to the field of hearing; yet this compensation is not sufficient everywhere else. Some kind of intermittent light stimulus can become downright painful to us through its interruptions; moving over a rough surface, the roughness of which rests only on interruptions, will suit no one, and in the same way, an irregular clattering displeases everyone. Sudden, strong changes come close to a complete interruption in their effect. Thus, generally speaking, everything rough, glaring, harsh, angular, abrupt, and jagged stands at a disadvantage in terms of **delight** compared to the gentle, round, flowing, self-contained, sequential, and transitionally mediated; and we not only involuntarily attach the idea of unpleasantness to those former expressions, but also use them directly to designate such a feeling.

<margin>Thin</margin>
The aesthetic advantage or disadvantage from the previous points of view can, of course, be outweighed in countless cases by counter-effects. The fact that woman has rounder, more flowing forms than man establishes, generally speaking, a beauty advantage for her over man, which falls under the previous point of view; but it would be impossible to understand and measure female beauty solely from this point of view. The thick, fat woman displeases us despite the fact that she surpasses the most beautiful man in the flowing roundness of her forms, even more so because, for a contemplation that is not too brief, the need for <term>manifoldness</term><note>original: *Mannichfaltigkeit*; the variety or diversity within a form that keeps the observer's interest.</note> is less satisfied by the simpler roundness of the form. She even displeases us because the forms of **corpulence** are linked to the unpleasant idea of a burdening of the body by a mass that adds nothing to its strength, only harms its free mobility, of vanished youth, and of a sluggish life. Meanwhile, among the Persians and Turks—to whom sluggish rest is more pleasing than displeasing—young girls are even <note>original: *gemästet*; literally "fattened up" or "stuffed," often used for livestock, reflecting the author's critical view of the practice.</note> fattened because of a lesser need for variety and the receding of those <term>associations</term><note>original: *Associationen*; mental connections where one thought or sight triggers another based on experience.</note>, in order to make them all the more charming through rounder forms.

A square cup would not be able to please us as well as a round one, even though it would meet the purpose just as well, because, all else being equal, the round is generally more pleasing than the angular; but in countless cases we prefer...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on aesthetic preferences, specifically following the previous page's mention of how a "fat woman" might displease the eye because her form lacks the necessary variety (manifoldness). It moves from the disadvantage of pure angularity toward how variety and uniformity must be balanced for beauty.</meta>

...yet for the sake of purpose or other secondary conditions, one prefers the angular, even the sharp corner.

If a surface, initially imagined as uniform, white, or colored, is marbled, hatched, or speckled, the <term>manifoldness</term><note>original: *Mannichfaltigkeit*; the variety, diversity, or complexity within a form that keeps the observer's interest.</note> increases, but the <term>unitary relation</term><note>original: *Einheitsbezug*; the cohesive connection or "oneness" that binds different parts of an object together in the mind of the viewer.</note> of all parts of the surface is more or less lost. If the variation introduced into it is completely without principle—if, for example, large blobs are placed here and small ones there, regular here and irregular there, red blobs here and black ones there, with straight, curved, and jagged lines all mixed together on the surface—experience teaches us that this pleases no one. Even the <term>tattooing</term><note>original: *Tättowiren*; here referring to the 19th-century anthropological observation of cultural body markings.</note> of savages adheres to principles of regularity: proof that <term>pleasurableness</term> cannot be achieved through the greatest possible variety alone. 

If, however, a certain common character persists through the marbling, hatching, or speckling without a strict rule—and even these terms themselves point toward such a character—then such a surface can not only be quite pleasing, but the unitary relation presupposed by that character (while less distinct than the lost <term>uniformity</term><note>original: *Gleichförmigkeit*</note>) can still be pronounced enough to provide a <term>pleasurable outcome</term><note>original: *Lusterfolg*; literally a "success of pleasure," referring to the psychological reward of aesthetic satisfaction.</note> in view of the increased variety. Indeed, to many people and under many circumstances, such things are more pleasing than a monotone color, though it cannot be calculated generally what *must* please more, because secondary circumstances and subjective moods come into play here. For instance, it was not so long ago that one saw marbled bookbindings everywhere; now, one sees them nowhere.

This last example, however, already touches upon the consideration of higher unitary relations, to which we now turn.

Closest to the simple unitary relation of untroubled uniformity is the uniform repetition of the same simple impressions in space or time. This is offered only approximately in the previous examples, but is offered decisively through completely regular speckling, striping, or <term>fluting</term><note>original: *Cannelirung*; the decorative grooves or channels often found on architectural columns.</note> of surfaces, or the regular rhythmic sequence of simple auditory impressions. Beyond this, however, every more complex rule, lawfulness, or order establishes a more or less high and complex unitary relation—for example, in symmetry, the golden [section...]

<meta>This page continues the exploration of aesthetic laws, specifically how simple uniformity is elevated into higher forms of beauty through patterns and rhythms. It follows the discussion of how variety must be balanced with order to remain pleasing.</meta>

Cross-sections, wave-lines, spirals, meanders, various kinds of wallpaper and carpet patterns, poetic meter, rhythm, rhyme.

Through every ascent to a higher <term>unitary relation</term><note>original: *Einheitsbezug*; the cohesive mental connection that binds different parts of an object together into a single "oneness."</note> above simple <term>uniformity</term><note>original: *Gleichförmigkeit*; the basic repetition of identical elements.</note>, the lower order of uniformity is itself violated. This is because a higher unity can only exist when there is a greater degree of difference between the parts than a mere spatial or temporal one. Through this, <term>manifoldness</term><note>original: *Mannichfaltigkeit*; the variety, diversity, or complexity within a form that keeps the observer’s interest.</note> is gained. As previously noted, the breaking of the lower unitary relation is compensated for by the higher one. This provides a double advantage: first, the greater variety is less likely to give way to boredom; second, the higher unitary relation meets a higher intellectual demand. Yet, these advantages do not entirely escape certain disadvantages, which may, under some circumstances, carry more weight.

On one hand, we find that in many cases the disruption of the lower unitary relation is felt with a stronger sense of displeasure than can be balanced by the ascent to the higher one. Secondly, the rule that establishes the higher unitary relation may be so complicated or of such a high order that it is no longer graspable; in such cases, it appears to us as disorder rather than order. In general, the difficulty of perceiving a unified relationship increases with its level of complexity. To be sure, with a simple pattern, we do not yet feel such a difficulty; however, the unified relationship of simple uniformity remains, so to speak, the most insistent. Even if, under certain conditions, the simple unitary relation of uniformity can maintain an advantage over a higher one, it is generally impossible to raise <term>pleasurableness</term><note>original: *Wohlgefälligkeit*; the quality of being aesthetically pleasing or agreeable to the senses.</note> as high through the former as one can with higher—though not *too* high—unitary relations; hence the frequent use made of them.

Thus, one gives all <term>vessels</term><note>original: *Gefässe*</note>, tools, and furniture a regular form insofar as the purpose permits—even when an irregular form would have served the purpose just as well. We love to cover clothes, carpets, and walls with regular patterns; we give furniture and pictures on the walls a symmetrical position in relation to one another; we flute columns; we arrange the bars of a gate according to a rule, and so on. In all of this, however, we still seek to preserve the advantages of the lower unitary relation of uniformity as well as possible, by...

<summary>The author explains how aesthetic pleasure is increased by moving from simple repetition to complex patterns (like rhythm and symmetry), while cautioning that over-complexity can lead to a sense of disorder.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how higher-order aesthetic relations (unitary relations) function compared to simple uniformity, specifically addressing how visual clarity and symmetry contribute to "pleasingness."</meta>

...that the parts which enter into the higher relationship are kept, as far as the purpose permits, smooth, in pure white or color, and in clean contours.

In all cases of practical use, of course, <term>co-determinants</term><note>original: *Mitbestimmungen*; external factors like utility, material, or tradition that influence how we perceive an object's beauty alongside its formal structure.</note> assert themselves; these factors, quite apart from the aforementioned internal conflicts of the principle, can either diminish or support the advantage of the higher <term>unitary relation</term><note>original: *Einheitsbezug*; the mental connection that links various parts into a single, cohesive whole.</note>. Otherwise, we would not still see so many white and monochromatic clothes and walls; nor, on the other hand, would we so often be in doubt as to whether we should attribute the <term>pleasingness</term><note>original: *Wohlgefälligkeit*; the quality of being aesthetically agreeable or satisfying.</note> of an object to its regular form or to the associative appropriateness of that form to its purpose. Generally speaking, however, the advantage of the higher unitary relation over the lower one of <term>uniformity</term><note>original: *Gleichförmigkeit*; simple, unvaried repetition.</note>—and certainly over total irregularity—shines through all these co-determinants, appearing all the more purely the more such factors are absent. To have this effect as purely as possible, one must exclude such factors as much as possible; and in this regard, nothing is more instructive than the almost magical performance of the kaleidoscope, which is noticeably removed from all external co-determinants.

Indeed, no matter how indifferent an arrangement or how unpleasing a disorder may be, the kaleidoscope forces pleasingness through the composite unitary relation of regular repetition with all-around symmetry. A well-known game achieves something similar with simple bilateral symmetry: whatever kind of <term>scribble</term><note>original: *Krakel*; a messy, meaningless scrawl or ink blot.</note> we make with ink on a piece of paper, if we fold the paper in the middle or at the edge of the scribble so that a symmetrical impression is created on the opposite side, a sense of pleasingness arises for the composition of the scribble and its reflection. This pleasingness suffers only a certain detraction from the "impurity" or blurring that the act of printing lends to the individual lines.

Following this, it is unquestionable that bilateral symmetry also plays an essential part in the pleasingness of the human figure. A single side of a human being on its own—aside from our prevailing habit of viewing the human form from associative perspectives—would appear to us merely as an irregular scribble. Indeed, if one violates this symmetry with a crooked nose or a crooked mouth, it becomes...

# 65
## Fechner, Propaedeutic to Aesthetics
### 5

...feel beauty strongly; this does not, however, exclude the possibility that entirely different factors contribute to human beauty, for it is, so to speak, as complex as the human being itself. But on this occasion, one can again recognize the effect of the <term>principle of help</term><note>original: *Hülfsprincip*; Fechner’s theory that multiple aesthetic factors reinforce each other to create a total impression greater than the sum of its parts.</note>. If one takes away the symmetry of the human body, its beauty loses much more than one would expect the effect of simple, meaningless symmetry to provide.

In cases of deviation from symmetry—just as with deviations from <term>uniformity</term><note>original: *Gleichförmigkeit*; the simplest level of aesthetic connection where parts are identical.</note>—it is evident that the decrease in <term>pleasingness</term><note>original: *Wohlgefälligkeit*; the quality of being aesthetically agreeable.</note> is not proportional to the degree of the deviation. If a rectangle is only very slightly skewed, we do not notice the deviation at all, and the <term>displeasingness</term><note>original: *Missfälligkeit*</note> remains, along with the perception of it, below the <term>threshold</term><note>original: *Schwelle*; a key concept in Fechner's psychophysics referring to the point at which a stimulus becomes perceptible to the senses.</note>. Yet even a small deviation, as soon as it becomes noticeable, can significantly disturb the pleasingness or turn it into displeasure. If the deviation increases, the displeasure also increases up to certain limits; however, it is by no means the case that we are affected by twice the deviation with twice the displeasure. Beyond certain limits, where the feeling of an approach to symmetry is lost, a further increase in the deviation no longer has a noticeable influence on increasing the displeasure.

However, the following proves that there can indeed be cases where—by ascending to a higher <term>unitary relation</term><note>original: *Einheitsbezug*; the mental connection that links various parts into a single, cohesive whole.</note> above that of uniformity—pleasingness can be lost because of too strong a violation of the lower relation, regardless of all other factors:

It is certain that if one strokes one's finger over the teeth of even the most regularly carved gear, one does not receive the same pleasant impression as when stroking over a completely smooth surface. This is because the frequent, total interruption of the uniform impression (which establishes the lower unitary relation in between) outweighs the advantage of the regular repetition (which establishes a higher unitary relation). For the same reason, even the most regularly intermittent light stimulus torments us. However, the fact that this is indeed a case of an outweighed advantage, rather than a missing one, is proven by the fact that the unpleasantness increases if the repetition is irregular; thus, the regularity does subtract something from the unpleasantness. There are also

<summary>Fechner explains that symmetry's aesthetic value is amplified when combined with other factors and that even small, noticeable deviations from symmetry can sharply decrease pleasure. He also notes that while regular repetition is a "higher" aesthetic form, it can be less pleasing than simple smoothness if it creates jarring interruptions.</summary>
<keywords>symmetry, pleasingness, principle of help, threshold, unitary relation, uniformity, sensory perception, aesthetics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the psychological perception of regularity and symmetry, specifically addressing how the "principle of help" applies to temporal patterns like musical rhythm compared to spatial patterns like grids.</meta>

# 66

...there are other cases where the disadvantage of frequent interruption does not carry the same weight as the advantage of regular repetition. For instance, a regular grid appears more pleasing to us than a smooth, continuous wall—thus, what applies to temporal interruptions in the field of vision does not necessarily transfer to spatial ones—and a regular, empty beat is at least no less pleasing than a continuous noise.

The fact that the behavior in these various cases differs cannot, of course, be predicted *a priori* <note>Latin for "from the beginning" or "before experience."</note> from the principle itself; however, this establishes no contradiction against it, since the conflict can quite easily be decided differently depending on the variation of the conditions.

No one will deny that a regular beat has a decided advantage in <term>pleasingness</term><note>original: *Wohlgefälligkeit*</note> over an irregular sequence of strikes; one also follows the regular course of even a few empty beats not unwillingly—certainly longer than a continuous, merely monotonous noise—in that the attention finds itself, so to speak, "cradled" in a not unpleasant manner. Only a longer continuation of empty beats fails to captivate the attention, just as any other uniform impression would. The decisive proof, however, that the regular beat is something more in the sense of pleasure than displeasure—though on its own it does not easily rise significantly above the <term>threshold</term><note>The point at which a stimulus is strong enough to be consciously perceived.</note>—lies in the fact that, when combined with other conditions of the same nature which music adds to it, it satisfies the <term>principle of aesthetic help</term> or <term>enhancement</term>. This means it produces a greater sum of pleasure than could be expected from the contributing elements on their own. The beat by itself admittedly signifies very little, but music without a beat could hardly exist. If the beat is then filled with the variety of elements that music brings to it, it is then tolerated almost indefinitely.

In the melodic and harmonic relationships of the tones themselves, our principle unquestionably plays its role. This does not happen in the manner described by <note>Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841) was a German philosopher and founder of pedagogy who attempted to apply mathematical formulas to psychology and aesthetics.</note>Herbart, who broke down pitches into "the same" and "the different." This approach led him to the oddity of finding in the <term>octave</term><note>The interval between one musical pitch and another with double its frequency.</note> the complete opposite of the root note, and to no less strange calculations; rather, it happens in the manner...

<summary>Fechner explains how regular repetition, such as a musical beat or a visual grid, creates pleasure through the "principle of aesthetic help," where simple elements combine to create a more powerful effect. He also begins a critique of Herbart’s mathematical approach to musical harmony.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the aesthetic principle of pleasure, moving from the physical perception of musical beats discussed on page 66 to the more complex psychological and conceptual "unity" found in higher art forms and cognitive representations.</meta>

# 67

just as <term>Helmholtz</term><note>Hermann von Helmholtz (1821–1894), a pioneering German physicist and physiologist who studied the perception of sound and "overtones" in his work *On the Sensations of Tone*.</note> considers the equality and difference of tones regarding their **overtones**. However, our principle cannot claim to offer more than a very general viewpoint of musical **pleasingness**; calculations cannot be based on its verdict at all.

With the examples so far, we have remained in the field of purely *intuitive* relations, by which we mean—for the sake of brevity—the relations of sensory impressions in general. But the principle reaches far and high beyond that into the entire realm of our **representations**<note>The term *Vorstellungen* refers to the mental images, ideas, or conceptions we form in our minds.</note> rising above it; while we will not fully traverse that realm here, we do wish to touch upon it in some points, as it has already been mentioned occasionally earlier regarding the contributing factors that flow from it.

This is seen, for instance, in asserting the viewpoint of **purposiveness**<note>original: *Zweckmässigkeit*. In aesthetics, this refers to the quality of an object appearing as if it were designed for a specific goal or "purpose," which creates a sense of harmony.</note>. Indeed, one of the reasons why the purposive pleases us—though not the only one—is that we find all parts of the purposive whole uniformly linked through the reference to the "idea of purpose." However, other ideas can also take the place of the idea of purpose. And so, ultimately, we demand of every **work of art** that all its parts are linked by a **unified idea** or the awakening of a unified mood. It is, in a sense, the supreme demand we must make of a work of art, whereby demands on the content contained under the idea are not excluded; the demand for **unity**, however, must be fulfilled even with the most varied content, lest the work of art suffer from an essential defect.

What now do we mean by a "unified idea" in this context? A still relatively simple, because abstract, **connection of representations**, in which not only all partial representations are linked by a common relation, but which also establishes a connection between all moments of concrete execution by the fact that they all relate to it as something common, either directly or through intermediaries.

No less than in actual works of art, however, our principle plays its role in so many small "artistic games," such as ingenious and witty comparisons, wordplays, and other trifles of subordinate **aesthetic interest**, whereby

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 67 regarding the aesthetic principle of "unity in diversity." While the previous page applied this to musical tones and the general "idea" of a work of art, this page explores how the principle functions in cognitive play, specifically through riddles and anecdotes.</meta>

# 68

certainly there are still several other factors at play, which we will not go into for now. Let us consider just one example here:

**Riddles** please us because they make us first seek out the **unified connection** in the riddle's resolution for a given <term>manifold of representations</term><note>A "manifold" (Mannichfaltigkeit) is a philosophical term referring to a diverse or fragmented collection of sensations and ideas that the mind seeks to organize into a single, coherent thought.</note>. The charm of a successful resolution lies in discovering this relationship, while the anticipation that a solution *can* be found provides a foretaste of that pleasure. This anticipation is actually necessary for us to find joy in the act of guessing itself; for no one wants to guess riddles that they know have no solution. In such a case, one would experience only the pure **displeasure** of a fragmented complex of representations. Likewise, anyone who is aware that they are poor at guessing riddles finds no taste for them. With **charades**<note>A word-guessing game where each syllable of a word is described or acted out as a separate hint.</note>, however, it is always an advantage if the task for the various syllables or word divisions is somehow unified and interwoven, rather than appearing as an independent riddle for each part.

Undeniably, the charm of riddle-guessing is also aided by the pleasure of overcoming a difficulty that we find ourselves equal to. According to another principle, alongside the need for unity, we also have a need for a certain degree of **occupation**<note>Original: *Beschäftigung*. This refers to the active mental engagement or "busyness" required to stay interested in a task.</note>. However, we demand a unified connection for this occupation by directing it toward a specific goal—even regardless of the nature of that goal itself. Therefore, riddles that are too easy to guess do not interest us. But in general, we want something more than just the overcoming of a difficulty to result from our efforts. We therefore like to read through a longer riddle even after it has been solved, in order to enjoy the unified connection of the entire content through the keyword of the riddle; at the same time, we notice with displeasure anything that does not quite seem to fit.

However much we may delight in clever and witty comparisons, puns, pretty riddles, and charades—and however much **anecdotes** may amuse us from one perspective or another—and however gladly we might read or hear several of them in a row, we cannot bring ourselves to listen to or read a long series of them. Even before reaching the twentieth, we have thoroughly

It also notes that while the mind enjoys the challenge of overcoming difficulties, it tires quickly of repetitive intellectual games like anecdotes.</summary>

<keywords>riddles, charades, unity in diversity, mental representations, pleasure and displeasure, cognitive aesthetics, anecdotes, psychological engagement</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Principle of Unity," specifically how a "connecting thread" maintains interest in longer works like novels compared to fragmented collections of anecdotes. It then transitions into the second half of the principle: the necessity of "Diversity" (Mannichfaltigkeit).</meta>

...satiated; while we might easily read an entire volume of a good novel in one sitting, unable, so to speak, to tear ourselves away from it—even though we might derive a greater <term>yield of pleasure</term><note>Original: *Lustertrag*. This refers to the specific "profit" or amount of aesthetic satisfaction gained from an experience.</note> from each individual anecdote than from any segment of the novel of equal length. One might imagine that the constant change of content in a collection of anecdotes would keep our <term>excitability</term><note>Original: *Erregbarkeit*. In 19th-century psychology, this refers to the mind's capacity to remain responsive to stimuli before becoming fatigued or bored.</note> perpetually fresh. Yet it is this very change, lacking a connecting thread, that prevents us from persisting in the reading for long. Indeed, if each comparison or anecdote did not satisfy the principle of unified connection in itself, and interest us through its content, we would be able to endure them even less.

So much for the explanation of the aspect of **Unity**, which is central to our principle. Let us turn now to that of **Diversity**. We should first remind ourselves generally that the feeling of **monotony** sets in sooner and more intensely the more diversity is lacking. Thus, pure uniformity is more prone to monotony than the uniform repetition of a simple form, and that in turn more than the repetition of a complex pattern. We can also point specifically to many theatrical plays whose charm—while not resting on diversity alone—grows as diversity increases, provided the sense of unity is not lost, so as not to conflict with the first part of our principle.

In a **kaleidoscope**, one never places only one or two small stones, but a great many of them. This offers no benefit to the unified relationship—which always remains the same kind of symmetrical arrangement—but serves only to enhance **diversity**.

One can take delight for a long time in the **evolutions**<note>Referring here to the rhythmic, shifting patterns of movement, similar to a choreographed dance or military drill.</note> of a flight of pigeons or starlings; the more varied the turns, swoops, and changes of shape, the longer the pleasure lasts. Now the swarm bunches into a sphere, now it stretches into an ellipsoid; now it shows us a broad side, now a narrow one; now it contracts and thereby darkens; now it expands and thereby grows bright; now the mass divides, now it reunites, and often, quick as lightning, one change passes into the next;

<summary>The author explains that while individual anecdotes provide high "pleasure yields," a novel's unified narrative thread is what prevents mental fatigue. The text then introduces the importance of diversity, using the visual complexity of kaleidoscopes and bird swarms to show how variety sustains aesthetic interest.</summary>
<keywords>unity, diversity, monotony, aesthetics, novel vs. anecdote, kaleidoscope, bird flight, psychology of pleasure</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the "Principle of Unity and Diversity," moving from literary examples to visual phenomena like nature, military formations, and the decorative arts. One does not grow tired of watching this. It is similar with the <term>evolutions</term><note>In a 19th-century military context, "evolutions" refers to the complex, disciplined movements and tactical formations performed by troops on a parade ground or battlefield.</note> and maneuvers of soldiers. Indeed, one can even follow the movements of a pennant fluttering in a strong wind with entertainment and interest for some time—as it now spreads out flat, now bulges, now entangles itself so thoroughly that one thinks it could never be undone, only to release itself again, enter a new entanglement, now rearing upward, now driven downward or to the sides. In the year 1870 <note>This refers to the Franco-Prussian War (1870–1871), a period of intense German national sentiment where victory news was frequently celebrated with public displays of flags.</note>, the decoration of houses with flags, which was repeated with every new report of victory, frequently offered the opportunity to be entertained by this spectacle.

In all these cases, we are not dealing with a purely fragmented <term>diversity</term><note>Original: *Mannichfaltigkeit* (Manifold). A core concept in 19th-century German aesthetics, referring to a variety of different parts that must be organized into a whole.</note>; rather, the togetherness of all parts is mediated on the objective side: in the flight of pigeons or starlings by their social instinct, in the evolutions and maneuvers of soldiers by the will of the commander and the intent of the maneuvers, and in the fluttering flag by the force of material connection. A unified impression of this remains subjectively consistent through all the changes. However, the pleasure of the entertainment does not grow with the impression of this consistently uniform connection, but rather with that of the diversity.

Among the most effective means of countering monotony in objects are **ornamentations**. To be tasteful, such decorations must always be motivated by a unified relationship to the form, to the purpose of the object, or to the circumstances in which it is to be viewed. That is, they should subordinate themselves to the unified impression of the object rather than damage it. Insofar as they fulfill this condition, they will—generally speaking—make the object appear more pleasing the more diverse they are.

In **works of art**, where an entire structure of higher relationships exists above lower ones, culminating in the "idea" of the artwork, diversity grows with the height of this structure. This happens not only by increasing the variety of the underlying sensory material but also through the stages of the relationships rising above it—to put it briefly, not just in "breadth" but also in "height." According to this...

### 3) Material Conflicts and Aids

We have had several opportunities to speak of <term>associative co-determinations</term><note>This refers to how our previous experiences, memories, and learned meanings (associations) influence our current aesthetic judgment of an object.</note> of our principle, and Section IX will go into this perspective in more detail; for now, however, we highlight another very general perspective of co-determination. This perspective frequently combines and intersects with the previous one and can condition <term>conflicts</term> as well as <term>aids</term> for the principle in equal measure.

In every object, besides the relationships of equality and inequality to which our principle refers, the nature of what enters into these relationships also matters. We categorize the former briefly as the **formal side** and the latter as the **material side** of the object.<note>In 19th-century aesthetics, the "formal" side refers to the structure, pattern, or arrangement of parts (unity in diversity), while the "material" or "substantive" side (sachliche Seite) refers to the actual content, such as the specific colors, sounds, or the sensory "stuff" being arranged.</note> *) This does not exclude the possibility that relationships of equality and inequality may again enter into the material side itself, though only in a subordinate way. In any case, the nature of an object is not entirely exhausted by such relationships; rather, one can still distinguish a **content** or **substance**, which is subject to these relationships, as the material side. Now, the aesthetic impression determined on the formal side by our principle can simultaneously be aesthetically determined on the material side in conflict or agreement with it.

The simplest example of such a conflict is found in the fact that a purely bitter taste or a purely foul smell will, in any case—unless we have become desensitized to it—displease...

---

*) <margin>Regarding the formal side, we will hereafter also include relationships of **consistency** <note>Original: *Widerspruchslosigkeit*, literally "freedom from contradiction."</note> and **clarity**, which will be discussed in the two following sections, and to which a material side of the content corresponds no less.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "material side" of aesthetic objects. The previous page ended mid-sentence, suggesting that even if a sensation (like a bitter taste) is simple and "pure," it still displeases us. The first word on this page, "fällt," is the conclusion of the German word "missfällt" (displeases).</meta>

...displeases us, despite the fact that it satisfies the **principle of uniform connection** <term>Principle of Uniform Connection</term><note>Also known as "unity in diversity," this is the idea that we find pleasure in seeing different elements tied together by a single rule or character.</note> as well as a pure surface of color, the pure stroke of a line, or a purely sustained tone. However, a purely bitter taste and so on does not displease us because there is something inherently displeasing about "purity" itself, but rather because as the purity increases, so does the quantity of the <term>displeasure-effect</term> that depends on that specific quality. In this simplest case, the material or substantive side is reduced simply to its **quality**.

Conversely, the pleasurability of a pure surface of color, a pure stroke of a line, or a purely sustained tone cannot be attributed solely to their quality while ignoring the principle of unity. If that were the case, one would be able to "beautify" any uniform surface simply by adding irregular and randomly scattered splotches of an inherently pleasing color—which is clearly not the case. Furthermore, a purely and sharply drawn line pleases us more than an uncertain and wavering one, even though the "quality" of the line (the ink or graphite) is the same in both instances. We would also much rather hear a song sung with a rough tone that remains consistent in character than a song where inherently "pleasant" tones are mixed in irregularly, breaking away from that established character.

If, on the contrary, one requires a simple example of **substantive support** for our principle, one only needs to point out that (setting aside <term>associations</term><note>The mental connections or memories a person brings to an object.</note> which can change the outcome) the same surface pleases us more when it is coated in a deep or fiery pure color than when it is covered in pure gray or even black.

Through the preceding examples, we are made aware that we must not only look for the existence of a uniform connection, but also pay attention to the **nature of what is being uniformly connected** in order to correctly judge the overall aesthetic result. What these examples from the "lowest" aesthetic realms (simple colors and tones) have taught us applies equally to the "highest" realms. Accordingly, works of art that satisfy the principle of uniform connection in a way that should be pleasing can still displease us if their content is repulsive; conversely, they can please us all the more if their content is inherently agreeable.

Thus, through the harmony of all means of representation toward an unpleasant idea, the unpleasantness of that idea will assert itself with even greater force, while the good harmony itself can still please us; in such cases, it cannot be said in general what will prevail in the whole, as it will rather depend on the direction the mind takes during perception—whether it leans more toward the **formal** or the **substantive** side.

The fact that the **associative** and **substantive** factors of our principle can cross one another lies in the fact that associative ideas—which attach themselves as co-determinants to a direct impression—are themselves subject to the relationships of unity and diversity. Thus, they can exert this influence not only on the formal side through their unified connection but also on the substantive side through the connected content.

### 4) Specific Determinations.

Diversity <term>Diversity</term><note>The original German "Mannichfaltigkeit" (manifoldness) refers to the "variety" side of the "unity in variety" equation.</note> in an object can grow from three different points of view: first, insofar as the quantity of spatially or temporally distinct elements increases; second, insofar as the number of differences increases or differences occur in several respects; third, insofar as the degree of difference grows. These may be briefly distinguished as the *extensive*, *multiple*, and *gradual* aspects of diversity. 

aaaaaa, ababab, abcdef possess equal **extensive** diversity, insofar as they include an equal number of spatially or temporally distinct parts, but they follow the established order regarding **multiple** diversity. A polygon <margin>polygon</margin> retains the same extensive diversity as long as the number of sides remains the same, however the relationship of the sides and angles may change; but the multiple diversity grows if sides or angles change from being equal to unequal, and the gradual diversity grows with the degree of this inequality.

The equality or the same relation, which constitutes the unity-relation <term>Unity-relation</term><note>original: *Einheitsbezug*. This refers to the specific way different parts are connected or relate to one another to form a whole.</note>, can involve more or fewer parts and, by virtue of this, be more or less complete; it can occur from more or fewer viewpoints; and finally, it can be more or less approximate, or rather, perfect. Accordingly, differentiating terms can be applied here just as they are to the various aspects of diversity; however, instead of a "multiple" unity-relation, one can also speak of a "composite" one.

Diversity and unity can grow quantitatively in all their aspects simultaneously, but they can also grow at the expense of one another. They grow **extensively** together, for example, when uniformity or a regular pattern extends over a larger surface, or when the number of sides of a regular polygon <term>Polygon</term><note>original: *Vieleck*.</note> increases while maintaining the equality of its sides and angles. They grow **multiply** <note>Meaning from multiple perspectives or "multi-sidedly."</note> together when the sides of a regular polygon are colored differently but in a regular alternation. They grow **gradually** together insofar as stronger differences between the members of a diversity allow the higher unity—which rests on the equality or harmony of these differences in something shared—to express itself with greater power, where such unity exists. However, the number of distinct elements and the degree of differences can also grow without the increase being subordinated to the old unity or creating unity where none exists. Generally speaking, it is easier to maintain a smaller diversity in a unified connection than a larger one; and a composite unity-relation may itself lack a unified connection between the various unifying viewpoints from which it is composed.

This primarily concerned the objective relations of diversity and unity; ultimately, however, our principle depends on diversity and unity as they appear *within us*—in short, the **subjective** side. While this certainly depends essentially on the objective side, it is also significantly co-determined by purely subjective conditions, namely the direction and the relative degree of concentration <term>Concentration</term><note>original: *Concentrationsgrade*.</note> of attention, the sharpness...

a) Every unified connection that occupies our attention is in the sense of **pleasure**, provided it does not claim to occupy us for too long or to too great an extent.

Of such extremes one can say that they are unconditionally displeasing, and displeasure is to be expected with all the more certainty the closer a case comes to one extreme or the other.

Should unity or diversity be favored from this point onward at the expense of its opposite, the pleasurableness decreases, the continuation of the observation is tolerated for a shorter time, or actual displeasure occurs. The unrefined person perceives the existence of higher unified relations with as little pleasure as he misses them with displeasure when they are absent.

h) If, after all this, the **principle** <term>principle</term><note>original: *Princip*. Here referring to the "Principle of Unitary Connection."</note> still leaves a great deal of uncertainty regarding the conclusions to be drawn for an individual case, one can nonetheless judge in each case by the feeling of monotony or **fragmentation** <note>original: *Zersplitterung*. The feeling that things are falling apart into disconnected pieces.</note> whether the balance has been violated in one direction or the other.

### 5) Universality of the Principle.

Although we are primarily considering our principle here in terms of its significance for **aesthetics** <term>aesthetics</term><note>original: *Aesthetik*.</note>—and thus only for receptive impressions mediated through the senses—it may nonetheless be useful to add something about its broader general scope, especially since there is no strict boundary in this regard.

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Principle of Unitary Connection of the Manifold," moving from mental development to how this principle governs both active physical movement and receptive experiences, specifically through the concept of rhythm.</meta>

Right at the beginning, the principle was stated in its generality: that it applies just as much to **active** as to **receptive** occupation. Every physical and mental **activity** <note>original: *Thätigkeit*.</note>, in order to please us, must be carried out within a certain context; it cannot tolerate frequent interruption, but it can also become tiring through **monotony**. Furthermore, anyone capable of higher **perspectives** <note>original: *Gesichtspuncte*.</note> also demands such perspectives to link the various moments of their activity. Aimlessly flailing with hands and feet does not satisfy us because it lacks a connecting, ideal motive for the individual movements; but physical activity also requires a unified internal connection, and it is of no small interest to see how **rhythm** <term>rhythm</term><note>original: *Tact*. In this context, it refers to a regular beat or pulse, similar to a musical measure.</note> plays a corresponding role in this area as it does in our receptive auditory impressions.

Indeed, we prefer to perform all our movements rhythmically rather than without rhythm, unless the irregularity serves some specific purpose. We walk rhythmically, breathe rhythmically, swallow rhythmically when drinking, bring the spoon to the mouth rhythmically, hammer in a nail rhythmically, and drum our fingers on the table rhythmically for entertainment. In dance, however, the effect of the rhythm of our own bodily movement is heightened by the rhythmic impression of the music and its other elements, achieving a higher level of performance. Even in entirely involuntary movements, the advantage of rhythm asserts itself; generally, a person feels better the more regular their **heartbeat** <note>original: *Herzschlag*.</note> and the **peristaltic movement** <term>peristalsis</term><note>original: *peristaltische Bewegung*. The wave-like muscle contractions that move food through the digestive tract.</note> of their intestines are. Indeed, one generally feels better the more regular their overall lifestyle is—that is, the more regular the periods in which one repeats the same tasks, provided there is sufficient variety between them. In contrast, strong deviations from this order may be enjoyed as exceptions, but they must only occur exceptionally. Regular periods and rhythm share the common feature of repeating the same moments at equal intervals of time. However, a continuous periodic movement has a twofold aesthetic advantage over the rhythm of separate, short beats: first, each period still contains its own **variety** <note>original: *Mannichfaltigkeit*.</note>, and second, there is no full interruption of the movement.

In passing, here are a few [points] concerning the inner **psychophysics** <term>psychophysics</term><note>original: *Psychophysik*. A field of study, pioneered by Gustav Fechner, exploring the functional relationship between physical stimuli and mental sensations.</note> of aesthetic fe—

It argues that regular, rhythmic patterns in movement and lifestyle provide aesthetic pleasure and physical well-being by balancing unity and variety.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Principle of Unitary Connection of the Manifold," specifically finishing the thought on rhythmic bodily functions (like the heartbeat) before moving into a theoretical discussion of "vibrations" and the distinction between internal and external psychophysics.</meta>

...feel pertinent remarks, to which the great role played by the regular period and its subordinate rhythm <term>rhythm</term><note>original: *Tact*. Here referring to a regular beat or measure.</note> in both **active** and **receptive** occupations may give occasion.

Presumably, both visual and auditory impressions rely on **vibrations** <term>vibrations</term><note>original: *Schwingungen*.</note> within our nervous system. Based on this, we can trace the pleasantness of a purely uniform surface of color, or a purely sustained tone, back to the principle of consistent periodic movement; in the first case, all particles of our retina are set into the same periodic motion, while in the latter, the particles of the auditory nerve are continuously maintained in such a state. Furthermore, we could imagine that every pleasing color pattern and musical piece pleases us only through more complex but **commensurate** <note>original: *commensurabel*. This refers to cycles that synchronize or share a common measure.</note> **periodicity ratios** \*) of the nerve vibrations. Finally, expanding and intensifying this hypothesis even further, we might think that all sensations and activities of consciousness generally rely on vibrations in our nerves, and all pleasure and displeasure depend on whether these vibrations—in simpler or more complex periods—approach or depart from full harmony in commensurate ratios beyond a certain limit. It is very possible, and in my opinion even probable, that something of this kind takes place; it also fits essentially into a hypothesis I have expressed elsewhere in a more generalized form (see page 12). However, for one thing, it is so far only a **hypothesis**, which can neither replace nor confuse the reference to factual relationships and laws, insofar as they can be found. Secondly, it would require a more precise definition to enter into exact considerations and be compared with experience. Finally, it is just a hypothesis of **internal psychophysics** <term>internal psychophysics</term><note>A term coined by Fechner to describe the relationship between the mind and the physical processes inside the body, such as nerve impulses.</note>. As noted earlier, we should make no use of this in our aesthetics, given the limitations we have set here; this is because aesthetics does not deal with the relationship of our sensations to the physical activities underlying them in our nerves, but rather (by skipping this unknown intermediate link) with the relationships of sensations to the influences of the **outside world**, insofar as pleasure and displeasure are involved. Accordingly, aesthetics transitions from the facts of **external psychophysics** <term>external psychophysics</term><note>The study of how external physical stimuli (like light or sound) relate to conscious experience.</note> into the purely psychological rather than the internal psychophysical domain.

In science, the **formal** joy (independent of the actual content of scientific consideration) that we find in **unified perspectives** <note>original: *Gesichtspuncte*.</note> rests upon our principle...

Refers to the variety or diversity of components in an experience.</note>, but only by an interpenetration of the one through the other.

# VII. Principle of Non-Contradiction, Agreement, or Truth.

Generally speaking, it is in the interest of pleasure <term>pleasure</term><note>original: *Lust*. In this psychological context, it refers to a positive feeling or aesthetic satisfaction.</note> to become conscious of the agreement <term>agreement</term><note>original: *Einstimmigkeit*. Literally "one-voicedness" or harmony.</note> or lack of contradiction between mental representations or thoughts that present themselves from different sides regarding one and the same object; conversely, it is in the interest of displeasure <term>displeasure</term><note>original: *Unlust*.</note> to perceive a contradiction between them. However, it is necessary to correctly understand the expression "contradiction" and its opposite, "agreement," as the absence of contradiction.

There is no contradiction in imagining a thing as simultaneously black and white if the representation of black and white refers to different sides or spots of the thing—which are fundamentally not the same thing. Likewise, there is no contradiction in imagining a specific spot of the thing as black and then white in succession, which is fundamentally no longer the same thing; indeed, the concept of such changes is quite familiar to us. However, there *is* a contradiction in imagining the same spot as simultaneously black and white. And, although such a contradiction cannot arise within us from the direct observation of reality, it can nevertheless happen—where reality is not or not fully subject to our experience—that there is an occasion from one side, or a demand, to harbor one representation, while from another side, there is a demand for a contradictory representation; or there might also be an occasion for a...

<summary>This page explores the aesthetic and psychological need to reconcile the many into the one and introduces the principle that cognitive harmony (non-contradiction) produces pleasure while contradiction produces displeasure.</summary>
<keywords>non-contradiction, harmony, pleasure and displeasure, manifoldness, mental representation, cognitive truth, aesthetic principles</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Principle of Non-Contradiction, Agreement, or Truth," moving from the definition of contradiction to how our memory and expectations influence our aesthetic reaction to consistency or inconsistency in thought.</meta>

...an idea can be held that contradicts a mental representation <term>mental representation</term><note>original: *Vorstellung*. This refers to the images, ideas, or thoughts present in the mind.</note> derived from direct experience. Thus, historical records may conflict with each other, or historical accounts may conflict with conclusions drawn from facts; likewise, theoretical deductions may conflict with one another or with observed facts—or, alternatively, they may be in agreement.

It can happen that such occasions for contradictory ideas occur at different times. By yielding to one occasion at one time and to another later on, we may not notice the contradiction at all. For example, today we might read a report stating that the sun was eclipsed on a certain historical date, and a year later read a report that the sun was supposed to have shone during that same time, without ever comparing the two statements. Or we might read in the Bible today that "man lives, moves, and has his being in God," <note>A reference to Acts 17:28. The original German is *der Mensch in Gott lebe, webe und sei*.</note> yet find another occasion to see God as an entity standing over and against us, just as one human stands before another. Where a contradiction is not perceived—because memory fails to build a bridge between the conflicting ideas—there is no occasion for displeasure <term>displeasure</term><note>original: *Unlust*. A feeling of cognitive or aesthetic discomfort.</note>, and the contradiction remains aesthetically indifferent. However, displeasure crosses the threshold of our awareness more easily the more our memory of one idea asserts itself against its opposite.

Just as the existence of a contradiction does not always arouse displeasure, the agreement of ideas does not always arouse pleasure. To see that a planet occupies a certain place at a certain time contains no contradiction; however, even if we are aware that no contradiction exists here, that alone provides no occasion for pleasure. Pleasure arises, however, when we become aware that its observed position matches its calculated position, or when two different calculations conducted from different starting points agree with one another. Thus, the <term>consciousness of the possibility</term> <note>original: *Bewusstsein der Möglichkeit*.</note> of a contradiction, or the actual resolution of one between two ideas presented from different sides, is always required to establish pleasure in their agreement.

The agreement or conflict between two ideas or "circles of ideas" can affect our broader field of knowledge more or less deeply, in that...

->Fechner, Propaedeutic to Aesthetics.<- <note>original: *Vorschule d. Aesthetik*.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Principle of Non-Contradiction, Agreement, or Truth," specifically exploring how the mind reacts to cognitive harmony or conflict. ...agreement or a contradiction is established with more or fewer other mental representations <term>mental representations</term><note>original: *Vorstellungen*. This refers to the images, ideas, or thoughts present in the mind.</note> or circles of ideas; it can also be more or less practically consequential for us. The more one or the other or both is the case, the more easily the pleasure <term>pleasure</term><note>original: *Lust*. An aesthetic or psychological sense of satisfaction or "rightness."</note> in agreement or the displeasure <term>displeasure</term><note>original: *Unlust*.</note> in conflict, respectively, crosses the threshold <note>A reference to the "threshold of consciousness," a key concept in 19th-century psychology (specifically Herbartianism), meaning the point at which a feeling or thought becomes strong enough to be consciously noticed.</note>, whereas under opposite conditions it easily remains below the threshold. 

Indeed, a contradiction that is theoretically and practically indifferent to us can even—instead of awakening displeasure—create the impression of humor or ridiculousness. In such cases, the pleasure we find in the surprising connection of contradictory ideas gains the upper hand. This happens through the mediation of the very thing to which both ideas refer (a point to which we shall return elsewhere). A conflict certainly takes place here regardless; this explains why one person finds a contradiction amusing, while another is annoyed by its folly.

Summarizing the previous points and setting aside conflicts for a moment, we can briefly say: when differing occasions arise to imagine one and the same thing <term>thing</term><note>original: *Sache*. An object of thought or a matter of fact.</note>, it is conducive to pleasure to perceive that they actually lead to a consistent mental representation; it is conducive to displeasure to perceive that they lead to a contradictory representation. However, we are dealing with the representation of one and the same thing only when we relate that representation to the same space, the same time, and an otherwise internally consistent complex of ideas related to that space and time.

If, within a coherent circle of mental representations, no contradiction exists between any of its parts, we ascribe **inner truth** to this connection—regardless of whether the ideas refer to external reality or whether anything in external reality corresponds to them or not. We speak, however, of **outer truth** where a coherent set of ideas or a single idea refers to the existence of external reality and exists without contradiction alongside the totality of ideas that can be awakened by external reality—since, after all, we cannot have external reality itself within our mind;

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Principle of Non-Contradiction," specifically focusing on the "formal" versus "objective" reasons why humans find pleasure in truth. ...without our having an absolute criterion for external truth.
According to this, the pleasure <term>pleasure</term><note>original: *Gefallen*. In this context, it refers to a psychological or aesthetic sense of satisfaction or "rightness."</note> we take in the recognition of inner and outer truth—regardless of the nature of the content and the utility of that truth—depends on our principle; likewise, the displeasure <term>displeasure</term><note>original: *Missfallen*.</note> we feel toward untruth and lies—regardless of the nature of the content and the ill consequences of the lie—also depends on it. This we can designate as the **formal side** of pleasure and displeasure in truth and untruth.

Since, however, a thing can please or displease us based on the nature of its content (regardless of whether it is true or false), and because truth generally has useful consequences while untruth has harmful ones, or because they enter into a pleasant or unpleasant context, our consciousness of these factors can also help determine our pleasure or displeasure in what is true or false. This we can designate as the **objective side** <term>objective side</term><note>original: *sachliche Seite*. This refers to the "matter" or substance of the information—its practical or material meaning—rather than its logical structure.</note> of pleasure and displeasure. This side, however, does not strictly concern truth and untruth themselves, but rather that in which they are expressed and what follows from them, insofar as they fall under other principles.

The formal side of the pleasure in truth is sufficient on its own—without being the only effective factor in every case—to drive the search for truth in science and the demand for "truth of representation" in art. Therefore, in science, we do not rest until no contradiction can be found from any side between two mental representations <term>mental representations</term><note>original: *Vorstellungen*. The basic units of thought, such as images, ideas, or perceptions.</note> or complexes of ideas. Similarly, we are only satisfied by works of art that, firstly, satisfy the demands of **inner truth**—according to which no individual element may contradict the idea that the whole awakens (or, what is related if not identical, no part of the representations may contradict the totality of the others)—and, secondly, satisfy the demands of **outer truth** as far as we find reason to assume a correspondence between the artwork and external objects according to their idea or purpose.

However, insofar as this correspondence is not the case at all in music, and only to a limited degree in the visual arts <term>visual arts</term><note>original: *bildende Kunst*.</note>, a lack of agreement between a work of art and external reality will not be generally displeasurable. One can also consider how far deviations from external truth in given...

An angel with wings does not occur in reality; but we also do not assume that the painted angel is intended to represent a really-existing angel—in which case it would truly displease <term>displease</term><note>original: *missfallen*. Thus, there is no contradiction in the mental representation. However, it must not contain real or psychological impossibilities or strong improbabilities that contradict the general conditions of existence, the awareness of which accompanies us as a requirement while reading the novel.

->three highest formal principles.<-

Both...

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of aesthetic pleasure, specifically focusing on the "formal principles" of the mind. It moves from the specific discussion of truth and contradiction into a summary of the three "supreme formal principles": Unity, Harmony (Truth), and Clarity. It explores how these principles interact and conflict in both philosophy and art.</meta>

I combine these principles with the principle of **clarity**—which I will mention only briefly here rather than discuss in depth—under the designation of the three supreme **formal principles** <term>formal principles</term><note>In 19th-century aesthetics, these are rules governing the *form* or structure of our experiences (like harmony and logic), rather than the *content* or subject matter itself.</note>.

This third principle intersects with the other two, in that the pleasure <term>pleasure</term><note>original: *Gefallen*. This refers to the positive aesthetic response or "liking" we feel toward an object or idea.</note> derived from its perspective depends on how clearly the similarities and differences, the harmonies and contradictions, within a **complex of representations** <term>complex of representations</term><note>original: *Vorstellungscomplex*. A grouping of mental images, memories, or thoughts occurring together in the mind.</note> rise above the "threshold of consciousness." They must be clear enough to allow these principles to exert an aesthetic effect in one direction or another. However, it can happen that we find joy in the *clarity* of an observation, even though the other two principles make us feel the *displeasure* of that same observation. For these formal principles can come into conflict with one another, just as they can with the substantive principles regarding the nature of the content itself.

Insofar as **philosophy** sets itself the highest scientific tasks, it also seeks to satisfy the demands of the three supreme formal principles as one. Philosophical striving finds no satisfaction until the entire **field of knowledge** <term>field of knowledge</term><note>original: *Erkenntnissgebiet*. The total scope of what can be known or understood through reason.</note> is not only internally consistent, but also unified through more general viewpoints—ideally a single, most general viewpoint—and its explanation has reached full clarity in every direction. Furthermore, the formal joy of engaging in philosophy would not only be the highest—if we judge the height of joy by the "height" or importance of the field in which it is expressed—but also the greatest, were it not for the fact that as viewpoints ascend higher, or as these higher viewpoints are applied to specifics, certainty, comprehensibility, or clarity usually tend to suffer.

**Art** does not set itself tasks as general as those of philosophy, since the study of its own principles belongs to the tasks of philosophy; however, within the realm of the representations it awakens through its methods, it must do justice to the three formal principles no less than philosophy or any science in general.

## IX. Aesthetic Association Principle.

### 1) Introduction.

By the **Association Principle** <term>Association Principle</term><note>The idea that our aesthetic pleasure in an object often comes from the memories, ideas, or emotions we "associate" with it, rather than just the object's physical form or colors.</note>, I mean a principle whose importance and scope has long been known and recognized in psychology, but which, on the whole, has been little appreciated in aesthetics thus far. It would be going too much to say that it has not been appreciated at all; indeed, how could it be important for aesthetics if its importance had not already made itself felt? In fact, the achievements of this principle are recognized everywhere because they appear everywhere, though without clearly recognizing or acknowledging the principle from which they flow. People certainly remember it from psychology when discussing aesthetics, but they do so more to eliminate it from the consideration of beauty—viewing it as an improper intrusion—than to use it for clarification.

It is true that the English thinkers Locke, Home, and Sayers, and among the Germans, Oersted and especially **Lotze**, have given it greater and more just attention as an aesthetic principle; but none of this has made a breakthrough here in Germany; only the neglect and rejection of it has truly taken hold. **Kant**, in his doctrine of so-called **dependent beauty** <term>dependent beauty</term><note>original: *anhängende Schönheit*. Kant’s term for beauty that depends on a concept of what the object is "supposed to be" (like a building or a human body), as opposed to "free beauty" (like a flower or a pattern).</note>, thought of the principle only to discredit it in matters of "pure" beauty. He found many followers in this regard, and once it was declared settled from that side, no one bothered with it further. **Schelling**, **Hegel**, and their successors ignored it from the very beginning; judging by their work, one might believe that nothing of the sort even exists. What **Herbart** says about the principle (see No. 11) could only contribute to the disregard of it. 

It is no wonder, then, that even art connoisseurs and writers on art, who depend on these philosophers, know nothing of it or want to know nothing of it—and the same goes for the artists and laypeople who, in turn, depend on them. In fact, when I gave a lecture on this principle in 1866 at the **Leipzig Art Association** <term>Kunstverein</term><note>A society of art lovers and patrons; these were central to the cultural and social life of 19th-century German cities.</note>, the essential content of which can be found in the following pages (though somewhat expanded), it piqued the interest of the laypeople as if it were something simultaneously problematic and new—yet something worth listening to. However, it was quite a **fiasco** <note>The author likely refers to the dismissive reaction from established art critics and academic philosophers who favored abstract idealism over his psychological, "bottom-up" approach to art.</note>...

<summary>Fechner introduces the Aesthetic Association Principle, arguing that while psychology recognizes the power of mental associations, German aesthetics has largely ignored or rejected them. He lists the few thinkers who supported the idea and notes the cold reception his own lecture on the topic received in 1866.</summary>

<keywords>Association Principle, Aesthetics, Psychology, Dependent Beauty, Kant, Lotze, Art Association, Leipzig, German Philosophy</keywords>

<meta>The previous page introduced the "Association Principle," noting that while it is established in psychology, it has been neglected or dismissed in German aesthetics. among the philosophically trained experts, whose circles of thought it threatened to disturb; a reprint of it in the Lützow-Seemann *Journal for Visual Arts* (1866, p. 179) was described by the editor in a note as an "original" attempt to "introduce a new deity into aesthetics." As little as the principle is actually new or original, a somewhat more detailed and emphatic advocacy of it in aesthetics than it has received so far seems appropriate. And so, in the face of the prevailing neglect and disregard for it since then, I will try to show that, so to speak, half of aesthetics depends on it—noting that Lotze had earlier made almost the entirety of aesthetics dependent upon it.*) However, because he provided no system, but merely a history of aesthetics and a few aesthetic essays,**) he found or took no opportunity to develop the principle as thoroughly as will be done here.

Admittedly, I understand such a far-reaching dependency only from a certain point of view. However, many general points of view intersect in aesthetics, upon which it could be made half or more than half dependent; and nothing will prevent us from doing justice to this elsewhere with other considerations.

In accordance with our "path from below" <term>path from below</term><note>The author’s inductive method of starting with simple, physical observations and everyday experiences rather than abstract, top-down philosophical theories.</note>, we begin again with an explanation of the simplest examples.

In what, then, lies the charm of its appearance? Naturally, everyone thinks first of its beautiful, pure gold color and its perfect roundness. And certainly, much lies in this; perhaps one even thinks that everything lies in this. Indeed, what else could it be? But if the reader were to ask this, it would be proof that our principle is not present in their mind; or, should something else occur to them, it would surely fall under that same principle. So, let us reflect for a moment whether the entire charm of this fruit’s appearance is truly based solely on its beautiful gold color and pure roundness!

I say no; for why would a yellow-varnished wooden ball not please us just as well as the orange, if we know that it is merely a wooden ball rather than an orange? Indeed, despite the fact that the orange has a rough skin—and roughness in general is less pleasing than smoothness, as is proven by comparing different wooden balls and aligns with a principle discussed earlier—we still like the rough orange better than the varnished wooden ball *).

This cannot lie in a superiority of the pleasing nature of the form and color in themselves; in this respect, both objects are equal, or the wooden ball might even have the advantage. The superiority of the orange can only lie in the fact that we see an orange in it, and not a wooden ball; that we link the meaning of the orange to its form and color. The meaning of the orange, however, certainly lies partly in its form and color, but by no means alone; rather, it lies in the totality of what it is and does, especially in—
