its relationship to ourselves and its effects. Even if only form and color are immediately present to the sense, memory adds the rest—not individually, but in a collective total impression. It carries this into the sensory impression, enriches it, and, so to speak, paints it in. We may call this, for short, the *spiritual color* <note>original: "geistige Farbe"; this refers to the mental or emotional "tint" added to an object by our memories and thoughts</note> that is added to the sensory color, or the *associated impression* that joins the direct one. It is for this reason that an orange appears more beautiful to us than a yellow wooden ball.

<margin>W. v. Humboldt</margin>
Indeed, does someone seeing an orange see merely a round yellow spot? With the physical eye, yes; but spiritually, he sees a thing of delightful scent and refreshing taste, grown on a beautiful tree in a beautiful land under a warm sky. In it, he sees, so to speak, all of Italy—the land toward which a romantic longing has always drawn us. From the memory of all these things, the *spiritual color* is composed, with which the sensory color is glazed and beautified. Meanwhile, he who sees a yellow wooden ball sees merely dry wood behind the round yellow spot—wood turned in a turner’s workshop and painted by a varnisher. In both cases, the impression resulting from memory associates itself so immediately with the <term>perception<gloss>Anschauung: the immediate sensory viewing or "intuition" of an object</gloss></term> and merges so completely with it, that it essentially defines its character as if it were an inherent part of the perception itself. Therefore, we are naturally inclined to count this association as a quality of the thing itself, and it is only through comparisons, such as the one we just made, that we can realize it is not.

Another example:

Why does a red cheek on a youthful face please us so much more than a pale one? Is it the beauty or the charm of the red in itself? Undoubtedly, that plays a part. A fresh red pleases the eye more than gray or a muddy color. But, I ask again, why then does an equally fresh red on a nose or a hand not please us just as well as on the cheek? Rather, it displeases us. The pleasing impression of the red must therefore be outweighed by a displeasing element in the case of the nose and hand. What could be the cause of this? It is not hard to find. To us, the red cheek signifies youth and health—

<summary>This page explains the "spiritual color" of objects, where memory and associations (like Italy for an orange) enrich sensory perception, and introduces how these associations can also make certain colors, like red, pleasing or displeasing depending on context.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual color, associated impression, perception, aesthetics, Wilhelm von Humboldt, orange vs. wooden ball, symbolism of red</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Association Principle" in aesthetics, moving from the example of the orange to how social status and cultural habits shape our perception of human beauty.</meta>

joy, blooming life; the red nose reminds one of drinking and copper-skin disease <note>original: "Kupferkrankheit"; a historical term for skin conditions like rosacea or redness associated with heavy drinking</note>, the red hand of washing, scrubbing, and messing about; these are things that we neither wish to have nor to do. We do not even wish to be reminded of them.

If, conversely, the red nose and pale cheek had appeared from time immemorial as a sign of health and moderation, and the pale nose and red cheek as a sign of the opposite, then the direction of our pleasure in them would also be reversed. North American and Polish women actually prefer a pale cheek to a red one, and if necessary, seek to obtain that paleness even at the expense of their health by drinking vinegar or using other means. Now, does one think this is because paleness in itself pleases them better than redness? Certainly not, but because they have become accustomed to seeing in the pale cheek the sign of a refined <term>constitution<gloss>the physical makeup or health of a person</gloss></term>, higher education, and social standing, and in the red cheek that of merely peasant health, and they prefer the former to the latter. For the same reason, crippled feet on their ladies appear pleasing to the Chinese, while the most beautiful natural ones seem peasant-clumsy; and they give their idols thick bellies because they are accustomed to seeing the most distinguished dignitaries of their empire with thick bellies, and they link to this the idea of a certain sublimity above earthly need and labor—which, of course, does not allow for thick bellies to develop <note>The author suggests that physical labor prevents the accumulation of fat, thus a "thick belly" becomes a status symbol of the leisure class</note>.

I once heard a lady say that one could only truly judge the beauty of a human foot when it is shod. If a particular sincerity had not been among this lady's virtues, she would probably have been shy about making this statement, as curious as it may seem to most. Yet it has something very true about it. We learn the meaning of the human foot almost exclusively while the shoe hides it, and we are only truly oriented toward the meaning of the shod foot. After all, we see almost only our own foot naked—which is not always the most beautiful—and the feet of statues, which we are accustomed to look at last when viewing a statue; therefore, the associations of the foot that help determine our pleasure in it are not nearly as familiar to us in the naked foot as in the shod one; and, while a certain artistic experience is required for the judgment of the beauty of the former, there is a need

...to judge the elegance and daintiness of the latter, one requires only ordinary social experience.

A blind woman, who could only grasp forms through the sense of touch, was asked why she liked the arm of a certain person so much. One might guess: she answered because she felt the gentle line, the beautiful fullness, the elastic swelling of the arm’s forms. Not at all; rather, she said it was because she felt that the arm was healthy, active, and light. She could not feel those things directly, but could only associate them with what she touched. Now, I do not believe that the direct impression—in which some would see the sole reason for pleasure—was without its part; but one see clearly that the <term>associated impression<gloss>the secondary meanings or feelings we link to a primary sensation</gloss></term> came even more vividly to her consciousness. For us sighted people, it is the reverse. We think we can simply "see" the beauty in a lovely arm, without suspecting that we are actually "looking" most of it into the object <note>The author uses a play on words: "absehen" (to see/deduce) versus "hineinsehen" (to project/read into).</note>.

No less than through the realm of the visible and tactile, this principle reaches through all other sensory areas, for which the following insertion offers a selection of further examples.

A woman who loved her husband very much said to him: "How happy I am that you have such a pretty name." The name was not actually very pretty, but she loved the man, and therefore the name pleased her. I myself remember that as a child, the name Kunigunde <note>original: "Kunigunde"; an old-fashioned, traditional Germanic female name</note> pleased me very much, until I met a girl of dreadful appearance and character with this name; immediately, the name became dreadful to me. And since I have not met a particularly lovely Kunigunde since then, the impression has remained.

The croaking of frogs is not graceful in itself, and in a concert hall—where we are primarily concerned with the direct impression of the music—one would certainly not want to hear a frog concert or a quacking singer. In the great outdoors, however, the frog’s cry pleases us, partly as an expression of the frogs' own well-being, and partly as an <term>attribute of spring<gloss>a characteristic or symbol that naturally belongs to the season</gloss></term>. If it were to express the pain of the animals, or if it were heard in November instead of May, it would be unbearable. The song of the nightingale and the sound of Alpine bells also belong to the "concert voices" of nature; though they do not appeal to us purely through association like the frog's cry, they nevertheless speak to us far beyond their own direct musical performance through these mental links.

In earlier times, the sound of the post-horn <note>The post-horn was a small brass instrument used by mail carriers on horse-drawn carriages to signal their arrival.</note> also possessed a charm through the memory of travel that it awakened, which stood in no proportion to its direct musical effect, as I still remember very well from my youth. ...reduced to a minor musical effect, if not sunken even lower, since people now prefer to travel by railway. The mail coach <note>The "Post" refers to the horse-drawn mail carriages that were being replaced by trains during the 19th century.</note> now seems like a snail to us, whereas it formerly seemed to lend us wings to carry us into the distance.

An educated <term>economist<gloss>in this context, likely a land manager or agricultural scientist</gloss></term> told me that it aroused a peculiarly pleasant feeling in him to step into a cattle stall and perceive the smell of manure when it had just been cleared or stirred; he explained that the impression of fertility, which is produced by the fertilizer, was awakened particularly vividly within him by that scent.

The roast in the kitchen, still-warm fresh bread, freshly roasted coffee, or chestnuts placed on a hot stove spread a scent that appears pleasant to most people. Here one may ask: does this pleasantness depend more on the specific nature of the scent itself, or on the enjoyment of the food, the idea of which is awakened by the scent? I confess that I have not been able to reach clarity on this for myself; the <term>direct moment<gloss>the immediate physical sensation</gloss></term> and the <term>associated moment<gloss>the ideas and memories triggered by the sensation</gloss></term> of the impression are so difficult to separate.

In Persia, the use of a knife and fork is unknown, and when a Persian reaches into a rice dish, he recognizes immediately by the feeling whether the rice has been prepared tastily or not. This goes so far that a Persian Shah remarked to a European envoy that "he did not understand how anyone in Europe could use knives and forks, since taste already begins at the fingers." But it can only begin there *associatively*. And a dog follows the principle of association just as well as a Shah. <term>Burdach<gloss>Karl Friedrich Burdach (1776–1847), a famous German physiologist</gloss></term> recounts somewhere: a dog who was so spoiled that he would not eat dry bread nevertheless did so when a dry plate was wiped with it before his eyes; the dog believed he could taste the meat gravy that was usually wiped up with the bread.

But, I hear a voice calling down to me <term>from on high<gloss>A reference to "Aesthetics from Above," the traditional philosophical approach that starts with abstract ideals of Beauty</gloss></term>: "Why this whole display of examples? What is gained for aesthetics by this, or what is there to be gained at all? The orange, the cheek, the nose, the hand, the foot, and so on, are merely dependent parts of nature and the human body; an aesthetics that does not wish to keep itself low focuses above all on the Whole, and considers the parts only as such."

Well then, let us also keep in view the significance of this principle for the beauty of an entire landscape, the whole human figure, or an entire work of art. We shall find it no less significant than for the parts; rather, we will find it expanded and heightened in the same proportion as the whole exceeds its parts. The principle can simply be explained most easily with the simplest examples, and on our path <term>from below<gloss>"Aesthetics from Below" is the author's empirical method, building a theory of beauty from psychological observations and data</gloss></term> we cannot go in the direction intended for the...

### 3) Statement of the Principle.

Every thing we deal with is mentally characterized for us by a resultant of memories <note>original: "Resultante von Erinnerungen"</note> of everything we have ever experienced externally or internally, heard, read, thought, or learned regarding this thing and even related things. This resultant of memories is attached just as immediately to the sight of the thing as the idea of it is attached to the word with which it is designated. Indeed, the form and color of the thing are, so to speak, nothing but visible words that involuntarily bring to mind the entire meaning of the thing; of course, we must first have learned this visible language just as well in order to understand it as we have the language of words.

We see a table—essentially just a rectangular patch—but in that rectangular patch, we see everything a table is used for; that is what first makes the rectangular patch into a table. We see a house, but along with it, we see everything a house serves for and everything that happens in a house; that alone turns the patch into a house. We see it not with the physical eye, but with a spiritual eye <term>spiritual eye<gloss>the inner mind or cognitive perception</gloss></term>. We do not remember every individual thing that contributes to the impression; how would that be possible, if everything claimed the right to enter consciousness at the same time? Rather, as it attempts to do so, it merges into a unified, emotional impression that we have called the spiritual color <term>spiritual color<gloss>the overall mood or qualitative feel of an object based on associations</gloss></term>, an expression that is very significant in more than one respect. 

No matter how many different colors we mix together, the mixture always makes the single impression of one color, which, however, changes according to its constituent parts; and when applied as a glaze <note>original: "lasirend," referring to a transparent layer of paint</note> onto a solid color base, it once again produces a single impression that depends on the composition of both. Thus, from all the diverse memories attached to the sight of a thing, there always results only a single impression, which, however, varies according to its composition from different memory-ingredients...

<summary>Fechner defines his core principle: that our recognition of objects like tables or houses is actually a "spiritual color" created by the subconscious merging of all our past memories and experiences associated with that object.</summary>
<keywords>Principle of Association, perception, spiritual color, memory, aesthetics from below, mental impressions, symbolism of form</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between direct sensory perception and the "associated factor"—the memories and meanings we attach to objects. It follows a discussion on how these two factors combine to create an aesthetic experience.</meta>

...meet, sum up, and compose themselves upon it, the **associated impression** can become very strong and rich in content. Think of all the things an **orange** reminds us of, and how interesting those associations are compared to its mere form and color. When experiences are repeatedly made in the same sense, the **associated impression** that accumulates in the mind can eventually far outgrow the **direct impression**; whereas in cases where experiences are very vague and frequently change in contradictory ways, the associated impression remains vague and weak, as the contradictions weaken or cancel each other out, leaving the **direct factor** as the main determinant of the impression.

To what extent this dominance of the associated over the direct impression can go under certain circumstances may be shown by an everyday example. If one holds a finger at double the distance from the eyes, one believes they still see it as exactly the same size; and yet its image in the eye is only half as large, and it might appear only half as large to someone <term>born blind</term><note>A reference to a famous philosophical problem (Molyneux's Problem) regarding whether a person born blind who gains sight could recognize shapes by sight alone. Fechner likely refers to 18th-century surgical cases like those of William Cheselden.</note> who has just undergone surgery. The knowledge flowing from our entire life experience—that the finger remains the same size at every distance—so completely overwhelms the sensory appearance of its inequality that we believe we see it as equal at every distance even with our eyes. However, if distances exceed our common sphere of experience, objects actually appear diminished in proportion to the distance, such as the sun and moon high above, and objects viewed from high mountains. Is it then any wonder if we also take the <term>pleasantness</term><note>original: "Wohlgefälligkeit." This refers to the aesthetic appeal or the quality of being pleasing to the senses.</note> of many things, which results from previous experiences, to be a matter of their sensory appearance, when it is rather a matter of our <term>mental contribution</term>?<note>original: "geistigen Zuthat." This implies the "ingredients" the mind adds to raw sensory data to form a complete perception.</note><gloss>2</gloss>

As much weight as should be given to the associated impression based on the foregoing, one must still take care not to give it too much weight, which one might easily be tempted to do once its importance has been recognized. If we imagine an **orange** with a gray, inconspicuous color instead of its beautiful golden yellow, or a lopsided, stunted shape instead of its pure roundness, then all the linked memories will not make it appear beautiful or pleasing; the **direct impression** also has its rights, and we shall explicitly safeguard these for it in the future. <meta>This page continues Fechner’s investigation into how our memories and mental associations influence our aesthetic appreciation of objects. ...again, not place too little importance on the **associated impression**. The comparison of the orange with the wooden ball, or the red cheek with the red nose, forbids it. Neither the direct nor the associated impression achieves much on its own; but they achieve much in connection, yielding, according to the **Principle of Aesthetic Assistance** <note>original: "Hülfsprincipe." This refers to Fechner's theory that when various conditions for pleasure work together, the resulting satisfaction is greater than the simple sum of its parts.</note>, a pleasure that is greater than a merely additive product. This success of the Principle of Assistance repeats itself everywhere that direct and associated impressions meet in the same sense; thus, the occasion to return to this topic will often recur.

### 4) Association through Similarity.

Since similar or related things mutually remind us of one another, the **associated impression** easily transfers from one thing to the other. When an object encounters us for the first time, the entire associated impression even depends on such a transfer. Meanwhile, with objects whose own meaning we have become very familiar with through life, the influence of these transferred associations recedes significantly compared to the associations that inherently belong to the object. Furthermore, associations transferred from different sources can essentially cancel each other out or interfere, thereby leaving the field to the inherent associations.

When a **llama** came to Leipzig for the first time, everyone looked at it with <term>pleasure<note>original: "Wohlgefallen." In 19th-century aesthetics, this refers to a specific type of disinterested, positive aesthetic response.</note></term>, even though no one had seen such an animal alive before. Why? Because its feet reminded them of everything slender, light, and agile; its eyes of everything gentle and pious; its hair of everything neat, clean, abundant, and warm.

The yellow wooden ball, however, does not transfer its impression of dryness, mechanical origin, and so on, to the orange, because we are familiar enough with the orange’s different nature through life. Furthermore, all round yellow bodies claim to transfer their associations to the orange, and these associations from one side or another do not agree at all with those of the wooden ball.

Instead of a one-sided dominance, however, a conflict between inherent and transferred associations can also assert itself in the impression, in which the victory remains uncertain. Let us take, for example, an artificial flower. The similarity with the real—

<summary>Fechner explains how the Principle of Aesthetic Assistance reinforces pleasure and how we transfer associations from familiar things to new experiences, such as the first sighting of a llama. He distinguishes between associations that are inherent to an object and those that are merely transferred through similarity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion of how mental associations affect our aesthetic experience. It completes a comparison between natural and artificial flowers and begins a new section on "Supplementary Association," which explains how our minds fill in missing parts of an object based on prior experience.</meta>

[artifi]cial flower makes it appear as a living thing, and all the associations of a real flower would like to be transferred onto it; but the associative feeling that it is, after all, artificially made, prevents those associations from truly coming into effect, without being able to banish them entirely. This creates a conflict that everyone feels, even if they do not clarify it to themselves. In a certain way, we rejoice in the artificiality—as we do with any successful imitation—all the more so as something pleasing is being imitated; but in another way, the pleasure that we would have in a natural flower is diminished by the fact that we cannot imagine the artificial one as possessing the real advantages of the natural.

### 5) Supplementary Association.

Association can not only flesh out details but can also add entire pieces as supplements; it is on this, much more often than on the relationships of direct impression, that our sense of whether things seem to "fit" or "not fit" together depends.

Suppose in a picture book the figure of an animal—for example, a dog—is presented half-covered, so that only the head or the body is visible. The associating imagination will add the body to the dog’s head, or the head to the body, as a supplement. This happens with more or less **clarity** <note>original: "Bestimmtheit." In this context, it refers to the vividness or specific detail of the mental image.</note>, depending on how well one knows the particular breed of dog from experience or other illustrations. However, the associative supplement will never reach the same level of clarity as the part that is directly visible. If the covered part is then revealed, it will seem to "fit" or "not fit" with what was seen before, depending on whether it corresponds to or contradicts our associative mental image within the limits of the clarity it currently possesses. From this, according to the **Principle of Harmony** <note>original: "Princip der Einstimmigkeit." This refers to the aesthetic satisfaction found when new information matches our internal expectations or mental models.</note>, a feeling of satisfaction or dissatisfaction can arise, which under certain circumstances can reach considerable strength. What is demonstrated here between two parts—one of which is visible from the start while the other is subsequently opened to view—also occurs when both are visible from the start. Each part, by way of association, makes certain demands of the other, depending on their

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Supplementary Association," specifically focusing on how the mind demands harmony between the parts of a whole based on past experiences and artistic conventions.</meta>

Whether these demands are fulfilled or unfulfilled, we experience a feeling of harmony or contradiction. It is essential to every beautiful work that no such conflict asserts itself; that is, every part must satisfy the <term>associative demands</term><note>The "associative demand" (associative Forderung) is a core concept for Fechner. it refers to the expectation our mind has that if one part of an object is present, other specific parts or styles should accompany it based on our past experiences.</note> evoked by the totality of the other parts. Conversely, it is a mark of good taste to be so educated that one only makes associative demands that are actually justified.

Every <term>architectural style</term><note>original: "Baustil"</note> demands a certain inner consistency based on general aesthetic and structural considerations. A specific part of a building may deserve the disapproval of a connoisseur simply by stepping outside of this consistency. However, even without specialized knowledge of these rules—and even without a technical violation of them—any part that wanders from one architectural style into another where it does not belong will immediately cause displeasure. This happens because the part contradicts the associative demands that the overall style of the building imposes on each of its components. We are right to reject such things, even if the element is not objectionable in itself; for once an associative demand is grounded in a very widespread fact, one must take that fact into account.

One might ask, however: why do we not dislike a sphinx, a centaur, or a winged angel? These are all compositions where parts are joined together that never occur together in nature; therefore, they cannot "demand" one another in our imagination based on our real-world experiences. The answer is that what nature has never joined, art has done so frequently that it eventually appears to "fit" together—though only within the realm of art. In nature, such a sight would inspire horror. Even so, the associative demands of nature easily come into conflict with those of art in these depictions. No matter how clever the illustrations of *Reynard the Fox* <note>original: "Reinecke Fuchs," a classic European fable cycle. Fechner likely refers to the famous 1846 illustrations by Wilhelm von Kaulbach which depicted animals in human clothing and poses.</note> may be—with figures looking and behaving half-human and half-animal—and no matter how much they please us from other perspectives, a certain disturbing quality remains.

If we ask further: how could art have come to create <term>hybrid forms</term><note>original: "Zwittergestalten," referring to beings of a dual or mixed nature.</note> in the first place, since their sight must have been offensive from the very beginning, the answer is this: art would never have...

<summary>Fechner explains that beauty requires "associative consistency," where every part of a work fits the expectations raised by the whole. He notes that while hybrid creatures like sphinxes are unnatural, they become acceptable through long-standing artistic tradition, though they can still feel "disturbing."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Supplementary Association" law, specifically addressing why hybrid mythical creatures like the Sphinx do not offend our aesthetic sensibilities, before moving on to how association forms the basis of what we call "feeling."</meta>

...would have come to this if it had stood in the service of **beauty** from the very beginning, to which it is now made subservient as a bond-slave; instead, it stood from the outset in the service of **religion**, and knew no other way to express those initially clumsy and monstrous ideas than in correspondingly clumsy and monstrous forms. Although we have long since moved past these religious ideas, the head still seems to fit the body of the Sphinx to us, so firmly has habit fused the two together through association.

### 6) Temporal Association. Judgments of Intellect and Feeling.

When two people look at a building whose roof rests on supports that are too weak, it may happen that the *intellect* of the first person, and the *feeling* of the second, tells them that the supports will break. Consequently, both express the same dissatisfied judgment regarding this style of construction. The difference between the two judges, however, is that the former is conscious of the experiences or rules concerning the <term>load-bearing capacity</term><note>original: "Tragkraft"</note> of columns which mediate his judgment, while the latter is not. Yet, one must admit that it is not *innate* even for this second person to see by looking at a column whether it has enough strength for its load; thus, this rapid perception is indeed a result of earlier experiences, which asserts itself immediately upon sighting the structure. 

If someone sees a child leaning so far forward that its <term>center of gravity</term><note>original: "Schwerpunct"</note> is no longer supported, they will jump forward quickly, because an unreflective feeling tells them immediately that the child will fall. Here, too, one must admit that a quiet mediation through earlier experiences lies at the foundation, if one considers that the child itself—and at one time we were all children—does not yet even have the feeling of how to position its own center of gravity to remain standing upright. It only discovers this through practice. Therefore, what we call "feeling" in this context is, in fact, merely a matter of a rapid association mediated by earlier experiences. Through this, the mental representation of the expected breaking of the column is linked to the representation of excessive thinness, and the representation of the expected fall is linked to the representation of the current leaning forward. These individual experiences have vanished from our...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Law of Association," specifically how our past experiences with physical objects (like falling children or weak columns) condense into immediate, instinctive feelings that guide our aesthetic and practical judgments, even when we forget the specific events that taught us these lessons.</meta>

...the memory has faded, but its result remains in the form of associative feeling.

Thus, not only spatial but also temporal connections can be reflected in our mental representation. This gives rise to involuntary expectations of the future, which play a role in aesthetics because the <term>hedonic content</term><note>original: "Lust- oder Unlustgehalt," referring to the specific degree of pleasure or displeasure an object provides.</note> of the outcome can be transferred directly to the impression of the cause.

Insofar as we generally distinguish between <term>judgments of the intellect</term><note>original: "Verstandesurtheile"</note> and <term>judgments of feeling</term><note>original: "Gefühlsurtheile"</note> based on whether we are conscious of the underlying reasons, the previous points show how judgments of feeling are mediated by association. However, there are varying degrees of clarity in this process. Generally speaking, columns that are too thin displease us. One person might not even know from which <term>perspective</term><note>original: "Gesichtspuncte"</note> they displease him; unpleasant elements simply associate themselves with the sight, and without being able to separate or clarify these elements, he expresses the result in a <term>judgment of rejection</term><note>original: "Verwerfungsurtheile"</note>. A second person knows they displease him because they threaten to break. A third person also knows *why* they threaten to break. For the first person, the intellect retreats entirely behind feeling; for the third, the feeling is, so to speak, completely transparent to the intellect.

If experience often presents us with circumstances that do not essentially belong together but frequently appear in connection—though frequent and forceful instruction can often take the place of experience—a "false association" arises, and with it, a false feeling. In this way, things are linked in the mind that are not linked in the nature of things. We then instinctively assign meanings to objects that they do not possess. Consequently, we may like what we should dislike, and dislike what we should like.

### 7) The Associative Character of Simple Colors, Shapes, and Positions.

Not only whole concrete objects but also sensory qualities and visual relationships—such as colors, shapes, and positions—possess an associative impression alongside their direct one. This associative impression is derived from the totality of objects in which these...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Law of Association, specifically how general properties (like a specific color) derive their aesthetic value from the objects they are usually associated with, and how these associations can either strengthen or neutralize one another.</meta>

property, the relationship is found, it depends on it and is transferable from there to other objects. Even if this impression does not everywhere carry an aesthetic character in itself, it can nevertheless help to characteristically determine aesthetic impressions originating from elsewhere, and therefore deserves to be considered within the study of <term>aesthetics</term><note>The philosophical study of beauty, art, and taste.</note>.

Where, as is often the case, the same property occurs in objects of the most diverse kinds and in the most diverse relationships, this impression cannot have the same clarity and power as the impression of <term>concrete objects</term><note>Specific, physical things rather than abstract qualities.</note>, whose appearance and effect are bound to specific circumstances; however, it can attain such power through special circumstances and in particular <term>modifications</term><note>original: "Modificationen," referring to variations in shade, intensity, or form.</note>.

In the meantime, the <term>associative impression</term><note>The secondary feeling triggered by memories or ideas we link to an object.</note>—even of those colors, forms, and positions where it emerges most clearly—generally requires support, be it through a <term>direct impression</term><note>original: "directe Eindruck," the immediate sensory impact of a stimulus (like a bright color) before our brain begins to associate it with memories.</note> of the same nature, or through other associative factors, if it is to become very decisive. For its part, it is only capable of providing support in this sense, without being able to assert its character against a decided contradiction from another side.

There are yellow things that are pleasant to us, such as wine, and those that are repulsive to us, such as <term>jaundice</term><note>original: "gelbe Sucht," a historical term for the yellowing of the skin caused by liver issues.</note>; there are those of high significance and great value, such as the sun, the moon, the crown, and gold, and those of common significance, such as a sandy plain, a stubble field, straw, withered leaves, or clay. We encounter yellow on clothes, in sulfur, on lemons, on canaries—indeed, on the most diverse objects in the most diverse uses. How should a very decisive associative character of "yellow in general" emerge from this, since these opposite influences neutralize one another? Thus, it seems that only the *direct impression* of yellow comes into consideration. But that changes when we move to specific *variations* of yellow and categorize the examples accordingly. In one portion of these—the sandy plain, the stubble field, the straw, the withered leaves, the clay—we encounter, across a great extent and in frequent repetition, a pale, dull, weak yellow, always with the impression that we are seeing common earthly things of low interest or

<summary>This page explores how the aesthetic feeling of a color like yellow is not fixed, but is a battle between its direct sensory impact and the conflicting memories we associate with it (e.g., the "nobility" of gold versus the "commonness" of straw).</summary>

Now, pale, dull, powerless colors generally appeal very little to the eye directly, whereas brilliant, light colors are inherently pleasing to the eye; thus, <term>associative</term><note>The mental connection between a sensation and a memory.</note> and <term>direct</term><note>The immediate physical impact on the senses.</note> disadvantages as well as advantages coincide in both cases. Of green, one can say in general that it awakens in us a certain <term>feeling for nature</term><note>original: "Naturgefühl."</note> because nature as a whole is green; meanwhile, in the impression of a saturated red, the memory of blood and fire <note>original: "Blut und Glut," literally "blood and glow/embers."</note> undeniably plays a primary role, and in the impression of pink, the memory of the rose, because these colors encounter us in these objects not only particularly frequently, but also with a special claim on our attention.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the context and frequency of color usage solidify our psychological associations, moving from general impressions to specific cultural and historical examples.</meta>

subsequent use is co-determining, in that it contributes no less than the character of the direct impression to making a use appear appropriate or inappropriate, depending on whether it harmonizes with the character of the use itself or not. Through frequent use from this perspective, however, the <term>associative character</term><note>The emotional or conceptual meaning we attach to a color based on past experiences and cultural context.</note> is only ever more firmly established and enhanced.

According to this, one can only find the green color of garden chairs and garden tables appropriate insofar as it reinforces the impression of the natural surroundings, as it reminds one of natural green through association and prevents other associative impressions from asserting themselves from other colors—provided that one truly seeks to produce the impression of a complete immersion in the natural environment, a "becoming one" with it, so to speak. But one can, conversely, prefer to let the impression of a contrasting supplement to the natural surroundings prevail through the arrangements of social interaction; in that case, one will have to prefer white over green.

The <term>rhapsodists</term><note>Professional reciters of epic poetry in ancient Greece.</note> who sang the *Iliad* dressed in red in memory of battles and bloodshed, which is the main subject of the *Iliad*; but those who sang the *Odyssey* dressed in sea-green to remind of the voyages of Ulysses <note>Also known as Odysseus.</note> at sea.\*) The red cap fits the <term>Jacobin</term><note>A member of a radical political club during the French Revolution; the "Phrygian cap" became their iconic red symbol of liberty.</note>, and the red flag fits the <term>Communard</term><note>A supporter of the Paris Commune of 1871, which adopted the red flag as its banner.</note> not just because red is more exciting than any other color, but also because it reminds one of blood and fire. And who would want to give a water-blue garment, which reminds one of the pure heavens, to a robber or even to **Mephistopheles**<note>The devil figure from the Faust legend, typically portrayed in red and black to signify hellfire.</note>, whom one imagines living in the infernal glow itself? Black and blood-red, or simply fire-red, are the most appropriate colors there. Now, however, after these colors have so often been found truly appropriate for such purposes, we also have a <term>Rinaldinian</term><note>Referring to Rinaldo Rinaldini, the "noble bandit" hero of a popular 1798 adventure novel by Christian August Vulpius.</note> or Mephistophelean impression of such clothing, and we will not look for an idyllic shepherd beneath it.

Similar considerations to those regarding colors can be applied to white and black; but let us leave that for now. Regarding forms, I will content myself with considering the contrast of the **convex** and **concave**, and regarding positions, that of the **horizontal** and **vertical**.

--- Page 115 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of color associations to the psychological and physiological associations of geometric forms, specifically convexity and concavity, explaining how our physical interaction with shapes informs our aesthetic feelings.</meta>

If a **convex** curvature <note>A shape that curves outward, like the surface of a ball.</note> appears directly before the eye and is followed from its outer edge toward the peak of the curve—as if toward the eye's point of rest—the eye must adjust its focus to an increasingly closer point; conversely, it must focus further away if it is a **concave** curvature <note>A shape that curves inward, like the inside of a bowl.</note>. In the first case, our gaze seems to be pushed back by the convex surface; in the second, it seems to be drawn into the concave space, even though there is no direct physical cause for this. For the eye does not actually move backward or forward; indeed, to see a closer point, the lens of the eye must curve more, and to see a more distant point, it must flatten. In this sense, the eye actually meets the convex shape by becoming more convex itself, rather than receding from it—and the opposite occurs with the concave. 

Now, however, we see the convex everywhere as something that pushes back, wards off, and excludes, while the concave is seen as something that takes in, receives, and welcomes. Thus, an <term>associative feeling</term><note>A feeling triggered not by the object itself, but by the memories and experiences we link to its shape.</note> of this nature is not only transferred to every newly perceived convex or concave object but even implants itself into the act of looking itself.

Indeed, a hump curves outward against a blow it wishes to ward off; the chest of a proud man swells against everything he wishes to keep at a distance; the fist clenches against the enemy to scare him off and strike him back. Horses stand in a circle to ward off the wolf; a bridge arches over a stream to keep the traveler safely away from the water; a cannonball <note>original: "Bombenkugel"</note> rolls off the vault of a cathedral dome, and rain runs down the convex surface of an umbrella.

Conversely, a cupped hand, a hollow vessel, or a sack can desire nothing other than to take something into itself; the hollow of a flower receives the sunbeam and the dewdrop. A pit cannot "wish" to ward anything off; one falls into it unless a railing, with its outward-facing convexity, prevents it. Anyone looking through an open door into the hollow space of a house finds there an invitation to enter; and when he sees nothing but that hollowness surrounding him, he is inside. As long as only the house’s exterior convexity is turned toward him, he is outside, excluded from the home. 

From thousands of experiences of this kind, our impression of the convex and concave is gathered. Depending on whether a situation requires exclusion or reception—urges awakened from other sources—these shapes can carry a pleasing or displeasing character. A gaze into the high heavens or a...

<summary>The author explains that convex shapes are psychologically associated with resistance and exclusion, while concave shapes represent reception and inclusion, rooted in both physiological eye movement and life experience.</summary>

of the convex, which is gathered from most cases and thus
applied back to most cases, is overridden by the 
exceptional character of soft, elastic bodies, 
where we do not see a concavity, but we press one 
into them ourselves; after repeated experience has taught us 
that we rest more comfortably in this concavity the 
larger the convexity was from which it grew.

Regarding the **horizontal** position and the **vertical** stance, 
it is inherently more familiar to us, and falls more easily to 
us, to follow a horizontal line back and forth with our eyes 
than to follow a vertical one ascending and descending; and 
already the newborn child would rather look around than look up 
and down. Thus, the vertical already demands more 
power in its direct impression than the horizontal, and the

In fact, we encounter the horizontal position in sleeping and dead humans, the fallen tree trunk and the toppled column, the calm surface of the water, and the plain across which one walks easily. In general, everything that seeks to rest lies down, and one only lies down upon the horizontal; whereas the human, the tree, and the <term>column</term><note>German: *Säule*.</note> that stand upright must still resist gravity and defend their balance against it. A wave requires power to rise, and it requires power to climb a mountain. All of this works together with the direct impression to grant horizontal extension the relative impression of rest, and vertical elevation the impression of powerful striving. 

Accordingly, on columns, <term>fluting</term><note>German: *Cannelirung*. These are the vertical grooves carved into the shaft of a column.</note> contributes very significantly to supporting the impression of upward striving; this impression repeats itself in every <term>groove</term><note>German: *Riefe*.</note>, whereas it would seem absurd to surround a column with horizontal rings or grooves, though they are permitted to rest on horizontal cushions. By this, I do not mean to say that this is the only motive for <term>fluting</term>; however, it aids the direct pleasingness found in the unified relationship of the <term>flutes</term> among themselves and with the eye-catching <term>boundary lines</term> of the column. A landscape in which many horizontal lines occur—for example, in mountain ranges, riverbanks, the tiers of various foregrounds and backgrounds against one another, broad low buildings, etc.—appears to us to have a calmer character than one which presents many vertical lines in its rocky peaks, solitary towering trees, high houses, and towers.

I believe further that height seems less great than depth, and that we are more strongly moved when we look down into an abyss than when we look up at an equally great height."

### 8) **The Human Being as a Center of Associations.**

Insofar as nature has linked certain <term>moods</term><note>German: *Gemüthsstimmungen*.</note> and emotional states, as well as intellectual and moral qualities of the human being, to specific physical modes of expression—recurring in the same or similar ways in tone, facial expression, gesture, posture, and movement—and since humans not only interact most with themselves and their own kind but also have the greatest interest in this interaction, it is in the nature of things that associative memories of such modes of expression must play an important role in the entire field of association. Therefore, every form, tone, movement, or posture that reproduces or even merely reminds us of the natural expression of a human mood, passion, intellectual and moral quality, or utterance will—even when encountered in the inanimate world—be significantly determined in its impression by this memory. Thus, the falling over or the firm stand of a tree in the wind, the rushing of the clouds, etc., will undeniably be determined in their impression through associative memories of the human; and many mournful sounds of nature owe their impression primarily to such memories.

In a very appealing presentation, <term>Lotze</term><note>Rudolf Hermann Lotze (1817–1881) was a hugely influential German philosopher and psychologist who sought to bridge the gap between the natural sciences and the humanities.</note> has asserted this point of view in his <term>essay on the concept of beauty</term><note>German: *Abhandlung über den Begriff der Schönheit* (1845).</note> (p. 13, etc.), similarly in his work <term>Microcosmus</term><note>German: *Mikrokosmus*. This was Lotze's massive three-volume work attempting to explain the whole of human nature.</note> (1st ed. Vol. II, p. 192), and at several points in his <term>history</term><note>Likely his *History of Aesthetics in Germany* (*Geschichte der Aesthetik in Deutschland*, 1868).</note>. I will not refrain from quoting the following from it:

> "The power of the [inner] prevailing strivings does not affect the sequence of ideas and feelings alone; it also reveals itself through innate necessity in external bodily movements, which form a bridge from the spiritual value of a thought to its sensory representation. To be sure, even without this, simple, strict drawings in space—meaningless in themselves—would reveal the first traces of a still playful beauty through the pleasant alternation of tension and rest they afford to the wandering eye; but anyone who has once found their own voice broken by pain, and felt the trembling tension of limbs in suppressed anger, finds that which is sensory and perceivable has become eloquent; and whatever they were forced to express externally themselves, they will assume to be present under every similar appearance presented by another. One may believe that our judgment of beautiful spatial—"

<meta>This page continues the extensive quotation from Rudolf Hermann Lotze begun on the previous page, concluding his argument about how we project our bodily experiences onto physical forms and architecture. It then transitions into the author's own commentary on how poetry utilizes these human associations to create emotional impact.</meta>

...on clear outlines. If it has always been in vain to find a scientifically calculable condition for the beauty of such an outline, it is because it works not through itself, but through memories. Anyone who has ever seen a beloved figure bend and sink in wistful exhaustion under the weight of grief will find that the outline of such leaning and bending—hovering before the inner eye—predetermines the interpretation of infinite spatial forms; he will reflect fruitlessly on how such simple lines of a drawing could stir such inward feelings within him. In the entanglements of sounds, everyone finds their own spirit again and surveys its movements. This would hardly happen if a pre-determination of our bodily organization did not drive us to give our feelings a seemingly useless external expression through sounds. Thus, the memory of transitions in the magnitude and nature of strivings and feelings—driven by which we would form these same sounds—is linked to the sounds and their changes. Indeed, even the memory of the measure and tension of bodily activity in the production of tones teaches us to seek in these tones themselves, and in their pitch and depth, a suggestion of greater or lesser strength, of more courageous or more waning striving. The spatial relationships of <term>architecture</term><note>German: *Baukunst*.</note>, its aspiring pillars and the broad-based loads above them, would be only half-understandable to us if we did not ourselves possess a moving force, and if, in the memory of felt loads and resistances, we did not also know how to estimate the magnitude, the value, and the slumbering <term>self-awareness</term><note>German: *Selbstgefühl*. In this context, it refers to the internal physical sense of one’s own strength and existence.</note> of those forces that express themselves in the mutual bearing and being-borne of the structure. Thus, bodily life—driven by the necessity of expressing the inner through external determinations—forms a transition to the understanding of sensory forms and outlines; and even the moral realm, conditioning first a balance of strivings and then a specific way in which inner events unfold, will ultimately be able to find things related and similar in those sensory images.«

Commonly, it is true, one does not realize how much the reflection of our own being and doing in the objective world contributes to the impression it makes on us. <term>Poetry</term><note>German: *Poesie*.</note>, however, assists here to some extent by bringing the associations upon which the impression depends to expression. Thus, <term>Maria</term><note>A reference to the character Mary Stuart in Friedrich Schiller’s 1800 play *Maria Stuart*. In a famous scene, the imprisoned queen watches clouds and addresses them as messengers of her longing for freedom.</note> sees the clouds passing—not as the <term>meteorologist</term><note>German: *Meteorolog*. The author contrasts the scientific view of nature with the poetic, empathetic view.</note> sees an indifferent mass of vapor driven by the wind—but as one person sees another wandering or sailing, and as she herself would like to depart. Indeed, poetry finds a primary advantage in translating the natural object, relationship, or event directly into a human one, in order to awaken the associative impression of it as powerfully as possible in the shortest way. That one sees the moon through the clouds is, according to this, not so poetic—

<summary>This page concludes a quote by Lotze explaining that beauty in art and architecture stems from our "bodily memories" and projections. The author then argues that poetry works by explicitly linking natural phenomena (like moving clouds) to human emotions and experiences.</summary>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page concludes the thought from the previous page regarding Rudolf Hermann Lotze’s theories on empathy and personification. It specifically finishes a sentence about how we project our own internal sense of force into the spatial relationships of architecture, transitioning into how these associations function in poetry and the limits of purely bodily empathy.</meta>

...thetically effective than that he himself looks out from the clouds; that the wave makes a soft, changing sound is not as effective as saying it "lisps." In a poem, the black abyss is not content to gape idly before us, but rather "yawns" toward us. "The teasing breezes come," "the trees stir eerily," "the morning makes a red glow," etc., etc.

Nevertheless, one would go too far—a temptation one might easily fall into after the previous points—to restrict the aesthetic <term>principle of association</term><note>The theory that our enjoyment of art comes from the ideas and memories we associate with an object, rather than just its physical form.</note> entirely to this particular mode of origin and effect. This is impossible if only because the principle is generally not limited to similarities; rather, spatial, temporal, and causal connections play an equally important role. Thus, aesthetically very effective associations can also arise through memories of objective conditions of <term>pleasure and displeasure</term><note>German: *Lust und Unlust*. These are the foundational terms in 19th-century German aesthetics and psychology for positive and negative sensory or emotional experiences.</note> that essentially share nothing in form with instinctive or voluntary expressions of pleasure and displeasure through our own bodies, and therefore do not work through memories of them.

For instance, the associative charm of the sight of an **orange** certainly does not lie in a similarity between its appearance and any external expression of our own moods, but rather in the fact that the orange is an objective center of causal conditions of pleasure for us, and the sight of it carries a memory-echo of this pleasure—which is something else entirely. Who would attribute the contribution that **frog croaking** can make to our spring mood to the idea that we ourselves would like to express that mood through croaking? If it is not our own way of expressing the mood, then it cannot be the memory of such expression that awakens it—for letting out one's voice in general can correspond to quite opposite moods; rather, it is the fact that we objectively find frog calls in constant connection with spring that gives it its associative value. And so, it cannot be claimed that a **sword**, a **crown**, or a **bridal wreath** owe their aesthetic character to a memory of a sword-shaped, crown-shaped, or wreath-shaped expression of violence, power, or love through the forms or movements of our own bodies.

<vocab>Association principle, aesthetic, pleasure, displeasure, causal connection, orange, frog croaking, frog calls, value</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that while we often personify nature in poetry (the "pathetic fallacy"), aesthetic pleasure also stems from objective associations—like linking the smell of an orange to its taste, or frog calls to the season of spring—rather than just projecting our own physical movements onto objects.</summary>
<keywords>aesthetic association, personification, poetry, Lust and Unlust, objective pleasure, Rudolf Hermann Lotze, symbolism, psychology of art</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of "empathy" theories in aesthetics. On the previous page, the author began discussing how our enjoyment of objects (like an orange) stems from associations rather than just physical shape. Here, he extends that logic to cozy landscapes and introduces his famous "web" metaphor for human consciousness.</meta>

Many a little spot in front of a house—perhaps with a linden tree, a bench, and a table beneath it—appeals to our sense of coziness. Why? Because we can imagine ourselves sitting there comfortably; it is not because the tree, the bench, and the table themselves appear as if *they* are sitting comfortably.

If there were indeed—as I am not inclined to admit—a fundamental correspondence between our own physical forms and the forms of the external world that please us, then it would not be necessary to attribute our liking of them to an *associative memory* of our own forms. Rather, things like symmetry and the <term>Golden Ratio</term><note>A mathematical ratio (approx. 1:1.618) often found in nature and art, long considered to be inherently pleasing to the human eye.</note> would please us because we are innately predisposed to find only those things pleasing which correspond to our own forms—fitting into them, so to speak, *directly*, without the need for a *memory* of our own forms to get there.

->9) Analysis of associated impressions. Remarks on the creative power of the imagination.<-

While I previously emphasized that the various elements within an aesthetic **total impression** do not separate themselves, aesthetics must nonetheless separate them in order to provide a clear account of how that impression comes to be. It must ask: what belongs to the immediate or direct impression, what depends on <term>associations</term><note>The mental connections made between a current sensory perception and past experiences or ideas.</note>, and what do these various elements contribute? To be sure, such an analysis can never be exhaustive, because generally, countless memories contribute to every associated impression. In fact, strictly speaking, the entire **echo of memories** from our whole life contributes to every single impression, though the different moments of our life carry different weights. If we strike a single point on a stretched web—and the entire <term>interconnection of our ideas</term><note>German: *Vorstellungszusammenhang*. This refers to the psychological theory that all our thoughts and memories are linked together in a vast, interconnected network.</note> is comparable to such a web—then the entire fabric vibrates; only those points closest to the one struck, or those connected by the strongest and tightest threads, vibrate most intensely. Every perception, however, strikes more than one point of our mental web at the same time. Yet, while recognizing this cooperation of our entire mental storehouse in every impression, one can still set the task of...

<summary>The author argues that aesthetic pleasure comes from imagining ourselves within a scene rather than the objects themselves possessing human traits. He introduces a metaphorical "web" to describe how every new experience triggers a resonant vibration across our entire history of memories.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s analytical breakdown of aesthetic experiences. On the previous page, he argued that while we experience things as a single "total impression," we must scientifically deconstruct that impression into its direct and associative parts. He concludes the previous sentence here by suggesting that such an analysis can find the primary factors that determine our feelings.</meta>

...find the primary factors that predominantly determine the impression—indeed, to truly study the impression from this perspective.

Aesthetics has all the more reason to examine the composition of the **total impression** from its constituent elements, since unified impressions cannot be described in isolation, but can be characterized by their makeup of various components—an opportunity that arises often enough. Who can describe the impression made by an orange, a golden sphere, or a wooden ball? On the other hand, such an impression can certainly be characterized by the <term>mental representations</term><note>German: *Vorstellungen*. This refers to the mental images, ideas, or concepts that the mind "places before" itself when perceiving an object.</note> that have merged with it.

It is characterized not only by the ideas that have merged within it, but also by those that can once again emerge from it, which offers a new and important perspective. In fact, all the mental representations that have contributed to the mental impression can, under certain circumstances, emerge from it again; all that is required are specific external or internal triggers. This establishes the possibility of engaging with an object in depth—following various yet interconnected directions—once the total impression has been gained. This forms a second major part of the aesthetic effect of objects, which, after all, does not rest solely on their unified total impression. This impression is, so to speak, merely the seed from which a plant can unfold, similar to the one from which it originated. At the same time, that resulting blend of memories is the source from which the <term>imagination</term><note>German: *Phantasie*. The author uses this to mean the mind's capacity to recall and recombine past associations into new thoughts.</note> draws; and since beauty as a whole has recently been so frequently explained by reference to the imagination, this ought to be seen as a call to investigate this source more thoroughly than I find has been done until now.

According to the common view, one might think that the imagination is granted an unlimited power to attach this or that to the sight of an object out of its own absolute authority. Upon closer inspection, however, the **associated impression** is the pre-existing material which the imagination can certainly work with, but cannot create; the circle of **associative factors** is the only arena in which it can move. Now, while the imagination does have the freedom to move in the most diverse directions and to various distances—given the indefinite paths and the all-encompassing chaining together of these factors—as well as

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on how the "total impression" of an object acts as a seed for further thought. He is explaining how the mind moves from a single perception (like seeing an orange) to a web of associated ideas based on past experiences.</meta>

...also allows for new combinations of the **associated elements**; however, those elements that predominate within the associated impression will always exert the strongest determining and guiding force. One will more easily think of Italy or Sicily when seeing an orange than of Lapland or Siberia; one will more easily think of wealth rather than poverty when seeing a golden sphere. In fact, upon seeing these objects, one finds no immediate cause for thoughts of the latter kind—though this does not prevent one from eventually reaching such thoughts through various intermediate links as the association radiates further from its center.

Instead of an external starting point, the <term>imagination</term><note>German: *Phantasie*. In this context, it refers to the mind's ability to create and manipulate mental images and ideas.</note> can have an internal reason for its creations; but the source from which it draws remains the same everywhere. It is always the resonance of whatever was once in consciousness, which has since sunk into and merged with the **unconscious**, and which can return to consciousness in this or that combination through various external or internal triggers. Every associated impression is a ready-made, specific combination called into consciousness by an external trigger; it can then be spun out into detail by the imagination according to laws and motives that we need not pursue here, serving as the starting point for a further web of thought. One is therefore right to seek the source from which the imagination draws in the **unconscious**—though not in a <term>primordial unconscious</term><note>German: *Ur-Unbewusstsein*. Fechner is distinguishing his view from more mystical theories. He believes the unconscious is built from previous conscious experiences, rather than being an innate, mystical force.</note>. Rather, it is a source that first had to be filled by consciousness and can only be drawn from again through conscious activity. In sleep, neither associated impressions are formed, nor is any of the material formed with which the imagination operates and weaves.*)

<margin>*) Even if <term>Hartmann</term><note>Eduard von Hartmann (1842–1906), a philosopher famous for his *Philosophy of the Unconscious*. Fechner is critiquing Hartmann's idea that the unconscious is a mystical, creative primary force.</note> wishes to derive the unity of brilliant imaginative conceptions from a **primordial unconscious**, one sees no reason for it; after all, this unity—like the entire unity of consciousness from which it stems—is merely a matter of consciousness itself. Naturally, everything that a person has grasped and thought of as a unit under the general unity of consciousness will leave its unified remnant in the unconscious. When this remnant is lifted back into consciousness, it can join with new conscious elements to form new unified creations, without one needing to call upon a "primordial unconscious" to assist the mind with its unity.

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s psychological exploration of aesthetics. ### 10) The Gradual Development of the Associated Impression.

The spiritual color <note>original: "geistige Farbe." Fechner uses this metaphor to describe the layer of mental meaning—emotions, memories, and ideas—that we "paint" over our raw sensory perceptions.</note>—to recall this expression again—which objects take on for a person, can naturally only develop in the course of life according to the extent of one's interaction with them. The younger and rawer a **human being** is, and the less the **spiritual paintbrush** <note>original: "geistige Pinsel." This refers to the mind's internal capacity to process and color our experiences.</note> has worked within them, the more the **direct impression** of things predominates. The older and more experienced a person becomes, the more they have come to know things according to the **totality** of their **relationships** and **effects**, the more the **spiritual impression** of them begins to predominate.

An **adult**, seeing the stormy sea for the first time, will feel the <term>sublimity</term><note>The "sublime" is an aesthetic quality of greatness, often associated with the overwhelming power of nature that inspires both awe and fear.</note> of the spectacle quite differently than a **child** seeing it for the first time, because the former can interpret the new visual impression according to old ones, while the latter cannot. The latter feels nothing but a surging and waving on the color-palette of their eye, at which they can only stare in dull wonder; they cannot know, as the former does, that power, danger, fear, and shipwreck are attached to it. And if for the adult the impression reaches its peak through a ship just being swallowed by the raging sea, for the child, the impression of the ship itself will be swallowed by the **impression of the sea**.

For a **person born blind** who has just been successfully operated upon, an **orange** will make no different impression than a **yellow wooden ball**; a **red hand and nose** will appear just as pleasing as a **red cheek**, provided the **redness** is equally pure and vivid. However, such a person will find a kaleidoscopic figure more beautiful than the most beautiful painting, and probably even more beautiful than the most beautiful **face**. Although one might ask whether instinct can replace some of the <term>association</term><note>The mental process by which one idea or sensation triggers another based on past experience.</note>, which we will speak of in the future (Section XII).

This is often expressed by saying that we must first learn to understand **forms** in order to receive the right impression from them—and why not express it so? Only one must truly understand this "learning to understand" itself. This is not the case if one believes, as many seem to believe, that objects betray their **meaning** to the observer through themselves, provided the observer merely immerses themselves deeply enough in **contemplation**. Rather, as already stated, the meaning of forms must be learned just as well—

<summary>Fechner argues that the meaning and beauty we find in objects are not inherent in the objects themselves, but are "associated impressions" built up through a lifetime of experience and memory.</summary>

<keywords>associated impression, direct impression, perception, aesthetics, sublimity, child psychology, sensory experience, learning, forms</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on how we learn to "read" the world through association. He compares the way we understand visual forms to the way we learn a language—starting with basic meanings and building up to complex interpretations.</meta>

...must be learned just like the meaning of words, and becomes familiar to us only through the same process; that is the path of **association**. However, once the basic meaning of words and forms, as well as their daily recurring ways of linking together, are familiar, one can certainly understand and interpret long sentences and entire works of art based on them.

Insofar as things present themselves under different circumstances to different people, nations, and eras, the **associated impression** also takes on a different character for them; this is one of the primary reasons for their **differences in taste**. This point will be revisited later.

### 11) The Principle in Higher Application.

The preceding section contains the most general viewpoints of the principle, mostly explained through the simplest examples. However, these remain valid when we now ascend to higher and thus more complex examples from nature and art; only then do the relationships of the components come into play.

Just as an object spreads out before us with parts that are distinguishable from one another, so too do their **associative meanings** and impressions distinguish themselves. They enter into relationships and connections with one another, which in turn fit into a unified result—concluding in a whole—without the distinction of the individual contributions being lost in the process. What can one *not* distinguish in the sight of a person? Eyes, mouth, the entire head, chest, belly, limbs; each part has its own **associative meaning** and makes its own correspondingly different impression. But the whole person also has such a meaning and makes such an impression, in which the meaning and impression of the individual parts is not lost, but rather enters into it to conclude in a <term>total impression</term><note>original: "Gesammteindruck." This is a key concept in Gestalt-like psychology, where the whole is experienced as something more than just the sum of its parts.</note>, from which the individual moments can emerge once again.

Just as **direct impressions** <note>Direct impressions refer to the immediate physical sensations of color, light, or sound, before the mind adds any meaning to them.</note> can not only be pleasing or displeasing themselves, but also enter into pleasing and displeasing relationships with one another, the same applies to the **associated impressions** and the <term>individual ideas</term> <note>original: "Einzelvorstellungen." These are the specific mental images or concepts triggered by parts of an object.</note> triggered by them; and since direct impressions—at least as long as we...

<summary>Fechner explains that aesthetic taste varies because associations are learned through different cultural and personal experiences. He introduces the "total impression," arguing that complex objects like the human body are understood through the harmonious combination of the individual meanings of their parts.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s investigation into the "Principle of Association," moving from basic definitions to its application in high art and the human form. He argues that while visual math (like symmetry) matters, it is the "associated" meaning that creates true beauty.</meta>

...if we were to remain strictly within the realm of visibility, staying poor and lowly compared to the associated elements, we can see in general how much art owes its richness and its heights to association.

To explore this more deeply, the next section will use the impression of a landscape from a relatively low elevation as an example. We will discuss architecture from the same perspective in later sections (XV, XVI). However, we consider the human figure to be the most beautiful visible work of creation. The highest works of the <term>visual arts: *bildende Kunst*, referring to sculpture, painting, and architecture</term> have the human form as their subject or essential element. Now, there is undoubtedly much in the flow of forms, bilateral symmetry, and perhaps (pending closer consideration) simple proportions—as some suggest—or certain rhythmic relationships—as others suggest—or the <term>Golden Ratio: a mathematical ratio of approximately 1:1.618, long considered a standard of perfect proportion</term>—as <note>Adolf Zeising (1810–1876), a philosopher who claimed the Golden Ratio was the universal law of beauty.</note> Zeising suggests—and even something instinctive that can please us in a single figure, regardless of any attached meaning. In a whole painting, one must also add the relationships of grouping and color, where certain harmonic or disharmonic connections can assert themselves on their own. But all of this is merely the lower foundation for the associated expression of the human figure's fitness for the tasks and joys of life, and the even higher expression of the soul and the <note>original: *Seelenbewegungen* (movements of the soul)</note> movements of the soul. We can find all of this even in a single figure, and finally, in the more general and higher human—even superhuman—relationships found in a complete painting. Yet, we only project all of this into the observed combinations of form and color based on the experiences we have gained regarding their significance; all of this is a matter of the **associated** impression, not the **direct** one.

In the realm of the visible, no aesthetic impression of any significant height or strength ever comes about without association. Without it, the most significant things this realm can produce are kaleidoscopic patterns and fireworks. Only the <term>auxiliary principle: *Hülfsprincip*, the idea that direct sensory pleasure and associated meaning reinforce one another</term> grants the direct impression, when combined with the **associated** one, a manifold...

<summary>Fechner argues that high art and the beauty of the human form rely on 'association'—the meanings and life experiences we project onto shapes—rather than just mathematical proportions like the Golden Ratio. He suggests that without association, visual art would be as shallow as a firework display.</summary>
