<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on the "Principle of Association," specifically focusing on how it differentiates the various arts. He previously established that human beauty relies more on associated meaning than on pure geometry; here, he applies that logic to painting, poetry, and music.</meta>

...greater significance in this area. If one turns even the most beautiful painting upside down, the internal proportions—on which the <term>direct factor: sensory elements like color and shape that please the eye immediately</term> depends—remain the same. However, the pleasure we take in it ceases because the associations, which give the image its higher meaning, are tied only to the upright position. This remains true unless one is able to mentally reconstruct the upright position from the inverted one. However colorful an image may appear to the physical eye, it appears "colorful" in an entirely different way through its associations; it is here that the mind first finds the higher charm, and where the unified connection of the whole is to be sought and found.

Poetry, too, reaches its peak in the <term>associative factor: the meanings, memories, and ideas triggered by a work of art</term>, for the meaning of a poem is merely linked to the words. Meter, rhythm, and rhyme gain significant importance only to the extent that they harmonize with this meaning. This does not prevent them from contributing much to the strength of the aesthetic impression according to the <term>auxiliary principle: *Hülfsprincipe*, the idea that secondary sensory pleasures can amplify the primary emotional effect</term>.

But one would be mistaken to expect to find an equally dominant importance of the associative factor in all arts. Rather, music stands in contrast to the visual arts and poetry in this regard. In music, the direct factor plays the primary role, while the associative factor plays only a secondary one, as will be discussed more closely in Section XIII. It is simply a matter of attributing very much, but not everything, to the associative factor.

In the effort to establish unified principles, many have tried to make the main impression of a painting depend on the directly perceivable—one might say "musical"—impression of its forms and colors, just as the impression of music depends on the relationship between tones and combinations of tones. However, in this respect, painting is more closely related to poetry than to music, though they are not comparable in every way (a point we will return to with some observations in Section XI). Conversely, others have tried to trace the main impression of music back to association, but in doing so, they have only abolished the distinction between music and painting from the opposite side.

In itself, of course, the endeavor to bring all the arts, so to speak, "under one hat" is justified; but one misses the point of unity when searching for it where, instead, the distinc—

<summary>Fechner argues that while painting and poetry rely on mental associations (like recognizing an upright figure), music is unique because its primary beauty comes from direct sensory experience of sound. He cautions against aesthetic theories that try to treat painting like music or vice versa.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Gustav Fechner's exploration of his "Principle of Association." In the preceding text, he argued that while music relies heavily on direct sensory pleasure (the "direct factor"), visual arts like painting and poetry rely primarily on the "associative factor"—the ideas and meanings we attach to what we see or hear. This page specifically tackles the argument against "pure" formalists who believe beauty should only be judged by its shapes and colors, independent of any external meaning.</meta>

...lies. All fine arts have this in common: they combine sensory means in such a way that more than mere sensory pleasure arises from them. Here lies the point of unity. However, this success can be achieved just as predominantly through relationships between direct impressions <note>The physical qualities of the art, such as color or pitch.</note> as through associated impressions and the mental images they trigger. This is one of the distinguishing viewpoints of the various arts—a point one cannot grasp if one does not clearly distinguish the associated impressions from the direct ones.

<margin><unclear>Lucretius</unclear></margin> If there are aestheticians who deny the <term>associative factor: the meanings, memories, and emotions we link to an object</term> an essential share in beauty altogether, and claim that its effect must be subtracted from the effect of an object to perceive its beauty in its "pure" state, then this is merely a dogmatic separation. The living effect of beauty and our actual, everyday use of the concept know nothing of such a division. These thinkers confuse the fact that these two factors can be distinguished with the idea that one should be removed. In doing so, they leave behind, so to speak, only the skeleton of the total beauty of visible objects; the clothing of that skeleton with living flesh happens only through associations.

Indeed, what remains of the <note>The Sistine Madonna is a famous Renaissance painting by Raphael; Fechner uses it as the ultimate example of beauty that transcends mere geometry.</note> **Sistine Madonna** after subtracting all association is a motley board of colors, surpassed in pleasantness by almost any carpet pattern. In a carpet, one at least still has the direct charm of color harmony and symmetry, which in that painting has been sacrificed to give room for the attachment of sublime ideas and their unified connection. If one does not wish to count this as part of the beauty of the painting, then one is creating a concept of beauty that might work within some theoretical system, but is of no use in real life—thereby making the system itself useless for life.

It is indisputable that some associations must indeed be set aside as non-essential to the beauty of visible objects; but these are only associations that are too accidental <note>For example, a personal memory triggered by a color that most people would not share.</note> to count. To separate *everything* associated from the object is to strip away the beauty itself.

Admittedly, some have tried to deny any essential contribution of association to beauty precisely from the viewpoint that beauty would then depend entirely on accidental factors, varying between different...

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s argument against "formalists" who believe beauty only exists in pure shapes and colors. He is refuting the idea that associations (the meanings we bring to an object) are purely subjective and therefore irrelevant to "true" beauty. He transitions from a general defense of association to a specific critique of the famous philosophers Immanuel Kant and Johann Friedrich Herbart.</meta>

...people would depend on very different, even changing circumstances for the same person, whether to declare something as beautiful or not. However, the most important associations are generally forced upon humans by the general nature of human, earthly, and cosmic conditions. Because of this, for example, no one can confuse the expression of frailty with that of strength and health, nor the expression of kindness or mental talent with that of malice or stupidity. 

As for associations that change according to individuality, time, or place—which play a part in the differing development of taste among various individuals, nations, and eras—they are only essential for determining the *fact* of taste, but not its *justification*. The concept of true beauty in the sense previously stated (page 16) does not have to follow these individual differences any further than those differences themselves are justified. Indeed, they are justified up to certain limits, thereby allowing room for different "modulations" of beauty. Only that which provides immediate pleasure and is beneficial to the whole—when considering all consequences and connections—is to be regarded as truly, objectively beautiful; and the involvement of associations is not excluded from this.

As noted at the beginning, **Kant** <note>Immanuel Kant (1724–1804), the immensely influential German philosopher whose "Critique of Judgment" defined much of modern aesthetics.</note> is chiefly to blame for the widespread view that the associative factor is merely an unessential addition to the impression of pure—or, in Kant's terminology, "free"—beauty. For this addition, he uses the term "adherent" beauty <note>original: "anhängende Schönheit"</note>; however, he assigns it no actual aesthetic significance. Even if he grants value to the meaning attached to a work of nature or art from another perspective, he misses a primary viewpoint of beauty by essentially excluding what that attached meaning contributes to it.

**Herbart** (Collected Works Vol. II, p. 106) <note>Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841), a philosopher and educator who advocated for a "formalist" aesthetics based on the relationships between simple elements.</note> does not go as far as Kant. He commands that the effect of what he calls "apperception" <term>apperception: the process where the mind interprets new sensory data through the lens of existing knowledge and memories</term>—which is inseparable from association—should only be set aside in the appreciation of a work of art "insofar as it does not essentially determine the perception." Yet, he does not make it clear what he counts as "essential." From the way he explains several examples, it is clear that instead of seeking a main factor of aesthetic impression in the associative element, he sees it only as a helpful element that is not entirely dispensable, yet should be rejected as much as possible. He places the main weight on the direct factor (so-called "perception"). Now, there is certainly in the...

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s critique of formalist aesthetics. He is specifically addressing the views of Johann Friedrich Herbart and Rudolph Hermann Lotze regarding the "direct factor" (the immediate sensory pleasure of form) versus the "associative factor" (the meaning and memories we attach to an object).</meta>

In music, the primary weight really lies on the **direct factor** <note>The "direct factor" refers to the immediate physical or sensory impact of a work, such as the harmony of sounds or the symmetry of lines, independent of any external meaning.</note>, as will be discussed further on. **Herbart** <note>Johann Friedrich Herbart (1776–1841), a philosopher who argued that beauty lies strictly in the formal relationships between elements, not in their "content" or associations.</note>, however, mixes examples from the visual arts—where the weight lies much more on the **associative factor**—and from music in a way that shows the great difference existing between these two arts in this regard has completely escaped him. Moreover, **Lotze** <note>Rudolph Hermann Lotze (1817–1881), a philosopher and former student of Fechner who sought to reconcile scientific mechanism with moral and aesthetic values.</note> has already defended the associative factor against Herbart’s undervaluation of it (History of Aesthetics, 229).

The most frequent reason for downplaying the importance of association for the beauty of visible objects is always that people attribute to the impression of the form itself what is actually added through association. Otherwise, it would not be possible to misjudge its importance as much as is done. This depends on the power with which the direct impression asserts itself through its originality, clarity, and definiteness, and the very gradual fusion of the associative factor with it—a fusion that becomes ever firmer, more intimate, and finally indissoluble.

Thus says **Vischer** <note>Friedrich Theodor Vischer (1807–1887), a leading German aesthetician who attempted to bridge the gap between Hegelian idealism and psychological realism.</note> in his *Critical Paths* (p. 137), and I have encountered similar views elsewhere:
> "Actually, in the beautiful, the appearance—the form—should not mean anything; it should want nothing but to express itself. A lion does not mean magnanimity; he is simply a lion, and the content of his forms is simply the formative power of nature in this type of configuration, with these external and internal properties."

But form and power are entirely different things in themselves. A form can certainly remind us that it was produced by a power, because we have seen similar forms produced by such powers, but it cannot contain the power within itself. It therefore signifies power only through **association**; and in the case of the lion, it is not only the power that formed him, but also, and much more, the power he himself is capable of exerting according to our past experiences. In fact, previous experiences are necessary for both interpretations; the form cannot interpret itself in this sense. Rather, one simply believes they are seeing *out* of the object what they are actually seeing *into* it.

Unquestionably, something of the powerful impression of the lion’s shape may also depend on the fact that one needs more "living power" for the eyes to follow the angular outline of a lion than the rounded one of a pig, which requires no memory. However, if the main point depended on this direct impression, then the equally angular cow would have to appear just as powerful to us, and arbitrarily drawn angular lines would have to appear even more powerful than the lion’s strength. Thus, while the direct effect of the lion’s shape may not be indifferent to the impression of strength, it would have no effect worth mentioning without the more powerful help of association.

It must be admitted that a lion does not "mean" magnanimity. It

<summary>Fechner argues that the perceived "power" of a lion's form is not an inherent sensory property of its lines, but a result of associations we have learned through experience. He critiques philosophers like Herbart and Vischer for failing to recognize how deeply memory and meaning are fused into our immediate perception of beauty.</summary>
<keywords>direct factor, associative factor, Herbart, Lotze, Vischer, aesthetics, form vs content, psychology of perception</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion regarding why we associate certain character traits (like magnanimity) with specific animal forms—specifically the lion—before transitioning into a broader theoretical summary of Fechner's aesthetic principles. It maintains the distinction between the "direct factor" (the immediate sensory experience) and the "associative factor" (the meaning we project onto an object based on past experience).</meta>

...we lack the experiences that would allow us to link magnanimity to similar forms; therefore, the form of the lion cannot associatively signify such a trait to us.

### 12) Some More General Considerations.

If pleasure <note>original: "Wohlgefallen." In 19th-century German aesthetics, this term refers to the specific kind of satisfaction or "liking" one feels when perceiving something beautiful or well-formed.</note> in things essentially rests upon the memory of pleasant things, it is self-evident that there must also be things that are inherently pleasant in themselves; consequently, association can only be considered a secondary source of pleasure compared to direct sources. Here, however, the task was not to seek out these direct sources, but rather merely to show that among the various sources of pleasure in general, the secondary source—association—plays one of the most important roles by absorbing into itself inflows from all sources that are more original than itself.

Every direct as well as associative impression <term>Eindruck (Impression): The immediate effect an object has on the senses or the mind before it is fully processed.</term> depends simultaneously on the nature of the object that makes the impression and the inner (physio-psychological) makeup of the individual upon whom the impression is made—in short, on an **objective** and a **subjective factor**. An impression of an object is **direct** insofar as it depends, on the subjective side, on an inner makeup that is either innate or developed and refined through attention and practice in dealing with objects of the same kind. It is **associative** insofar as it depends on a makeup that has arisen because the object has repeatedly presented itself in connection and relationship with given objects of a different kind.

Besides direct and associative impressions, one can speak of **combinatory** ones; however, they can always be resolved into direct and associative ones and are thus not actually coordinated <note>In this context, "coordinated" means existing as a separate, equal category of the same rank.</note> with them. Every house already makes a direct impression on its own through its form and color; an associative one, insofar as it appears to us as a dwelling place for humans; and a combinatory one according to its relationship to its surroundings. This latter is **direct** insofar as the present form and color of the house enters into a relationship with the present form and color of the surroundings, and **associative** insofar as the associative ideas of the house's habitability <note>original: "Bewohnbarkeit."</note> are influenced by associative ideas awakened by the surroundings.

<summary>Fechner clarifies that while aesthetic pleasure has direct sensory sources, the "associative factor" is crucial because it integrates all previous experiences into our current perception. He also argues that complex "combinatory" impressions are simply mixtures of direct sensory data and these learned associations.</summary>

<keywords>aesthetics, association, direct factor, subjective factor, objective factor, habitability, combinatory impressions, pleasure, psychology of art</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the theoretical exploration of aesthetics by Gustav Fechner. It follows a discussion on how we associate certain qualities (like the lion’s "magnanimity") with physical forms. Here, Fechner begins to distinguish between the "direct factor" of beauty (sensory) and the "associative factor" (conceptual/memory-based), warning against confusing the two or overemphasizing one at the expense of the other.</meta>

The difference between direct and associated impressions is not to be seen as coinciding with the difference between "lower" and "higher" impressions. Rather, direct impressions themselves distinguish between lower and higher forms, and, at least in the field of music, can rise to great heights. Conversely, many an associated impression can remain very low, as in that example (p. 92), where the mere touch of a food is linked to the vivid sensation of its pleasant taste, or where a specific word is linked to the idea of a simple object.

In order to gain clarity regarding the nature and origin of aesthetic impressions, it is now above all important to make the distinction between their direct and associative factors in general; and it has already been noted several times that this does not happen as easily as it should. As a rule, the contributions of both factors are more or less lumped together. Specifically, the contribution of the associative factor is very commonly miscounted as part of the direct factor. From another perspective, the effect of the direct factor is often seen as being absorbed into, or disappearing against, or being reducible to the associative factor; for as unfamiliar as the **principle of association** <term>Association principle: The psychological theory that mental states follow each other because they were previously experienced together; here, Fechner applies it to how we "learn" to find things beautiful.</term> is to today’s aesthetics, it is quite common for that same aesthetics to speak of the results of that principle.

Both of these tendencies, however, result not only in deep-seated obscurities and skewed perceptions, but have also given rise to two one-sided fundamental views on the origin of beauty, insofar as an exclusive or exaggerated weight is placed on one factor or the other.

In a one-sided consideration or an undue overestimation of the direct factor, one can imagine that inherently pleasing relationships of form and color—that is, those which please regardless of any attached meaning, purpose, or generally without the cooperation of association—transfer the impression of their **pleasingness** <note>original: "Wohlgefälligkeit." A key term for Fechner, referring to the immediate, formal satisfaction one feels when looking at something well-proportioned.</note> onto the objects where they occur. They "endow" the objects, so to speak, with their own beauty and thereby make them beautiful. Secondly, however, in a one-sided consideration or undue overestimation of the associative factor, one can also imagine that, conversely, the beauty that we attribute to the forms and relationships of many objects...

<summary>Fechner argues that aesthetic pleasure comes from two distinct sources—direct sensory experience and mental associations—and warns that failing to distinguish between them leads to flawed theories of beauty.</summary>
<keywords>direct factor, associative factor, aesthetics, music, formal beauty, Wohlgefälligkeit, psychology of art</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues Gustav Fechner's exploration of aesthetic theory. Having established that the "associative factor" involves mental connections rather than just sensory input, Fechner now argues that our sense of beauty often stems from transferring the value of a meaning or purpose onto an object's physical form. He transitions from these general principles to a specific case study: the beauty of a landscape.</meta>

...be attributed; it lies in the fact that the value of a meaning that appeals to us, of a purpose that pleases us, or the fulfillment of a conceptual or ideal demand we place on objects, is transferred through association onto their external forms and relationships as we perceive them. In doing so, it makes them appear beautiful as the expression of this meaning, or as a sign of this fulfillment.

In reality, both views assert themselves in aesthetic and artistic considerations—now one, now the other, with varying degrees of dominance—though they rarely confront one another with full consistency, as neither allows for a pure application in isolation. Consequently, one mostly wavers uncertainly between the two or becomes confused by both, without ever reaching clarity regarding their relationship.

Now that we have attempted in the preceding sections to give the associative factor its due, we intend to do justice to the direct factor in a later section (XIII). Before that, however, we shall address several themes that are more closely related to the associative factor.

## X. Explanation of the Impression of Landscape through the Principle of Association.

Let us attempt to account for the impression that a view of a landscape makes upon us! There is something ineffable in it, something that cannot be exhausted by any description. How can we explain the nature and the reasons for this impression? To provide an example of the different ways in which <term>Aesthetics from Above</term> <note>Fechner’s term for philosophical aesthetics that starts with grand, abstract ideas of "the Beautiful."</note> and <term>Aesthetics from Below</term> <note>Fechner’s own empirical approach, which starts with psychological observation and sensory data.</note> generally proceed in their explanations, I will contrast two explanations of this phenomenon. The first, following the former path, is drawn from one of the most esteemed modern textbooks of aesthetics, that of Carriere <note>Moritz Carriere (1817–1895), a prominent German philosopher and historian of aesthetics.</note>; the other—

<summary>Fechner argues that perceived beauty often arises when we associate mental concepts or purposes with physical forms, and he introduces a comparison between philosophical and empirical methods for explaining the emotional impact of landscapes.</summary>
<keywords>associative factor, direct factor, Aesthetics from Above, Aesthetics from Below, landscape aesthetics, Moritz Carriere, psychology of beauty</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on how we interpret the beauty of a landscape. He is contrasting two methods of explanation: the "top-down" philosophical approach (Aesthetics from Above) and his own "bottom-up" empirical approach (Aesthetics from Below). He introduces a lengthy quote from the philosopher Moriz Carriere to illustrate the idealist, "top-down" perspective before he presents his own associative explanation.</meta>

...as it results from the second path based on the principle discussed in the previous section. The former represents the most remote explanation, linking to the highest and most ideal viewpoints; the latter represents the most immediate explanation, linking to the most basic <term>viewpoints</term> <note>Fechner refers here to his "Aesthetics from Below," which starts with empirical observations rather than metaphysical assumptions.</note>.

> "The essence of nature—says Carriere (I. 243)—corresponds in itself to beauty; for it is a manifestation for the spirit, to which it represents ideal content in sensible forms and illustrates spiritual laws, and it is precisely this that delights us so deeply when, in the external and material, a related soulfulness meets the mind. Yet everywhere, the primary purpose of life is life itself; every being exists for its own sake and was not created so that its form might delight us; it is a favor of fate if, in the totality of the universe, the interrelationship of things—the way they exist for one another—presents itself to our standpoint in such a way that we perceive the inner essence on the surface offered to us, and see how the forms of things do not merely correspond to the purposes of the universe, but are also in accordance with the conditions and demands of our personality. Indeed, we may especially praise the goodness and glory of the <term>primordial ground</term> <note>original: "Urgrundes." This refers to the fundamental source or origin of the world in German Idealist philosophy.</note> of the world when substances that appear indifferent to the life of the organism, particularly plants, or are excreted by it, refresh us as essential oils or pigments through their fragrance or brilliant color," etc.

And to show how the consideration of the individual instance enters into this general consideration, it is said of the plant as an element of the landscape (p. 258):

> "The powers of inorganic nature find a meeting point in the plant, where an individual idea appears as a body-forming life-force and acts in the constantly renewed formation of an organism that is connected to the earth through its roots, but strives upward toward air and light, spreading out to the sides with branches and leaves. The plant illustrates the concept of organic shaping, which we previously demanded for beauty; the variety of leaves and branches emerges from unity and is visibly supported by it, and the interaction..."

<summary>Fechner contrasts his empirical approach to aesthetics with the idealist philosophy of Moriz Carriere, who argues that nature's beauty is a manifestation of spiritual laws and a "favor of fate." Carriere views the plant as a meeting point where inorganic forces are organized by an "individual idea" into a beautiful, unified form.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the high-minded philosophical quote from Moriz Carriere and transitions into Gustav Fechner's core argument for "Aesthetics from Below." Fechner uses the psychological example of a person born blind who gains sight to explain how our past experiences and associations create the beauty we perceive in a landscape, rather than beauty being a purely abstract spiritual law.</meta>

“...of the individual forms closes together into a harmonious whole.”

Against this lofty flight of contemplation, our "Contemplation from Below" admittedly has nothing equivalent to offer. Let us take the following as simply as it presents itself.

To the eye of a person born blind, who sees the outdoors for the first time after a successful operation, all of nature initially appears only as a marbled sheet <note>original: "marmorirtes Blatt." This likely refers to the chaotic, swirling patterns of marbled paper used in bookbinding, representing a mess of colors without recognizable shapes.</note>, for he is not yet able to see the meaning within what is perceived. He looks out into the distance: there are meadows, fields, forests, mountains, lakes; yet he sees nothing of meadows, fields, forests, mountains, or lakes. He sees only green, yellow, light, and dark spots. Only the feeling of the far-reaching gaze—the sensory or slightly-above-sensory charm of light and dark, color contrast, variety, and change—determines the impression he has of the landscape. 

But is that all that we gain from the landscape? We have all of those sensory things too; they contribute to the impression the landscape makes on us and the mood it awakens in us—and not a little at that. But at the same time, in the distant forest—which to the inexperienced eye is only a green spot—we see something that is alive, bustling, and growing; something that provides shade and coolness; where the hare and the deer run, the hunter walks, the birds sing, and many a fairy tale haunts the woods; even if we actually see and hear none of it at that moment. 

In the lake, where that person recognizes only a shiny or blue spot, we know the waves roll, the sky is reflected, the fish play, the ships sail, and so on. <term>Mental associations</term> <note>original: "Vorstellungen." In Fechner's psychological framework, these are the mental images, ideas, or "representations" that our minds attach to a raw sensory input.</note> of everything else that bustles and grows and waves resonate alongside it. Fundamentally, we too see no more of the forest and the lake with our physical eyes than the newly-operated blind person or the newborn child; that is, green and shiny or blue spots. However, everything we have ever seen, heard, read, experienced, or thought about the forest and lake—as well as everything with which they offer a <term>point of comparison</term>—contributes to the impression these objects make on us. This makes the sight of them something unspeakably more significant, richer, livelier, deeper for the emotions, and more productive for the imagination than it is for someone who has seen, heard, or thought nothing of them. And as it is with the forest and lake, so it is with all elements of the landscape: meadow, field, mountain, house. To everything is linked...

<summary>Fechner argues that aesthetic pleasure in a landscape comes not just from raw sensory data (colors and shapes) but from the vast web of memories and associations we link to those sights. He illustrates this by comparing a normal viewer's experience to the "meaningless" visual field of a person who has just gained their sight after being born blind.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s psychological analysis of aesthetic pleasure. Having established that a person without experience sees only meaningless colors, he now explains how "associative circles" of memory give depth to a landscape and why novelty often strikes us more powerfully than familiarity.</meta>

memories and **comparative mental images** <note>original: "Vergleichs-Vorstellungen." This refers to the psychological process of comparing what we see now to what we have seen before to make sense of it.</note> arise, through which these objects acquire a certain meaning for us; their arrangement also gains meaning for us in the same way. The totality of these memories and mental images now manifests itself—in fusion with the sensory foundation and its inherent relationships—as the **overall impression** of the landscape. Every detail of the landscape contributes from a different side, bringing in a different circle of memories and mental images; and whatever "plays in" through these associations can also "play out" again in our feelings.

From this, it is easy to understand the nature of the **unsayable**, the **inexhaustible**, and the **unexplainable** qualities that belong to the impression of a landscape. Who can track, exhaust, or clarify all the mental images that have contributed to it? Even a single object possesses a certain inexhaustibility in this regard; the landscape offers us, so to speak, an inexhaustibility of such inexhaustible objects, whose <term>associative circles</term> <note>These are the clusters of related ideas and memories that "ring" or surround a specific object in our minds.</note> branch into one another indefinitely. Yet even here, we can consider the main elements and thereby characterize, clarify, and explain the impression—at least to certain limits—of which an example is given below.

Now, one might object to the previous consideration: if meaning comes from experience, then those who have lived among mountains and lakes—and have thus had the most experience with them—ought to carry away a richer feeling when viewing a mountain or lakeside landscape than those seeing it for the first time. Yet the opposite is the case. Precisely the person who has never seen a lake or a mountain is most seized by the first sight of them. 

But the connection is as follows: through earlier experiences, everyone already knows what a "pond" is and what a "hill" is. If he now approaches an immeasurable pond (a lake) or an unclimbable hill (a mountain) for the first time with those prior associations, the very act of being prompted to **quantitatively expand** his previously limited associative circle provides one of the most effective means of stimulating and enlivening his feelings. By contrast, for the person who always lives among lakes and mountains, this <term>ferment</term> <note>Fechner uses the word "Ferment" (leaven or yeast) to describe the mental catalyst that causes a "lump" of stagnant ideas to "rise" into a powerful emotion.</note> is missing—the catalyst that would cause the "lump" of connectable mental images to rise into a powerful and living feeling. His feeling, in short, is blunted, as happens to everyone after...

<summary>Fechner explains that landscape beauty arises from a "fusion" of sensory data and vast, overlapping circles of memory. He notes that newcomers are more moved by nature than locals because the sudden expansion of their existing concepts (like a "pond" becoming a "lake") acts as a powerful emotional catalyst.</summary>

...one finds oneself dulled by long journeys through beautiful regions. What the newly added element of magnification beyond the usual measure does here, another new element or a different way of combining the same elements can do in other cases. To be sure, there is something the carpet cannot provide, which no painted landscape—but only a real landscape—can offer. This, supported by the gentle impression of color, is most strongly felt when looking into a clear sky, yet it is not absent in earthly vistas. <meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on the "aesthetic auxiliary" and the physical sensation of depth. He transitions from how the eye physically adjusts for distance to how these sensations interact with our mental associations when viewing art versus reality.</meta>

Associated with this is the impression that the former is larger than the latter; therefore, even the most faithful landscape painting often increases the desire to see the actual landscape more than it satisfies it through this artificial substitute—just as is the case with small models of great architectural works. This does not exclude the possibility that a painted landscape might surpass a real one in other respects. Namely, the artist can compose associations more favorably than nature usually does by arranging the points of connection for these associations accordingly; however, we will not pursue that further here.

Since we cannot go into all the elements of the landscape in detail, let us try to account for the impression of one main element that—without considering the **principle of association** <note>original: "Associationsprincip," a core concept in Fechner's psychology where past experiences and memories color our current sensory perceptions.</note>—one might not consider a landscape element at all, yet its significant landscape importance is easily explained by it.

It has likely occurred to everyone what charm an otherwise seemingly insignificant landscape can gain through human **architectural works** <note>original: "Bauwerke"</note>. Many views from small mountains owe their charm essentially to looking out over a village in the foreground of an otherwise rather empty region; in other views, a castle or a ruin on a height provides the delightful focal point; others become graceful through country houses or farmhouses scattered here and there; many a green **valley floor** <note>original: "Thalgrund"</note> owes its landscape interest merely to the mill nesting within it, along with the rickety wooden bridge leading across the water. To imagine the human handiwork removed from such **locations** <note>original: "Oertlichkeiten"</note> often means leaving nothing behind of the charming landscape but indifferent land.

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s investigation into the "Principle of Association." Made by human hands and intended for external purposes, they emerge with straight-lined, sharp, right-angled boundaries from the free play of forms created by nature's power. They set their white walls and red roofs in stark contrast to the greens and the sallow colors of the earth and rocks. Now, while **variety** <note>original: "Mannichfaltigkeit," a term often used in aesthetics to describe the "manifold" or the diversity of elements within a single experience.</note> can indeed increase the charm of a thing, it cannot do so through a principle-less, jumbled variety, which usually only gives the impression of displeasing disorder, fragmentation, and distraction; why not here as well? A principle inherent in this visual variety—such as the one that, regardless of meaning, makes **symmetry** more pleasing than asymmetry—is certainly not to be found in the juxtaposition of human **architectural works** <note>original: "Bauwerke"</note> with nature. And if some place great value on **rhythm** as a primary condition of beauty, a building tends to interrupt the rhythm inherent in freely-shaping nature rather than fitting into it. What, then, finally remains to explain the charm that buildings add to the landscape?

Only the **meaning** <note>original: "Bedeutung"</note> remains, which we attach to human structures. Human buildings are the products, the focal points, and the anchorages of human activity; they are the dwelling places of human suffering and joy. The memory of this weaves itself into the **associations** <note>original: "Associationen"</note> which the natural environment, for its part, evokes, and powerfully increases the significance of its content. If nature and human life and activity stood directly opposite one another without mediation, the impression of both could only remain disconnected or mutually disturbing. Instead, we find that through the buildings themselves, human life and activity have grown *into* nature, and from there radiate back *out* into nature. Through this, the unified connection—which the impression of the forms lacks in itself—is mediated in the most multifaceted way. Every other type of building, every other way in which they join together socially or scatter in freedom, plays into the impression of the landscape with different kinds of ideas about the life and activity of the inhabitants. <meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on the "Principle of Association," moving from the theoretical reasoning for why architecture fits into nature to specific visual examples. He argues that the beauty of a building in a landscape is not just about its shape or color, but the human stories and comforts we subconsciously associate with it.</meta>

...roof of a small cottage, a tiny light twinkling from a window, add a significant charm to the landscape—not merely as a gray column or a red point, but as a **focal point for memories** <note>original: "Angriffspunct für die Erinnerung"</note> of the warming stove, the kitchen fire and its comforts, and the evening seclusion of the home. All of this does not float loosely in the air but is woven into the landscape along with the house itself, contributing to the **spiritual coloring** <note>original: "geistigen Colorit"</note> that rests upon its physical appearance.

Now, one must not say—though it has been said to me—that all which **association** <note>the psychological process where a visual trigger brings a memory or idea to mind</note> adds here to the **perception** <note>original: "Anschauung"</note> of a building in nature could also be had without this perception, simply by bringing it to mind through imagination. If that were the case, one would not experience the landscape-specific impression of the building in nature; therefore, it is argued, the impression cannot rest on such associations.

—But what one might try to bring before the mind individually, one after another, incompletely, with the effort of reflection, and without an essential connecting bond, is given to us all at once in a **total impression** <note>original: "Gesammteindruck"</note> through the perception of the building in nature, as if it were a component of the perception itself. That is surely something very different from mere imagination, and a very different impression can depend upon it.

I wish to recall a small example from my own experience where all of this became very vivid to me.

During the holidays of 1865, I spent a few weeks with my wife in a forester’s house, about fifteen minutes from Lauterberg in the **Harz Mountains**. Opposite our dwelling was a green slope that we often climbed, from which we had a view over a wide, wooded mountain landscape with simple, undeveloped forms. Aside from the forester’s house and a neighboring house in the foreground, no human dwellings were visible anywhere; only in the distance, out of the monotony of the green forest rising against the mountains, a single red roof protruded. This, however, brought a quite unique charm to the otherwise simple **mood** <note>original: "Stimmungsverhältnisse," referring to the emotional atmosphere of the scene</note> of the view. It was precisely the focal point of the entire landscape. And I said to myself: what if one were to put a similar red spot on a green wall? Would it look just as idyllic, sentimental, romantic, and fairytale-like as that red roof in the forest landscape? Certainly not. But could the red spot on the...

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s defense of the "Principle of Association" against formalist critics. In the previous section, he used the example of a red roof in a forest to show how human associations (warmth, home, safety) enrich a visual scene. Here, he addresses a hypothetical objection from a follower of Immanuel Kant.</meta>

...could a green wall bring to mind the bustle of human life, with its joys and sorrows, in the midst of a lonely forest, as suddenly as a red roof in the woods?

When I mentioned this example to someone raised in the school of modern **aesthetics** <term>the philosophical study of beauty and art</term>—who would not tolerate the introduction of this "new deity," as he called the **Principle of Association** <term>the psychological process where one thought or visual trigger automatically brings another to mind</term>—I had to hear the following objection, entirely in the spirit of **Kant** <note>Immanuel Kant (1724–1804), who argued that aesthetic judgment should be "disinterested" and based on form rather than personal utility or external associations</note>:

"All that," he said, "which memory adds to the impression of the red roof and the green forest—those secondary ideas that latch on—does not belong to the essence of the aesthetic or the truly landscape-specific impression. It would first have to be stripped away to have a pure experience. For the pure landscape impression, which is precisely what the artist strives to evoke, rests only in those internal 'musical' relationships of form and color that enter us directly through the eye. Through these, we mentally complete the truly visible reality, such as the roof belonging to the house or the green expanse belonging to the forest. Only what the house and the forest are according to their own visible essence, and how they interact with the other visible conditions, is relevant for the landscape impression."

But this objection is based on a delusion: that a house and a forest, in their visible essence, are significantly more than just meaningless lines <note>original: "Lineamente"</note> filled with color, interacting pointlessly with their surroundings. It is only the **utility** of the house for living, only the **capacity** of the tree for growing, and all that follows from both, that brings content, life, and depth to the impression of what we see. Indeed, how could we even speak of a romantic, idyllic, or historical character in a landscape if it weren't for the meaning that these visual conditions hold for human life? This meaning grants them a higher painterly significance that goes beyond the—admittedly important—contrasts, harmonies, and rhythms of colors and shapes. Insofar as these visual elements are considered, they only gain a higher significance for the landscape by being absorbed into those higher relationships. Consequently, according to the **auxiliary principle** <note>original: "Hülfsprincipe"; refers to Fechner's "Auxiliary Principle of Association," where lower aesthetic factors are boosted by higher associations</note>, they serve as the carriers of this higher meaning and are also...

<summary>Fechner rebuts the Kantian view that beauty comes only from pure form and color, arguing instead that a landscape's "romantic" or "idyllic" character depends entirely on our associations with human life and nature's vitality.</summary>
<keywords>Gustav Fechner, aesthetics, Association Principle, Kantianism, landscape painting, visual perception, formalism, utility</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Principle of Association," moving from the theoretical defense against Kantian formalism to practical examples of how architecture and landscape interact. It addresses the aesthetic "mismatch" that occurs when a building's purpose or style clashes with its natural environment.</meta>

...to be estimated as having a higher value than it does on its own. However, we consider the dispute with this objection to be settled for now, as there are no arguments against the stubbornness with which it is occasionally maintained; let us continue our observation a bit further.

It can happen, although the case is not frequent, that a building, instead of enhancing the charm of a landscape, enters into the impression of it unpleasantly; be it that the **associative** <term>referring to the mental connections, memories, and cultural ideas triggered by an object</term> requirements of the building contradict those of its surroundings, thus their characters do not harmonize, or that the building itself, through its purpose, evokes unpleasant associations. We would have the first case if we were to see a **Greek temple** <note>Often used in 19th-century aesthetics as the ideal of classical, southern harmony</note> in a Nordic ice landscape or a **Swabian peasant hut** <note>Swabia is a region in Southwestern Germany; the author is contrasting humble, local rural architecture with exotic environments</note> among palms. In the meantime, such structures simply do not arise in such places, or only quite exceptionally; rather, buildings almost always appear not merely to have grown *on* the ground, but to have grown *out* of it. Every dwelling seeks, so to speak, the appropriate environment, and every environment the appropriate dwelling—which does not prevent the same hut from finding just as suitable a spot at the foot of a mountain as on its peak, and for a hunting lodge and a forest tavern to fit into the same place in the woods; there is a certain latitude in this regard, which must only not be exceeded, so as not to establish the displeasing impression of a mismatch according to the principle stated on (p. 97).

Yet there are truly cases where the building appears to us, so to speak, detached from the surroundings and only as if placed into them; we sense this immediately in the aesthetic impression. This occurs especially where the building is placed artistically in architectural perfection without regard for connection to the surroundings, or intended for purposes that have nothing to do with the environment. For example, a decorative palace or a factory building does not easily enter a landscape with advantage. The palace wants to rule over an environment of gardens or houses, but not over an untamed natural environment, and the factory building unites workers and labors that we do not imagine to be linked by any threads of interest or activity with the surrounding nature. On the other hand, nothing is more scenic than a castle on a rock, which, without regard for symmetry and the **golden ratio** <term>the mathematical ratio (roughly 1.618) traditionally used to achieve aesthetic "perfection" in proportions</term>, all pre-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how buildings integrate with or disrupt the natural landscape based on the "Principle of Association." ...follows the crags of the rock, like the mill whose machinery reaches directly into the lively, rushing water, or the village whose houses climb roadlessly up a mountainside or scatter among orchards, and so on.

The factory building, to a certain degree, realizes the second case—where a building disturbs the impression of the landscape through its own unpleasant **associations** <term>mental links where one idea triggers another based on experience or social context</term>, as we involuntarily think of the toil of labor and all the misery of the **proletariat** <term>the industrial working class</term>. In this regard, however, it is worst with asylums and penitentiaries. Many old castles and monasteries on hills and mountains are now used for such purposes; as soon as we learn this about such a structure, it is as if the charm it lent to the landscape were extinguished with cold water. Even the impression of railway buildings outside the landscape suffers somewhat from this. One might well say that these now belong among the most significant achievements in **architecture** <note>original: "Baukunst", literally "the art of building"</note>. What magnificent, characteristic works of this kind, maintained in the purest forms of architectural symmetry, can be seen not just in one but in many places! Furthermore, they can demonstrate the most perfect **utility** <term>original: "Zweckmässigkeit"; the philosophical idea that an object’s beauty is derived from how well it fulfills its intended function</term>, and who does not know the great role that utility plays in the aesthetics of architecture—ultimately also only through association. Yet the impression of these buildings always lacks something of full satisfaction and the ultimate height; they never grant the joyful impression of a palace or the uplifting one of a temple. Why? Because we see in them the scene of a bustle and business-like activity that displeases us.

But what, one might ask, causes the great charm that the ruin of an old castle, an old fortress, or an old church—for the ruin of a hut or the ruin
<margin>?</margin>
of a newly built house does not do it—is able to bestow upon a landscape? Does it not remind us of destruction, the decay of something rich, bold, great, and holy? And are these not unpleasant memories? Yet it can only be memories, and thus associations, that bring about this charm; for everyone will admit that it cannot depend on the direct impression of the ruin’s form and color. Indeed, nothing is more suitable to [demonstrate] the power of the associative factor in the landscape impression...

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of architectural aesthetics, specifically moving from the functional "utility" of modern buildings like factories and railways to the evocative, associative power of historical ruins. It bridges the gap between the displeasure caused by modern "misery" and the aesthetic pleasure derived from romanticized decay.</meta>

...prove [this] better than the power with which a gray, formless ruin operates—one that hardly distinguishes itself from the craggy rocks beneath it.

To be sure, if the ruin signified our own downfall, its sight would not please us; and even the thought of a ruin that does not concern us at all could, in itself, only cause displeasure due to its inherent feeling of sadness. However, there are countless impressions where **displeasurable elements** <term>original: "Unlustmomente"; factors that cause discomfort or negative feelings</term> are outweighed by **pleasurable elements** <term>original: "Lustmomente"; factors that cause delight or satisfaction</term> with which they are connected. Thus, when faced with the impression of a ruin, we must seek out what causes this, and do the same whenever we encounter similar cases.

The ruin of an old castle easily leads us from the idea of its decay back to the romantically charming visions of ancient **chivalry** <note>original: "Ritterthum", referring to the age of knights and medieval nobility</note>. Not only do we prefer to linger on such ideas rather than those of decay because they are more enchanting, but a lively receptive excitement and engagement—which pulls us out of the cycle of things to which we have become dulled by habit—generally appeals to us. Consequently, we are even quite happy to hear of terrors, provided they do not affect us personally. After all, people even flock to the site of a fire in an ordinary house just to enjoy the thrill of that excitement; but once the charm of novelty has passed, the **displeasurable element** of the thought of destruction gains the upper hand. We then wish to see the fire site replaced by a new house, which we then no longer care about. For the history of the burned-down common house offers no incentive to immerse ourselves in it, and the new house has no history at all in which we could dwell. 

It is different with the ruin of something grand, rich, bold, and strong. Even if we know nothing of its actual history, such a history is linked to it through **association** <term>the mental process where one thought calls up another based on past experience or general knowledge</term> based on what we generally know about the past of such ruins, and this can be endlessly exploited by the **imagination** <note>original: "Phantasie"</note>. Thus, the ruin of the old castle acts as a **focal point** <note>original: "Brennpunct", literally "burning point" or focus</note> of memories of a strange, powerful, and varied character, introducing a strong element of interest into an otherwise sleepy landscape. It evokes an elegiac alternation of pleasurable and displeasurable associations, with pleasure predominating on the whole—much like a spring that bounces back all the higher after every momentary pressure.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the aesthetic of ruins, specifically contrasting the pleasure derived from historical decay with the displeasure triggered by modern functional buildings associated with suffering, like prisons.</meta>

This becomes fully clear if we now imagine a **prison** <term>original: "Zuchthaus"; a house of correction or penitentiary often associated with forced labor</term> in place of the ruin. The prison brings with it only a very limited circle of associations, and these consist of purely and intensely displeasurable ideas. There, instead of the long, eventful history of a proud life belonging to rich and bold lineages—which spins backward from the ruin of a castle or a fortress—we see the huddled prisoners with their sinful lives in the background and their current tragic existence; in short, the worst of what painfully touches us in life is concentrated here. No matter how beautifully and newly built the prison may be, the negative associative impression will outweigh the pleasing direct one, or at least disturb it dreadfully; whereas the direct, displeasing impression of the ruin cannot prevail against its associative pleasantness.

Ruins on mountains have a more powerful effect than those on the plains, partly because our attention is naturally drawn to the high points in a landscape, and partly because the impression of the building's former mastery over its surroundings is strengthened by its elevation.

The **staffage** <term>original: "Staffage"; the placement of human or animal figures in a landscape to provide scale, life, or narrative interest</term> of a landscape with human figures falls partly under the same viewpoint as man-made structures. However, the human being is not as firmly rooted in the landscape and appears, to that extent, as a more accidental component that does not define the impression as essentially—unless they are grown into nature through their very occupation, such as the shepherd on the Alpine meadow or the fisherman by the sea. These are truly landscape elements; but not all figures seen in painted landscapes are.

To be sure, there are also landscapes that, without any buildings—indeed, without a trace of human existence or activity at all—nevertheless make a strong impression on the **spirit** <term>original: "Gemüth"; a person’s inner emotional and mental state</term>, such as a magnificent, lonely mountain region, or a vista through the woods in the sunshine, or rocks by the sea where the waves break. The sight of man and his works is not the only thing that can stimulate human feelings through association; a person can even be moved tragically by the *absence* of the human, which again presupposes an associative memory of it. But the sight of the...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how human elements within a landscape, such as buildings or figures (staffage), enhance its aesthetic appeal through the Principle of Association. The previous page contrasted the romantic appeal of ruins with the grim reality of functional buildings like prisons.</meta>

The representation of man, his works, and his traces is, in any case, the most abundant and effective means of awakening aesthetically significant feelings within the realm of the visible. The landscape painter will rarely know how to get by without including them; but where this is the case, they will almost always seek a surrogate for the human element in animal life, which provides the closest associative bridge to it.

Thus, a lonely glimpse through the forest is rarely without **game** <note>original: "Wild"; refers to wild animals like deer or boar</note>, a cliff amidst the surf is rarely without a fluttering seagull or a resting seal. Take the cranes or herons from one of the most beautiful landscapes by **Lessing** <note>Karl Friedrich Lessing (1808–1880), a prominent German Romantic landscape painter and great-nephew of the philosopher G.E. Lessing</note>—specifically, a lake at a rock wall—and you have removed a primary element of the work.

In this regard, we might recall a statement made, if I remember correctly, by **A. v. Humboldt** <note>Alexander von Humboldt (1769–1859), the polymath and explorer who often wrote on the "physiognomy" of plants and landscapes</note>: that motifs useful for the landscape painter are actually found only in **cultivated** lands. This may seem surprising when one thinks of the lushness of nature in so many regions where the foot of man has not yet found a place and the cultivation of the soil has not yet taken hold. In truth, however, the elements of nature arrange themselves in a new way under the cultural influence of man; and where nothing reminds us of this ordering influence, the impression of the landscape easily remains raw and artistically unusable.

## XI. Relationship between Poetry and Painting from the
### Perspective of the Association Principle.

It is a much-discussed question as to what the boundaries between poetry and painting are, and as is well known, **Lessing’s Laocoon** <note>A seminal 1766 essay by Gotthold Ephraim Lessing titled *Laocoon: An Essay on the Limits of Painting and Poetry*</note> refers mainly to this. His presentation, like everything by Lessing, is very attractive and ingenious; yet I believe that by drawing in considerations prompted by the **Association Principle** <term>the psychological concept that our aesthetic pleasure is derived from the ideas and memories we link to an object, rather than just its physical form</term>, it can be partly supplemented and partly deepened in several respects, while the principle itself can thereby find a further illustration of its applicability.

<summary>The author argues that human traces or animal "surrogates" are essential for effective landscape painting and introduces a discussion on the boundaries between poetry and painting using the Principle of Association.</summary>
<keywords>landscape painting, Alexander von Humboldt, Karl Friedrich Lessing, Association Principle, Laocoon, cultivation, staffage, aesthetics</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into how visual elements trigger mental associations. Having previously discussed how human "staffage" or traces of cultivation give life to landscapes, the author now pivots to a theoretical comparison between the "language" of visual art and the language of words.</meta>

As previously discussed (p. 117), a **point of equivalence** <note>original: "Gleichungspunct"; a term referring to the functional intersection where two different artistic mediums share a common principle</note> exists between poetry and painting: the visible forms used by painting, just like the audible words used by poetry and language in general, are carriers of a meaning that has become familiar through **association** <note>The "Principle of Association" was a central concept in 19th-century psychology and aesthetics, suggesting that an object's beauty or meaning is derived from the thoughts, memories, and feelings it triggers in the viewer's mind</note>. According to this view, the forms of things themselves can be called **visible words**. However, as important as this point of equivalence is, it still leaves room for equally significant differences, which we wish to explore further.

The primary difference lies in the fact that the "visible words" of painting do, after all, directly reproduce something of the object to be represented—for example, a person’s external shape and color. While these certainly do not constitute the whole person, they do comprise a part of them, leaving only the remainder of what belongs to that person to the associative imagination. Meanwhile, the words of language (with few exceptions) are entirely indifferent to the object they represent and leave *everything* to such imagination; thus, the word *man* triggers the idea of the whole man, and the word *tree* the idea of the whole tree. 

Linked to this is a second, albeit less pervasive and important, difference: the associative meanings of words are **conventional** <note>original: "conventionell"; referring to meanings established by social agreement or tradition rather than natural likeness</note> and change between different languages, whereas the meanings of forms are—at least to certain limits—forced upon us and are thereby a common human heritage. Thus, the words for "eye" and "mouth," and the associative meanings attached to them, could be swapped between two languages. In contrast, the associative meanings of the forms of the eye and mouth—whereby one serves for seeing and the other for speaking and eating—cannot be swapped. Yet, this applies only to the most fundamental or, so to speak, **natural meanings** <note>original: "Naturbedeutungen"</note> of forms. Beyond that, it is well known that their meanings change according to the variety of experiences associated with them, just as the meanings of words change according to the variety of conventions. And once the conventional meanings of words have become established through habit, they cling to them just as firmly as natural meanings cling to forms. Hence, the second difference is of lesser importance than the first, at least from the **points of view** <note>original: "Gesichtspuncten"</note> that we shall consider here.

<summary>This page explores the semiotic relationship between painting and poetry, arguing that while both rely on mental association, painting uses "visible words" that share a physical likeness with their subjects, whereas linguistic words are arbitrary conventions.</summary>
<keywords>aesthetics, association, philosophy of language, semiotics, painting vs. poetry, natural meaning, convention</keywords>

--- Page 148 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between the "visible words" of painting and the conventional words of language. The author is exploring why painting excels at sensory immediacy while language excels at expressing what lies beneath the surface or across time.</meta>

Insofar as painting provides the entire visible side of a thing directly and all at once, in full coherence and with full clarity—details which the mind, when hearing the words signifying that thing, must first add through **association** <note>The author uses "association" to describe how our brains link a word like "tree" to a mental image of a tree, a process he considers less vivid than seeing a painted tree.</note>, and even then only in vague generality or with weakened clarity—painting holds an advantage. This advantage is not limited merely to the **sensory side** of the impression made by visible objects; it also extends, within certain limits, to the range and play of the associations depending on it. This is because the coherence, completeness, and clarity of the association itself are partly determined by the sensory foundation of those associations.

For instance, a painted face immediately and "in one stroke" <note>original: "in einem Schlage"</note> gives us the entire **total sensory appearance** of the face: the expression of a specific age, a certain degree of health, a particular mental talent, or a person’s specific mood. It thus provides a **total associative impression** that a linguistic description cannot match in any way. While language can indeed speak of all these things, it can neither exhaust them nor reproduce them in their full coherence as a single associative impression. It is impossible to provide a beautiful description of even the most beautiful face—the more beautiful it is, the less possible the description becomes. Consequently, writers often prefer to omit the description entirely and speak only of the *effect* the beauty has on others; the same applies to a landscape. Painting, however, may venture to depict both.

On the other hand, what painting provides directly is always only the **surface** of visible objects, and even then, only in a single moment. Painting cannot directly present what is behind the surface, nor the movements and changes that precede or follow a thing, nor what is connected to it spiritually or through cause and effect, nor anything invisible at all. Much of what is required to represent things and events is only so distantly and vaguely connected to a visible surface that painting must either renounce representing it altogether, or rely on a very uncertain associative awakening of it through the means at its disposal. In contrast, [language] covers and exhausts

<meta>This page continues the comparison between painting and language. While the previous page highlighted painting's ability to provide a complete sensory "total impression" at a glance, this page explores the unique strengths of language—specifically its ability to navigate concepts, time, and the internal world.</meta>

the words of language with their meanings and, through their possible combinations, encompass the entire realm of human imagination and conceptual thought. They are thus able to guide the flow of mental representations and thoughts—and the resulting flow of feelings—along very specific paths. These paths stretch through everything external and internal, spiritual and physical, past and future, visible and invisible, general and concrete. In this way, language can compensate for, or even surpass, the advantages that painting holds in certain respects by offering advantages of a different kind. For instance, the impression made by a lyrical poem, a drama, an epic, or even a simple story cannot be replaced by any painting, though it may be supplemented by one in certain ways.

Accordingly, painting will generally find its most advantageous use where the primary aesthetic impression depends either directly on the coherent perception of an external appearance captured in a single moment, or on the immediate and certain connection and play of aesthetically effective and satisfying <term>associative ideas (Associationsvorstellungen): the mental images and memories triggered in a viewer's mind by a stimulus, such as a painting or a word</term> triggered by it. In this regard, poetry and linguistic representation in general cannot keep pace. Language offers nothing of the direct sensory impression, and from the surrounding circle of associations, it can only powerfully evoke certain moments one by one. It is unable to exhaust the full richness of these associations or establish the <term>total impression (Totaleindruck): the unified, holistic effect of a work of art that strikes the viewer all at once</term> that depends on their simultaneous coherence. On the other hand, poetic and linguistic representation are used to greater advantage when the primary aesthetic impression depends on relationships that reach further through time, space, and the inner life—things which the sensory appearance of a visible surface, and the ideas most closely linked to it, cannot match.

There are, then, certain subjects and motifs that, based on these viewpoints, are better left to poetry, while others are better suited to painting. Yet there are also plenty of subjects that can serve as common material for both. In such cases, however, if each is to remain within its proper boundaries, they must cross paths in their treatment of the same material rather than coincide. This is because poetry, with its representation of the passage of time, moves across that "slice of time" <note>The author is likely referring to the "pregnant moment" in art—a single frozen moment in a painting that implies what happened before and after.</note> which the

<meta>This page continues the comparison between painting and poetry. The previous page established that while painting provides a sensory "total impression," language excels at navigating the internal world and temporal flow. Here, the author explores how these two distinct media complement each other to form a complete understanding of a subject.</meta>

A painting, by representing a single moment, reaches beyond its own limits; conversely, the painting’s spatial breadth reaches beyond the passing flow of time found in poetry. Literature <note>original: *Dichtung* – referring to poetry or creative writing in a broad sense</note> provides a spiritual depth to the same subject matter for which painting provides the colorful surface. Because both arts meet within the same realm of imagination but then diverge from those points of contact, they enter into a sisterly and complementary relationship. This occurs not only between the arts in general, but also within specific works, where they can combine to mutually reinforce and complete their overall impact.

For example, if a battle scene in an <term>epic (Epos): a long narrative poem telling of heroic deeds</term> is accompanied by a picture, the vague and inadequate idea that a linguistic description creates of the battle's spatial layout can be completed, strengthened, and enriched by the image. Or conversely, the painted depiction of a battle—which might be incomprehensible to us regarding its motives and its historical or legendary context—can be completed by an added historical or epic description.

Here and there, one certainly finds the claim that every good picture must be understood on its own, without needing any outside explanation. Nothing, however, is less accurate than this claim. On the contrary, every historical, mythological, or religious work—essentially every picture whatsoever—requires the addition of <term>insights (Erkenntnisse): the external knowledge or context a viewer brings to a work</term> that cannot be drawn from the picture itself. This is necessary not only to understand the work, but also to appreciate its full value and feel its total beauty. It is only that we already draw much of the knowledge required to understand pictures from everyday life, or can assume it based on the level of education that makes art accessible to enjoyment in the first place, without needing to awaken it through a specific explanation added to the image. Therefore, if one wishes to speak of understanding pictures "from" or "through" themselves, one can only speak of it in this sense. And so there are indeed countless images that are immediately understandable and enjoyable in this way, but there are others that are not, yet must still be recognized. Who still needs a

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the "sisterly relationship" between painting and poetry. It argues that while some subjects are universally understood, others—particularly historical scenes—require a balance between visual impact and textual explanation.</meta>

special explanation when he sees a Nativity of Christ, an Ascension, a Dutch tavern scene, or a landscape; everyone already knows everything required to understand them. In contrast, many scenes from secular history and even some **genre scenes** <term>Genre scenes: Depictions of everyday life, such as markets, domestic interiors, or parties.</term> still require explanation or, at the very least, a caption. What one can rightfully demand of such works is only that they make a sufficiently appealing or interesting impression even without the added explanation, so as to prompt the viewer to seek a deeper understanding. Thus, the intended impact should not be created entirely by the explanation, but rather the explanation should allow the impact to reach its full potential, so as not to burden the supplement with the work of the whole.

When a painting shows **Luther** <note>Martin Luther (1483–1546), the central figure of the Protestant Reformation.</note> standing before the assembled princes and bishops, the courageous, calm, and God-fearing features of the simple, sturdy man—contrasted with the splendor, pride, and arrogance of the assembled **Imperial Diet** <note>This likely refers to the Diet of Worms in 1521, where Luther famously refused to recant his writings.</note>—must contain something appealing and uplifting even for someone who knows nothing of this history. It must stir a desire for further inquiry into what all of this means. If the painter were not capable of giving the painting an effect that allows us to sense through vague features what the added history makes more definite—thereby granting us full internal satisfaction—then either he was not fit for the task, or the task was not fit for the art of painting. However, the achievement of the image is not settled by that alone. Rather, without the added explanation, the power of the impression would be lost. In the vagueness and the riddle of how everything in the picture connects, and which motives drive the movement and expression, the impact would lack the focus of a unified point of climax.

Now, poetry <note>original: *Poesie* – used here to encompass literature and narrative art.</note> can enter into an explanatory relationship with painting in various ways, and vice versa. Indeed, painters draw some of their most effective motifs directly from poetry—the Homeric, Dantesque, Shakespearian, and Goethean—and they must then, of course, presuppose a knowledge of these poems for the understanding of their works. These motifs become most effective because the entire interest that the poem takes in the subject of the representation...
