presentation through a connection and sequence of ideas that cannot be painted. For someone familiar with the poem, this sequence is transferred onto the painting and is capable of unfolding again into a play of these ideas—specifically, a poetic play. This is why such motifs have an advantage over those from **prosaic history** <term>Prosaic history: Factual or literal historical accounts, which may lack the inherent emotional resonance of a well-known fictional or poetic narrative.</term>. After all, one generally demands that a painting make a poetic impression; motifs drawn from poetry bring such an impression into the painting, if not entirely, then at least halfway.

<margin><unclear>Annotation in pencil</unclear></margin> Furthermore, a relationship similar to that between poetry and painting can be found in this regard within poetry itself. Among all of **Goethe’s** <note>Johann Wolfgang von Goethe (1749–1832), the preeminent figure of German literature.</note> lyric poems, there are likely none that win a more vivid interest from us or shake our feelings to such a degree as the songs of **Gretchen** <note>The tragic heroine of Goethe's *Faust*.</note>, of **Mignon**, or of the **Harper** <note>Mignon and the Harper are mysterious, tragic characters from Goethe's novel *Wilhelm Meister's Apprenticeship*.</note>—essentially those interspersed within his dramas and novels. Likewise, the greatest lyric power dwells within those poems by **Schiller** <note>Friedrich Schiller (1759–1805), a major German playwright and poet.</note> that appear in his plays, such as: "The oak forest roars, the clouds drift" <note>From the play *Wallenstein*.</note>; "Farewell, you mountains, you beloved pastures" <note>The heroine's farewell in *The Maid of Orleans*.</note>; or "Hurrying clouds, sailors of the air" <note>From the play *Mary Stuart*.</note>. I recall a novel by **Eichendorff** <note>Joseph von Eichendorff (1788–1857), a key poet and novelist of the Romantic era.</note> titled *Premonition and Presence* <note>Original: *Ahnung und Gegenwart*.</note> which, although not among his best works, is nonetheless breathed through by a poetic spirit, and in which several songs receive and provide a special charm through their connection to the narrative.

The reason for this advantage is easy to understand based on what has been said. The song, incapable of summarizing on its own everything that might motivate and support the emotion ruling within it, relinquishes this task to the larger whole in which it is incorporated. It can then more easily content itself with offering only that to which the emotion is most directly linked—that in which the emotion is, so to speak, most condensed. Yet, in the songs of Mignon and Gretchen, the entire novel *Wilhelm Meister* or the entire *Faust* unconsciously plays into this feeling. From the full richness of meaningful relationships woven together in this way, the song offers us the golden fruit in its small shell. While reading Mignon’s song, one sees her standing there, hears her singing, and her past and future fate drift by like a dream.

<summary>This page argues that art and lyric poetry gain emotional depth when they are embedded in a larger narrative context, such as a novel or a play. It highlights how familiar characters from Goethe and Schiller evoke a world of feeling that a standalone work cannot easily achieve.</summary>
<keywords>intertextuality, Goethe, Schiller, lyric poetry, painting vs. poetry, emotional resonance, Mignon, Gretchen, Eichendorff</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how external literary context enriches the viewer's experience of a painting. The previous page established that motifs from poetry or drama provide a "poetic head start," allowing a painting to unfold into a sequence of ideas that visual art alone cannot depict. This page explores the specific role of text and captions in bridging the gap between the silent image and the spoken word.</meta>

Some ancient visual representations explain themselves in the simplest way because the names of the figures are written alongside them—and archaeologists are often happy enough to find them explained this way. Some old German paintings do this in the most naive way, where the speech of the persons presented as talking hangs out of their mouths on a long ribbon <note>These are known as phylacteries or speech scrolls, a precursor to the modern speech bubble used in medieval and Renaissance art.</note>. To our modern taste, which is arguably better in this regard, such "tapeworms" cause stomach aches because they are, in fact, alien parasites within the image. The image wants to be colored by associative memory, but it does not want to be interrupted by such devices. In general, including text within the image itself will always do more harm by disrupting its cohesion than it will benefit by explaining its meaning—unless the **hermeneutic** <term>Hermeneutic: Relating to the branch of knowledge that deals with interpretation, especially of the Bible or literary texts.</term> interest outweighs the **aesthetic** interest. On the other hand, it benefits many a painting if—even if only to refresh the memory—the specific passage from poetry or the Bible to which it refers is attached directly to the frame or, to avoid cluttering its decorative setting, included in a written supplement below it.

The Düsseldorf painter **Hübner** <note>Julius Hübner (1806–1882), a prominent member of the Düsseldorf School of painting known for his highly detailed biblical and historical scenes.</note> has depicted the scene of the old mother **Naomi** saying goodbye to her daughters-in-law from the **Book of Ruth**. Weeping and turned away, the younger daughter-in-law <note>Orpah, who, unlike Ruth, chose to return to her people in Moab.</note> departs; meanwhile, **Ruth** cannot tear herself away from her mother and places her hands on the shoulders of the woman, who looks mournful and deeply moved. However true and beautiful all of this may be depicted, the painted silent mouth of Ruth cannot reveal her moving speech—in which she declares her resolve not to leave her mother-in-law—nor the associated meaning of the entire painting as clearly as the added biblical passage, which provides the speech itself, is able to do; it reads:

> "Entreat me not to leave thee, or to return from following after thee: for whither thou goest, I will go; and where thou lodgest, I will lodge: thy people shall be my people, and thy God my God: Where thou diest, will I die, and there will I be buried: the Lord do so to me, and more also, if ought but death part thee and me." <note>original: "Rede mir nicht drein, dass ich dich verlassen sollte und von dir umkehren; wo du hingehst, da will ich auch hingehn, wo du bleibest, da bleibe ich auch. Dein Volk ist mein Volk und dein Gott ist mein Gott. Wo du stirbest, da sterbe ich auch, da will ich begraben werden. Der Herr thue mir diess und das, der Tod muss dich und mich scheiden." This is the famous vow from Ruth 1:16–17.</note>

Certainly, a large, perhaps even the largest, part of the scene’s meaning for us rests in these words, which the painter simply could not paint and could not allow to be guessed from the painting, and of which few viewers will have retained a clear memory from reading the Bible. On the other hand, for someone who reads the biblical passage on its own, it will be impossible to con- <note>The text likely continues with "concretize" or "conceive" on the following page.</note>

<meta>This page continues Gustav Theodor Fechner’s analysis of the relationship between poetry and visual art. He is specifically addressing whether illustrations truly enhance a literary work or if they create a cognitive distraction that disrupts the "flow" of the reader's experience.</meta>

->Fechner, Primer of Aesthetics. 10<-

...for the poem to intervene in this impression, since it is painted only with this prerequisite in mind. On the other hand, for the impression of a poem that was written and is capable of standing on its own without the prerequisite of illustration, it is anything but necessary to intervene with the circle of imagery provided by an illustration. And if I previously mentioned the advantage of supplementation—which, for example, the illustration of a battle scene in an epic poem can provide through a picture—upon closer consideration, many deductions must also be made from this advantage.

From the outset, it is clear that poetry and painting, in such an external connection, do not harmonize into a correspondingly unified total impression. Thus, they cannot grant each other such equally effective support as poetry and music do in a song. This is because the poetry and music of a song flow in the same stream, so that their effects penetrate one another directly. In contrast, one can only follow the poem and the illustrating picture alternately. One can certainly, while laden with the ideas of the one, "unload" them, so to speak, onto the other; but this happens only by means of an interruption. Just as a poet, while reading a poem aloud, does not like to be interrupted by the listener, he would also not like to find the reading of the poem interrupted by the contemplation of a painting. In this regard, the following must also be considered:

When the epic poet describes a battle, generally only certain moments of the battle will intervene with aesthetic effect into the context of the representation. While it is, from a certain perspective, a disadvantage of poetic representation that it cannot depict all moments of the battle, from another perspective, it is an advantage that it does not *need* to depict all of them. Instead, by setting aside the indifferent details, the poet can highlight those which are essential for poetic effect and weave them into the overall context of poetically effective moments. Thus arises the easy, pure flow of poetry. In contrast, painting is compelled to present everything about the battle in full breadth—everything that belongs to its visible context as seen from a given spatial and temporal standpoint. In doing so, it conjures up a context of associations <term>Associations: The mental process where one thought or image naturally leads to another based on their relationship in the mind.</term> which, though it [aligns] with those evoked by the linguistic representation...

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on the potential friction between poetry and the visual arts. He is exploring the psychological "interruption" that occurs when a reader moves from text to image, weighing whether this distraction is a detriment or a beneficial palate cleanser for the mind.</meta>

supplemented, but also leads out from it in various directions that are not relevant to the matter at hand. We are, after all, accustomed to considering a picture not merely based on what it represents, but also *how* it represents it and how it fulfills its artistic task. Through this, we are, so to speak, thrown out of the **poetic stream** and onto the dry land from another side.

Nonetheless, the intersection of both modes of representation can, in certain respects, strengthen and enrich the impression on both sides, provided that the point of most effective intersection is brought into prominence. It is precisely in this highlighting that the **illustrating artist** must prove their skill. Furthermore, the mental images we carry over through association from one representation to the other naturally tend to cluster around this most effective point. Insofar as all this cannot prevent the image from leading out of the poetic context in some ways, one can accept the possibility of switching between the two as an advantage; any long progression in the same type or class of impression eventually causes fatigue, whereas the switch between the two loses the unpleasantness of a disruption due to the elements they still share. 

And if the images follow one another more rapidly than the need for variety arises, one is free to skip over a whole series of them, only to browse through them later at will. If the poem is sufficiently captivating, one will do so anyway; if the poem is boring, one can sometimes compensate with the entertainment of the picture—provided the image is not even more boring than the text. In my opinion, **poetry** and the **visual arts** complement each other most perfectly in <term>ABC books</term><note>original: "Abecebüchern," or children's primers, where the letter, the word, and the image are inextricably linked for learning.</note>, the <term>Munich broadsheets</term><note>original: "Münchner Bilderbogen," a highly popular 19th-century series of illustrated broadsheets featuring everything from fairy tales to technical drawings.</note>, and <term>broadsheets</term><note>original: "fliegenden Blättern," likely referring to the popular satirical magazine *Fliegende Blätter*, famous for its caricatures.</note>; there, there is neither too little nor too much from either side. Each needs the other and possesses as much of the other as is required—though, of course, much of that material is altogether too little <note>in quality</note> and therefore too much <note>to bear</note>.

One might be tempted to reject the explanation of poems through accompanying images (and vice versa) based on the frequently cited <term>aesthetic principle</term> that a work of art must leave room for the **imagination** and not anticipate everything for it. What the poem still leaves for the imagination to supplement

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s investigation into the relationship between text and image. On the previous page, he discussed how switching between reading and looking at illustrations can prevent mental fatigue. Here, he addresses a specific counter-argument: the idea that if an artist depicts everything too clearly, they "rob" the viewer of the chance to use their own imagination.</meta>

...is left remaining <note>This continues a sentence from the previous page, likely referring to the details of a scene.</note> would be supplemented by the added picture, and vice-versa; thus, nothing would remain for the **imagination** <note>original: "Phantasie"</note>; therefore, it would be better to separate the two than to combine them. Indeed, this principle is also counted among the various reasons for the rejection of **painted statues** <note>This refers to "polychromy," the historical debate over whether classical statues were originally colored and whether modern sculpture should be painted.</note>. And certainly, if statues are considered objectionable for this reason, then every illustration must be so for the same reason. 

But as much as this principle can appeal to such high authority, I nonetheless consider it fundamentally unsound. On the contrary: the more specific details of the subject an artist pre-empts for the imagination, the more opportunities he gives it to go beyond them. For one should not think that the artist, with everything he is able to provide, can bind the wings of imagination and narrow its scope; to do that, he would have to pre-empt and provide the entire world that constitutes this scope. From every piece he provides, however, the imagination is free to take a further flight; the larger the circumference of what he gives, the more starting points it has from which to fly further, and the less it finds itself hindered by having to search for such starting points in the first place.

Thus, the view of **Lessing** <note>Gotthold Ephraim Lessing (1729–1781), an influential German philosopher who argued in his essay "Laocoön" that visual art should avoid depicting the "climactic moment" of an action.</note>—from which the above rule originated—also seems unsound to me: namely, the idea that an **emotion** <note>original: "Affect"</note> must not be depicted at its peak by the visual arts, in order to leave something for the imagination to complete.

If a screaming **Laocoön** <note>A famous ancient Greek statue showing a father and sons in a death struggle with sea serpents. Lessing argued the statue doesn't show him screaming because a wide-open mouth is "ugly" in art and leaves nothing to the imagination.</note> would displease us, it is in fact not because nothing more would be left for the imagination, but because a man screaming in pain simply displeases us in general. The same applies to the other examples put forward by Lessing: **Medea** murdering her children and the raging **Ajax**. In general, we are displeased by any peaked expression of physical pain or of a repulsive passion—and especially of frenzy. 

The fullest expression of a noble pain, a noble love, joy, or enthusiasm, on the other hand, will never displease us; rather, it will please us all the more, the more we say to ourselves: our imagination can achieve nothing beyond this. Yet the imagination still has "room beyond" in that it can continue to envision the motives, the consequences, and the entire context of the fate that called forth that expression, immersing itself in its full significance. And the imagination will find itself all the more strongly stimulated to do so, the more it finds the expression depicted at its most **pregnant moment** <term>pregnant moment: the single instant in a story that most clearly implies what happened before and what will happen after</term> at its highest stage.

<summary>Fechner challenges Lessing’s famous aesthetic theory, arguing that a highly detailed or "peaked" artistic depiction does not stifle the viewer's imagination but actually provides more jumping-off points for deeper reflection.</summary>
<keywords>Gotthold Ephraim Lessing, Laocoön, aesthetics, imagination, emotion, visual arts vs. poetry, pregnant moment, artistic expression</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of the idea that visual art "stifles" imagination by being too specific. On the previous page, he challenged the view (associated with Gotthold Ephraim Lessing) that an artist should only depict the moment "just before" a climax to leave room for the viewer's mind to work. Here, he provides a counter-example from the works of the painter Cornelius.</meta>

If the principle were correct in its generality, then **Cornelius** <note>Peter von Cornelius (1783–1867) was a leading German painter of the Romantic era, known for his massive fresco cycles and illustrations of classic literature.</note> would have done very poorly in his *Nibelungen* cycle <note>The *Nibelungenlied* is an epic poem in Middle High German; Cornelius's illustrations of it helped revive interest in German national myths.</note> by having **Siegfried** run through and through by the spear of **Hagen**. According to that principle, only the drawing back of the spear would have been permitted; whereas in the picture, leaving absolutely nothing left for the imagination, the spear has even completed its journey through the entire body and protrudes with its tip from the chest. "Aha," they say, "the imagination is not finished with that yet, for the entire past and consequence of the poem is conjured up by the imagination upon this occasion, in which everything culminates." Exactly right! That is precisely what I am saying; the same will be the case at every peak toward which an artist represents his subject; and the **Siegfried** completely run through in the picture is more effective in this regard than one who is only yet to be run through by the imagination.

It may well happen that one still prefers the indeterminacy of the visual idea which a poem leaves for itself over the determinacy in which the picture attempts to fix it. It is not easy for a pictorial representation of **Mignon**, **Gretchen**, **Lotte**, **Ottilie**, or **Clärchen** <note>These are all famous heroines from the works of Johann Wolfgang von Goethe: Mignon (*Wilhelm Meister's Apprenticeship*), Gretchen (*Faust*), Lotte (*The Sorrows of Young Werther*), Ottilie (*Elective Affinities*), and Clärchen (*Egmont*).</note> to satisfy everyone; but that is not because the painter shortened the imagination's labor, but because he does not satisfy it, in that he would have to be as significant an artist as the poet, and in a corresponding direction. That does not happen easily. We would like to find the anchor points of the entire, deeply internal poetic description of those personalities—held in the most individual traits—again in the picture; but the picture does not provide them sufficiently, or it provides others than those we seek. Meanwhile, there is no lack of poetic descriptions where the impression can only gain by the painter filling in the indeterminacy left by the poet, so that we are enriched rather than impoverished. Thus, one can illustrate **Tasso** and **Ariosto** <note>Torquato Tasso and Ludovico Ariosto were Italian Renaissance poets. The author suggests their characters are more like "archetypes" (beautiful knights and ladies) compared to Goethe's deeply psychological characters.</note> more easily than **Goethe**; for with the former, much can be achieved with generally beautiful knights and ladies, because the poem itself provides no more; with the latter, it does not.

A "Bänkelsänger" (bench-singer) was a traveling performer who stood on a small bench, sang sensational stories, and pointed to a series of painted illustrations with a stick. It was an early form of mass media.</note>, in that the written supplement is here represented by the living word, and the linguistic impression by rhythm and

Look at the folk surrounding the ballad singer—how they crane their necks, gape with open mouths, and prick up their ears. Neither the singing alone nor the painting alone would hold their attention. Therefore, the combination must surely be beneficial. 

If the common folk are pleased by a crude image on a dirty canvas accompanied by a monotonous song—stemming from an aged, hoarse, or croaking voice in a half-starved figure and based on poor rhyming—then one would think that a beautiful, expressive song, set in a fitting relationship with a sequence of high-quality images, and perfected in every way that <term>ballad singing</term> <note>original: *Bänkelsängerei*; a traditional form of street performance where a singer told stories, often sensational or moralistic, while pointing to illustrations on a board. Only, the poem and the image would have to be designed specifically for mutual completion rather than mere repetition of one another. How tedious a poem can become by describing the appearance of a person or a landscape in every detail; this entire, yet never sufficient, enumeration could be replaced by a finger pointing to the picture. On the other hand, how long must we often gaze back and forth at a painting before our imagination finds the path to understanding; here, it is guided directly and correctly by the narrative, while simultaneously being maintained in the right mood by the song.

That all sounds quite lovely, as everything that can speak in favor of such an art has been gathered here; yet in most people, a feeling might speak against its legitimacy, and this feeling could possibly be right. ## XII. Physiognomic and Instinctive Impressions.

It can happen, and often does, that we find ourselves attracted to or repelled by persons at the very first meeting, before they have done the slightest thing to deserve our affection or justify our dislike; that they are, as the expression goes, "sympathetic" or "antipathetic" to us without our being able to account for why. Women especially are strong in such, so to speak, *a priori* sympathies and antipathies; a face is often a worse crime to them than an action. Meanwhile, their feeling is usually a correct one and often guides them better than reason guides us. Hartmann <note>Eduard von Hartmann (1842–1906), a German philosopher whose "Philosophy of the Unconscious" argued that most human behavior is driven by hidden mental processes.</note> says: the "wisdom of the unconscious" does it. Well, yes—the only question is where it gets this wisdom. I believe, in any case, that it mainly comes from all the experiences we have gathered from youth onward regarding the goodness, love, wickedness, and meanness of people in connection with their appearance and behavior—experiences which are countless, but of which we...

<summary>The author concludes a discussion on combined media arts, arguing for experimentation in aesthetics despite practical hurdles, then introduces "physiognomy"—the theory that our "instinctive" first impressions of people are actually based on a lifetime of subconscious observations.</summary>
<keywords>aesthetics, Art of the Future, Gesamtkunstwerk, physiognomy, intuition, Eduard von Hartmann, the unconscious, first impressions</keywords>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's exploration of why we feel immediate like or dislike for strangers. He argues that these "instinctive" reactions are actually based on forgotten past experiences that our brain associates with new faces.</meta>

...can no longer individually remember—make themselves felt as an **associative result** <note>referring to the mental process where past experiences are subconsciously linked to a new encounter</note> upon seeing a person new to us. This can predispose us more or less decidedly in favor of or against the personalities in question, depending on whether it aligns with a more or less decided direction of our own inclination or aversion.

It has been objected to me that small children, who have had very little experience with people, display the most definite liking or dislike for people who approach them for the first time. However, that same person, from whom a child initially hid in its mother’s lap, often becomes its favorite after only a few hours if the person simply knows how to handle the child. A few biscuits can do much to uproot an "innate antipathy," as one might call it. Generally, small children are like a delicate **balance beam**, easily following the smallest impression in one direction or the other. Furthermore, as little experience as a child may have had, the experiences it *has* had already form a foundation for **associations**. Due to the freshness of the child's mind, these are vividly imprinted and remain effective until countered by opposing experiences. But who has ever observed a child so closely that they could say which associations—favorable or unfavorable—have already formed, which still persist, and which have faded when meeting a new person? Often, it may simply be the person's clothing that displeases the child. Thus, "childish sympathies and antipathies" carry no weight in this question.

The fact that we do not easily become conscious of the **associative mediation** of <term>physiognomic</term> <note>the assessment of a person's character or personality from their outer appearance, especially the face</note> impressions is the reason why people often seek a mystical explanation for them. It is sometimes thought that two people, like two strings, might be tuned harmoniously or disharmoniously toward one another by their very nature. <note>The author is likely referencing the 19th-century "theory of resonance" used to explain spiritual attraction.</note> Thus, they might feel this harmony or disharmony immediately upon seeing each other, without needing any prior experience to facilitate it. I do not wish to say that this is strictly impossible, but it is highly doubtful when compared to the clear, rational explanation provided above; and should such a thing occur...

<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into whether our immediate reactions to people and objects are truly "innate" or simply forgotten associations. Having dismissed the behavior of small children as proof of innate antipathy on the previous page, the author now turns to the broader biological question of instinct versus learned experience.</meta>

...which I leave undecided for now due to the lack of decisive evidence for or against it; however, this does not nullify the previous reasoning, but rather complicates it.

Impressions of this kind, which are not mediated by prior experiences, would have to be categorized as **instinctive**. This leads to the more general question: what is the relationship between instinctive and associative impressions, and to what extent can the former replace the latter? I wish to offer some reflections on this, which at least touch upon aesthetic interests.

The instincts of animals prove, in any case, that many <term>psycho-physical arrangements</term> <note>the author's term for the interconnected systems of the mind and the body's physical responses</note> which a human must first acquire through practice or experience can also be innate. A chick that has only just crawled out of its egg immediately snaps at a spider hanging from a web beside the egg; how did it know that this was something to be eaten? The bee seeks honey in flowers during its very first flight; what leads it straight to the correct hiding place? The sight of the spider or the flowers must, according to an innate mechanism, trigger a similar interplay of sensations and drives as the sight of a tasty fruit triggers in us based on previous experiences, when it immediately makes us want to reach for it.

One can seek the origin of these instinctive arrangements—and thereby try to bring them under a common viewpoint with associatively acquired ones—by suggesting that they were actually acquired by the ancestors of these creatures over the course of their lives or generations, and were merely transplanted onto their descendants through **inheritance**. This aligns essentially with **Darwinian theory** <note>Charles Darwin’s "On the Origin of Species" had been published in 1859, roughly 17 years before this text; the author is engaging with these then-modern ideas about evolution</note> and finds support in the fact that, demonstrably, some instincts of domestic animals have arisen in this way—such as the instincts of the shepherd dog, the dachshund, and the pointer. Against this, one can certainly object that if bees first had to learn that honey is found in flowers, or if spiders had to learn how to spin a web, they would have starved to death long before. This is because chance and the <term>struggle for existence</term> <note>original: "Kampf ums Dasein," the standard German translation for Darwin's "struggle for life"</note>, which would have to take the place of a human teacher, would not feed the animals until they had mastered the skill, as a human teacher would. The fundamental instincts seem, after all, to have a more fundamental basis,

<meta>This page continues the author’s philosophical investigation into the origins of our reactions to the world. ...which does not prevent them from being developed and modified in certain directions through education. Rather, I prefer to imagine—in the spirit of a view I developed in my "Ideas on the History of Creation" <note>This likely refers to *Ideen zu einer Schöpfungsgeschichte der Organismen* (1873) by Gustav Fechner</note>—that the organization of bee and flower, which was once a unified whole or fused within the earthly primal system, split apart during the **differentiation** <note>the process by which a single original form evolves into diverse, specialized parts</note> of this system into specific kingdoms and their members. This occurred in such a way that both remained linked through mutual functional relationships for the sake of preserving the whole and their own living existence. This is certainly not **Haeckelian**, <note>referring to the views of Ernst Haeckel (1834–1919), a famous biologist who promoted a more mechanistic and competitive version of Darwinian evolution</note> to be sure.

However, let us leave the decision on this question for now. The fact of instinctive mechanisms in animals remains regardless, and even humans are not entirely without them. This includes the way a child recognizes the mother's breast—whether seeing it or being placed against it—as an object and means of satisfying an urge; or how they begin to suck on any round object placed in their mouth. Furthermore, while not immediately present at birth, sexual desires awaken later from an innate predisposition, developing naturally upon seeing or touching what can satisfy them.

Accordingly, the task is to consider an **instinctive factor** alongside the direct and associative factors of the impressions objects make on us. That is, we must investigate what in these impressions is influenced by an innate rather than an acquired mechanism alongside the direct ones. Incidentally, there is no such strict conceptual divide between direct and instinctive factors that one could not also explain the pleasure in symmetry as a matter of an instinctive mechanism.

An aesthetic interest in considering instinctive impressions is particularly evident in the question regarding the reasons for **human beauty**. Does human pleasure in the human form depend essentially on an innate mechanism—or one developing naturally from an innate predisposition—or on a mechanism acquired through **associative paths** <note>learning by making mental connections between different experiences or social interactions</note> in social interaction with other people?

In this regard, I believe the following should be considered.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the origins of human attraction and behavior. While, generally speaking, instincts with a very specific direction occur less frequently in humans than in animals, one must nevertheless grant humans an instinctive **sexual drive** and likely also a **social drive**. Since all animals seek their own kind for sexual purposes, and many do so for social ones, and since the instinctive mechanism for this includes the effect of seeing the bodily form, the same may apply to humans in a **state of nature**. <note>The "state of nature" (*Naturzustand*) is a philosophical concept referring to the hypothetical condition of humanity before the development of organized society and culture.</note> Indeed, the pleasure humans take in the human form—as a factor in the attraction people feel for one another—may essentially be an instinctive one.

In the meantime, all purely instinctive impressions and drives in both humans and animals are of a very simple and lower kind. Far more in humans than in animals, the results of instinct are modified over the course of life through interaction with their own kind and external things, and are guided into higher channels. Therefore, among different peoples, this pleasure is attached to very different proportions of the human form; and among **civilized peoples**, the expression of character and of physical and mental talent—which can only be understood through the **pathways of association** <term>Associationswege: the mental process where one idea or sensation calls to mind another based on past experience</term>—determines a pleasure from a higher perspective.

This leads to a question of some interest, which I neither consider settled nor dare to decide myself: namely, whether the expression of the simplest **emotions** <note>original: *Seelenbewegungen*, literally "movements of the soul"</note> in a person's face—joy, pain, affection, anger—finds its interpretation by others only through association as a result of earlier experiences, or in an innate, instinctive way. There is no reason why the smile of the mouth or an angry look should initially reveal more or anything different about a person’s inner spirit than this or that position of the legs and hands. Just try looking angrily for the first time at a child who has never seen an angry look linked to angry actions, and see if they can be frightened by it. In all likelihood, a child who always saw acts of friendliness from others accompanied by an angry look would eventually begin to accompany such acts with an angry look themselves, owing to the innate human urge to imitate, despite an inborn drive to do the opposite.

> »Spon <note>Jacob Spon (1647–1685), a French physician and archaeologist.</note> tells us in his *Recherches d’Antiquité* a strange story relevant to this, concerning the famous physiognomist <term>Campanella: Tommaso Campanella (1568–1639), an Italian philosopher and author</term>. When he had the desire to investigate the inclinations of those with whom he associated, he assumed, as accurately as he could, the face, the gesture, and the entire posture of the persons he was examining. And then he paid close attention to what kind of state of mind he was transported into by this change. In this way, says my author, he was able to penetrate as perfectly into the sentiments and thoughts of the other as if he had been transformed into that very person. I have often experienced this much myself: that when I imitate the expressions and gestures of an angry, gentle, bold, or fearful person, I find in myself a completely involuntary inclination toward the passion whose visible signs I am attempting to mimic.«

Now, if the recognition of a foreign state of soul from its physical expression were a matter of an instinct just as decisive as the expression itself, then imitation would not first be required for more precise recognition. From another side, it must not be overlooked that we are dealing here with complicated states of soul, which would not exclude the possibility that the expressions of the simplest movements of the soul might be understood just as instinctively as they are performed. However, we can leave these questions undecided all the more easily, as they do not intervene very deeply into the field of our aesthetic considerations.

## XIII. Defense of the Direct Factor of Aesthetic Impressions Against the Associative.

### 1) Preliminary Remarks.

The fact that, in the change and conflict of aesthetic views, not only the associative but also the direct factor <term>Direct and Associative Factors: the 'direct factor' refers to the immediate sensory pleasure of an object (like a bright color), whereas the 'associative factor' refers to pleasure derived from what the object represents or reminds us of</term> of aesthetic impressions occasionally suffers an injustice, has been discussed previously in general;

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the discussion of physical mimicry and empathy (the "first" point) to the "second" point: the direct psychological impact of sensory forms. It marks the start of a detailed analysis of how music and visual arts differ in their reliance on direct sensory experience versus associative meaning.</meta>

...and since we have previously sought to uphold the rights and significance of the first <note>The "first point" refers to the associative or empathetic factor discussed on previous pages.</note>, we now wish to do equal justice to the second with the following considerations.

No one doubts that forms, colors, tones, and even the proportions between them—the impression of which already goes beyond the purely sensory—can please or displease us to a greater or lesser degree, regardless of any linked sense, meaning, or purpose, and without any memory of previous external or internal experiences; in short, by virtue of their direct effect. Everyone, setting aside association, prefers a pure, saturated red or blue over a dirty, pale one; the combination of red and blue over yellow and green; a pure, full tone over an impure one or a screech; and a perfectly symmetrical rectangle over a lopsided one. In general, a unified variety <note>original: "Mannichfaltigkeit" (multiplicity or variety). In 19th-century aesthetics, the "unity in variety" principle was central to defining beauty.</note> is preferred over monotony or an irregular tangle of forms. However, where association intervenes, it can just as easily disrupt as enhance the pleasure derived from the direct impression. All of this has been partly tacitly assumed and partly specifically discussed in earlier considerations; however, in the course of this discussion, it has been claimed that while the associative factor plays the primary role in the visual arts <term>Künsten der Sichtbarkeit: Literally "arts of visibility," referring to painting, sculpture, and architecture</term>, in music, this role falls instead to the direct factor.

This latter claim shall now find its detailed explanation and, as far as possible, its justification in demonstrable relationships. Following this, it will be shown that, although the direct factor plays a much more subordinate role in the visual arts than in music, its contribution here is by no means to be disregarded.

### 2) The Direct Factor in Music.

In the impression made by music, all distinguishable elements <note>original: "Momente." Here referring to the constituent parts or specific qualities of the sound, such as pitch, timbre, or rhythm.</note> that enter into it or of which it is composed also play a distinguishable role, insofar as the overall impression changes in a different way with the alteration of any one of them. Language, however, possesses no means to adequately and exhaustively describe all the modifications and variations of the impression, except by specifying the causal elements themselves upon which the impression depends.

The author contrasts this with visual arts, where association and meaning play a more dominant role.</summary>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the direct sensory effects of art, specifically narrowing the focus to music. It builds on the previous page’s distinction between the "associative factor" (meanings and memories) and the "direct impression" (pure sensory experience) by categorizing the elements of music that produce these effects.</meta>

For the sake of clarity, however, we can group the various aspects of musical impression—which depend on changes in tempo, meter, rhythm, direction, and the rising and falling scale of volume and pitch—under the term **musical moods**. On the other hand, we can categorize those effects that depend on the relationships between tones, mediated by **overtones** <term>Obertöne: Naturally occurring frequencies that sound above a fundamental note; 19th-century theorists believed these formed the physical basis for harmony and musical "kinship."</term>, as the perception of melody and harmony. Accordingly, we can briefly distinguish between a *mood element* and a *specific element* of music, insofar as the latter is more unique to music than the former.

The essential effects of music rest upon these two elements—which are essentially *collective elements*. They are independent of the **association of ideas** <note>The theory that our response to art comes from mental links to memories or concepts rather than the object itself.</note>. No matter how many ideas, memories, or resulting thoughts regarding things and circumstances outside of music may attach themselves to these elements, they remain incidental to these essentially musical effects and vary within certain limits even for the same piece of music, depending on accidental circumstances.

These so-called musical moods correspond, at least in part, to those moods that can exist within a person even without the influence of music—such as cheerfulness, seriousness, or even sadness, excitement or calm, strength or gentleness, sublimity or loveliness, and the more or less easy flow of inner movement. Let us call such moods, for lack of a better term, the *life-related moods* of music. Although musical moods are by no means exhausted by this category—for there are many for which one could find no other description than the musical figures or passages themselves upon which they depend—they are nonetheless of particular importance. It is through them that music finds one of its means to enter into a relationship with other arts and with life outside of music. *)

<meta>This page continues the investigation into "musical moods," specifically exploring why certain musical structures (like tempo and rhythm) naturally trigger emotional responses that mirror human physical and vocal expressions. It transitions from general moods to the role of specific associations and the physical basis of melody according to 19th-century science.</meta>

Regarding these **life-related moods** <term>lebensverwandten Stimmungen: Emotional states in music that correspond to those we feel in daily life, such as joy, sadness, or excitement.</term>, one might aptly state as a **principle** <term>Princip</term> that the qualities and relationships of music that evoke such a mood coincide in the most essential **points** <term>Puncten</term> with the active way that same mood is expressed in the human voice and movements, insofar as this is possible given the different mechanics of musical instruments and the human organism. A piece of cheerful music has a different **tempo** <term>Tempo: The speed or pace of a given piece.</term> and a different **rhythm** <term>Rhythmus</term> than a tragic one, and a similar contrast is seen in the way cheerfulness and sadness are naturally expressed through our own voices and gestures.

However, it is by no means necessary to assume that in order for music to put us into a specific mood, we must first remember a previously witnessed expression of that same mood. Rather, the correspondence between the **rhythms of motion** <term>Bewegungsverhältnisse</term>—those created within us by the music and which are generally characteristic of a mood—and those which already stand in a natural relationship with our moods, ensures that the alignment of these moods feels naturally grounded. Since the active expression of our moods is not essentially melodic or harmonic, there is even less reason to make the impression of **melody** <term>Melodie</term> and **harmony** <term>Harmonie</term> in music dependent on a memory of such physical expressions.

There are, however, various kinds of feelings which have a greater degree of definiteness than the aforementioned life-related moods. While those general moods can be common to many different sequences of ideas, these more specific feelings are distinguished by being linked to **associative ideas** <term>Associationsvorstellungen: Mental links where one thought or sensory impression automatically triggers a related memory or concept.</term> of—

---

—whether this is the case may be doubtful; yet it is certainly not necessary to assume it. Indisputably, the direction of the rising and falling tones and the changes in the **pitch scale** <term>Höhenskala</term> have an influence on this, and in the past, melodic relationships themselves were attributed to this. But if the views of **Helmholtz** <note>Hermann von Helmholtz (1821–1894), a pioneering German physicist and physiologist who wrote "On the Sensations of Tone," arguing that the physics of sound explains musical aesthetics.</note> are correct, as they certainly appear to be, it is not the pitch relationships in themselves that create **melody**, but rather the relationships between the **overtones** <term>Obertönen: Subtle higher frequencies that ring out along with a fundamental note; they are the "DNA" of a sound's character.</term> that accompany them. Without these, the **pitch relationships** <term>Höhenbeziehungen</term> simply could not exist.

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s analysis of how music affects the human psyche. Having previously established that certain musical rhythms correspond to our natural physical expressions of joy or sorrow, he now addresses the limits of what music can represent. He engages with the famous aesthetician Eduard Hanslick to distinguish between "general moods" (which music triggers directly) and "specific emotions" (which require conscious thought or association).</meta>

<page-num>164</page-num>
<header>Fechner, Proseminar on Aesthetics.</header>
<sig>11</sig>

...future, past, or lost happiness or unhappiness, or are complicated by relationships of affection and aversion toward others—such as the feelings of hope, fear, longing, melancholy, love, hate, anger, and revenge. In this regard, *Hanslick* *) is undoubtedly quite right when he denies music the ability to evoke or, as they say, to express such feelings with *definitiveness*. It cannot do so because it cannot evoke the characteristic *associative ideas* <term>Associationsvorstellungen: Mental images or concepts that become linked with a stimulus through experience.</term> of these feelings with *definitiveness*. It is different, however, with those general moods <term>Stimmungen</term>. Indeed, no *association* is required to be put in a gentle mood by gentle music, an excited one by lively music, or a tragic one by sad music. A merry melody does not fit a sad song, nor a sad one a merry song. Insofar as associative ideas themselves can carry one character or another, their emergence will certainly be favored by this or that mood—and thus by music of that mood—but the mood itself is not first called forth by the *association*. And since the same character of a mood can be shared by very different series of ideas (for example, sadness can stem from very different causes, which form the content of sad thoughts), it remains, generally speaking, indefinite. It depends only on accidental subjective or objective side-conditions whether music of a specific mood-character carries with it one or another of the series of ideas generally compatible with it.

Furthermore, the musician's sense for the impression of musical relations is not developed and refined by him extracting more and more associative, non-musical meaning from them over time, but rather by his living more and more deeply into the realm of **tonal relationships** <term>Tonbeziehungen: The mathematical and aesthetic connections between different musical notes.</term> themselves, learning to grasp higher and more complex relationships between them that escape a crude, untrained perception. Yet, in this way, they still remain a matter of *direct impression*.

To be sure, the rhythmic movements and relationships of music, the changes and contrasts of strength and weakness, and even the sound of certain tones within it can also [directly] <note>The original German cuts off with "unmit-", likely "unmittelbar" (directly/immediately).</note>...

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s argument regarding the "direct" versus "associative" effects of music. On the previous page, he was discussing how music can sometimes <note>The text begins with the end of the word 'unmittelbar' (immediately/directly) from the previous page.</note> directly remind us of things outside of music.</meta>

<page-num>162</page-num>

...directly remind us of many things outside of music, such as the beating of waves, the roaring of the sea, the trickling of a brook or the rushing of a waterfall, the sighing or howling of the wind, the rolling of thunder, the falling of snowflakes, the gallop of a horse, the beating of birds' wings, the trilling of a lark, the song of a blackbird, and so on; and thus, a coexistence of **associative ideas** <term>Associationsvorstellungen: Mental images or concepts that become linked with a stimulus through experience.</term> of this kind must be granted in music in the same sense as memories of all possible yellow, red, concave, or convex objects may play a part when viewing such objects; yet they play only a secondary role alongside the actual impression itself. And so, one does not see why the primary impression of musical relationships should be attributed first to the imitation of others or to memories of others—why they should not have the right, from the very beginning, to assert their own impression primarily and independently of such memories. The silent play of these memories is not excluded, but it is simply not essential and almost always only runs along in occasional echoes. After all, no music represents the beating of the sea's waves or the gallop of a horse perfectly throughout its entire performance; rather, any memories of such things that the music might awaken are just as easily disturbed, displaced, or destroyed as they are asserted—if they assert themselves at all. However, for specifically musical sensations—which depend on the interlocking melodic and harmonic relationships of tones—there are only highly incomplete analogues in our other spheres of experience, none of which remotely reproduce the magic of music. Why, then, should we point to such analogues to explain this magic as a result of remembering them?

To be sure, one might accept it from the perspective of a very general comparison when **Lotze** <note>Rudolf Hermann Lotze (1817–1881), a German philosopher and logicist who influenced the development of scientific psychology.</note> (*History*, p. 490) <note>original: "Gesch. S. 490"; likely referring to Lotze's *Geschichte der Aesthetik in Deutschland* (1868).</note> says:

> "that the **musical keys** <term>Tonarten</term> represent that infinite capacity for relation, comparability, kinship, and graduated diversity of the world's content in general, through which it can happen that the **multiplicity** <term>Mannichfaltigkeit</term> of reality—which is equally subject to general laws—is at the same time an ordered whole of elements pointing toward one another and merging into one another...

<summary>Fechner argues that while music can mimic natural sounds like wind or waves, these associations are secondary to the unique, direct pleasure derived from musical harmony and melody. He maintains that music’s "magic" is an independent sensory experience rather than a mere memory of non-musical events.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on the philosophy of music. On the previous page, he argued that while music can trigger external associations (like the sound of a waterfall or a horse's gallop), these are secondary to the "primary impression" of the musical relationships themselves. He concludes a sentence here about how musical forms might relate to broader categories of existence.</meta>

<page-num>163</page-num>
<sig>11*</sig>

...or forming mutually exclusive categories;" but I would not likewise wish to agree with **Lotze** <note>Rudolf Hermann Lotze (1817–1881) was a prominent philosopher and physician who argued that music’s beauty comes from its ability to mirror the "rhythms" of life and moral values.</note> that it is the "memory" of these relationships within the **world-content** <term>Weltinhalt: A philosophical term referring to the totality of everything that exists or happens in the world.</term> which makes the figures, rhythms, and relationships of music valuable to us.*) Rather, precisely because music itself offers the most beautiful example of a valuable arrangement, relationship, and gradation of world-content, it requires no memory of something beyond the music to awaken a valuable impression. Nor do I believe that **Mozart** and **Schubert** were the types of people to be guided by memories of the world's course—beyond the world of music itself—when creating their symphonies; indeed, one might ask whether a movement of the spirit within the grand or harmonic relationships of life and thought *outside* of music makes one more productive for such movements *within* music. For in this regard, an antagonistic relationship arises just as easily as a sympathetic one.

Undeniably, a person's entire **spiritual property** <term>Besitzthum: The collective inner "wealth" of a person, including their knowledge, experiences, and cultural upbringing.</term> can be set into vibration by the intervention of music; and depending on whether this property is significant or insignificant, or of one kind or another—which depends on a person's prior **path of education** <term>Bildungsgang: The sequence of development and learning that shapes an individual's character and intellect.</term>—music will be able to manifest more or less significant effects through the vibration or mood into which it places the content of this "property." However, a person can possess relatively little general education and yet receive stronger and higher direct musical impressions, understanding and enjoying music in the truest sense better than the "educated" person, provided they are more practiced in perceiving and following musical relationships and have a greater musical aptitude. This is true despite the fact that the uneducated person can associate very little, while the other can associate much of significance; it is simply that the **byproduct** of the music is more significant for the latter.

The preceding does not prevent us, in a general consideration of the arts, such as must be taken in the **path from above** <term>Gange von Oben: Fechner’s term for a "top-down" approach to aesthetics that starts with abstract philosophical principles, as opposed to his preferred "path from below" which starts with empirical observation.</term>...

---

*) "We do not consider their value to be their own; they appear beautiful by awakening the memory of countless goods that are conceivable within the same rhythm of occurrence, and only within it." <note>original: »Den Werth derselben halten wir für keinen eigenen, sie erscheinen schön, indem sie die Erinnerung der unzähligen Güter erwecken, die in dem gleichen Rhythmus des Geschehens und nur in ihm denkbar sind.«</note> (**History** <note>Referring to Lotze's *Geschichte der Aesthetik in Deutschland* (History of Aesthetics in Germany).</note> p. 487). <meta>This page continues Fechner’s investigation into why music is aesthetically pleasing. He concludes his disagreement with Lotze, arguing that music’s value is inherent in its own structure rather than being a "memory" of external cosmic or moral rhythms. He then introduces his own psychological principles for musical beauty and engages with the theories of Eduard Hanslick.</meta>

<page-num>164</page-num>

is, which we need only approach through our own progress, reminds us of the subordination of musical relationships to more valuable, general relationships of the universe; however, I would not wish to seek the specific effect of music itself in such a memory.

Regarding the rules upon which **musical pleasingness** <term>Wohlgefälligkeit: A technical term in 19th-century aesthetics referring to the quality of being "agreeable" or sensorially pleasing, often used to describe the immediate beauty of a work before deeper meaning is considered.</term> depends, one must refer to specialized musical and technical works. The most fundamental laws in this regard, however, seem to me to still lie in the dark, or at least still lack a sufficient level of definiteness. As mentioned on page 66, I believe that the **Principle of the Unitary Connection of the Manifold** <term>Prinzip der einheitlichen Verknüpfung des Mannichfaltigen: Fechner's core psychological law of aesthetics, which suggests that pleasure arises from finding a single, unifying pattern or "oneness" within a complex variety of parts.</term> plays a major role, to which—regarding the resolution of dissonances—a principle of **aesthetic reconciliation** might also be considered. The relationships of meter and rhythm, the relationships of different fundamental tones regarding the equality and inequality of overtones as well as among each other, and the construction of higher-level relationships over the lower ones in between, provide points of application for the first principle—and indeed, highly varied and changing points of application. The principle seems, so to speak, to unfold and wander within these relationships with pleasure, moving in regions that are all the higher, the further musical development rises. No other field offers a scope of equally favorable conditions in this regard. But it must be admitted that this principle, in the general formulation it has been given thus far, is much too indefinite to serve as the basis for a musical theory executed in detail, or even for a standard measurement of musical pleasingness. The fundamental task in this respect will be difficult to fulfill at all, and I myself renounce the attempt to do so.

**Hanslick** <note>Eduard Hanslick (1825–1904) was a highly influential music critic and aesthetician. His 1854 book, "On the Musically Beautiful," argued that the beauty of music lies purely in its formal sound-structures, not in its ability to represent emotions or external ideas.</note> compares the impression of music in his writing (pp. 32, 33) at one point with that of **arabesques** <note>In 19th-century art, an arabesque is a flowing, decorative line or pattern. Hanslick used this as a metaphor for music to show how "lines" of sound can be beautiful without needing to represent a specific person or story.</note>, and at another with that of a kaleidoscopic figure. Both comparisons are very apt and explanatory up to certain limits, though only up to certain limits. The points of comparison lie in the fact that, firstly, both types of figures act aesthetically—just like musical ones, if one wishes to speak of "figures" in music—without the essential cooperation of association; except that one would have to imagine arabesques as consisting of winding and intertwining lines of unitar-

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s analysis of the "Principle of the Unitary Connection of the Manifold." He is specifically comparing the aesthetic pleasure derived from musical structures to that of visual patterns like arabesques, leading into a technical discussion of acoustics and overtones.</meta>

...of a [purely formal] character without the plant, animal, and human formations that, admittedly, usually accompany them; — secondly, in that for those visible figures, just as with audible ones, the ground of pleasingness can be sought in the principle of the unitary connection of the manifold. But with all that, those visible figures are far from making a musical impression; and this is due to more general differences that deny the visual arts in general the ability to create music. Although the fact that arabesques and kaleidoscopic figures present themselves to the eye as permanent, while the figures of music unfold in time, does not establish an essential difference; for not only can one follow arabesques temporally with eye and attention, but one also finds the temporal progression of music mirrored in the play of changing colors in the **color piano** <term>Farbenklavier: Also known as a "color organ," this refers to various 18th- and 19th-century inventions designed to map musical notes to specific colors to create a "music for the eyes."</term>, and especially in the magnificent spectacle of the **Kalospinthechromokrene** <note>original: "Kalospinthechromokrene". A compound Greek term meaning "beautiful-spark-color-fountain," referring to illuminated water displays or "color fountains" which were popular technological spectacles in the 19th century.</note>. Indeed, one might say that if anything in the realm of visibility approaches the impression of music, it is such a spectacle. Yet this closest approximation is still a very great distance between the two. Wherein does this lie? — The following differences are easy to find:

Every musically useful *tone (sound)* <note>original: "Ton (Klang)"</note> that we hear is composed of a fundamental tone and a series of specifically graduated *overtones*, which differ by **vibrational ratios** <term>Schwingungsverhältnisse: The mathematical relationship between the frequencies of different notes, which determines whether they sound harmonious or dissonant.</term> in simple whole numbers and can be distinguished by directing one's attention up to certain limits\*),

---

\*) This applies even to tones that are produced externally to our ear as simple tones; according to the ear's own mechanism, they all produce within our ear—along with the externally generated fundamental tone—the series of so-called **harmonic tones** <term>harmonische Töne: A sequence of sounds whose frequencies are integer multiples of a fundamental, creating a natural musical scale.</term>, which a struck string provides as overtones along with the fundamental, even if at a lower intensity than when the objective conditions for their creation are present. It may also be, since these harmonic tones constantly accompany ordinary music through the human voice and stringed instruments by virtue of objective production, that association plays a role in calling the internally generated ones more strongly into consciousness even where they are objectively absent. Compare *Helmholtz, Tone Sensation* <note>original: "Tonempfindung", referring to Hermann von Helmholtz's 1863 work "On the Sensations of Tone as a Physiological Basis for the Theory of Music," the foundational text of modern acoustics.</note> *(3rd ed.). 248. 249 and a treatise by J. J. Müller in the Reports of the Saxon Society 1871. 115.* — The conscious separation of overtones from the fundamental tone without the aid of special instruments is, however, only successful

<summary>Fechner distinguishes between visual "music" (like color organs or arabesques) and actual music, arguing that the true difference lies in the physics of sound. He explains that every musical tone is a complex composite of a fundamental tone and integer-ratio overtones.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s comparison between the perception of sound and light. He is arguing that while colors and tones both have "composite" natures, the human mind cannot distinguish individual colors within a mixture the way a trained ear can distinguish overtones within a musical sound.</meta>

through which, as already noted, manifold relationships of equality and inequality, varying in pitch, become possible between the different tones, which are not possible in the same way between the different colors. For there are indeed composite colors just as there are composite tones (sounds), indeed it is very probable *) that even every objectively simple, homogeneous ray of color triggers a mixture of colors—though with a preponderance of a certain color—in the **optic nerve fiber** <term>Opticusfaser: The fibers of the optic nerve that transmit visual information from the retina to the brain.</term> or the bundle of optic nerve fibers it strikes. But the components of a **color mixture** <term>Farbengemisch: The combination of different wavelengths of light perceived as a single hue.</term> are absolutely not separable by attention **), and they remain entirely within the limits of an **octave** <term>Octave: A musical interval where the frequency of the higher note is exactly double that of the lower note.</term> plus a fourth—for the visibility of colors does not extend further than that—whereas even the second **overtone** <term>Oberton: A higher frequency component that sounds along with the fundamental pitch, giving a note its specific character or timbre.</term> exceeds this limit; and generally speaking, they are more analogous to the composition of noises than to the composition of a musical tone (sound). With equivalent means, no music could be made in the realm of sound either, and the previous differences already explain why the spectacle of the **Kalospinthechromokrene** <note>original: "Kalospinthechromokrene". A 19th-century Greek-derived term for a "beautiful-spark-color-fountain," an illuminated water display used for public entertainment.</note> gives us more the impression of a magnificent succession and juxtaposition than of something internally related like melody and harmony. Yet it is questionable whether the points mentioned above hit upon or exhaust the most fundamental differences between tones and colors that matter here; in any case, there are even more profound but as yet insufficiently explained differences. Why, for example, does the sensation of **pitch** <term>Tonhöhe: The perceived highness or lowness of a sound, determined by its frequency.</term> rise continuously with the **vibration frequency** <term>Schwingungszahl: The number of vibrations per second, now measured in Hertz.</term> without a change in character, while in colors, a change of characteristically different impressions—red, yellow, blue—appears, which has nothing in common with differences in the sensation of pitch? <meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on the analogies between sound and light. On the previous page, he argued that while both color and sound are produced by vibrations, the way we perceive mixtures of color (which blend into a single hue) differs fundamentally from how we perceive musical chords or overtones. He now concludes that specific technical questions regarding why certain frequency ratios produce "noise" versus "pure" sensations remain unsolved.</meta>

octave, an unpleasant noise, whereas one should expect a correspondingly pleasant one, as from white light, etc.*) I will not delve further into these as yet unresolved questions.

Since musical impressions are transplanted to the **soul** <term>Seele: In this context, Fechner uses "soul" to refer to the conscious mind or the seat of subjective experience.</term> through the mediation of the nerves, and the external vibrations of music likely evoke corresponding internal nerve vibrations, one has often turned back to these internal nerve movements to explain the psychological effects of music. And why not? Only, one gets no further by doing so than by referring to the external vibrations; for why do these internal vibrations now evoke these psychological effects? That is a question of **inner psychophysics** <term>inneren Psychophysik: The study of the direct relationship between mental states and the internal physical activities of the nervous system.</term>, which, however, has no more definite answer than **outer psychophysics** <term>äussere Psychophysik: The study of the relationship between external physical stimuli (like sound waves) and the resulting mental sensations.</term> has for the question of why—that is, according to what laws—external vibrations have this effect. Should the former ever be able to provide the answer, it could only be on the basis of experiences in outer psychophysics. And it has already been noted several times that aesthetics in general cannot yet comfortably engage with questions of inner psychophysics. The incidental remarks related to this (p. 79) are also only to be accepted as incidental.

Let us set aside all fundamental questions that cannot yet be resolved and return to some points lying closer to the surface, and thus more accessible to discussion, which were previously touched upon rather than discussed, but which have recently occupied the musical world in many ways and have been subject to conflicting views. For the sake of brevity, let us henceforth refer to the life-related **moods** <term>Stimmungen: Generalized emotional states like cheerfulness or sadness, as opposed to specific "feelings" directed at an object.</term> of cheerfulness, seriousness, excitement, gentleness, etc., which music has the power to evoke or—as they say—to express, simply as moods; whereas those feelings of love, longing, etc., which, due to their complication with associations of a specific kind, are determined—

*) A more detailed compilation and discussion of the relationship of kinship and difference between tones and colors can be found in my **Elements of Psychophysics** <note>original: "Elem. d. Psychoph." refers to Fechner's 1860 work, *Elemente der Psychophysik*, specifically Volume II, page 267 and following.</note>.

<summary>Fechner argues that while music affects the mind through nerve vibrations, the internal mechanics of this process (inner psychophysics) are currently as mysterious as the external ones. He moves from abstract theory to the practical categorization of "moods" versus "complex feelings" in music.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s investigation into the psychological effects of music. On the previous page, he touched upon the physical vibrations and how they relate to the internal "inner psychophysics" of the soul. He concludes that sentence here by distinguishing between music's ability to evoke specific "feelings" versus more general "moods."</meta>

...to call forth is not within the power of music, simply as feelings, and let us consider the relationship of music to both somewhat more closely.

A piece of music with a given **mood-character** <term>Stimmungscharakter: The general emotional atmosphere or "vibe" of a musical work.</term> can demonstrate this character in four ways; and when it is said that music provides the expression of a certain mood, one basically understands nothing other than such a demonstration. 

First, by the fact that—assuming the person is receptive to music in general—it places them into the relevant mood if it finds them in an indifferent state. Second, if the person is already in the relevant mood, the music maintains and intensifies it. Third, if the person is in an opposite mood, but it is not too strong, the music overcomes it and puts its own mood-character in its place. Fourth, however, if the opposite mood is too strong, the music does not overcome it, but instead asserts its persistent contradiction with a sense of **displeasure** <term>Unlust: In Fechner’s aesthetics, this refers to a negative sensation or a lack of psychological "harmony."</term>; conversely, if the character of the music coincides with the existing mood, this harmony is felt as beneficial, which also occurs once an opposite mood has been overcome and the corresponding one is now present. In fact, besides the aesthetic effect that music is able to exert regardless of an already existing mood, the harmony or contradiction with the mood itself must be considered an **aesthetic element of effect**.

The basis for what was said above is that cheerful music can gladden a sad person whose grief does not go too deep; however, if the grief goes deeper, the music only awakens discomfort in him and makes him, if possible, withdraw from it. Meanwhile, a piece of music that expresses sadness can appeal to him, despite the fact that its mood-character in itself can only tend toward reinforcing his displeasure and letting him sink even deeper into the ideas that carry this character. But this **moment of displeasure** <term>Unlustmoment: A specific instance or component of negative feeling.</term> is outweighed by the interaction of two **moments of pleasure** <term>Lustmomente: Specific components of positive aesthetic or sensory experience.</term>: the harmony between the character of the stimulation he receives from the music and his existing mood, and the specifically pleasing impression of the music itself, which remains even in a work of a tragic character. The

<summary>Fechner explains how music interacts with a listener's existing mood through four distinct scenarios, arguing that aesthetic pleasure arises either from the music's inherent beauty or its harmony with the listener's internal state.</summary>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how music interacts with the listener's internal state. Having established that music can either reinforce or clash with an existing mood, Fechner now applies this logic to specific emotional states like cheerfulness and sadness, while touching upon the structural debate between strophic repetition and through-composed music.</meta>

One can easily make a corresponding application of this to what is cheerful. A sleepy person—if they are not *too* sleepy—will find themselves enlivened by lively music; however, if they are very sleepy, they will only find the music a disturbance, felt with a sense of **displeasure** <term>Unlust: A foundational concept in Fechner's psychology, referring to a negative sensation or a lack of psychological "harmony."</term>.

The fact that the specifically pleasing impression of music itself is essential to the beneficial effect that sad music can have on a sad person is shown by this: if one removes that pleasing element, the beneficial nature of the impression also disappears. A piece of music with heart-wrenching dissonances might correspond perfectly to someone's mood, yet they will not wish to hear it. <note>Fechner argues that music must remain "beautiful" to be therapeutic; mere mimicry of pain is insufficient.</note> On the other hand, generally speaking, one will prefer to hear cheerful music more often than mourning music—assuming equal musical composition—because the character of the mood is more advantageous there. 

But if one enjoys listening to mourning music even without being in a state of grief, it is (aside from the charm of variety) because once we are placed into a sad mood by the music, we then feel its beneficial influence just as a sad person would. In this way, the preservation of a mood, once initiated by the music, grants the advantage of **harmony** <term>Zusammenstimmung: A state where the musical stimulus and the listener's internal state are in sync.</term> between the subsequent musical cues and the mood already present. 

In this respect, the varied **through-composition** <term>Durchcomposition: A style of song where the music changes for every verse to match the specific words, rather than repeating the same melody for each stanza.</term> of a song is at a disadvantage compared to the repetition of the verses' composition (which is also advantageous in other ways), provided that the dominant **mood-character** <term>Stimmungscharakter: The general emotional atmosphere or "vibe" of a musical work.</term> of the song is well-captured. This does not exclude the possibility, however, that deviations from a fundamental mood—which reconcile as the piece progresses—can be beneficial, and that through-composition possesses advantages of another kind than repetition, which under certain circumstances may outweigh them; however, we will not go into further detail on that here.

Regarding those **feelings** <note>original: "Gefühle"</note> which music lacks the power to evoke with the same distinctness as it does **moods** <note>original: "Stimmungen"</note>, music is nevertheless not entirely indifferent to them. This is because these feelings, while not consisting entirely of moods, still share in their character; depending on which mood-character they appear in, they are then influenced by the music in the manner described previously. However, since the mood-character can change even within the same feeling, it will...

<summary>Fechner explores how music’s beauty must balance its emotional content to be effective, arguing that even sad music must be aesthetically pleasing. He also compares the psychological benefits of repeating a melody in a song versus changing it for every verse.</summary>
