<meta>This page continues Fechner’s investigation into the "associative" and "direct" factors of musical aesthetics. On the previous page, he argued that music's primary power lies in creating a general mood-character (cheerfulness, sadness, etc.) rather than specific, concrete images. Here, he explores the limitations of that expression—why some emotions overlap in music and why others remain fundamentally distinct.</meta>

...the influence of music upon it also changes, just as, for example, the feeling of love can be gentle or fiery, even becoming stormy—that is, rising to the highest degree of excitement—and anger can be quiet or highly agitated. From this it becomes clear once more that one cannot tell from a piece of music with absolute certainty whether it intends to express love or anger, if indeed it intends to express anything at all. <note>Fechner is arguing against the idea that music has a "dictionary" of meanings. Because love and anger can both be "intense," the music for both might sound similar.</note>

Meanwhile, not every feeling can adopt every mood-character with equal ease, and some feelings cannot adopt certain characters at all; for instance, the feeling of hate or fear cannot adopt that of cheerfulness or loveliness; melancholy cannot adopt that of intense excitement. And even if love can admittedly appear in a state of the highest excitement, and anger as a silent grimness, these will nonetheless only be exceptions. Thus, not every piece of music of a specific mood-character will fit every feeling equally well or equally often. Melodies for songs expressing love, hope, longing, or melancholy may be easily mistaken for one another, as they are equally suitable for one feeling as for the other; however, they will not likely be mistaken for melodies of songs expressing anger, hate, revenge, or fury, because the mood-characters of these two types of feelings do not—or only exceptionally—overlap. Furthermore, among feelings with a shifting mood-character, not every character or every modification of it will be aesthetically equally advantageous; thus, a choice can also take place in art from this point of view. In short, through the mediation of musically expressible **moods** <term>Stimmungen: For Fechner, these are general psychological states (like "relaxed" or "tense") that serve as the bridge between the physical sound of music and specific human emotions.</term> that enter into more definite feelings, these feelings also become accessible to a more or less suitable expression through music within certain—though admittedly very indefinite—limits.

In the case of melodies for songs that express a specific feeling of love, longing, and so on, there is also an interest in keeping the character of the music as adequate as possible to its mood-character and thus to the feeling itself; one might then ask whether the character has been missed. In this regard, it is the same with the melody of a song as it is with its **meter** <term>Versmass: The rhythmic structure of a poem. Fechner compares musical character to poetic meter—both provide a structural "container" that fits some emotions better than others.</term>, which can also be interchanged for songs of different content, yet not for *every* kind of content. On the other hand, in independent musical pieces, such as so-

<summary>Fechner explains that while music cannot distinguish between specific emotions like love and anger based on intensity alone, certain "mood-characters" are naturally incompatible (e.g., hate and cheerfulness). He concludes that music expresses general moods that then "color" our specific feelings within certain aesthetic limits.</summary>
<keywords>mood-character, expression of emotion, aesthetics of music, love vs. anger, musical suitability, poetic meter, psychological association</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on the inherent ambiguity of instrumental music compared to vocal music. On the previous page, he argued that because different emotions (like love and anger) can share similar intensities, music alone cannot always distinguish between them. Here, he explores why sonatas and symphonies should be enjoyed for their musical form rather than their supposed emotional labels.</meta>

In sonatas, symphonies, and the like, there is absolutely no interest in asking which feelings they might be appropriate for, or in trying to resolve the unsolvable <term>indeterminacy<gloss>Unbestimmtheit; the quality of being vague or not having a single, fixed meaning</gloss></term> in this regard. These works are not, like the composition of songs, calculated to serve as a support for specific feelings of love, longing, and such. Instead, they are meant to delight through their specifically musical and rhythmic relationships, along with the <term>mood-character<gloss>Stimmungscharakter; the general emotional "atmosphere" of a piece, such as "joyful" or "somber," as opposed to a specific narrative emotion like "reunion with a lost friend"</gloss></term> that is simply attached to them—a character that cannot always be easily or purely traced back to a "life-related" mood produced without music. Trying to guess at such feelings would not only be futile in itself but would also distract from the essentially musical impression. By contrast, in the composition of a <term>song<gloss>Lied; specifically a German art song where music and poetry are combined</gloss></term>, this indeterminacy—and thus the guessing—disappears on its own. This is because, among all the feelings to which the composition could be related, the song explicitly voices the one it *should* be related to and *actually does* relate to, because the feeling is truly expressed and carried along within the song itself.

Similar considerations, partly connected to the previous ones, should be made when asking to what extent music is capable of expressing—and has the right to express—any non-musical <term>event<gloss>Geschehen; an occurrence, happening, or dramatic action</gloss></term>. On page 162, we named various things that music has in common with the world outside of music; indeed, as <note>Hermann Lotze (1817–1881) was a German philosopher and contemporary of Fechner who wrote extensively on the "microcosm" of human experience and aesthetics.</note> Lotze emphasized, music can share even more general and higher things through its rhythm and its relational forms. Insofar as this is the case, music will be able to serve appropriately as an introduction or <term>accompaniment<gloss>Accompagnement; the musical background to a voice or dramatic scene</gloss></term> to the poetic or dramatic representation of an event, or to an event itself outside of music. This serves four perspectives: first, to more effectively highlight the moments or the rhythm of this event (to the extent that these moments truly matter); second, to support the mood that might be attached to it; third, to mediate the unified connection of the specifically musical element with the content of the event; and fourth, through this mediation, to make possible a commonality and mutual intensification of pleasure in both.

<summary>Fechner argues that instrumental music like symphonies should be enjoyed for its internal structure rather than for specific emotional meanings, which only become fixed when music is joined with words in a song. He also outlines how music can effectively accompany dramatic events by reinforcing their rhythm and mood.</summary>
<keywords>instrumental music, symphonies, mood-character, song vs. sonata, Hermann Lotze, musical expression, aesthetics of music, rhythm</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s analysis of the relationship between music and text. While the previous page focused on how vocal music resolves the "indeterminacy" of instrumental music by providing specific lyrics, Fechner now examines the necessary balance between musical beauty and dramatic expression.</meta>

Naturally, it is best when these viewpoints are fulfilled in combination as much as possible; however, the first two—upon which the **characterization** of the music depends—can also come into conflict with the demands of specifically musical **pleasingness**, and in such cases, one must not grant unconditional and exclusive rights to either side. For no matter how "characteristic" music might be, if it did not also include moments of musical pleasingness or allowed these to fade away too much, it would easily bore and tire the listener. This is because music demands our attention just as much as the poem does, and not merely through its relationship to the poem. Conversely, if the music were not to concern itself at all with the content of the poem to which it is explicitly related, but instead followed only its own paths of musical beauty, it would create a fragmented or even contradictory impression when combined with that content.

Indeed, in **opera**, a strong concession is already made to musical beauty at the expense of <term>appropriateness<gloss>Angemessenheit; refers here to dramatic realism—the fact that in real life, people do not sing their conversations</gloss></term> simply by the fact that people sing more than they speak. If the relationship to the content were also to disappear, then, so to speak, everything would come to an end; and in practice, one has never gone to an absolute extreme in this regard. Meanwhile, music must not seek to be entirely consumed by characterization, if only because it *cannot* be entirely consumed by it; its specifically musical element and, in part, even its mood-element, reach beyond characterization and present their own demands that seek satisfaction.

In the main, characterization must always be maintained such that only those moments are brought to prominence which simultaneously correspond to the intended **mood-character**. However, a definite boundary between characterization in this sense and what is commonly rejected as <term>tone-painting<gloss>Tonmalerei; the use of musical sounds to literally imitate external things, like a bird's chirp, a thunderclap, or a spinning wheel</gloss></term> may not be found. The "objectionable" quality of such imitation begins only where either this intended mood-character or the specifically musical pleasingness is no longer satisfied.

In music that appears independently <note>Original: "selbständig auftretender Musik"; meaning instrumental music like symphonies or sonatas that have no accompanying text</note>, naturally no weight is to be placed on characterization in the sense described above. This is because the task here is not at all to represent something existing outside of the music or to support its impression. The

<meta>On page 173, the author continues the philosophical investigation into the nature of "absolute" or independent music, arguing that while listeners often project stories onto music, the music’s true value exists independently of these interpretations.</meta>

This does not exclude the possibility of finding *common characteristics* shared between music and other things; however, one can leave it to *ingenuity* to seek these out and to *imagination* to flesh them out, for the actual meaning of *independent music* <note>The term "independent music" (selbständige Musik) here refers to what we now call "absolute music"—instrumental music that is not intended to tell a specific story or represent an image.</note> is not to be found there. If, as sometimes happens, a musical composition intended for independent performance is explicitly linked by the *composer* to a *poem*, *drama*, or a *historical event* with the claim that it provides a corresponding overall impression, this may indeed be the case regarding its *emotional character* and other shared elements—up to certain *limits*—but only in a very general way. The *primary impression* of the music will depend neither on a *knowledge* of, nor a *guessing* at, its connection to something outside of the music. In any case, it is completely wrong to demand of every piece of music that it should represent something that is not music.

The fact that it can be of a certain interest to provide an *interpretation* beyond the music itself—especially in the case of more significant musical compositions, even those performing independently—is proven by the fact that one encounters such interpretations frequently. Furthermore, the preceding arguments establish the possibility that interpretations of the same composition, even by different people, may agree in their general character and certain main features; yet at the same time, it is certain that these interpretations (provided they are not dependent on one another) will result in very different specific details. The execution of such an interpretation is always added to the music after the fact, without that specific detail hovering before the mind during the enjoyment of the music itself, without that detail being necessary for enjoyment, and without exhausting the musical enjoyment—indeed, without even touching the specifically musical enjoyment which remains the core of the whole.

If one analyzes the preceding interpretation of the meaning of Beethoven’s symphony more closely, one finds that it moves almost entirely within elements of mood related to life. It is only these elements of which the author’s claim holds true—that the effect was »exactly« the same everywhere. Meanwhile, the »dimly dawning shadows of fog,« the »painful gaze to heaven,« the »dark, threatening spirit-figures,« and so on, undoubtedly belong to what he considers »diversity of expression,« since the execution of the mood through possible modes of expression will have taken a different shape for every other interpreter.

However, such instances exist only as exceptional cases; otherwise, nothing would indisputably prevent us from replacing the words "father" and "mother," for example, with a musical <term>fifth and third</term> <note>These are fundamental musical intervals—the distance between two notes—that form the basis of harmony in Western music.</note> or a major and minor chord, and still being understood just as well as we are now. To achieve this, one would only need to consistently show a child their parents from the very beginning while playing a fifth, a third, or one of those chords, rather than using the words "father" and "mother" or "papa" and "mama." Indeed, one could even raise the curious question of whether a musical language might be possible—one that allowed the meaning of a poem to be understood from a song sung purely on the vowel "a," relying entirely on the musical intervals, just as well as we now understand articulated words; thus, the melody of a song would simultaneously provide its meaning. However, upon closer consideration, overwhelming practical difficulties would undoubtedly arise, making it seem pointless to pursue such thoughts further.

Frequently, the impression of vowels has been compared with that of specific colors, and a certain degree of comparability must indeed exist. At the very least, it exists in a negative sense: no one would find the impression of the vowel *u* analogous to that of white or red, or the vowel *i* to that of black or violet, without encountering an equally sharp contradiction when comparing other vowels and colors. If one grants that a comparative relationship exists at all, one might ask whether it is direct or associative. It is likely a combination of both. In that case, one must investigate the common underlying factors upon which this direct comparability rests; however, we do not wish to concern ourselves with that here. Associatively, it is obvious that the primary influence must be the name of the color—and the colored objects—in which the vowel appears. Yet, the interaction of these various factors results in a great deal of uncertainty regarding the color-impression of vowels, as different people express very different views on the matter—insofar as they express anything at all—as the following observations prove.

For myself, *e* creates the very distinct impression of a pale yellow, which I attribute to the fact that *e* appears in the word *gelb* <note>German for "yellow."</note>, and pale yellow is more common than any other shade. But *a* does not give me the impression of black, despite it appearing in the word *schwarz* <note>German for "black."</note>, and it would probably...

### 3) The *direct factor* in the visual arts.

Turning to the visual arts, we encounter an undervaluation of the *direct factor* <term>direct factor: the immediate sensory pleasure derived from a color or shape itself, independent of any meaning or "associative factor."</term> there, which seeks support in considerations of the following kind.

<meta>This page continues Gustav Fechner’s discussion on aesthetics, shifting from the subjective, internal experiences of synesthesia to the objective "associative factor" in art. He explores how the "meaning" or purpose of an object overrides pure formal beauty (the "direct factor").</meta>

...meaning is added, which reaches beyond the direct relationships of form and color. Indeed, objects of minor or secondary aesthetic importance, such as a carpet or a room wall, can achieve **direct pleasingness** through the color and form proportions of their surface, edges, and patterns. However, they prove by this very fact that they cannot be elevated to any higher or independent aesthetic significance, and how small and lowly the aesthetic achievement of these relationships is. Even on such objects, one often likes to see ornaments in plant and animal forms applied, which, through the memory of their **meaning**, help determine the impression associatively. In actual works of art, finally, one can no longer attach any importance to direct pleasingness compared to the higher pleasure that grows out of the connected sense of meaning.

In fact, as pleasing as **symmetry** may appear in a **kaleidoscope**, it is tolerated neither in a landscape nor a historical painting because it does not suit the meaning of the depicted objects; whereas the greatest irregularities, which apart from their meaning might appear only indifferent or even displeasing to us, can arouse interest and become pleasing in works of art through the connected meaning. Likewise, the **coloring** <term>Colorit: the characteristic use of color in a painting</term> of a picture is determined much more by the requirements of the meaning than by the rules of **color harmony**; for however well blue or green may look next to red outside of a picture, one cannot paint a face blue or green to match the red of the cheek.

Most frequently, there is talk of the beautiful pure proportions of a building, or the beautiful forms and proportions of a human figure—that is, in inorganic and organic **architecture** <note>original: "Baukunst." Fechner uses this term to refer to both the construction of buildings and the "design" of living organisms.</note> in general—and nowhere more frequently than here is the pleasure of **dimensional and form proportions** made ruthlessly dependent on the connected meaning. But the tower and temple require different proportions than the palace and the dwelling house; the woman and the child require different ones than the man and the adult; **Jupiter** <note>The Roman king of the gods, representing power and maturity.</note> and **Hercules** <note>The divine hero known for physical strength.</note> different ones than **Apollo** <note>The god of light and music, representing youthful, slender beauty.</note> and **Bacchus** <note>The god of wine, often depicted with softer, more fluid forms.</note>. Everywhere, therefore, the proportions must change according to the purpose of the building material, and according to the gender, age, and character of the individuals, in order to count as pleasing or beautiful. They appear pleasing everywhere only,

<summary>Fechner argues that while simple objects like carpets rely on direct visual harmony, high art is defined by "meaning," where the purpose and identity of a subject (like a god or a building) dictate what proportions and colors are considered beautiful.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Gustav Fechner’s investigation into the "associative factor" of beauty. He argues that in high art, the "direct factor"—the simple, pleasing geometry or color harmony—is secondary to how well those forms serve the "meaning" or "idea" of the work. He is currently critiquing the idea that beauty is merely a matter of pleasing proportions.</meta>

...to the extent that they suit the meaning of the objects, and are beautiful insofar as they appropriately enter into the expression of higher and more appealing ideas. They serve these ideas, not through their own charm—which instead merges into the higher charm or vanishes before it—as can be seen from the fact that they cease to please wherever they cease to fit. However, because they never fit perfectly, they also never appear perfectly pure in works of art of a higher style. Thus, in many religious paintings, one sees an approximate symmetrical *composition*, but never a perfect one. The artist, therefore, must generally abstract away from any consideration <note>original: "Rücksichtsnalime," an OCR error for "Rücksichtnahme"</note> of directly pleasing proportions and only ensure that the proportions of form and color he employs suit the intended meaning, and that the meaning itself is an agreeable one, regardless of whether the proportions used to represent it are pleasing in themselves or not.

Insofar as one forms a concept of the meaning of objects, one can also say: a form only comes under aesthetic consideration to the extent that it fulfills the concept of what it is supposed to represent. Thus **Bötticher** <note>Karl Bötticher (1806–1889), a German archaeologist and architect known for his theories on Greek construction</note> says in his *Tectonics of the Hellenes* <term>Tectonics: the science or art of construction, specifically how the structural parts of a building or body express their function through their form</term>:

> »**Bodily form**, viewed entirely in the abstract, can be neither beautiful nor unbeautiful. The criterion for bodily form is provided by the analogy with the concept of the **essence**, the **function** of the body. In every case, the **most beautiful** form is that which corresponds most consistently and **intimately** to its inner concept, and which represents its essence in its external appearance **ethically** (spiritually and morally) in the truest and most **striking** way. Therefore, if we speak of the development of a form, it can only mean: developing its technical-plastic schema perfectly for its inherent concept.«

Just as these previous considerations—carried out from a one-sided perspective—hardly hit the mark exactly or exhaust the subject, they remain valid insofar as they contradict the opposite one-sidedness. For it will always remain impossible to explain the beauty of visible things solely, or even from a higher viewpoint, through forms and proportions that please regardless of any attached meaning; but they suffer from two **fundamental errors**: first, that the undeniable pleasingness of a lower character, which

<summary>Fechner argues that in high art, formal beauty (like symmetry) must be subordinated to the "meaning" or "concept" of the object. He quotes Karl Bötticher to support the idea that a form is beautiful only when it perfectly expresses its intended function or essence.</summary>
<keywords>Karl Bötticher, Tectonics, formal beauty, associative factor, symmetry in art, religious painting, functionalism, aesthetics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Gustav Fechner’s argument against the idea that formal beauty (proportions and shapes) disappears when a work of art expresses a "higher" idea. He argues for his "aesthetic auxiliary principle," where simple formal pleasure and complex intellectual meaning reinforce one another rather than canceling each other out.</meta>

...that some forms and proportions possess [beauty] in themselves, and that their aesthetic effect disappears through an appropriate entry into higher relationships; for, rather, this effect increases—according to the auxiliary principle—reciprocally with the higher effect. Secondly, [one must reject the idea] that because forms and proportions pleasing in themselves begin to displease us when they do not fit a meaning they are meant to correspond to, or an idea they are meant to represent, they can only contribute to pleasure based on the service they perform for that idea, or through their fittingness to the sense and meaning. On the contrary, they increase this pleasure through their own inherent **pleasure-value** <note>original: "Lustwerth," a central term in Fechner's psychology referring to the quantitative capacity of an object to produce pleasure</note>, and indeed, according to that principle, they increase it more than one would conclude from their individual performance alone.

Indeed, if the **aesthetic auxiliary principle** <note>The "aesthetic auxiliary principle" (ästhetische Hülfsprincip) is Fechner's theory that multiple conditions for pleasure, which may be weak on their own, can produce a disproportionately large total pleasure when they coincide</note> has proven itself everywhere in works of **poetry**, **music**, and even **nature** (p. 50), why should it fail in its validity and soundness regarding works of the **fine arts** and **architecture**. Rather, one may assume that in the field of these arts, forms and proportions—which awaken a pleasure through their own unique character outside of art, even if only a low, slight pleasure or one that is only effective by comparison—will be capable of contributing something effective to the beauty of their works when they enter without contradiction into the purpose and motive of the art. This occurs not merely insofar as they serve the purpose or motive, but also insofar as the purpose or motive makes use of precisely these forms and no others. They must only not contradict the purpose, the motive, the meaning to be asserted, or the sense at hand; because then they do not appear as a condition for pleasure, but as a hindrance to the pleasure that depends on this factor.

Upon closer inspection, however, it is found that such a contradiction occurs more easily and more frequently in works of the fine arts than in works of poetry and, most especially, music. Music is generally not essentially dependent on associations. Consequently, a pure development of directly pleasing proportions is not as easily possible in the fine arts as is **meter** and **rhyme** in poetry, or **time** and **euphony** in music. From this follows, indeed, a more limited applicability—

<vocab><term>aesthetic auxiliary principle</term> (ästhetische Hülfsprincip), <term>pleasure-value</term> (Lustwerth), poetry, fine arts, architecture, music, <term>meter</term> (Versmass), <term>time/measure</term> (Tact), <term>euphony</term> (Wohllaut)</vocab>

<summary>Fechner argues that basic formal beauty and high intellectual meaning reinforce each other through his "auxiliary principle," though he notes that visual arts struggle more than music or poetry to harmonize these two elements.</summary>
<keywords>Gustav Fechner, aesthetic auxiliary principle, pleasure-value, fine arts vs. music, formal beauty, architectural theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on the "aesthetic auxiliary principle," focusing specifically on how formal properties like symmetry function in the visual arts. He transitions from the theoretical grounding of the principle to practical examples in Renaissance masterpieces.</meta>

...[pleasingness] and the more limited importance of directly pleasing forms and proportions in the **fine arts** <note>original: "bildende Kunst," referring to the visual arts like painting and sculpture</note> than in poetry and music; however, this importance is not negligible, as countless cases remain where—instead of a contradiction between the **direct** and **associative factors** <note>Fechner distinguishes between "direct" pleasure (purely formal, like symmetry) and "associative" pleasure (linked to meaning, memory, or ideas)</note> of pleasingness—there exists either full or partial harmony between the two. Within these boundaries, beauty can be enhanced by the pleasingness of the former. Indeed, it is one of the requirements of a so-called **good style** (though style does not depend on this alone) to prefer the more directly pleasing forms and proportions over the less pleasing ones, so long as it is compatible with their **appropriateness to the meaning**; even when the meaning of the work does not strictly demand them.

Thus, in the *Sistine Madonna* and the *Holbein Madonna*, in Leonardo’s *Last Supper*, and in countless other paintings of religious art, one sees **symmetry** in the primary arrangement carried out as far as is compatible with the sense of representing a living scene, without being essentially required by it. One would feel a reduction in this symmetry as a considerable loss of **pleasingness** <note>original: "Wohlgefälligkeit," a core term for Fechner describing the capacity of an object to produce a pleasant sensation</note>. And even in landscapes and genre paintings, where such an extensive implementation of symmetry would contradict the meaning of the scene, painters nonetheless pay attention to a certain balancing of masses, ensuring that the main content does not fall too heavily to one side unless this is specifically required by the meaning itself.

I found a striking violation of this rule interesting in an *Entombment* by **Titian** (in the gallery at Verona), in which all the figures are found huddled together in a clump on the left side of the painting (from the viewer's perspective), which then tapers off toward the right, almost empty side; this creates a very unpleasant impression.

One might find a certain contradiction in the fact that even a slight deviation from symmetry in a **rectangle** displeases us, while the approximation of a symmetrical arrangement in a religious painting pleases us—even though the latter is fundamentally a much greater deviation from symmetry than the deviation in the rectangle that we find so unpleasant. However, it must be considered here that with the not-quite-symmetrical rectangle, we draw a comparison with **full symmetry**; whereas with the not-quite-symmetrical religious painting, we compare it instead with paintings that lack symmetry entirely. Consequently, the former appears to us as a deviation from symmetry (a flaw), while the latter appears as an approach toward symmetry (a gain)—a gain that is, of course, nullified whenever that approximation contradicts the work's appropriateness.

<vocab>Wohlgefälligkeit, bildende Kunst, Symmetrie, Angemessenheit, Madonna, Abendmahl, Tizian, Grablegung</vocab>

<summary>Fechner argues that while formal symmetry is not the primary goal of visual art, it significantly enhances a work's pleasingness when balanced with the painting's meaning. He uses famous Renaissance works to illustrate how symmetry functions as a "gain" when compared to total chaos, even if it is not mathematically perfect.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "aesthetic auxiliary principle" by applying it to coloring. Fechner argues that formal qualities like color harmony provide a "direct" pleasure that should ideally harmonize with the "associative" meaning of the work, reinforcing the overall aesthetic effect.</meta>

Even **coloring** <note>original: "Colorit," referring to the overall use and harmony of color in a painting</note> is in no way determined solely by its **appropriateness to the meaning** <note>original: "Angemessenheit zum Sinne," or how well the colors fit the subject matter</note> in good paintings. Instead, care is taken to ensure the image as a whole is not irregularly blotchy, motley, or held in garish contrasts, nor too inconspicuous or monotonous in its coloring. This is because all of these qualities—quite apart from any meaning—are less pleasing than a certain gradation and variety of tones without harsh transitions, even though strong demands of the meaning can sometimes necessitate exceptions to this. From this **point of view** <note>original: "Gesichtspunct"</note>, one painting makes a more delightful impression than another from a distance, even before we recognize its content or if we abstract away from it. To make this **abstraction** easier and to judge a painting more reliably regarding its mere color effect, some suggest the rule of viewing it upside down.

If a color scheme that is pleasing in itself coincides perfectly with the demands of the meaning, the result of the **auxiliary principle** <note>original: "Hülfsprincip"; Fechner’s theory that multiple factors of pleasure reinforce each other to create a greater total effect than they would individually</note> is a charm of coloring that lends a high **aesthetic value** to a painting. Of course, some artists strive for this even at the expense of the demands of the meaning. Insofar as the proportions of the larger color masses are of primary importance for the total color effect, the colors of garments—where a certain freedom regarding appropriateness exists—are often chosen to produce pleasing color relationships that have nothing essential to do with the meaning of the painting.

In general, one can observe: first, that the **idea, purpose, and meaning**—without prejudice to their essential or main point of view—usually leave considerable leeway in the application of this or that form or proportion. This leeway can be used to advantage by preferring those that are most directly pleasing. Or, what essentially amounts to the same thing: one can often modulate the idea, purpose, or meaning according to subordinate or secondary conditions so that they provide an opportunity for the application of pleasing rather than unpleasing proportions. Second, even if the idea, purpose, and meaning set the higher requirement according to the main points of view (to which the consideration of direct pleasingness must yield), the opposite must not infrequently occur according to subordinate conditions when an important advantage of direct pleas[ingness]... <note>The text cuts off mid-word "Wohlgefälligkeit" (pleasingness)</note>

<summary>Fechner explains how color harmony provides "direct" pleasure independent of a painting's subject, and how artists use the "auxiliary principle" to balance formal beauty with the work's intended meaning.</summary>
<keywords>coloring, aesthetic auxiliary principle, color effect, abstraction, aesthetic value, artistic style, color harmony</keywords>

--- Page 193 ---
<meta>This page continues Fechner’s analysis of the "auxiliary principle," moving from a specific focus on coloring to a broader discussion of how direct aesthetic pleasure (form and color) interacts with associative meaning (the "Idea" or purpose of the work) across different art forms.</meta>

pleasurableness <note>original: "Gefälligkeit"</note> can be purchased at the cost of a slight disadvantage to the **appropriateness to the meaning** or to the sensory gratification. Thus, in a poem, even a less favorable turn of thought must be preferred if the more favorable one simply will not fit the meter and rhyme. Similarly, in architecture, one usually allows the **symmetry** of the side sections of a building to prevail even when they serve different purposes. According to more general principles of art, this situation would rather demand an expression of inner difference through a symbolically or **teleologically** <term>teleological: relating to the design or purpose of something</term> related exterior; however, this does not exclude the possibility that there may also be buildings in which symmetry is set aside entirely in favor of overriding **associative motifs** <note>associative motifs: the meanings, memories, or purposes we "associate" with an object, as opposed to its "direct" visual appeal</note>.

If one cannot deny the important significance of the factor of **direct pleasurableness** <note>original: "directer Wohlgefälligkeit"; pleasure derived from the immediate sensory experience of form or color</note> even in the higher visual arts, this significance nevertheless grows as we descend from sculpture and painting to architecture, and from there to the industrial arts—the so-called technical arts—and ornamentation. In this descent, on the one hand, the **associative factor** itself loses importance relative to the direct factor; on the other hand, conflicts between the direct and associative factors occur less easily. In these artistic fields, visually linked variety gains an increased importance; this includes symmetry, the **golden ratio** <term>golden ratio: a mathematical proportion (roughly 1:1.618) often considered naturally aesthetic</term>, regular patterns, the **wavy line**, the **volute** <term>volute: a spiral, scroll-like ornament common in Ionic columns</term>, the **meander** <term>meander: a decorative border constructed from a continuous line, shaped into a repeated motif</term>, and so on. All of these can more easily be absent in the higher visual arts and, for the reasons stated, usually must be absent, because in those arts, the visual connection is replaced by the associative connection provided by the **Idea** <note>the "Idea" refers to the intellectual or spiritual content of a work of art</note>. Furthermore, brilliance, purity, and saturation of color, as well as pleasing color combinations, play a more important role in the lower visual arts than in the higher ones, which only forgo these lower advantages in order to offer higher ones in their place.

<summary>Fechner explains that while higher arts (painting, poetry) sometimes sacrifice formal perfection for deeper meaning, lower arts (ornamentation, industry) rely heavily on direct sensory pleasures like symmetry and the golden ratio.</summary>
<keywords>aesthetic pleasure, symmetry, golden ratio, architecture, ornamentation, associative motifs, direct pleasurableness, volute, meander, technical arts</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the transition from the "auxiliary principle" discussed on the previous page to a new investigation into "direct" aesthetics. ## XIV. Various Attempts to Establish a Basic Form of Beauty. Experimental Aesthetics. The Golden Ratio and the Square.

### (1) Attempts to establish a normal or basic form of beauty.

Following the reflections made in the previous section, a question of more general interest arises: which proportions of form and color can claim a natural preference of **pleasurableness** <note>original: "Wohlgefälligkeit"; the quality of being pleasing or attractive to the senses</note> over others, regardless of their purpose or meaning—in short, regardless of **association** <note>association: the ideas, memories, or meanings we connect to an object, as opposed to its raw physical appearance</note>—and upon what conditions does this preference depend? The interest in this question (which will be pursued here only in relation to the proportions of forms) has already been sufficiently proven by the fact that many researchers have investigated it from various general or specific viewpoints. However, it must be admitted that, until now, these investigations have not been approached in an accessible way, nor have they yielded accessible results.

Rather, the investigations conducted so far—based on more or less inadequate principles and methods—have mostly led only to a one-sided or exaggerated preference for certain shapes or proportions as universal "normal forms" or standard ratios of beauty. These include the circle, the square, the ellipse, the wavy line, simple rational ratios, and the **Golden Ratio** <note>original: "goldner Schnitt"; a mathematical ratio (approx. 1:1.618) often found in nature and art</note>. To all of these, one can only grant a conditional preference, or a preference within certain limits. It is more important to weigh these limits correctly than to generalize them vaguely. 

In many cases, people believed they could, so to speak, "settle" the beauty of visible objects using such forms alone. They did so without considering the much more important contribution of **association**, either ignoring it entirely or treating it as a secondary matter. They failed to properly separate these two factors <note>The "two factors" are the direct sensory impact of a shape and the learned meanings associated with it</note> during their investigations. Consequently, with the exception of the work by **Zeising** <note>Adolf Zeising (1810–1876), a German psychologist who argued that the Golden Ratio was the universal law of beauty in both nature and art</note>—which, while not beyond reproach, is valuable for its results—all those previous attempts are essentially now only of historical interest.

The circle, in particular, has since antiquity been regarded as the line of perfection and thus of beauty, whereas angles—

<summary>Fechner introduces "Experimental Aesthetics," questioning whether certain geometric forms like the Golden Ratio or circle are inherently beautiful. He critiques previous researchers for ignoring the "associative factor"—the meaning we bring to an object—and focusing too narrowly on pure geometry.</summary>

<keywords>Experimental Aesthetics, Golden Ratio, Form and Proportion, Direct Pleasurableness, Associative Factor, Adolf Zeising, Geometry in Art</keywords>

...man holds the proposition and seeks to justify it: “The line of beauty is elliptical.” — **Hogarth** <note>William Hogarth (1697–1764), an English painter and theorist who famously proposed the "S-curve" or serpentine line as the basis of all beauty in his 1753 book *The Analysis of Beauty*.</note> established the wavy line winding in a single plane and the serpentine line winding in three-dimensional space as the line of beauty and grace, alongside which he also preferred the **pyramidal form**, which is also popular among artists as a form for grouping. — The **square**, and more generally the ratio of **1:1**, has recently been claimed by **Wolff** in his *Contributions to the Aesthetics of Architecture* as the most easily grasped and therefore aesthetically most advantageous ratio of dimension and division. Meanwhile, others—most notably **Heigelin** (*Textbook of Higher Architecture*), **Thiersch** (*Textbook of Aesthetics*), **Hay**, etc.—generally prefer the simple rational ratios of 1:1, 1:2, etc. in this regard, partly because these ratios are consonant as vibrational ratios in music. <term>Zeising</term> <note>Adolf Zeising (1810–1876), a German psychologist and theorist who championed the Golden Ratio as a universal law of nature and art.</note> asserts the **Golden Ratio** <note>original: "goldne Schnittverhältniss"</note> not only as a normal aesthetic ratio, but as the most universal formative ratio in nature and art. He seeks to demonstrate its presence particularly through the structure and subdivision of the human body as well as the most beautiful works of architecture. A few more views, which could be mentioned here only for the sake of curiosity (by **Röber** and **Liharzek**), are discussed in my writing *On Experimental Aesthetics*.

The concept of the **Golden Ratio** <term>Golden Ratio</term>—discussed so extensively by and since <term>Zeising</term>—is based on the fact that the smaller dimension of an object relates to the larger (for example, in a rectangle, the shorter side to the longer) as the larger relates to the sum of both. Or, when dealing with the divisions of an object, that the smaller division relates to the larger as the larger relates to the sum of both, or to the whole. The smaller dimension or division involved in the ratio is called the <term>Minor</term> <note>Latin: "smaller"</note> by Zeising, and the larger the <term>Major</term> <note>Latin: "larger"</note>. If one now investigates what ratio the <term>Minor</term> must have to the <term>Major</term> to satisfy this condition, one finds that it is actually an **irrational ratio** <note>a mathematical value that cannot be expressed as a simple fraction, like Pi (π), where the decimal goes on forever without repeating</note> like that of a circle's circumference to its diameter. However, it can be represented in a rough approximation in whole numbers by **3:5**; nearly enough for the visual eye by **5:8**; and in increasingly closer approximations by **8:13**, **13:21**, and so on. These approximations can be increased at will by setting the larger number of each preceding approximation in ratio with the sum of both, which leads to **21:34**, and so forth. ...exerts an influence by determining the **main form**, the primary relationship of an object, and drawing the attention predominantly away from an indifferent environment and subordinate secondary parts. b) Regarding the influence of the surroundings specifically, in works of art, an isolation is usually artificially achieved through **framing** and, as far as possible, the intentional creation of an indifferent neighborhood; meanwhile, with many other objects, the environment changes randomly, which compensates for the <term>combinatorial influence|The aesthetic effect produced by how different elements or objects are placed together in a composition.</term> as a whole. For since this influence works just as often in a favorable as in an unfavorable sense, the advantage of **direct pleasingness** <note>original: "directe Wohlgefälligkeit"; refers to the immediate sensory appeal of a shape or color independent of any learned associations.</note>—as it exists without combinatorial influence—remains decisive on the whole. c) Since the great influence of combining one form with other forms on pleasingness can neither be denied nor entirely eliminated, but should rather be used as advantageously as possible, the task of investigating the relationships of direct pleasingness is not nullified but rather expanded. It now becomes necessary to determine the influence of these combinations as well; indeed, clarity and success can only be brought to this part of **aesthetics** by investigating what each condition achieves on its own and what results from the combination of each with others. Although the modes of combination are countless, their laws are not; therefore, the investigation must primarily focus on determining these laws. d) The influence of the direct pleasingness of a form is to be regarded as constant through all changes of **associative** and **combinatorial** co-determination. Even when it is surpassed in strength by such factors—whether acting in the same or the opposite direction—it always acts as an additional weight or counterweight. Consequently, the directly more pleasing form always remains at an advantage over the directly less pleasing one, regardless of whether both fit the environment equally well or poorly. The directly less pleasing form must, so to speak, first overcome a difficulty to surpass a more pleasing one by "fitting in" better—a difficulty that may sometimes be too great to overcome. ### 3) Methods of experimental aesthetic research. Example of an applica-
### tion of the Method of Choice. Results especially in relation to
### the golden section and the square.

In the previously mentioned writing on <term>experimental aesthetics<note>A field of study, pioneered by Gustav Fechner, that uses scientific experiments to understand what people find beautiful.</note></term> (p. 602), I establish three methods for application in our field of study, which I distinguish as the **Method of Choice**, the **Method of Production**, and the **Method of Use**.
According to the first, many persons are asked to choose between forms or formal proportions that are to be compared regarding their <term>pleasingness<note>German: "Wohlgefälligkeit"; refers to the direct aesthetic appeal or how "likable" a shape is.</note></term>; according to the second, they are to produce what is most pleasing to their own taste themselves; according to the third, one measures forms or formal proportions as they occur in actual use. All this is done with precautions and considerations to avoid as much as possible the errors designated under section 2, for which I must refer to the writing itself. All three methods must control and verify each other’s results as much as possible. Here I will restrict myself to citing an example of the execution of the **Method of Choice**, with a verification of its results through the **Method of Use**. To orient the reader regarding the specific intent of this investigation, however, a few things must be prefaced.

From the outset, the previously discussed principle of the **unified connection of diversity** <note>Also known as "unity in variety," the idea that beauty comes from a balance of order and complexity.</note> can be established as a very general principle of direct formal pleasingness. Besides symmetry, the other forms and proportions mentioned on page 183—which possess an advantage of direct pleasingness—can be effortlessly subordinated to this principle. In the meantime, as discussed, this generally valid principle leaves room for great indeterminacy in individual cases, and one would not be able to foresee the relative advantage of the pleasingness of various forms from theoretical reasoning <note>original: "a priori"</note> based on it. Let us take, for example, the **square** in comparison with the **rectangle**. In the square, the unified relationship of the parts—through the equality of all sides, all angles, and the equal symmetrical relationship of all sides to the center—is more perfectly realized than in any rectangle, but the diversity is at its lowest. The principle does not allow us to decide whether more is gained in the rectangle through the increased diversity than is lost through the diminished unity. Let us take one [form], according to the...

...a <term>rectangle</term> formed according to the <term>golden section</term> <note>A mathematical ratio (approximately 1:1.618) often found in nature and art, believed by many to be inherently beautiful.</note> in comparison with other <term>rectangles</term>. The former holds an advantage over the latter because it encompasses a higher <term>relationship to unity</term> <note>German: "Einheitsbezug"; the way parts of a shape relate back to a single cohesive whole.</note> than they do; and it may well be suspected that it thereby also gains an advantage in <term>aesthetic appeal</term> <note>German: "Wohlgefälligkeit"; the direct, pleasant feeling a form evokes.</note>, since none of the other conditions for a rectangle's appeal are violated by it. However, since higher relationships to unity are harder to grasp than lower ones, the question arises whether this advantage is significant or even perceptible at all; and ultimately, it must be asked whether, when dividing a length according to the <term>golden section</term>, more is lost than gained by violating the lower but more easily graspable <term>relationship to symmetry</term>.
<margin>.</margin> These questions also cannot be decided by the <term>principle of the unified connection of diversity</term> <note>Also known as "unity in variety," the idea that beauty arises from balancing multiple different elements into one harmonious whole.</note>; and if one believed it possible to decide this through <term>philosophy</term> alone, the uncertainty of that path is proven by the fact that results found to be universally valid through philosophy do not always hold true in <term>experience</term> <note>Empirical, real-world observation.</note>.
Furthermore, it may be asked whether the <term>principle of musically consonant vibration ratios</term> <note>The idea that the same mathematical ratios that make musical notes sound good together (like a perfect fifth) might also make visual shapes look good.</note> might come into play, asserting an advantage for simple rational ratios in a rectangle *), although analogy alone is not sufficient to prove it.
Through aesthetic <term>experiments</term>, however, a reliable decision on these questions has been found, which does not fully coincide with the views of <term>Wolff</term> <note>Christian Wolff (1679–1754), a philosopher who emphasized rational order.</note>, <term>Heigelin</term> <note>Jakob Friedrich Heigelin, an architectural theorist.</note>, or <term>Zeising</term> <note>Adolf Zeising (1810–1876), who claimed the Golden Section was the universal law of beauty.</note>. To summarize the results in advance, they are as follows (though not all, but the greater part, emerge from the investigation to be shared below, while for others, I must refer to the future continuation of the aforementioned writing):
a) Among all rectangular forms, the <term>square</term> and the <term>rectangles</term> closest to it on one hand, and very long rectangles on the other, are the least pleasing.
b) The <term>square</term> itself seems to be surpassed in aesthetic appeal even by the rectangles closest to it, or has at most a doubtful preference over them.

c) The simple rational ratios, which correspond to musical **consonances** <note>Harmonious intervals in music, like an octave or a fifth.</note> as vibration ratios, possess no advantage in **aesthetic appeal** as the side-ratios of a rectangle over musically **dissonant** ratios that can only be expressed in less-simple numbers.

d) The rectangle formed according to the **Golden Section**, along with those rectangles closest to it, does indeed possess an advantage in **aesthetic appeal** over the remaining rectangles.

e) However, even a slight deviation from **symmetry** in any rectangle detracts far more from its appeal than a proportionally much larger deviation from the ratio of the **Golden Section**; and in general, the advantage of the latter is disproportionately less decisive and noticeable than that of symmetry.

f) Regarding the division of a horizontal length (one parallel to the line connecting the eyes), the **Golden Section** ratio stands at a decided disadvantage compared to **equal division**. This provides us with another example of the case noted on pages 63 and 65: that by ascending to a higher **relationship to unity** <note>German: "Einheitsbezug"; the way parts relate to a whole. Here, the Golden Section is seen as a "higher" or more complex type of unity than simple symmetry.</note>, the loss incurred by violating the "lower" relationship cannot always be compensated.

g) Regarding the division of a vertical length (or more generally, one perpendicular to the line connecting the eyes), judging by experiments conducted on crosses, the most advantageous division of the longitudinal bar changes according to the proportions of the transverse bar; however, in the most favorable ratio of the transverse bar to the longitudinal bar, it is not the division according to the **Golden Section** that is most advantageous, but rather the ratio of the shorter to the longer part as 1 : 2.

Consequently, I cannot help but find the aesthetic value of the **Golden Section** to be overestimated by **Zeising** <note>Adolf Zeising (1810–1876), a German philosopher who argued that the Golden Section was the fundamental law of both nature and art.</note>. In saying this, I do not deny the interest and merit of Zeising’s discovery—namely, that this ratio possesses a noteworthy aesthetic value at all; indeed, I expressly see it as a **discovery**. <meta>This page continues the description of an empirical aesthetic experiment, likely by Gustav Theodor Fechner, regarding the mathematical proportions of rectangles and their perceived beauty.</meta>

surface area (= a square with 80 mm sides) but with different aspect ratios—the shortest being a square with a ratio of 1:1, the longest with a ratio of 2:5, and including the <term>Golden Section|A mathematical ratio of approximately 1:1.618, historically considered uniquely pleasing to the eye</term> rectangle at 21:34—were spread out on a black board. In each new trial (with a new participant), they were arranged in a new random order, crisscrossed in various angular positions to one another. Over the course of several years, they were presented to people from the most diverse—though always educated—classes and of the most varied character. No selection was made to choose only those whom one might expect to have "good taste" \*) <note>original: "guter Geschmack"</note>; participants were generally aged 16 and up, as they happened to present themselves for the trials. They were asked which of the various rectangles, when considered as abstractly as possible without a specific use in mind, made the most pleasing impression; often, the question of which was the least favorable was also included. The judgments of preference and rejection were totaled, separated into male and female individuals. The numbers obtained are given in the following table. It should be noted that if a person wavered between two or three rectangles in their preference or rejection, these were recorded as 0.5 or 0.33 each, so that each person ultimately counted as only "1" in a trial; this explains the fractional values in the figures (partly resulting from multiple summations). In this manner, a total of 228 judgments were obtained from male individuals and 119 from females, along with 150 male and 119 female rejection judgments. The square ratio is highlighted with the symbol ▢, and the Golden Section ratio is specially marked with the symbol ☉.

\*) <note>This was done from a triple perspective: first, that judging the taste of others is highly subjective; second, that determining the average degree of pleasingness has its own value regardless of differences in taste; and third, since "bad" taste can deviate from "good" taste in either direction, it is hoped that by averaging many cases without distinguishing taste, one arrives at the same result as if only people of "good taste" had been consulted. By simultaneously paying attention to the judgments of those expected to have particularly good taste, one also gains the opportunity to test whether this presumed agreement actually exists.</note>

Before discussing the results of this table, I will first describe how the participants behaved during the trials.

Most declared from the start that, depending on the application, one or another rectangle might be the most pleasing. I admitted this, but asked further whether they might not nevertheless—apart from all consideration of purpose and meaning—find one of these rectangles more pleasing, satisfying, harmonious, or elegant than the others by virtue of its aspect ratio, and which they would prefer as the average most pleasing form\*). Now, three cases were possible. Either everyone or the great majority would refuse to give a judgment because no difference could be found; or judgments were given that were scattered indifferently between preference and rejection of the various rectangles; or it [the data would show a clear trend].

showed, after totaling all cases, that a certain type of rectangle had such a great preponderance in the number of preferences—and in general, such an order emerged in the sequence of preferences—that this could not be attributed to mere chance.

<margin>*) The making of a preference judgment was made easier if one first had the most unpleasing rectangles sorted out.</margin>

The result was this: Only in very few cases was a judgment completely refused; but also only in a few cases, although there were some, was the judgment very decisive and certain. Mostly, a longer period of hesitation occurred; and even when a participant had already decided on a rectangle, they would sometimes later during the same trial, correcting themselves, prefer another, or remain wavering between two, three, or even four rectangles \*). If the experiment was repeated with the same people at another time, after the impression of the earlier trial had faded (as happened a few times), a different rectangle, adjacent in its proportions to the previous choice, was not infrequently preferred instead of the one chosen before. Despite this uncertainty in individual cases, the table above shows very decisive results on the whole.

Indeed, it will be noted with interest how, starting from the **golden section**, the <term>preference numbers Z</term> <note>original: "Vorzugszahlen Z"</note> decrease in both directions, while the <term>rejection numbers z</term> <note>original: "Verwerfungszahlen z"</note> increase in both directions. This holds true for both male and female individuals, and the percentage ratio of Z to the total number for ☉ <note>The symbol for the Golden Section</note> is almost exactly the same for men and women. This table also suffices to prove the aforementioned propositions a, b, c, d, and e; one must only be careful not to try to extract more from it than it can provide. If one wanted to design a <term>curve of pleasingness</term> <note>original: "Curve der Wohlgefälligkeit"</note> based on such a table, not only would the aspect ratios of the successive rectangles have to be at equal proportional intervals from one another—meaning their **logarithms** would deviate from one another by equal arithmetic differences—but the number of rectangles tested above and below the golden section would also have to be equal. Neither is the case in the table above, but both would deserve to be observed in any future resumption of these experiments.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of experimental results regarding aesthetic preferences for rectangular proportions. It specifically addresses why the "Golden Section" was not the primary focus of earlier musical ratio experiments and analyzes the statistical distribution of "pleasantness" across different shapes, noting a peak at the Golden Section and a curious secondary interest in the square.</meta>

The reason this was not done in the previous trials was that it was initially more important to me to test whether the ratios that are consonant in music really demonstrate the aesthetic advantage so often attributed to them; and also, regarding the preference for the **golden section**, I wanted to exclude any suspicion that its favor depended more on its central position among the rectangles in the experiments than on its own formal excellence. However, based on the results of the table, one could say that the interval of rectangular proportions encompassing the **golden section** ($21 : 34 = 1.6195$, or more precisely $1.6180$), which ranges from $1.558$ to $1.692$, accounts for about $1/3$ (more precisely $35.17\%$, as the average of $34.50$ and $35.83\%$) of the total preference judgments. One must, in fact, imagine the number *Z* <note>representing preference</note> or *z* <note>representing rejection</note> assigned to a rectangle in the table as being distributed across the half-proportional distance (to be determined logarithmically) between its neighboring intervals.

Despite the asymmetry of the series of rectangles on both sides of the **golden section**, the neighboring figures for the golden section are, remarkably, nearly equal for both men and women. I confess that, theoretically, it is not yet clear to me how this could have come about. Furthermore, it is interesting that, so far as the progression of the <term>pleasantness curve</term> <note>original: "Wohlgefälligkeitskurve"; a graphical representation of how much subjects liked a shape</note> can be predicted from the table, the male and female curves coincide at the peak at ☉ <note>The symbol used by the author to denote the Golden Section</note>, but they intersect further along the course, as the female percentages appear first smaller and then larger than the male ones starting from ☉.

The progression of the <term>rejection judgments</term> <note>original: "Verwerfungsurtheile"</note>, by its opposite movement, agrees well with that of the preference judgments; while *Z* is at its maximum at ☉, *z* is zero here. Only in the case of the **square** does a discrepancy appear: while the *Z* values sink more and more toward the square, they rise slightly again at the square itself, which seems to suggest that the square is somewhat more pleasant than its nearest neighbors; whereas the *z* values indicate that the lower maximum of unpleasantness falls precisely on the square itself.

I have reason, however, to consider the latter result more decisive than the former; for the preference for the **square** on the part of some people seems only to depend on the fact that they

In fact, one finds that as long as no counteracting forces arise from such influences, the <term>Golden Section ratio</term> and the closely related rectangles are preferred, whereas long rectangles and the <term>square</term> (along with closely related rectangles) are unpopular. This is evident to the naked eye; furthermore, I have performed many measurements on entire classes of rectangular objects as they occur in <term>everyday commerce</term> <note>original: "Handel und Wandel"; a common German idiom for the world of business, trade, and daily life.</note>, which confirm this, though I will not go into further detail here, simply mentioning the following results in brief.

One only needs to look at the average book covers, **print formats***), writing and stationery formats, cash vouchers, greeting cards, photographic cards, wallets, slate tablets, chocolate and bouillon bars, gingerbread, vanity boxes, snuffboxes, bricks, and others to be immediately reminded of the <term>Golden Section</term> <note>The mathematical ratio of approximately 1:1.618, often considered aesthetically pleasing.</note>—provided one has sufficiently internalized the ratio through observation—and to find, upon measuring individual examples from these classes, that they usually deviate only slightly, now a bit on this side, now on that, from the <term>Golden Section</term>.

Thus, German playing cards are somewhat longer, French ones somewhat shorter than ⊙ <note>The author uses the symbol ⊙ throughout this text to represent the Golden Section ratio.</note>; the octavo print formats of scholarly books are almost always slightly longer, those of children's books slightly shorter than ⊙, while the measurement of 40 novel print formats from a <term>lending library</term> <note>original: "Leihbibliothek"; a commercial precursor to the modern public library where patrons paid a fee to borrow popular fiction.</note> yielded almost exactly the <term>Golden Section</term> on average. Visiting cards, because they must stretch according to the length of the name, are on average somewhat longer; the business cards of merchants and manufacturers, in which several short lines are built upon one another, are somewhat shorter than ⊙. Contrary to expectation, however, gallery paintings measured within the clearance of the frame—of the most varied content, both where width exceeds height and height exceeds width—are on average significantly shorter than ⊙; according to this, the conditions of the content of paintings cannot, on average, be the most advantageous for the retention of this ratio **).

The <term>unpleasantness of the square</term> <note>original: "Ungefälligkeit des □"; the author argues that despite its mathematical simplicity, the square is aesthetically inferior to the Golden Section.</note> is generally proven in its applications by the fact that, despite the theoretical <term>prejudice</term> that exists regarding it (as has been seen), and despite its simple <term>method of construction</term>, it is used only quite exceptionally. One only needs to imagine those objects that were cited as evidence for the <term>superiority of the Golden Section</term> as being square—which would be perfectly permitted by their purpose—to receive the <term>impression of unpleasantness</term> from them. At the same time, practical applications seem to prove that the <term>square</term> is indeed even more unpleasant than the rectangles close to it, as people generally prefer the latter even when the ratio is quite low. In terms of visible dimensions, purely <term>square gallery paintings</term> do occur, though extremely rarely, whereas <term>portrait paintings</term> generally approach the square but are always somewhat taller than they are wide. The <term>print format</term> known as <term>Quarto</term> <note>A book size where the sheets are folded twice to create four leaves; often nearly square but usually slightly rectangular.</note> is generally the rarest for books, yet even it is not purely square but, as one can see for oneself, always slightly taller than wide. What would have prevented a pure square from being preferred if an <term>aesthetic advantage</term> <note>original: "Wohlgefälligkeitsvortheil"</note> were to be gained from it? In <term>chess boxes</term>, <term>sugar bowls</term>, and other somewhat tall boxes, I have often found the surface seen from above to be close to a square, but only *near* it. To be sure, <term>head and seat cushions</term> are almost always found to be purely square; however, this depends on <term>functional considerations</term>, so that material and space are not uselessly wasted on one side by protruding beyond the body part for which they serve as a support.

When <term>Wolff</term> <note>Christian Wolff (1679–1754), an influential Enlightenment philosopher who wrote on the mathematical basis of beauty.</note> and <term>Heigelin</term> <note>Karl Marcell Heigelin (1798–1833), a writer on architectural theory and aesthetics.</note> argue that the square is indeed used in beautiful buildings in <term>floor plans</term> and <term>elevations</term>, it must first be noted in general that <term>architectural objects</term> should only be brought into the discussion of the pure <term>question of aesthetic pleasure</term> very cautiously, because of the interplay of <term>functional considerations</term> and <term>combinatorial considerations</term> <note>The way different parts of a building must fit together structurally or logically.</note> that are rarely absent, without thereby...

<meta>This page begins a new section (Chapter XV) exploring the theoretical relationship between an object's utility and its aesthetic beauty, building on the preceding discussions of formal aesthetics.</meta>

## XV. The Relationship of Purposiveness to Beauty.

That an object, in order to be called beautiful at all, must satisfy the purpose of immediately awakening pleasure is not disputed—whether one bases the concept of beauty itself on the capacity for this performance, as we do, or whether one regards this capacity only as a property of beauty to be made dependent on its essence, while defining the concept itself otherwise. <note>The author is referencing Immanuel Kant’s *Critique of Judgment* (1790), where the definition of beauty is famously debated as "purposiveness without a purpose."</note> Kant called this kind of purposiveness, through which the beautiful adapts itself to the nature of our cognitive faculty, **subjective purposiveness**, to be clearly distinguished from **external purposiveness**, which consists in the property of an object to promote the well-being of humanity, to maintain it, or to prevent disadvantages through its use or the subsequent effects of its existence. The question arises whether this **external purposiveness**—henceforth understood simply as <term>purposiveness (Zweckmässigkeit): the quality of being fit for a specific practical function or goal</term>—is also essential to beauty. Generally speaking, certainly not, since paintings, statues, and musical compositions can appear very beautiful to us without fulfilling any purpose other than the subjective one; on the other hand, many externally very purposive objects, such as farm implements, machines, outbuildings, and manure heaps, appear to us not only not beautiful, but some of them even unpleasing or downright ugly. From which one concludes that even where external purposiveness is found in beautiful objects—as in works of fine architecture and the industrial arts\*), or a human form that is both beautiful and healthy—external purposiveness is to be regarded as accidental to beauty, and that beauty depends on other circumstances. Fine proportions, for instance, make a building,

---

<margin>\*) I use this expression as a summary designation for the art of vessels, tools, furniture, weapons, coats of arms, carpets, and clothing. In the treatise "On Experimental Aesthetics," I used the term **tectonics** for this, which Bötticher used in the same sense in his *Tectonics of the Hellenes* but including architecture, whereas Semper (*On Style*) understands it as merely carpentry, and for the term "industrial arts" in the above sense also has the expression "technical or minor arts." <note>Karl Bötticher and Gottfried Semper were influential 19th-century architectural theorists who debated how construction techniques (tectonics) relate to art.</note></margin>

<summary>This page explores the distinction between "subjective purposiveness" (the ability to please the mind) and "external purposiveness" (practical utility), arguing that while utility is important for many objects, it is not the primary source of beauty.</summary>
<keywords>Aesthetics, Kant, Purposiveness, Subjective Purposiveness, External Purposiveness, Industrial Arts, Tectonics, Architecture, Beauty vs Utility</keywords>

--- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter XV regarding the distinction between "subjective purposiveness" (immediate beauty) and "external purposiveness" (utility). It specifically addresses the counter-argument that practical function can indeed contribute to aesthetic pleasure through the association of ideas.</meta>

...[what makes] a vessel beautiful, it is not that a building is good to live in, or that a vessel can be used well. Kant <note>Immanuel Kant (1724–1804), whose "Critique of Judgment" is a foundation for these aesthetic theories.</note> thinks so, and others think so along with him. One can indeed say: if it is part of an object’s beauty that it pleases immediately, then the fact that pleasing consequences only arise from its use or its effects cannot yet fulfill this condition.

In the meantime, one must not overlook that the external purposiveness of an object can exert its influence through the association of ideas upon seeing it, and thereby contribute to immediate pleasure. This happens from a threefold perspective: first, that the pleasurable effect of the consequence is able to transfer itself in a certain way to the immediate impression of the object—we have, after all, spoken enough about this regarding the <term>Principle of Association: the psychological process where an object triggers thoughts of related experiences or feelings</term>—; second, that the perceived unified harmony of all parts toward the object's purpose will not fail to produce an aesthetic effect here either—the kind that belongs to the unified harmony of the manifold; third, that it pleases us in itself to see a task once set or an idea once conceived met without contradiction, all the more so the greater the danger of contradiction appears.

Therefore, be it a residential house, it will delight us to see immediately that it is built for living; be it a palace, that a higher social status and conduct is appropriately framed within it. But apart from this practical interest, it already pleases us to see all the details of the building linked together without contradiction through a common reference to its purpose, and the intention of the construction thus fulfilled. After all, in a theatrical portrayal of the Devil, it pleases us if everything accords correctly with the "idea" of the Devil, even though the idea of the Devil itself does not please us. In this kind of pleasure, it is not the factual content of the idea that matters; however, it is all the more favorable if the content also pleases us. This is exactly the case when a building corresponds in all its details to the idea of its purpose.

According to this, for all objects that have external purposes, the form must also correspond to those purposes in order to satisfy a <term>cultivated taste (gebildeten Geschmacke): the refined ability to judge beauty, typically associated with education and cultural exposure</term>, partly because otherwise the unpleasant idea is associated that...

<summary>This page explores how the practical utility of an object can enhance its beauty through the "association of ideas," arguing that seeing a design perfectly fulfill its intended purpose creates a sense of satisfying harmony. The author uses the example of a well-designed palace or even a well-characterized stage-devil to show that we enjoy seeing a concept executed without contradiction.</summary>
<keywords>Kant, association of ideas, purposiveness, architecture, aesthetic harmony, cultivated taste, functionalism</keywords>
