<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the utility of an object (its "external purposiveness") contributes to its beauty through the association of ideas. It transitions from arguing that utility is essential for beauty in architecture to addressing why some useful objects, like farm tools, are not necessarily considered beautiful.</meta>

...they do not achieve what they are supposed to achieve for the preservation or promotion of human well-being, partly because the contradiction in which their arrangement stands to their core idea, and the generally related breakdown of the unified harmony of their parts, displeases us.

All objects of architecture and the <term>art industry: a 19th-century term for applied arts or industrial design, such as furniture-making or pottery</term>, however, have external purposes to fulfill. Thus, for all of them, the fulfillment of the conditions of external utility <note>original: "Zweckmässigkeit"</note> is not merely incidental, but essential to beauty.

It is different with objects whose core idea or purpose does not involve external utility at all; the same demands are not placed on such objects. Thus, works of art without any external purpose can certainly gain beauty through their internal relationships or through <term>associative ideas: the psychological process where an object triggers thoughts of related experiences or feelings</term> of a different kind than those of external utility.

But now the question arises: why then do not all externally useful objects appear beautiful? Why, for example, does a broom, a flail, a plow, a dung heap, a barn, or a stable not appear beautiful to us despite all external utility, even though all the conditions for pleasure, which according to the previous points lie in such utility, are present?

Well then: let us imagine these things, instead of being useful, were so unsuitably arranged that we could see their uselessness immediately—would they not then decidedly displease us? Therefore, utility does contribute a pleasing element to their impression, which, however, without further aids or even in conflict with opposing elements, is not always sufficient to push the pleasure above the threshold of positive delight—or to push it so high and keep it so pure—that we would want to apply the term "beautiful" to such works. If the necessary aids are lacking or if too much works against them, the impression of beauty does not occur, or the impression of unpleasantness may even outweigh it in objects that are externally quite useful.

And so it is not to be said at all that works of architecture and the art industry can base their beauty merely on the fulfillment of conditions of external utility; on the contrary, supplementary conditions are still required for this, of which...

<summary>The author argues that while utility is essential for beauty in architecture and design, utility alone does not guarantee beauty; it provides a baseline of pleasure that must be elevated by other aesthetic factors to be called "beautiful."</summary>
<keywords>aesthetics, architecture, utility, purposiveness, industrial arts, association of ideas, beauty vs. usefulness</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the external utility or "purposiveness" of an object is a prerequisite for its beauty. The author is refuting the idea that beauty is something merely "added on" to a functional object, arguing instead that beauty arises from the perfection of that function.</meta>

...will be discussed; for these objects, the fulfillment of purpose <note>original: "Zweckerfüllung"</note> precedes all other conditions as the primary requirement. One or another of those other conditions may be missing or recede into the background, but the apparent <term>purposiveness: "Zweckmässigkeit"—the quality of being perfectly suited to a task or goal</term> must not be absent if beauty is not to be absent as well.

Rumohr <note>Carl Friedrich von Rumohr (1785–1843), a prominent German art historian who emphasized the importance of material and function in art.</note> once said (*Italian Researches* I. 88):
> "After architecture has satisfied necessity and strength, it may also strive for beauty."

It would be more accurate to say:
> "After architecture has satisfied the conditions of external utility, it may also strive to complete the impression of that utility into one of beauty";

for architectural beauty cannot be externally applied onto utility like a superficial addition.

First and foremost, however, an "inner aid" is required. It stands to reason that the contribution of utility to pleasure or beauty can only be recognized by someone who is sufficiently familiar with the conditions of the purpose's fulfillment, so that the feeling of this fulfillment makes itself felt upon immediate impression. With objects we are accustomed to handling, this happens automatically up to a certain point, and it can be assumed that the association has been formed; however, analogy leads us from such objects even beyond our direct experience. Thus, almost anyone, even one who understands nothing of <term>architecture: "Baukunst," literally "the art of building"</term>, enjoys proving their good taste by rejecting columns on a building that have little or nothing to carry—thereby wasting mass unnecessarily—just as they would reject those that have too much to carry and thus threaten collapse. 

A professional architect, however, will notice flaws in a structure at first glance, which can immediately arouse displeasure; these same flaws might not strike an untrained person and therefore do not cause the same displeasure. On the other hand, the architect can find pleasure in a building where everything is finely and correctly balanced for complete fulfillment of purpose—a pleasure denied to someone who understands nothing of the art of building. In the same way, only a horse connoisseur <note>original: "Pferdekenner"</note> can fully appreciate the beauty of a horse, or a soldier the beauty of a weapon—if one wishes to speak of beauty in such objects at all. And so it happens that an expert, in whom the feeling that a...

<summary>This page argues that beauty in functional objects is not a separate layer added to utility, but rather the perfection of utility itself, often requiring specialized knowledge to fully appreciate.</summary>
<keywords>purposiveness, utility, architecture, beauty, Rumohr, expertise, aesthetic judgment, functionalism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into "purposiveness" as a condition of beauty. The previous page established that for objects like architecture, functional success is not just a precursor to beauty but an integral part of it. This page explores why experts and laypeople perceive this beauty differently and how habituation affects our aesthetic appreciation of functional tools.</meta>

...makes the point particularly vividly that an object perfectly corresponds to its purpose, calling beautiful that in which the non-expert finds nothing beautiful; and, in general, anyone who lacks functional insight into the conditions of <term>purposiveness: "Zweckmässigkeit"—the state of being fit for a specific goal or function</term> in an object that claims external utility must humbly acknowledge that they cannot have a certain or sufficient judgment regarding its aesthetic value. Furthermore, such objects are judged by art-laypeople mainly through the transfer of taste from art-experts.

Furthermore: with all objects in general whose purposiveness merely aims to protect us from <term>unpleasure: "Unlust"—a technical term in German aesthetics referring to a feeling of dissatisfaction or discomfort</term> and harm, or to grant us the necessities of food, drink, clothing, and housing, the representation of this purpose can achieve no more than to ward off the associative unpleasure at the sight of these objects, thus preventing them from appearing disagreeable; and with many objects, the immediate use, or the circumstances under which they appear, actually brings about disagreeable rather than agreeable <term>associative representations: "Associationsvorstellungen"—the mental images or memories triggered by looking at an object</term> of effort or uncleanness, which outweigh those of the more distant purpose. Finally, with many objects, a disagreeable direct impression comes into conflict with the agreeable association of the purpose.

In all of this, the element of the unified connection of the <term>manifold: "Mannichfaltigen"—a philosophical term for the many different parts or sensations that make up a single experience</term> through the idea of purpose, as well as the lack of contradiction with that idea, remains intact; however, even for the expert, this alone will not easily suffice to push the impression of immediate pleasure over the threshold. This is because we encounter many purposeful objects daily which satisfy these conditions; thus, the dulling effect of habituation makes itself felt here. Yet, that which no longer stimulates us with pleasure due to habit can still be felt with unpleasure when it is missing.

Let us take a plow. Everyone knows that it serves to work the field, and thus belongs to the distant conditions of satisfying hunger. Should the plow seem to us not to correspond to this purpose, it would displease us, no matter what efforts art made to beautify it; but insofar as it only fulfills this purpose for us—

<summary>This page explains why experts perceive beauty in functional objects that laypeople find plain, while also noting that daily habituation often prevents us from feeling active pleasure in useful things unless they fail us.</summary>
<keywords>purposiveness, aesthetic value, unpleasure, associative representations, habituation, utility vs. beauty, plow example</keywords>

--- Page 218 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between utility and beauty. Having previously established that mere functional success does not always equate to beauty for the average observer, the author now illustrates this through the examples of the plow and everyday clothing, moving toward a theory of how "pleasure" is associated with certain objects.</meta>

it seems, we do not credit it with this fulfillment of duty in a positive feeling of beauty. Furthermore, the association with the heavy labor of plowing will be more immediate than the association with the harvest that it prepares. Finally, the plow has a complex, awkward form that does not conform to any principle of <term>visual unity: "anschaulicher Einheit"—the quality of a shape being easily grasped as a single, harmonious whole</term>. Because of all this, the plow appears ugly rather than beautiful and would disfigure any otherwise elegant <term>manor: "Edelhof"—a noble estate or grand farmstead</term>. However, someone who is well-acquainted with the conditions of the most functional design for a plow could have a decided, positive, and immediate pleasure at the sight of one that actually fulfills those requirements in a new and unusual way.

We see countless people dressed simply, cleanly, and entirely purposefully without finding either positive pleasure or displeasure in it, as the clothing does nothing more than satisfy a need, and we encounter such clothing every day.

Now, however, there are many objects whose purpose goes beyond the mere prevention or removal of <term>unpleasure: "Unlust"—a feeling of discomfort or dissatisfaction</term>; their goal is to promote a state of well-being—a <term>state of pleasure: "Lustzustand"</term>—in a positive way, or to assist in this promotion. The more effectively this association can be awakened, the more it will contribute to the pleasantness or beauty of the object. It also essentially helps if we do not encounter such objects as commonly as those that merely satisfy daily needs. A dwelling-house can look as though it merely grants the necessary protection against the weather, the necessary space, and the necessary light for the daily business of life; but it can also look as though one could live within it comfortably or magnificently. A drinking vessel can look as though it were only made to quench thirst, but also as though it were made for service at a festive banquet. Whenever such associative impressions are awakened, they will be powerful levers for stirring the feeling of beauty. For their part, they can experience support through moments of *direct* pleasantness, such as regularity and a structural division of form that occupies the eye, provided these do not contradict the purpose. And notably, ornaments can support the associative pleasantness of the purpose not only through *direct* pleasantness, but also through their meaningful nature—

# ###

...[the] unity can more clearly emphasize the pleasant character of the purpose. Indeed, the noblest and most <term>functional: "zweckmässig"—fit for a specific purpose</term> works of architecture and the <term>industrial arts: "Kunstindustrie"—applied arts or crafts</term> generally require such aids in order to make an immediate impression of <term>pleasurableness: "Wohlgefälligkeit"</term> or beauty, so as not to appear meager and empty; however, all these aids would be unable to achieve any beauty without the foundation of utility. One could hardly tell by looking at a building or a vessel to which no ornamentation has been applied that it is intended to serve higher pleasures of life. Associative and direct impressions must support one another; however, no matter how much one might wish to apply ornaments to a non-functional work of architecture or craft, one would not be able to banish the impression that the main thing <note>the object's function</note> has been neglected in favor of the incidental <note>the decoration</note>.

From the previous points of view, it is explained why some works of architecture and craft, even while maintaining external utility, can in no way be produced to be as pleasant or beautiful as others—be it because they do not allow space for equally favorable associations, or because one cannot employ the same aids to pleasurableness without coming into conflict with the utility itself or other conditions of beauty. Thus, one might wish to try all the means used to beautify other objects on a cooking pot, and yet one would not be able to make it as pleasant as a wine chalice; indeed, by the attempt to equal the chalice in beauty, one would only diminish the degree of pleasurableness of which the pot is still capable for a truly <term>cultivated taste: "gebildeten Geschmack"</term>.

In fact, the cooking pot—without contradicting its purpose and thereby becoming displeasing via the associative path—cannot take on the same <term>articulated: "gegliedert"—structured with distinct parts</term> form as the chalice, but rather demands a simpler, clunkier rounding. Secondly, in the case of the cooking pot, the unpleasant <term>associative ideas: "Associationsvorstellungen"</term> of the steam and toil of kitchen work and the raw material it must hold are much closer at hand; these determine the impression all the more strongly the more functional the pot is. Meanwhile, the pleasant association of its purpose—contributing to our joys at the table—recedes further into the background, whereas at the sight of a wine—

...chalice, the idea of immediate enjoyment is associated all the more, the more its shape and appropriate decorations remind one of it. This is partly because the application of ornaments would hinder its use (or the use would damage the ornaments), and partly because ornaments—which one might try to relate symbolically to a distant, pleasing purpose rather than its immediate use—would stand in contradiction with the more immediate <term>associative ideas: "Associationsvorstellungen"—the mental images and memories triggered by an object</term> of that use. All of this is perceived as distasteful by a refined taste, and would earn the decorated cooking pot the reprimand: "You are neither a cooking pot nor beautiful."

Thus, the plow also requires a <note>original: "verzwickten"—complex or intricate</note> form for its performance. If one wanted to paint it or carve it beautifully, it would appear to be a thing intended for looking at rather than for plowing, and it would not be handled as freely as an undecorated plow; but to a correct taste, it would already be spoiled in its appearance by this very fact.

## XVI. Commentary on several statements by Schnaase regarding architecture.

The false view—that external <term>functionality: "Zweckmässigkeit"—fitness for a purpose</term> in objects whose definition lies in external functionality merely exists, so to speak, alongside their beauty—frequently leads to false conclusions. One feels the beauty of a building, but one does not grant functionality the credit, or does not trust functionality to have evoked this feeling or even to have contributed to it; instead, one seeks the reason for the immediate pleasure in the building elsewhere. An example of this can be found in the following statements by a highly esteemed <term>connoisseur: "Kunstkenner"—an expert in art</term> <note>This refers to Carl Schnaase (1798–1875), a foundational figure in art history</note>, which may simultaneously provide an opportunity to carry out the general points of view developed in the previous section in several specific directions, and thereby to explain them all the more effectively—

<summary>Fechner explains why functional objects like plows and pots lose their aesthetic integrity when over-decorated and begins a critique of Carl Schnaase's theories on the separation of beauty and utility in architecture.</summary>
<keywords>aesthetics, functionality, Carl Schnaase, ornamentation, associative factor, architecture, domestic objects, fitness for purpose</keywords>

--- Page 221 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter XVI, where Fechner examines architectural aesthetics. He is refuting the theory that beauty in architecture is entirely separate from—or entirely reducible to—functional utility.</meta>

...ers. On the other hand, the previous section has already registered a protest against the idea that we should reduce the entire beauty of a structure to its functionality, and we will return to this point later.

Schnaase says in his <note>original: "niederländischen Briefen"—'Letters from the Netherlands' (1834) by Karl Schnaase, a foundational text in art history</note> while discussing <term>column arrangements: "Säulenstellungen"—the specific positioning and spacing of pillars</term> in temple buildings: "It is not functionality, but beauty that necessitates narrow <term>intercolumniation: "Intercolumnien"—the distance between two adjacent columns</term>, proportional to the thickness of the column shaft... The parts of the building must be harmonious; the column must not contradict the <term>entablature: "Gebälk"—the horizontal structure of moldings and bands supported by the columns</term> too harshly; though standing upright, it must carry a trace of the horizontal within itself, and the individual columns must become a row."

The meaning is this: the entablature runs horizontally; therefore, to prevent the eye from perceiving a displeasing contradiction of forms, the collective group of columns supporting the entablature must also exhibit a horizontal character. This happens when they are placed closely enough together to present a continuous row to the viewer. Conversely, this is no longer the case when they are so far apart that the gaps become conspicuous. We then view each column individually, and the contradiction between their vertical orientation and the horizontal orientation of the entablature emerges starkly and unpleasantly. Whether the columns satisfy the building's purpose by being wide or narrow is irrelevant to our sense of beauty. It does not pay attention to the *purpose* of the *forms*, but rather to the inherent harmony or contradiction among them, which has nothing to do with purpose.

Now, first of all, one must ask: does the eye typically demand that parts which differ so greatly in their significance—such as the "bearer" and the "borne"—accommodate themselves to a similarity of form? Following the same logic, shouldn't a table, in order to be beautiful, have its top supported by a continuous row of legs instead of just four? But to show more directly that Schnaase’s view is incorrect here, one need only change the building material. In stone construction, columns must not stand far apart, because the feeling would immediately arise that they are incapable of supporting the heavy stone load above. If one were to place columns in a wooden structure proportionally just as close, the feeling of something being unnecessary would naturally assert itself. In the former case, we would feel anxious

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on architectural aesthetics, specifically moving from the general principle of column spacing to how the material itself—wood, stone, or iron—dictates our sense of what is beautiful and functional.</meta>

...otherwise, the builder’s over-cautiousness and the lack of functional justification would displease us. Thus, in timber construction, widely spaced, delicately carved, slender columns take the place of the narrow column arrangements used in stone; indeed, columns can often be omitted entirely in timber construction where stone construction would find them indispensable. For instance, nothing is more appealing than the roof of a mountain hut reaching far beyond the outer wall, supported by nothing or only here and there by a single pillar. Now, consider this even more so in iron construction. Any column that appeared slender and towering in stone, shaped according to the purest <term>symmetry: "Ebenmaße"—harmonious proportion or balance</term>, would seem to us in iron to be fat, sluggish, and oppressive—as if it were suffocating, so to speak, in the "fat" of its own mass. The forms of iron construction generally demand to be even more slender than those of timber, and its columns, which transition almost into rods, should stand even further apart in relation to their thickness. Everything about iron aims to show that it is even stronger than it is heavy. It solves tasks with ease that would exhaust wood and stone, or which they would not even dare to attempt. Through casting, it molds itself into all shapes, and thus iron construction is able to climb upward with the lightest and most delicate members. It is capable of this, but our sense of beauty now also demands it of the material.

Of course, we must know the nature of iron, wood, and stone in order to perceive the contribution to a building’s beauty that depends on their appropriate use. However, we know them well enough from daily experience to be able to feel—without lengthy explanation or calculation—whether the given proportions contradict this nature or not; and where our judgment in this regard becomes uncertain, our sense of beauty will also become uncertain.

One might say that a portion of architectural beauty rests on experiment and calculation; for the knowledge of the most functional ratios of mass, form, and dimension rests upon these, and can be acquired in no other way. But a cultivated feeling for architectural beauty grasps the entire result of this with pleasure, and until the feeling is cultivated enough to be capable of this, this part of architectural beauty remains ineffective. The absolutely most functional proportions of all parts are now indisputably for no <note>The text cuts off here and continues on the following page.</note>

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s discussion on the relationship between architectural materials and the viewer’s sense of aesthetics. Having previously discussed how iron and wood demand different proportions than stone, he now explores the internal logic of stone construction and the specific case of Gothic versus Greek architecture.</meta>

...buildings in no particular architectural style have been found, but neither is any sense [of taste] so refined that it would perceive what is still lacking in absolute <term>functional appropriateness: "Zweckmässigkeit"—the quality of being perfectly suited to a purpose</term>; these two things correspond to one another.

In stone construction itself, we demand that the columns stand now closer together, now further apart (in proportion to their thickness and length); we demand this even if we understand nothing of the art of building. Yet if we investigate, we find that here too the correct sense of beauty always coincides with the correct sense of functional appropriateness. It would not please us to see slender Corinthian columns standing as far apart from one another as the stocky Doric ones. The former <note>The Corinthian columns</note> must necessarily stand close together if they are to remain unbroken at all, whereas short and thick columns, if they were to stand closely together, would appear half-idle; they would pointlessly waste material, space, and light. We can clearly see in the Corinthian column that it cannot claim the same <term>load-bearing capacity: "Tragkraft"</term> as the Doric, and we therefore want it to be supported more by its neighbors; meanwhile, we expect the Doric column to perform alone the share of work that it is capable of doing by itself, given its stronger build.

This does not seem to apply to the pillars inside our Gothic churches. They are slender and towering, and yet they stand relatively further apart than any actual columns found on Greek temples (which are more common on the exterior than the interior); why, then, are pillars not spaced just as widely on the outside as they are on the inside? <note>Fechner is noting a contradiction: Gothic pillars are slender like Corinthian columns but spaced widely like iron rods.</note> Schnaase <note>Carl Schnaase (1798–1875), a highly influential German art historian of the period</note> says on this matter (p. 196):

> “Conversely, pillars are unsuitable for the exterior of a building because the viewer's gaze, instead of clinging to a solid form, loses itself in the open, shaded spaces as if in a dark interiority, and would thus receive the image of a morbid, unfinished being.*) In the interior, however, this lack offers decided advantages; for the line of the pillars—precisely because it has so little physical connection and is marked only by separate points, thus being an ideal, mathematical line—reveals itself to us as something dependent, as the mere boundary of the surface.”

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s analysis of architectural aesthetics, specifically contrasting the structural and psychological roles of interior Gothic pillars versus exterior Greek columns. He transitions from discussing the load-bearing capacity of columns to the spatial and symbolic purpose of pillars within a church.</meta>

[...] I suggest the consideration should be framed as follows: the pillars in the interior have, on one hand, a different purpose, and on the other, they exist under different structural conditions than the columns on the exterior. They must stand far apart, because otherwise they would act like a kind of wall, inappropriately dividing into compartments the space that is intended to enclose the <term>congregation: "Gemeine"—the community of worshippers</term> and everything belonging to the <term>divine service: "Gottesdienst"</term> as if in a single, shared vessel. Meanwhile, the closely-spaced columns outside present a kind of grate, forming a partial barrier against the world outside. 

However, these interior pillars can also stand far apart—further than columns of equal slenderness—because, unlike columns, they do not have to carry the <term>load of the entablature: "Oblast des Gebälkes"—the heavy horizontal beams and moldings supported by classical columns</term>, but only have to support a vault. It is actually this vault which, by leaning on the side walls, keeps the roof suspended. Only when the vault falters—fearing that it cannot keep itself stretched entirely by its own strength over the vast space it sees spread beneath it—does it contract in certain places and descend as a pillar, "striking root" in the ground, so to speak. 

Therefore, as a mere means of support, the pillar does not need to fulfill the same conditions of stability that it would have to meet if it were a column carrying that same heavy load. Thus, the pillar steps further away from its neighbor so as not to block the space, which ideally wants to remain entirely free; meanwhile, columns huddle together to safely and easily carry their burden, and simultaneously to form both a gateway and a trellis—depending on the need—for the space they enclose. A correct aesthetic sense, however, feels all of this instinctively, without these ideas needing to be consciously present.

<note>Karl Schnaase (1798–1875) was a highly influential German art historian. Fechner is likely referencing Schnaase’s "Geschichte der bildenden Künste" (History of the Plastic Arts).</note> Schnaase offers yet another reason why columns generally require a narrower placement than pillars, which he attributes to their round and otherwise elaborately crafted form. This form is intended to give the column an appearance of <term>independence: "Selbständigkeit"</term>, which does not actually belong to it as a mere part of a whole. Because of this, the viewer's gaze is easily held fast by the individual column, and one thus runs the risk of losing the overall impression of the entire building—unless the clustering of the columns in their narrow arrangement counterbalances this isolating effect. By contradicting that independence, the close spacing forces the eye to always reflect upon a whole row of columns at once, rather than the individual parts.

<summary>Fechner explains that Gothic pillars stand further apart than Greek columns because they support vaults rather than heavy entablatures, and because interior space must remain open for the congregation. He also cites Schnaase’s theory that close spacing is necessary for columns to prevent their decorative, individual forms from distracting from the building's unity.</summary>

<keywords>architecture, pillars, columns, Gothic vs Greek, Karl Schnaase, vaulting, entablature, aesthetic perception, spatial unity</keywords>

--- Page 225 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the aesthetic and functional differences between architectural supports. The previous page concluded by noting that interior Gothic pillars, unlike exterior Greek columns, serve a more communal and spatial role, rather than just bearing the heavy weight of an entablature.</meta>

Let us observe, on the other hand, that a part of a building can indeed possess a greater degree of independence than another, provided it contributes more significantly to the fulfillment of the building’s overall purpose. Such a part appears less as a subordinate piece and more as a primary element upon which others depend. If such a part captures the eye more intensely, then it deserves to do so; there is no danger that the impression of the whole will suffer. On the contrary, the overall effect is properly achieved when each part asserts itself in the viewer’s <term>perception: "Anschauung"—the way we visually process and understand an object</term> according to its importance to the whole. Accordingly, the column of a Greek temple may indeed draw and hold the eye more than the pillar in a Gothic building because, as previously mentioned, it possesses a truly greater independence. Thus, even ornamentation may contribute to highlighting this significance of the column all the more.

Beauty and <term>utility: "Zweckmässigkeit"—the quality of being well-suited to a purpose</term> go hand in hand, not only regarding the positioning of columns but also their fundamental shape. Why is a column thicker at the bottom than at the top? Because this aids its stability. Why does it swell slightly toward the middle? <note>This refers to the architectural principle of "entasis," a slight convex curve in the shaft of a column used to correct the optical illusion of concavity and to provide structural reinforcement.</note> Because this is the point where it is most prone to buckling, and reinforcing this area provides protection against it. A dancer may hover on the tip of her toe; here, a downward tapering may be just as beautiful as the upward tapering of a column. But the dancer is meant to move, demonstrating the mastery of the soul and life force over gravity. The column, however, is meant to stand and bear weight, demonstrating total subordination to the laws of gravity and the durability of the material.

At first glance, one might find it striking that the legs of chairs and tables—which, like columns, must support a load—are shaped according to the exact opposite principle. Instead of tapering toward the top, they taper toward the bottom. Furthermore, while any significant tilting or curving of a column is to be avoided, chair and table legs (especially the former) often lean slightly outward or even curve outward at the base. Despite all this, they do not appear unpleasing; rather, these proportions are required for them to be aesthetically agreeable. Must we not, in this case, consider Schnaase’s <note>original: "Schnaase's Betrach-"—referring to the art historian Karl Schnaase (1798–1875). The word "Betrach-" is the start of "Betrachtung," meaning "observation" or "consideration."</note> observa-

<summary>This page explores the relationship between architectural form and function, explaining why Greek columns require more visual independence than Gothic pillars and comparing the structural aesthetics of columns to those of human dancers and furniture legs.</summary>
<keywords>aesthetics, architecture, Greek columns, Gothic pillars, stability, entasis, Karl Schnaase, functionalism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into why certain architectural forms are pleasing to the eye, moving from the physical stability of individual columns to the structural logic of entire buildings and furniture, and finally to aesthetic elements that transcend pure utility.</meta>

...help in its own way? <note>This fragment likely completes the word "Betrachtungsweise" or "Nachahmungsweise" (mode of observation/imitation) from the previous page.</note> On the contrary, how can it—how can it explain the pleasure found in such opposite proportions? By contrast, through **considerations of purpose**, the explanation is easily found: the regard for stability is shifted here from the individual legs to the interconnected whole in such a way that the furniture stands as long as the vertical line passing through the center of gravity falls within the base area between the legs; hence the advantage of directing or curving the legs slightly outward. A widened base for each individual leg would be of no help here, but would only make the piece of furniture clumsier, whereas the wide attachment at the top protects the legs from breaking off easily. In the case of columns supporting an <term>entablature: "Gebälk"—the horizontal superstructure of beams and moldings resting on the columns</term>, each must stand much more for itself and satisfy its task independently. Yet the consideration of stability, relating collectively to the entire arrangement, is not entirely absent from the placement of columns in a Greek temple; it simply asserts itself so subtly that the stability of the individual column suffers only imperceptibly from the tilt. Specifically, the outer columns of the temple fronts lean slightly inward toward the inner ones, and thus the column grouping as a whole mimics the individual column to a certain extent. <note>This refers to "inclination," where Greek architects tilted columns slightly inward to create an impression of greater strength and to counteract optical illusions.</note>

But now we return to the fact that not **everything** in a beautiful building is to be derived from functional motives, and its beauty is not to be traced **entirely** to them. The capital, the base, and the <term>fluting: "Cannelirung"—the vertical grooves carved into a column shaft</term> of the columns cannot be derived from external functional motives. Certainly <note>Karl Schnaase (1798–1875) was a pioneering German art historian who argued that art reflects the spirit of its age.</note> Schnaase is right when he maintains—regardless of external functional motives—that the formal mediation between adjacent vertical and horizontal parts, such as the column and the entablature, serves the viewer’s sense of well-being. However, one does not need to expect the columns to stand close together just to prevent a harsh contrast between the columns and the entablature from being felt; instead, one can call upon the capital, which continues the column vertically upward while simultaneously expanding it horizontally in the spirit of the entablature. By replacing the leap into the horizontal direction for each individual column with a pleasing transition, there is no longer any need for an appearance of horizontality for the entire row of columns; in fact, such an appearance would contradict the fact that the entirely different significance of the columns and the—

--- Page 227 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of architectural proportions, specifically how the physical requirements of stability in columns and the entablature interact with our sense of visual beauty. It transitions from the structural logic of the "inclination" of columns to the broader "principle of aesthetic aid."</meta>

<term>entablature: "Gebälk"—the horizontal superstructure of beams and moldings resting on the columns</term> is meant to make a different impression. The thickening of the **column** toward the base, the **swelling** <note>original: "Schwellung"—this refers to "entasis," the slight convex curve in the shaft of a column</note> toward the middle, and the inclination of the columns toward one another—though indeed in keeping with their purpose—are not so strictly required by that purpose that their role in making the column and the entire colonnade appear less monotonous and stiff—or, as it is put, more "alive"—does not seem even more important. One might even think that these features are intended solely for this enlivening effect. But a thickening and swelling of the column at the top instead of the bottom, or an inclination of the columns outward at the bottom instead of the top, would resist monotony and stiffness just as well as the proportions actually maintained, and yet would look abominable, even intolerable. Thus, both factors of pleasure support one another; though little effective on their own, in their harmonious meeting they achieve a significant result according to the "principle of aesthetic aid" <note>A key concept in Gustav Fechner’s aesthetics, suggesting that multiple small factors of pleasure reinforce each other to create a greater whole</note> that we have so often applied.

And thus, the contribution of ornaments, symmetry, the **golden ratio**, and whatever else one believes to find in the way of inherently beautiful proportions in **architecture** toward the beauty of the whole—indeed, the fulfillment of the whole as beauty—should not be denied or diminished by the fact that functionality remains the foundation of **architectural** beauty. Without the existence of this foundation, these aids are of no use, and by violating it, they only cause harm. Indeed, one can concede that functionality may be relaxed in favor of other conditions of beauty where functionality is only distantly or subordinately relevant, such that the disadvantage caused by violating it is not noticeably felt compared to the advantage gained by fulfilling the other conditions. In itself, external functionality implies that no more work, diligence, or cost should be spent on a building than its external purpose requires. But in the crafting of the **capital**, the base, and the **fluting** <note>original: "Cannelirung"—the vertical grooves carved into a column shaft</note> of the columns, more is spent on them. Now, however, these do not directly contradict the external purpose of the building; rather, they only relate to distant functional considerations regarding the manner in which it is built, and there even exists a demand that something beyond mere external purpose be done to elevate...

It explains that while functionality is the foundation of beauty, decorative elements are justified when they enhance the visual "life" of a building without undermining its utility.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "principle of aesthetic aid," transitioning from the structural proportions of columns to the relationship between an object's function and its beauty. It explores why purely functional forms (like a perfect sphere) are modified for both practical use and aesthetic variety.</meta>

...the pleasing nature of the building occurs; thus, any disadvantage that might associatively arise from the perspective of violating external **functionalism** <term>Zweckmässigkeit: the quality of being fit for a purpose or functional</term> is not felt, given the advantage that asserts itself directly through the pleasing nature of those parts.

In the preceding text, I have considered only a few specific parts of a building, from which one can easily apply the same logic to the rest and to the whole. Every object of **industrial art** <term>Kunstindustrie: the artistic design of functional or industrial objects; what we today call "applied arts" or "design"</term> can be subjected to similar considerations. Let us limit ourselves to a few explanations regarding one example.

A vessel generally has the purpose of containing something. Under otherwise identical circumstances—that is, given a specific mass and surface area—it will be able to hold the most if it is **spherical** <note>original: "kugelrund"</note>. If nothing else mattered, and if beauty depended entirely on the external fulfillment of purpose, then a spherical vessel would please us better than any other because we would see this most advantageous fulfillment in it. But a multitude of other functional considerations assert their claims on the form; they stretch, press, and bend the sphere, trim it, or add to it elsewhere, and our sense of beauty not only tolerates all this but demands it. At the same time, besides functionalism, a direct advantage for our pleasure is achieved: a **charm of variety** <note>original: "Reiz der Mannichfaltigkeit"</note> arises in each vessel on its own, and also between different vessels. This variety would be lost if all vessels were spherical, yet it remains unified through the perspective of functionalism.

Looking closer, it must be possible to fill something into the vessel from the top, and it must also be able to release its contents; therefore, we cut off a part of the sphere at the top and either set it aside entirely or, to seal the contents as much as possible, place it back on top as a lid with a knob for lifting it on and off. Furthermore, the vessel should be able to stand firmly, so the sphere sacrifices its lower curve; we flatten it, or at least level it, or give it a foot. A **hollow sphere** with a truncated top and a flattened bottom produces the simplest **bowl** <note>original: "Schaale"</note>. The vessel should also be easy to grasp; therefore, we either add a thin

<summary>This page argues that while a sphere is the most functionally efficient shape for a container, beauty requires modifications—such as adding a base or a lid—that balance utility with visual variety. This illustrates the "principle of aesthetic aid," where functional requirements and aesthetic pleasure reinforce one another.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how the ideal spherical form of a vessel is modified by practical requirements, moving from the basic volume to the specific anatomical features like handles, necks, and spouts. It then transitions into the relationship between these functional requirements and the higher aesthetic demand for beauty and ornamentation.</meta>

...cylindrical part for gripping with the hand between the foot and the body, which we also like to provide with a small bulge at the top or around the middle to fix the hand's position and prevent the vessel from slipping; or we attach handles to the side of the vessel, and sometimes both, depending on the circumstances. Thus, the sphere must often tolerate additions to its sides which, if one ignores their purpose, might appear as disturbing growths—especially in cases like cups, where there is usually only one handle that does not even benefit from symmetry with another. To make filling easier, the edges of the mouth are bent outward; to facilitate pouring, they are narrowed in places into a spout <note>original: "Schnabel," literally "beak"</note>; and to maintain the vessel's containing power as much as possible while facilitating filling and pouring, a neck-like constriction is placed between the mouth and the belly, wherever these functional considerations apply.

While the spherical shape is thus often completely destroyed in the vertical direction, the circular cross-section remains in every horizontal direction of the vessel, because all secondary purposes exert their influence only in that vertical direction—at least in most vessels. Yet even all-around symmetry must give way where purpose demands it; hence the one-sided spout, positioned opposite the handle, on vessels primarily intended to pour something out.

In all this, I have essentially had only vessels for liquids in mind. In the case of boxes, cases, chests, and trunks, the shape of what they are meant to contain generally resists the use of curved surfaces for the walls, or the construction from wooden planks naturally brings the rectangular shape with it.

But now, even in the case of vessels and the like, just as with buildings, not everything is to be attributed to **functionalism** <term>Zweckmässigkeit: the quality of being fit for a particular purpose or utility</term>. To increase our delight to the point where we begin to speak of **beauty** <term>Schönheit</term>, vessels—just like buildings—require the help of ornamentation and a direct **formal pleasurableness** <term>Formwohlgefälligkeit: the inherent beauty of a shape, independent of its use</term> (that is, pleasure independent of any associative ideas), insofar as such beauty is compatible with its function. It is true that, as noted above, functionalism itself appears as a unifying link for the **diversity** <term>Mannichfaltigkeit: the variety of different parts or elements within a single object</term> of every vessel; yet the visually unified connection must also be maintained to the extent that...

<meta>This page continues the transition from the functional requirements of vessels (such as handles and spouts) to the application of experimental aesthetics in the industrial arts. The author argues that because pure theory cannot determine the most pleasing form of a functional object, practical experimentation through his "Method of Choice" should be used to find the most successful designs.</meta>

...than is compatible with that highest condition, and in this regard certain forms and certain curves may be more advantageous than others. Since the principle mainly under consideration here—the unified connection of the manifold <note>A classic aesthetic principle suggesting that beauty arises when diverse parts are bound into a coherent whole</note>—lacks sufficient determinacy in itself, and must here also be considered as being co-determined by the purpose (which is modified differently for every other vessel), the aesthetic experiment might be profitably consulted to determine what is more or less advantageous in this field, for which indeed no apriorism <note>Apriorismus: reasoning based on theoretical principles rather than observation or experience</note> suffices. In the trials using the Method of Choice <note>A psychological testing method where subjects choose their favorite from a set of options</note> with 10 rectangles, varied according to their aspect ratio, one was preferred over all others in a certain proportion according to the table on page 195. Let us suppose instead that an artist produced 10 models of a cup, varied from some point of view, and then applied the Method of Choice in a corresponding manner; he could then count on selling the most frequently preferred cup shape most often, and at the same time perhaps provide a useful starting point for several theoretical considerations. The points of view from which the shape of a cup can be varied are, of course, far more numerous than those to which the side ratios of a rectangle are subject; but since a certain main form for cups for a given use is usually already established, the variation of perspectives remaining for further modification will naturally limit itself.

As is easy to see, the previous remarks can be transferred from the cup to any object of the art industry <term>Kunstindustrie: the production of decorative or functional objects that combine artistic design with industrial manufacturing or craftsmanship</term>. Indeed, the artist would generally have it easier when applying the Method of Choice to such an object than I had in my private experiments with so-called abstract rectangles, because he would only need to involve all the customers who buy anything from him at all as experimental subjects for this purpose. Thus, he would have no shortage of test subjects, and the choice of preference <note>Vorzugswahl</note> between concrete objects of a specific application is easier than between simple forms abstracted from such application. At the same time, he would achieve the practical advantage of getting to know the most appealing form specifically for the taste of those who form his customer base. Whether he would, of course, encounter the same turning up of noses <note>original: "Nasenrümpfen," a German idiom for dismissive contempt or sneering</note> from his colleagues as I have encountered from my aesthetic colleagues regarding aesthetic experiments, is something I would not like to vouch for.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on aesthetic principles, transitioning from the physical design of functional objects (like vessels) to the psychological mechanisms behind wit, humor, and intellectual pleasure. ## XVII. On Ingenious and Witty Comparisons, Puns, and Other Cases Bearing the Character of Delight, Merriment, and Ridiculousness.

In my opinion, in the field we are considering here, the **principle of the unified connection of the manifold** <note>A fundamental aesthetic theory suggesting that beauty and pleasure arise when a single idea or pattern links many diverse or "manifold" elements</note> plays the leading role. However, it still requires supporting secondary conditions to push the pleasure that these cases can provide—with its unique character—over the **threshold** <note>In 19th-century psychology, the "threshold" is the point at which a stimulus becomes strong enough to be consciously felt or noticed</note>.

Perhaps the most obvious illustration of this is found in ingenious and witty **comparisons** and **puns**. In the former, pleasure relies on our discovering, in a single quick glance, a unified conceptual viewpoint between things that are otherwise very different. In the latter, this unified viewpoint is mediated through the same or similar-sounding words. Indeed, comparisons and puns give us greater pleasure—and we find them more easily amusing or even ridiculous—the more striking and easily understood the unified connection is on one hand, the greater the difference or apparent contradiction it resolves on the other, and thirdly, the more unusual, unexpected, surprising, or remote the method of connection is. Meanwhile, the aesthetic effect of common or obvious connections falls below the threshold entirely.

Indeed, in the aesthetic effect of these "games," the principle of the threshold and the principle of **desensitization** <note>original: "Abstumpfung" — the process where repeated exposure to a stimulus reduces its emotional impact</note> toward familiar stimuli must be significantly taken into account. However, these principles are merely co-determinants, while the core of the effect lies, so to speak, in the principle mentioned above.

Aside from these co-factors, the effect of the principle can also be helped or hindered by the factual nature of the content itself, which may lean more toward the side of pleasure or displeasure. In any case, the purely formal effect of the principle appears most clearly when the content is neutral or indifferent.

Countless similarities encounter us daily but do not affect us because of their familiarity; they do not capture our atten—

<meta>This page continues the discussion on aesthetic principles, specifically how the "principle of the unified connection of the manifold" applies to wit. The opening fragment "keit" likely completes "Gewöhnlichkeit" (commonality) or "Verständlichkeit" (intelligibility), referring to the idea that common connections do not rise to the level of aesthetic pleasure.</meta>

...ility does not. In poetry, one is quite willing—even preferring it to the use of direct descriptions—to find a beautiful girl described as a blooming rose, a brave man as a lion, or a cruel one as a tiger. To be sure, such comparisons no longer truly interest us; we have become dull to them because of their frequent repetition. 

However, when Jean Paul <note>Jean Paul Richter (1763–1825) was a famous German Romantic writer celebrated for his humorous, complex, and highly metaphorical prose style.</note> calls the moon a "swan of the heavens," this comparison—though still quite obvious and not particularly funny—interests us more than the previous ones, as it does not share their commonality. Furthermore, our pleasure in it is strengthened because the graceful image appeals to us on its own merits. 

But if Jean Paul, elsewhere, calls the moon—in its waxing or waning phase—a "bitten poppy-seed oil cake," <note>original: *angebissenen Mohnölkuchen*. This is a multi-layered pun. In German, "Mohn" (poppy) sounds similar to "Mond" (moon). The cake's shape resembles a crescent moon, and the poppy seeds suggest sleep, creating a "unified connection" between the sky and a snack.</note> considering both its shape and the fact that the moon in the sky and poppy juice share a common relationship to sleep, then this far-fetched comparison between such heterogeneous objects seems funnier to us than all the previous ones. Even if it is less accurate in some respects, it would not please us at all if it did not fit in some way; for being far-fetched is not enough on its own—it only increases our receptivity. If someone were to say, for example, "the moon is a fox" or "a piece of bread," one would find that not funny, but merely absurd, because a mediating concept is entirely lacking.

If we consider <term>Wortspiele</term> <note>Wordplay or puns</note>, it does not affect us aesthetically in itself that a word occurs with different meanings and is listed as such in the dictionary. We know that it only asserts these various meanings in different contexts, and we instinctively categorize it accordingly. On the other hand, we find it delightful when the actual use of the same or a similar word or phrase brings to light a common "middle-concept" for the different meanings, whereby the shared use of the word is justified in an unexpected way. For example:

Someone said regarding a dancer who, for a salary of 4,000 Thalers, mainly danced the roles of elves in *Oberon* <note>A popular romantic opera by Carl Maria von Weber, first performed in 1826.</note> and elsewhere: "2,000 Thalers for each leg—that is expensive ivory." <note>A pun on the German word for ivory, *Elfenbein*, which is a compound of *Elfen* (elves) and *Bein* (leg/bone). The dancer plays an elf, and her legs are expensive.</note> — Saphir <note>Moritz Gottlieb Saphir (1795–1858) was a famous Austrian-Jewish humorist and critic known for his quick wit and puns.</note> had borrowed from a banker he knew 300 guilders—

...lent it to him. When he visited him after some time, the latter said: "Oh, you have come for the 300 gulden." <note>The humor here relies on the German phrase "kommen um," which can mean "to come for [to collect]" or "to be deprived of [to lose]."</note> "No," *Saphir* <note>Moritz Gottlieb Saphir (1795–1858) was a famous Austrian satirist and journalist known for his lightning-fast wit.</note> replied, "you have come to lose the 300 gulden." At a large family gathering I attended, after the main guests of honor had been sufficiently toasted, someone also proposed a toast to the uncles present in the assembly; immediately an Englishman stood up—who, although he had lived in Germany for years, still expressed himself awkwardly in the German language, yet nevertheless excelled at German wordplay—and offered a reproachful counter-toast: for his part, he let the uncles live with nieces. <note>original: *mit Nichten*. This is a pun on the German word *mitnichten*, which means "by no means" or "not at all." By saying he toasted them "with nieces," he was phonetically saying he toasted them "not at all."</note>

In the first example, it is the concept of dearness <note>original: *Theurung*. This refers to an example likely from the previous page where "dear" means both "expensive" and "beloved."</note>; in the second, the business of the 300 gulden; and in the third, the toast to the members of the party, which provides the unified mediation between the different meanings.

It is a brief, so to speak, quickly flickering and fading pleasure that an ingenious or witty comparison or a similar pun grants us. This is because it is a small circle of ideas in which we move, and to attempt to repeat the same movement often would immediately summon the unpleasantness of monotony. Yet this brief pleasure can be more intense than the longer and overall more substantial pleasure we gain from a context that allows us to follow a unified relationship through a larger series of diverse moments, but without ever heightening our attention so intensely or creating such a strong momentary tension as occurs in such wordplay through the unexpectedly appearing unified mediation between two seemingly entirely heterogeneous ideas or spheres of thought.

Many comparisons please us; we find them ingenious without finding them funny or even ridiculous, such as the following by *Jean Paul*: "Great pains make us insensitive to small ones, just as a waterfall provides cover against the rain." — "The happiness of life, like the day, consists not of individual flashes of lightning, but of a constant, quiet serenity." — It is easy to tell oneself that the humor of these and similar comparisons is prevented, from a certain perspective, by the seriousness of their content, which invites reflection; but no less—

<meta>This page continues the psychological analysis of wit, specifically exploring why certain comparisons create a sense of mirth or "Lustigkeit." It builds on the previous discussion of how unified connections between disparate ideas generate aesthetic pleasure.</meta>

...this contributes to the fact that the pleasure here depends predominantly on the aptness of the comparison, rather than on the contrast between the things being compared. For although one can find the physical and the spiritual—which were compared in the previous section—to be very contrasting, we are so accustomed to viewing the physical as a symbol of the spiritual and comparing the latter with the former, that in those previous comparisons, the point of equality actually strikes us as more novel than the point of difference. This, however, leads to the question: what, in general, does the specific character of **mirth** <term>Lustigkeit; the quality of being cheerful, funny, or jovial</term> depend upon?

By the **mirth** of a **subject**, we generally mean a **state of pleasure** <term>Lustzustand; a psychological state of gratification</term> that involves a light exchange of ideas and is itself based upon such an exchange; accordingly, something is **objectively** mirthful if it evokes or encourages such a state. The stronger the degree of pleasure and the more rapid the exchange in which it moves, the greater the mirth, whether in the **subject** or the **object**. A strong degree of mirth becomes **ridiculousness** <term>Lächerlichkeit; the quality of being laughable or absurd</term>. Comparisons and wordplays can thus become mirthful and even ridiculous by providing an opportunity to experience a strong, pleasurable exchange between ideas through a unified connection. The more heterogeneous or even contradictory the ideas are, the more mirthful the connection will be, all else being equal. However, the character of the content of the connected ideas can counteract the mirth conditioned by the form just as much as it can increase it. Just as the former can happen through a serious tone in the content, the latter can happen through playful allusion, insofar as people generally find pleasure in—as the saying goes—"picking a hole in someone's coat" <note>original: *etwas am Zeuge flicken*. A German idiom meaning to mock or criticize someone slightly, usually in a harmless or teasing way.</note> without actually intending to cause them harm. For example, when **Heine** <note>Heinrich Heine (1797–1856) was a prominent German poet and satirist known for his sharp wit.</note> says: "a girl is milk, a young woman butter, and an old woman cheese"; or **Saphir**: "a Bavarian is a beer barrel when he stands up, and a barrel of beer when he lies down."

The talent for witty comparisons and for wordplay do not coincide. **Jean Paul** <note>Jean Paul Friedrich Richter (1763–1825), a German Romantic writer famous for his highly metaphorical and idiosyncratic prose.</note> is rich in the former but not the latter, while **Saphir** is the opposite. The former requires the ability to visualize a great wealth of things and objective relations all at once or in the fastest succession; the latter requires a great wealth of words with their attached meanings to be called to mind, and the...

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s psychological analysis of mirth and wit. He moves from theoretical definitions to the practical mechanics of how similarity and contrast create humor, specifically exploring the perception of shared "points of equality" in disparate ideas.</meta>

Fechner, Introduction to Aesthetics. 15

to be able to perceive the points of equality lying between them. The former ability is undoubtedly of greater significance than the latter and can be linked with great spiritual importance in general; yet even a witty person can lack depth, which is easily understood, since every instance of wit is self-contained within a brief circle of thought <term>Vorstellungskreis; the range of ideas or mental representations active at one time</term>.

That the mirth of comparisons and wordplays—insofar as it occurs—truly rests on the specified conditions is confirmed by the fact that it appears with a very similar character in cases that, at first glance, have little or nothing in common with those games or with each other; but upon closer inspection, they share the specified main condition as well as the secondary conditions. This includes the mirth or even the ridiculousness of many a mistake, slip of the tongue, confusion, absurd answer, moment of absent-mindedness, disappointed expectation, and so on.

In this respect, typographical errors and slips of the tongue (confusing words) follow most closely after wordplays, albeit with the significant difference that here the word itself, without the intervention of an intermediary concept, mediates the sudden shift into heterogeneous meanings. If the sense merely becomes incomprehensible or somewhat skewed through the typo or the slip of the tongue, there is nothing ridiculous in it yet; indeed, it is necessary that the actual meaning shifts into one that is more or less contradictory or entirely unrelated through the use of the similar word—of which several examples are provided in the following insertion.

### Typographical Errors.

In the description of a school festival: "The celebration concluded with the singing of a cholera verse" (instead of a choral verse). <note>original: *Choleraverses* instead of *Choralverses*</note>

Advertisement: "A landowner intends to drink away all his estates" (instead of sell). <note>original: *versaufen* (to squander on drink) instead of *verkaufen* (to sell)</note>

In an edition of Goethe's poems, instead of: "His eyes overflowed whenever he drank from it" — "His eyes overflowed, so often did he drink from it." <note>original: *so oft er trank daraus* vs. *so oft trank er daraus*. The typo changes the word order, turning a sentimental expression of emotion into a repetitive, almost mechanical description of drinking.</note>

In the introductory poem of Uhland's songs <note>Uhland's *Lieder*</note>, 1st Ed., instead of: "Songs are we, our father sends us into the wide world" — "Leather are we" etc. <note>original: *Leder* (leather) instead of *Lieder* (songs)</note>

Obituary of a virtuoso who died after long suffering, instead of: "He endured for 3 years" — "He tooted for three years." <note>original: *dudelte* (to play an instrument poorly or aimlessly) instead of *duldete* (suffered or endured)</note>

Public expression of thanks to a physician from a husband because...

### Slips of the Tongue.

A young man whom I had hired to read aloud to me made, among others, the following errors while reading: Schiller's statue on the post office (pedestal). <note>original: "Postamte (Postamente)"</note> — An English bear (peer) is usually a man of great influence and reputation. <note>original: "Bär (Pair)"; a "peer" refers to a member of the British nobility.</note> — The moon rose on the horizon in full gallop (splendor). <note>original: "Galopp (Glanze)"</note>

During exams, <note>original: "Examinibus"; the Latin plural for examinations.</note> countless false answers occur; if the examiners were to laugh at every one of them, they would never stop laughing, though they often instead never stop being annoyed. False answers stemming from ignorance or poor memory are all too common; however, everyone will find the answers in the following insertion ridiculous because the link between the given answer and the required answer is just as visible as the discrepancy between the two is unexpected and diverging in completely different directions.

At an examination of Oxford students, one gave the following answer to the question: why did the Israelites leave Egypt? "Because, because, it must have been on account of that business with Potiphar's wife;" <note>The student confuses the individual story of Joseph—who was imprisoned after being falsely accused by Potiphar's wife—with the much later national exodus of all Israelites led by Moses.</note> another, to the question: why was John the Baptist beheaded? "Because he wanted to dance with the daughter of Herodias." <note>In the biblical account, it was the daughter of Herodias who danced for King Herod; as a reward, she requested the head of John the Baptist on a platter.</note>

Exaggerations can also become ridiculous when the perception of the correct measure or degree of an object suddenly flips into a completely false measure or degree. To be sure, no one will laugh at common phrases like: "the weather is hellishly hot," or "I am dying of boredom," even though they are gross exaggerations; but these and similar hyperboles are so common in everyday speech that they must occur in a truly peculiar way to still produce an effect.

In cases of ridiculous absent-mindedness, one usually sees a goal being pursued in a way that completely contradicts the way it could actually be achieved. The connecting link of this contradiction is the shared idea of the goal, in which the conflicting ideas converge. For instance, I myself saw a woman running through every room to look for her child, whom she was holding in her arms; for which she was, naturally, laughed at. Had she seen the child in a room...

<summary>Fechner categorizes different types of humor arising from linguistic errors (malapropisms), logical confusion in student exams, and the physical irony of absent-mindedness.</summary>
<keywords>slips of the tongue, malapropisms, hyperbole, absent-mindedness, Oxford students, biblical anecdotes, psychology of humor</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "ridiculous" by analyzing why certain behaviors or physical actions provoke laughter. It transitions from the verbal blunders discussed on the previous page to physical errors and the psychological mechanics of unexpected movement.</meta>

...searched, while one knew that it was in another <note>likely referring to a pocket or a place</note>, it would not have appeared ridiculous; because there is nothing strange to be found in someone looking for something in the wrong place, where his gaze has not yet reached, but rather when he does not find it where his gaze and feeling should have allowed him to find it immediately.

When I was in Ilmenau for a **water cure**, <note>original: "Wassercur"; a 19th-century form of hydrotherapy involving various water treatments.</note> <note>Ilmenau is a town in Thuringia, Germany, which became a popular spa resort in the 1830s.</note> a story was told about an anxious guest there: out of fear that the water he was supposed to step into might be too cold for him, he first tested it by dipping his walking stick into it. <note>The humor arises from the absurdity of using an inanimate object, which cannot feel temperature, to test warmth.</note>

If someone fakes a slip-up, or the action of an absent-minded person, and one recognizes the intention behind it, the character of the ridiculous disappears. This is because the contradiction between the way one *ought* to act toward a goal and the way one is *actually* acting vanishes for the imagination; for one sees the only purpose of the actor is precisely the deception, and the action is directed quite simply toward that.

In the case of disappointed expectations that bear the character of the ridiculous, instead of a shared **notion of purpose** <note>original: "Zweckvorstellung"</note> in which the contradictory ideas converge, the link is found instead in a shared starting-point for a type of event that branches out into contradictory modifications.

Nothing is more ridiculous than the **leaps of young cats**. Why? We are accustomed to expecting the following movement of every motion we see undertaken to follow with a certain **consistency**. <note>original: "Consequenz"</note> But the leaps of young cats contradict this natural expectation at almost every moment.

If a child runs after their cap that has been blown away by the wind, we do not find that ridiculous; we generally see a child running more often than walking. However, if a serious man runs after his hat, it appears ridiculous to us for the same reason as the **leap of a young cat**; and perhaps one will find this very comparison ridiculous, because one is not accustomed to seeing a serious man compared to a young cat.

If a tile falls from the roof, which one had not thought about, there is no reason for ridicule; but if a brick falls at someone’s feet while he [is expecting] the fall of a...

<summary>Fechner explores the psychology of the ridiculous through physical examples, such as a man testing water temperature with a stick and the unpredictable movements of kittens. He argues that humor arises from a contradiction between our expectations of logical behavior and the sudden, divergent reality we observe.</summary>

<keywords>the ridiculous, Ilmenau, hydrotherapy, absent-mindedness, kittens, physical comedy, expectations, consistency, behavioral psychology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the "ridiculous," specifically focusing on the threshold where a joke or a blunder stops being funny and becomes tragic or distressing. It introduces two competing aesthetic principles: the Principle of Conceptual Consistency and the Principle of the Unified Linking of the Manifold.</meta>

...expected a rose from a beautiful hand, he would find the situation ridiculous himself—provided the failure of that expectation does not cause him too much literal grief. In any case, we, who do not share his annoyance, would find it ridiculous, especially if we feel he deserves the disappointment. However, the situation would cease to be ridiculous even for us if the brick struck the man dead or severely injured him. In that case, the physical displeasure <note>original: "Unlust"</note> regarding the tragedy would prevent the formal pleasure of the ridiculous from coming into effect. This example can stand for many others where the ridiculous fails to arise due to serious material counter-effects.

One can note that in counter-productive actions, disappointed expectations, and other cases where an idea is contradicted by the facts, the **Principle of Conceptual Consistency** <term>Vorstellungseinstimmigkeit: the psychological satisfaction we feel when our thoughts and expectations align with reality.</term> enters into conflict with the **Principle of the Unified Linking of the Manifold** <term>Einheitliche Verknüpfung des Mannichfaltigen: a core aesthetic theory suggesting we find pleasure when diverse or contradictory elements are suddenly brought together by a single event or point of view.</term>, under which these cases are categorized. While the pleasure found in such cases can be attributed to the satisfaction of the latter principle, one might instead expect displeasure from the violation of the former. 

However, it was already noted earlier (p. 82) in general terms that the displeasure arising from the violation of this principle easily remains "below the threshold" <note>The "threshold" (Schwelle) is a key concept in 19th-century psychology, referring to the point at which a stimulus is strong enough to be consciously felt.</note> if the contradiction does not deeply infringe upon our theoretical or practical interests. It will have even less impact if it is outweighed by the strong opposing effect of another principle. In these cases, it also compensates for itself, so to speak. What does it matter to us, for example, if a cat makes a different leap than we expected? We find our expectation contradicted, certainly, but also immediately refuted and corrected. The contradiction is resolved in the same moment it arises; the new idea simply takes the place of the old one. The resolution of a contradiction serves the sense of pleasure just as the contradiction itself serves the sense of displeasure. Thus, the **Principle of the Unified Linking of the Manifold** has, so to speak, free reign here to exert its effect. 

It is a different matter when something seems to run against the usual course of nature, contradicting its laws and thus the presuppositions we must hold fast; that appears to us even...

<summary>This page explores why unexpected events (like a brick instead of a rose) are funny only until they cause real harm, explaining this through the balance between conceptual consistency and the pleasure of resolving contradictions.</summary>
<keywords>the ridiculous, aesthetics, psychology of laughter, conceptual consistency, unified linking of the manifold, Gustav Fechner, expectation, pleasure and displeasure</keywords>
