<meta>This page continues the exploration of aesthetic pleasure and displeasure, moving from the definition of the "ridiculous" to the concept of the "uncanny." It also transitions from theoretical examples in speech and behavior to physical objects in the "visual arts," specifically focusing on how everyday items are sometimes disguised or "travestied" for playful effect.</meta>

...then is it not ridiculous, even when all other conditions for it seem to be met? In place of the ridiculous, a feeling of **uncanniness** <term>Unheimlichkeit: a sense of the eerie or weird that occurs when the familiar becomes strange or supernatural.</term> emerges here. Let us suppose, for example, that after we have seen it stream downward as often as it has rained before, the rain suddenly began to flow upward again after falling, imitating the leaps of young kittens in every direction; this would not appear particularly ridiculous to us because our assumptions about the outcome could not merely be uprooted by the contradicting result—rather, the contradiction would remain permanent and lead us astray in our views of nature.

Therefore, it would be unsound and outright wrong to attribute the ridiculousness in the cases mentioned to the existence of the contradiction in and of itself; for indeed, a contradiction in our ideas usually betrays its own quality of causing displeasure. A stronger contradiction can only increase the humor insofar as the diversity, which forms the basis of the **manifold** <term>Mannichfaltigkeit: the variety or "many-ness" of elements that a mind must organize into a whole; a core concept in 19th-century German aesthetics.</term>, cannot become greater than when it grows into a contradiction; but the greater the manifold, the more pleasure is given by its unified linking.

Everything discussed so far concerned only examples from the **verbal arts** <note>original: "redenden Künste"; referring to poetry, oratory, and literature.</note> and real life; however, the **visual arts** <note>original: "bildenden Künste"; the fine arts, including sculpture and painting.</note> are no strangers to cases that can be categorized under similar points of view.

When a **pen-wiper** <note>original: "Tintenwischer"; a small bundle of cloth or a brush used to clean excess ink from a quill or nib.</note> is represented as a little doll, or as a slipper, or as a feather duster, or as a book, etc., we have in every such **travesty** <term>Travestirung: here, the playful disguise or transformation of a mundane object into the shape of something else.</term>—as with a comparison or a pun—the linking of two very heterogeneous ideas through a mediating one, as the interpretation of one and the same form gives space to two completely different meanings. Lighters, toothpick holders, small candlesticks, and other objects for minor purposes are often seen "travestied" in this way; one can find pleasure in them from a similar perspective as in the verbal games mentioned above, provided that a conflict does not assert itself too strongly—a conflict which, from a certain side, puts those games at a disadvantage compared to these. The **functional meaning** <term>Zweckbedeutung: the inherent purpose or "utility" of an object which, according to the author's previous arguments, usually ought to be apparent in its design.</term>, which (according to the discussions of Section 15) lies in the interest of aesthetics to make as visible as possible in functional objects, does not hide—

<summary>The author explains why supernatural contradictions (like rain flowing upward) feel uncanny rather than funny, and then explores how visual art uses "travesty" to turn everyday objects like pen-wipers into playful shapes.</summary>

--- Page 240 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "travesty" or aesthetic disguise of objects. The previous page ended by questioning how the "ridiculous" manifests in the visual arts compared to the verbal arts.</meta>

...not only more or less through the **travesty** <term>Travestirung: the act of dressing up an object or theme in a form that does not naturally belong to it, often for humorous or decorative effect.</term>, but equilibrates itself, so to speak, with it; in any case, the object can no longer assert itself visually with its full value. Where the purpose of an object is so insignificant that we do not place much weight on the aesthetic demand for its pure manifestation in appearance, the charm of the travesty—especially in connection with an interest or charm of the form itself which the object receives through it—can easily outweigh that disadvantage, and one can permit such playfulness.

In contrast, it would be entirely tasteless to travesty objects of more important functional significance in a similar way. Someone once made the suggestion, for example, to aesthetically enhance **locomotives**—which, admittedly, do not make the pleasing impression of a swan moving upon the water—by surrounding them with a casing in the shape of a swan. But not only would this hide the mechanical play of the engine <note>The author refers to the visible movement of pistons and wheels, which was a source of fascination in the early industrial age.</note>—which interests us in its own way at least as much as the swimming of a swan—it also contradicts our mental image to see a swan sliding along on land, or to imagine a locomotive swimming like a swan. These contradictions are too serious to be redeemed by the charm of the travesty, combined with whatever charm the swan’s form itself might have over the locomotive.

The circumstance that, in the case of playing with the travesty of minor functional arrangements, our pleasure is usually determined at least as much by the delicate or otherwise interesting design impressed upon the object as by the charm of the travesty itself, combined with the aforementioned conflict, may be the reason why the impression of ridicule does not easily arise here, even if the travesty has a character very foreign to the purpose. However, the ridiculous can enter the **visual arts** <note>original: "bildende Kunst"</note> through the fact that ridiculous circumstances or encounters of life, or strong exaggerations (in <term>caricatures</term> <note>original: "Carricaturen"; humorous drawings that exaggerate features or behaviors for satirical effect.</note>), can be represented through them.

Thus, for example, a small picture by **Biard** <note>François-Auguste Biard (1798–1882), a French painter famous for his humorous and crowded genre scenes.</note> *) portrays in a ridiculous manner the reception that awaits a traveler upon disembarking from a Rhine steamboat

<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the "ridiculous" in art and music before transitioning to a formal investigation of the concept of "Taste." It continues the theme of how exaggerated or displaced proportions create humor.</meta>

...expects at least 10 to 12 porters and bag-carriers to divide his travel belongings among themselves and lead him and his companion to the hotel. Two brawny fellows carry a light overnight bag on a heavy pole over their shoulders, while another transports a small case and a parasol on a wheelbarrow to which one or two assistants have hitched themselves. 

The satirical magazine **"Fliegende Blätter"** <note>original: *Fliegende Blätter* ("Flying Leaves"); a highly influential German humor magazine published in Munich between 1844 and 1944, known for its caricatures and wit.</note> is full of such ridiculous depictions, though admittedly the explanatory captions are an essential part of the humor.

Even **music** is not entirely devoid of the ability to produce laughter. At the very least, I remember the violin virtuoso **Wasiliewski** <note>Wilhelm Joseph von Wasielewski (1822–1896), a prominent German violinist, conductor, and music historian.</note> once performing a piece in a circle of acquaintances where one could not stop laughing, as it was composed, so to speak, according to the principle of the "leaps of young kittens."

In passing, I offer the following observation on how the body reacts to the impression of the **ridiculous** compared to impressions of an opposite nature. **Laughter** consists of a jerky or rhythmic exhalation, whereas **sobbing** consists of a correspondingly jerky inhalation. However, a sudden joy—one that meets us with the impression that its cause is lasting—does not move us so much to laughter as to a lingering in a state of held exhalation. I find this to be the case when I imagine just now that I have won the grand prize in a lottery. Conversely, in the case of a sudden fright, one stares at the cause of the terror with held inhalation. If one then suddenly realizes that the fright was in vain, the terror dissolves into laughter, and thus the state of inhalation shifts with a jerk into a rhythmic exhalation.

# XVIII. On Taste.

->1) Conceptual Definitions.<-

It is the same with the concept of **taste** as it is with all our general concepts: one cannot cinch them tightly, or they escape the drawstring on all sides. As a rule, however, a common core remains. And so, for the concept of taste, the common core is that it is a disposition of the soul for immediate pleasure or displeasure in this or that thing—a judgment that does not require prior reflection.

<summary>The author concludes a discussion on the physiological and artistic nature of the ridiculous—noting its presence in music and satire—before defining 'Taste' as an intuitive emotional response.</summary>
<keywords>humor, Fliegende Blätter, Wasiliewski, physiology of laughter, respiration and emotion, aesthetic taste, intuition</keywords>

--- Page 242 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the physiological reactions to the ridiculous (discussed on page 231) into a formal philosophical definition of "Taste" as a subjective faculty. It explores how taste supplements the objective qualities of an object and distinguishes between the literal sense of taste (food) and the higher aesthetic sense.</meta>

...requires [a stimulus] to be triggered. One sees an object and, without knowing or asking why, it pleases or displeases us; that is a **matter of taste**. And if someone does ask the "why," and one does not know the reason, it is considered sufficient to say that it is simply a matter of taste.

Taste is, in this way, a subjective supplement to the objective conditions of **pleasure and displeasure**. The object must possess its own properties to be able to please or displease; but if the human being does not have the corresponding **internal disposition** <term>original: *Einrichtung*; the physical or mental "setup" or constitution of an individual that allows them to perceive stimuli</term>, it will not please or displease regardless. With a different disposition, one person may like what another dislikes, and thus we speak of a *different* taste, depending on how different things please or displease different people.

Insofar as **aesthetics** concerns itself with objects and relationships of immediate pleasure and displeasure—and "beautiful" or "unbeautiful" in the broadest sense generally refers to whatever has the property of immediately pleasing or displeasing—the **theory of taste** <term>original: *Geschmackslehre*; a common 18th and 19th-century term for aesthetics</term> is synonymous with aesthetics. Taste is a faculty for being addressed by things in one way or another, and it is a matter of taste to find something beautiful or not beautiful. 

However, insofar as the concept of the aesthetic and the beautiful is restricted in a *narrower sense* to objects and relationships of a "higher" pleasure and displeasure, one also tends to apply the concept of taste in a correspondingly narrower sense to these. For example, the liking or disliking of something that tastes good or bad <note>The author is highlighting the linguistic connection: in many languages, the word for the aesthetic faculty (Taste/Geschmack) is borrowed from the physical sense of tasting food.</note>—despite the fact that the expression "taste" is derived from that source—is not counted as a matter of taste in the narrower sense; thus, one does not yet call a **gourmet** <term>original: *Gutschmecker*; literally "a good taster," or someone who enjoys fine food and drink</term> a man of "good taste" for that reason alone.

Yet, many relationships relating to taste in the narrower and higher sense can be explained all the more tangibly by observing them in the lower sense.

In a very broad interpretation, the concept of taste—like that of the beautiful or unbeautiful—is applied not only to pleasure and displeasure regarding the external world, but also to the internal world. Thus, one might say: "It is not to my taste to plague myself with many worries, to deliberate for a long time first," and so on. In a narrower sense, however, one relates taste—just like the beautiful and unbeautiful—only to the pleasure...

<summary>This page defines "Taste" as the subjective human disposition that allows us to find objects beautiful or unbeautiful. It distinguishes between the literal sense of tasting food and the "higher" aesthetic taste, while noting that the two are linguistically and conceptually linked.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on "Taste" (Geschmack) from page 232. It transitions from defining taste as a subjective internal disposition to categorizing it as a "judgment of feeling," comparing its role in aesthetics to the role of conscience in morality.</meta>

...and displeasure at things and circumstances that make their impression upon us from the external world.

Insofar as one distinguishes between **judgments of reason** <term>original: *Verstandesurtheile*; intellectual conclusions based on logical steps</term> and **judgments of feeling** <term>original: *Gefühlssurtheile*; immediate reactions where the underlying logic is not consciously processed</term> by the fact that one is conscious of the reasons for the former but not for the latter, then judgments according to **taste**—whether something is beautiful or unbeautiful—belong to the category of judgments of feeling, just as those of **conscience**—whether something is right or wrong—do. To be sure, there must always be reasons for a judgment; but they may reside in an internal constitution <term>original: *Einrichtung*; the mental or physical "wiring" of an individual</term> whose effects we are aware of, but whose origin and mode of operation we are not. Now, frequent rational reflection on the claims that things have to please or displease can itself contribute something to this constitution, by virtue of which they subsequently please or displease us even without reflection; yet that is only one of the means of cultivating taste, of which we shall speak further on. However it may have been formed, if taste is well-cultivated, it is so extraordinarily valuable precisely because it provides the result of a thousand good reasons—reasons which the intellect could eventually find—immediately and without any search for them.

Insofar as taste tells us immediately what is beautiful and unbeautiful, and conscience tells us what is right and wrong, taste has a similar significance for **aesthetics** as conscience has for **morals**. Whether they always state what is "objectively right" is a question for both, but it is not one we are concerned with here for the time being, as we are currently only dealing with conceptual definitions.

Thus far we have spoken of taste only in a subjective sense; however, the concept of taste is also applied to objects to describe the manner in which they appeal to subjective taste—for instance, when one speaks of a particular "taste" prevailing in architecture, furniture, or clothing.

Regarding the distinctions that can be made within the concept of taste, the following is to be said.

The most important distinction to be made is that between a **good and bad** or **correct and incorrect** taste, depending on whether a person is pleased or displeased by what they *should* respectively be pleased or displeased by, or the opposite. This immediately raises the question of the point of view...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "taste" from page 233. The previous page ended mid-word with "aesthe...", which completes here as "aesthetic points of this 'ought'." The author is exploring how taste is not a single quality but can be categorized by its refinement, its height, and its breadth.</meta>

...aesthetic points of this "ought" <term>original: *Sollen*; in philosophy, this refers to a normative requirement or how things should ideally be, as opposed to how they simply are</term>. We shall return to this below; for now, one can be satisfied with the common concept of the "ought."

Furthermore, one can distinguish between a finer and coarser, a higher and lower, a more one-sided and more multi-faceted taste, as well as different directions of taste, depending on whether a person is more capable of being aesthetically affected by finer or coarser, higher or lower, fewer or more, or various types of qualities and relationships of things.

One cannot simply say that a finer and higher taste is necessarily a more correct or better one; for as often as these conditions coincide, it is not always the case. Thus, the over-refined person <term>original: *Ueberbildete*; someone whose cultural education has become so specialized or artificial that they lose touch with natural or universal beauty</term> often has a finer and higher taste, yet not necessarily a more correct or better one, a point to which we shall return later. Even less is a multi-faceted taste necessarily a good one, as it could instead be bad in every direction; meanwhile, a taste that is too coarse, too low, or too one-sided is, of course, also not good.

There is also no necessary dependency between the **delicacy** <term>original: *Feinheit*</term> and the **loftiness** <term>original: *Höhe*</term> of taste. It is essentially a matter of a delicate but not lofty taste when someone takes pleasure in the fine execution of a painting, the subtle modulations of a musical piece, or those relationships between fine details that are themselves called "fine." But in such a case, it may well be that this sensitivity does not extend beyond the individual delicate relationships; it does not rise to the highest and final relationships that permeate the whole and bind the whole together. Therefore, despite the delicacy of sensation, it reaches no great height. Conversely, one might rise to a great height in one's receptivity for the relationships of large masses—such as those found in artworks of the so-called "high style" <term>often referring to the "Grand Style" in art, which emphasizes noble subjects and large-scale compositions over small details</term>—while losing delicacy of sensation regarding individual details. Art accommodates this distinction in that artworks of delicate execution are generally not simultaneously works of high style, and vice versa; nor can one say that a con-

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 234 regarding the distinction between "delicacy" (Feinheit) and "loftiness" (Höhe) in matters of taste. ...union of the delicate and the lofty is impossible in principle, it is nonetheless rarely found together in either the subject <note>the person experiencing the taste</note> or the object <note>the thing being judged</note>, and the attempt to unite them has its own "but" <note>original: *Aber*; here meaning a reservation, complication, or drawback</note>. This would provide an opportunity to digress at length; however, we initially only wanted to speak of the concept of taste itself.

While taste, generally speaking, can be good or bad, delicate or coarse, one usually attributes taste to someone in the former rather than the latter sense. This means that when we say someone "has taste," we mean they have a relatively correct and delicate one; in this narrower sense, we use "taste" as being equivalent to "taste as it ought to be."

The meaning of the adjectives **tasteful** and **tasteless** is connected to this narrower definition of taste; however, linguistic usage has its whims. We speak of "tasteless" people as those who lack good taste, but why not also of "tasteful" people as those who possess it? We actually have no fitting adjective for this at all; for "tactful" refers more to behavior <term>original: *Benehmen*</term> than to feeling or perception <term>original: *Empfinden*</term>.

Beauty in nature and art is primarily the object of a higher and more delicate taste; yet, no one would call a landscape or a historical painting "tasteful" or "tasteless" in regard to its primary qualities. In contrast, clothes, furniture, decorations, entire outfits <term>original: *Toiletten*; in this context, it refers to a person's full ensemble of dress and grooming</term>, or room furnishings are most liberally labeled with those adjectives. Even the arrangement of a painting or a statue—which one might call "beautiful" but not "tasteful" in itself—within a suitable or unsuitable environment can be regarded as tasteful or tasteless. Nevertheless, it remains true that judging the painting or statue as beautiful or unbeautiful, based on the immediate impression it is capable of making as a whole, remains a matter of taste. The adjectival meaning regarding objects of pleasure and displeasure does not, therefore, follow the noun's meaning regarding the subjects all the way up to the objects of higher pleasure.

Had language developed systematically, the adjectives would correspond better with the noun in all instances; but—

Our concepts have not developed in such a way, and thus language could not either. By **absurd** <note>original: *abgeschmackt*; literally "de-tasted," this term evolved from meaning "tasteless" in a culinary sense to meaning "nonsensical" or "ridiculous" in a social or aesthetic sense</note> one understands the highest or a most obvious degree of the tasteless—something that deviates entirely from a correct sense of taste.

### 2) The Dispute over Taste.

It is an old saying that there is no disputing taste <note>referring to the Latin maxim *de gustibus non est disputandum*</note>; yet, people dispute it nonetheless—indeed, they argue over nothing more than they do over taste. It must, therefore, be possible to dispute it. And it is not merely individuals who argue about it, but also nations and eras; or, if they do not argue directly because they are too far removed from one another, the directions of their tastes nonetheless clash, as they are usually just as divergent as the nations and eras are distant from each other. Even those who are close in time and space, and share similar scientific and religious views—who are otherwise the best of friends in all things—still tend to argue about taste. And the aestheticians and art critics, who should be the ones to settle the dispute, argue about it the most, as they also disagree over the very perspectives and grounds for a decision.

Let us first look at some particularly striking examples of conflicting taste purely as a matter of fact, partly to awaken a sense of the magnitude of the differences in taste that occur, and partly to find points of connection for later discussions. We shall begin with an example from the realm of **fashion**, a field that might lead one to doubt that taste follows any rules or laws at all. For although taste gives itself a new rule in every new fashion, it does so only to mock the old one and to eventually fall prey to the mockery of the next.

Probably the most tasteless thing imaginable to us now is a wig and its slightly later representatives: powder, the braided queue <term>original: *Zopf*; the long, braided hair or wig-tail common in 18th-century men's fashion</term>, and the hair-bag <term>original: *Haarbeutel*; a small silk bag used to contain the ponytail of a wig</term>, which made the head itself into a kind of wig. Yet how differently did a not-too-distant time view this. I myself have heard old people tell of the impression of wretchedness, lack of culture, and even coarseness...

<summary>The author transitions from the linguistics of "tastelessness" to the classic problem of the "dispute over taste," arguing that while people say taste is subjective, it is the most frequent subject of debate. He uses the changing fashion of wigs and powdered hair as a prime example of how aesthetic standards shift drastically over time.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of historical shifts in fashion and taste, moving from a general introduction to specific anecdotes regarding the transition away from the wig and the queue (pigtail) in Leipzig society.</meta>

...previously, a head without a hairstyle and a **queue** <note>original: *Zopf*; the braided pigtail of a wig or natural hair common in 18th-century men's fashion</note> seemed like nothing at all. A person without these looked like absolutely nothing. Here in Leipzig, my father-in-law, City Architect Volkmann, was the first who dared to appear without a queue on a formal occasion—namely, his doctoral disputation. His friend and opponent, the later famous philologist Gottfried Hermann, seconded him in this daring venture, which Volkmann likely would not have felt capable of facing alone. It also nearly cost him his entry into the City Council; for to imagine a "City Father" without a queue was almost like imagining the helmsman of a ship without a rudder. 

Yet the hairstyle and the queue were, at their core, only weak echoes and the final remnants of the once world-dominating **wig**. Through the wig and its—so to speak—polar opposite, the **train** <note>a long extension of a gown that trails behind the wearer</note>, impressions were once produced that might almost make us regret having lost these pieces of clothing. One of these increased human dignity just as much upward as the other extended it downward and backward. We have thus become poorer by a source of sublime impressions. 

Indeed, a magnificent **full-bottomed wig** <term>Alongeperücke</term> <note>an oversized, shoulder-length curly wig popular in the late 17th and early 18th centuries</note> in previous centuries unquestionably made a more sublime impression than Cologne Cathedral—which, precisely because the wig made such a great impression, made no impression at all and therefore remained unfinished <note>Construction on Cologne Cathedral halted in the late 15th century and did not resume until the mid-19th century, around the time this was likely written</note>. But it is also hardly an exaggeration to say that the wig once made a greater impression than Cologne Cathedral does now. I remember reading that when a child’s father received a visit from a councilman wearing a monstrous wig, the child afterwards asked with timid reverence whether that had surely been the "Good Lord." The child could not imagine the Supreme Being without the largest possible wig and, conversely, inferred the presence of the Supreme Being from the size of the wig. Thus, reverence for the wig had already taken root even in the youngest of minds.

Nor was it the same with these things as with today's **frock coat** <term>Frack</term>, which is rejected in theory as universally as it was, until recently, actually worn in society, and which even today people have not quite managed to cast off. Rather, the taste for those older fashions was considered so authoritative that even the official representatives of "taste" advocated for them. And at the time when the **Greek garment** <term>griechisches Gewand</term> and the **Roman toga** <term>römische Toga</term> were worn, everything else also harmonized with these garments.

Here we have two eras and peoples who, so to speak, understood nothing of each other regarding taste. For in general, differences in taste never assert themselves in isolation, and all examples to be cited here actually represent a larger context of such differences. Let us turn from **fashion** <term>Mode</term> to **art** <term>Kunst</term>, yet content ourselves with shorter references to this infinite field.

...wig-style <note>The author is likely completing the term "Zopfgeschmack" (queue-style or philistinism) from the previous page</note> of other nations, nor is our current musical taste that of past ages; however, the "music of the future" <note>original: "Zukunftsmusik"—a term often used in the 19th century to describe the progressive works of Richard Wagner</note> is already here with blaring fanfares to announce its victory over the present. > "That in terms of harmony, much of what formed surprising contrasts for our ancestors seems to us, on the contrary, little surprising and rather trivial, is not striking. But that combinations of harmonies sound completely false and nonsensical to the ear of one age, while they sounded beautiful and natural to the ear of another time—this is indeed a mysterious fact. Even the harsh and unprepared dissonances, which we now often consider very effective, were regarded as ear-splitting 100 years ago. Even more. The horrific sequences of fourths <note>musical intervals of four notes</note> by **Guido of Arezzo** <term>Guido von Arezzo</term> <note>an 11th-century music theorist and inventor of modern staff notation</note> from the 11th century so strongly oppose our ear that it takes the utmost self-discipline from trained singers just to bring such harmonic combinations out of their throats at all. And yet they must have sounded beautiful and natural to the medieval ear! Even dogs, who listen quietly to modern progressions of thirds and sixths, begin to howl miserably if one plays the barbaric progressions of fourths from the **Guidonian** <term>Guidonisch</term> **diaphonies** <term>Diaphonieen</term> <note>an early form of two-part polyphony</note> on the violin! This historically confirmed retraining of the musical ear is indeed incomprehensible."

To this, the author shows how **orchestral tuning** <term>Orchesterstimmung</term>, **tempo** <term>Tempo</term>, and so on, have been changed according to place and time.

<meta>This page continues Fechner’s investigation into the relativity of taste. Having previously discussed musical harmony, he now moves to architecture—contrasting Western ideals with those of Sumatra—and begins a discussion on the historical development of nature appreciation.</meta>

...but not, as legs do for the body of an animal, have the entire building laden upon them; and it is just as self-evident that they should taper more towards the top than towards the bottom. In fact, it would appear to us as a matter of completely inverted taste to see a building raised entirely above the ground by columns or supports—as if it were afraid to touch that upon which it must rather be entirely founded—and to see the thicker, and therefore heavier, part of the columns or supports turned toward the top rather than the bottom.

Yet both of these absurdities are found combined in the <term>architecture of Bencoolen</term> <note>Modern-day Bengkulu, Indonesia</note> on the island of <term>Sumatra</term>, as I have taken from a travel description. Here, namely, the floor of the houses does not rest on the earth, but on eight-foot-high supports, so that one can walk under the floor as if under a ceiling; and these supports are all thicker at the top than at the bottom. Furthermore, the inhabitants do not regard these merely as objects of utility, but truly of taste, as evidenced by the fact that they finish them cleanly and decorate their upper part in a similar way to how we decorate the <term>capitals</term> <note>the decorative top-most part of a column</note> of our columns. Their eye and sense of beauty or taste has adapted to these proportions of their structures just as our taste has adapted to the proportions occurring among us; and if we laugh at their stilt-legged houses, they, on the other hand, will undoubtedly find our houses like creatures whose legs have been cut off and which now lie flat upon the ground.

One asks: how is such an <term>aberration of taste</term> explained? It will not only be explained later (p. 259 f.), but also justified as no aberration at all; and precisely because it is so instructive, I have cited it. Now, only one last example regarding the <term>aesthetic conception of nature</term>.

That this was significantly different for the <term>Ancients</term> <note>Referring to the civilizations of Classical Antiquity, specifically Greece and Rome</note> than for us follows quite simply from the fact that, despite their otherwise highly developed art, they had no <term>landscape painting</term> in the same sense as we do. Admittedly, people knew how to appreciate smiling, blooming, well-cultivated regions rich in the variety of forest, mountain, and river—especially coastal areas of lakes and seas—and preferred to settle there; however, they did not yet maintain such a sentimental relationship...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relativity of aesthetic taste, transitioning from architecture to the historical shift in how humans appreciate nature. The text begins mid-word with "-niss," the conclusion of "Verhältniss" (relationship).</meta>

...relationship to nature was not yet so refined in the variations of aesthetic enjoyment of nature; people did not yet travel to beautiful regions merely for the sake of the landscape's beauty. The overall aesthetic impression of a landscape, while not purely sensory, was undeniably closer to the sensory for the Ancients than it is for us. Meanwhile, certain details of nature—specifically groves, springs, and rivers—may have gained a higher aesthetic significance for the Ancients through their mythological associations than they have for us.

However, the difference between antiquity and the present day in the aesthetic perception of sublime and "wildly romantic" regions is particularly remarkable. One might say that the Ancients completely lacked a taste for such regions; and if we today jokingly say of certain dog breeds that they are "all the more beautiful the uglier they are," the Ancients would have said in all seriousness regarding our taste for such landscapes: they seem all the more beautiful to us the uglier they are. Regions like the **Bernese Oberland**, the **Chamonix Valley**, and the high Alps in general, are now seen by us as the source of the most sublime travel experiences. They attract countless travelers every year, and perhaps nowhere else does one hear such a profusion of exuberant exclamations in every language as there—where the Berliner and the Leipziger <note>Residents of Berlin and Leipzig, used here to represent stereotypical city-dwelling tourists of the 19th century</note> glare at each other only because neither finds the other’s dialect "sublime" enough for the sublimity of the scene, and each wishes to enjoy the "sublime solitude" alone.

That solitude was easily achieved in earlier times, for such regions were once shunned by all travelers who were not forced to pass through them; even in memory, they left behind only the impression of a terrifying sight. Remarkably, in this respect, the taste of the ancient Greeks and Romans coincided perfectly with the taste of our own **pigtail and wig era** <note>original: "Zopf- und Perückenzeit"; a term for the 18th-century Rococo and Enlightenment period before Romanticism</note>. It is instructive to read the accounts by **Friedländer** <note>Ludwig Friedländer (1824–1909), a scholar famous for his work on Roman cultural history</note> in the second part of his *Descriptions from the Social History of Rome*. He cites passages from older travel journals in which the Salzburg and Tyrolean Alps, as well as the Scottish Highlands, are described as being devoid of all adornment and beauty. They are placed in the same category and sentence as the **sand deserts of the March** <note>the sandy landscapes of Brandenburg</note> and the **Lüneburg Heath**, and are contrasted with the "lovely, smiling regions" in which alone one was able to find joy...

...stood in opposition. To the Ancients, in its current state, it would have made the impression not only of an agricultural wasteland but also of scenic desolation and bleakness. Today, artists and friends of art already express regret in advance that, following the seizure of Rome by the <term>Piedmontese</term> <note>Refers to the 1870 capture of Rome by the Kingdom of Italy (centered in Piedmont), which led to the modernization and "cultivation" of the surrounding countryside.</note>, the Campagna will sooner or later face cultivation and thus lose those scenic charms; the Ancients would have seen in this regret only a sign of our wild <term>taste</term> <note>German: *Geschmack*; here referring to aesthetic discernment.</note>, and would have reproached us that, despite all we have learned from them, we have not yet entirely shed the "barbarian," and that our raw nature still manifests in our raw <term>taste for nature</term>.

Enough of these examples, which I have diligently drawn from every field where taste can assert itself—the realms of fashion, art, and nature. Everywhere we see the differences in taste going so far that it must be difficult for those standing at one extreme to understand the possibility of the other—indeed, almost to believe in it. And what variations of taste exist between these extremes! It would be of interest if one had the colors to present this infinite <term>variety</term> of shades of taste to the eye on a single chart; though admittedly, this collective painting of taste would likely appear to us as the most tasteless thing in existence.

To this great diversity of taste is added—to touch upon it here—an equally great uncertainty of taste. If one looks at the visitors to an <term>art museum</term> or an <term>art exhibition</term>, does one not find most of them in great embarrassment as to whether they should like this or that? True, regarding old, well-known masters and paintings, such indecision does not occur; everyone...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the subjectivity of aesthetic taste, shifting from the perception of nature to the influence of art history and the authority of experts in shaping what the public finds beautiful.</meta>

...knows that he must like Raphael, Michelangelo, Titian, Albrecht Dürer, the Dutch genre paintings, and above all <term>Cornelius</term> <note>Peter von Cornelius (1783–1867), a leading German painter of the Romantic period who sought to revive monumental fresco painting.</note> among today’s painters. But once one knows what *should* please—and that is why <term>connoisseurs</term> <note>German: *Kenner*; those with specialized, expert knowledge in art and aesthetics.</note> are there, to tell us—it immediately begins to truly please us. For most <term>taste</term> <note>German: *Geschmack*; the ability to discern and appreciate beauty.</note>, like most faith, is something implanted or imposed; we shall return to this below. And so, most judgments of taste in matters of art are merely "after-judgments" following the judgments—and often the prejudices—of a few connoisseurs by whom larger or smaller social circles are governed. 

However, in regards to paintings by new or unknown masters, the anchor of a famous name is missing. Unfortunately, when one stands helpless before a new work, one does not always have a connoisseur directly in front or behind them whose judgment could be overheard. Yet even the judgment of connoisseurs becomes uncertain when the name becomes uncertain. Only recently, an example was seen involving a famous painting: when its master changed from a known figure to an unknown one, the previously unanimous taste of all experts fell into complete confusion, and many from that point on renounced their admiration for the image entirely.

Whence, one may ask, comes this great diversity of taste on one hand and the indecision on the other, which divide the field of aesthetics? Beauty is supposed to have an absolute validity; why does it not assert itself? It is said to cast a spell over people; why does this spell so often fail? And what finally decides the conflict of taste and removes the uncertainty? Is every taste of equal value, and are there no authoritative points of view at all to distinguish a better taste from a worse one? That indeed seems to be what people mean when they say: <note>original: "über den Geschmack lässt sich nicht streiten"</note> "There is no disputing taste"; they mean to say that the dispute cannot be decided.

As far as the explanation for these differences in taste is concerned, one can adopt various levels of perspective. To take the highest possible standpoint, one might say: The entire development of the human spirit stands under the influence of an "Idea"—specifically, in the highest and final instance, the divine or absolute Idea—and all differences of taste are merely complementary and demanding of one another...

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the diversity of aesthetic taste. While the previous page discussed how art experts and famous names influence public opinion, this page examines the philosophical and historical theories used to explain why taste varies so much between individuals and eras.</meta>

...moments, links, and stages replacing and resolving into one another, in which the highest <term>idea of taste</term> <note>German: *Geschmacksidee*; the abstract concept of perfect beauty or aesthetic judgment toward which art strives.</note> works itself out, unfolds, and comes into appearance, without being able to exhaust itself in a single mode of appearance. Every lower stage, however, must be <term>sublated</term> <note>German: *aufheben*; a central term in German philosophy meaning to cancel, preserve, and lift up to a higher level all at once.</note> into a higher one, for which it is seen as a precondition and preliminary step; the sum total of all stages naturally ends in the one that has manifested itself as such in the respective representative of the highest idea. Since we are now precisely informed by <term>Schelling</term> <note>Friedrich Wilhelm Joseph Schelling (1775–1854), a philosopher who argued that Art is the "organon" or essential tool of philosophy.</note>, <term>Hegel</term> <note>Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel (1770–1831), who viewed the history of art as the "Spirit" manifesting itself in different material forms over time.</note>, and their successors about the "potencies," stages, and self-resolutions that the Idea must pass through to fulfill itself at the highest level, this path presents no other difficulty than fitting experience into the pre-ordained schema; and, if experience refuses to fit, one simply alters the schema through "philosophical sovereign authority." By this means, an absolute insight into the origin of all variations in taste is generated. It is only to be regretted that this insight is made difficult by the somewhat mystical character of the "Absolute Idea," and therefore will always remain the property of a few philosophers who do not expect a common clarity from either the Idea or themselves.

One takes a considerably lower, but therefore more accessible stand-point for <term>common sense</term> <note>German: *gemeiner Menschenverstand*; the practical, everyday reasoning of the general public.</note>, when one says: the differences in taste depend on one hand on the innate diversity of human nature, and on the other hand on the variety of circumstances under which people grow up and the different ways they are educated. Furthermore, in their more general directions, these differences are connected to the variations of the entire intellectual culture. This culture can then be pragmatically traced in broad strokes according to those moments that reach across entire nations and eras, showing how the development of individual differences in taste is integrated and subordinate to this larger whole. This remains a high and beautiful task, for whose execution one must refer to the histories of culture and art.

Finally, however, one can also ask about the ultimate psychological levers through which the taste of every individual receives its direction, and which, in the case of variations of taste across entire eras and nations, operate only in

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the philosophical attempts to understand the origins of varying tastes. While the previous page critiqued the high-flown "philosophical sovereign authority" of thinkers like Hegel and Schelling, this page shifts toward a more practical analysis of why individuals perceive beauty differently.</meta>

...on a large scale and in a broad context; and since cultural and art histories do not easily lower themselves to the consideration of these matters, instead of repeating what can be found there, I will proceed to examine these final levers with a few observations.

Alongside the subjective causes for the variations in taste, we encounter the objective fact that objects of taste generally present different facets to our perception. Now, depending on one's disposition, circumstances, or education, the attention of one person is drawn more to this side, and another person to that; depending on whether this facet is pleasing or displeasing, their liking or disliking of the entire object is predominantly determined by it. Thus, one person might focus almost exclusively on the composition of a painting, and the picture pleases them if it succeeds in this regard, regardless of how the coloration <note>German: *Colorit*; the specific use of color and light in a painting, as opposed to line or form.</note> is handled; for another, conversely, their pleasure is primarily determined by the relationships of the coloring. One person pays more attention to the nature of the content, the other more to the form in which it is expressed, and so on. In short, the diversity of taste is partly linked to its own one-sidedness, insofar as this focus can take different directions.

Regarding the decision between conflicting tastes, the primary question is: who should be the judge? Feeling? But a dispute over taste is precisely a dispute of feelings; therefore, it cannot be decided by feeling. The intellect? Well, if it succeeds in establishing criteria by which something is beautiful, it would be simple to justify or reject a specific taste based on whether it makes something appear beautiful or not—that is, whether it causes immediate pleasure or displeasure. But unfortunately, these criteria among <term>aestheticians</term> <note>Philosophers or critics who study the nature of beauty and art.</note> are so contentious, so wavering, and vague, or they hover at such a philosophical height, that one can justify the wig and train <note>German: *Perücke und Schleppe*; symbols of the artificial and highly structured fashions of the late 17th and 18th centuries.</note> just as easily as the Greek gown; one only needs to choose and twist the principle accordingly. – We generally prefer the fluid and curved to the straight and stiff in an aesthetic sense; <term>Hogarth</term> <note>William Hogarth (1697–1764), an English artist who famously argued that the "S-shaped" or serpentine line was the ultimate "line of beauty."</note> even went so far as to call the line of beauty wave-like, <term>Winckelmann</term> <note>Johann Joachim Winckelmann (1717–1768), an influential art historian who preferred the elliptical curves found in ancient Greek statues.</note> called it elliptical, and <term>Herder</term> <note>Johann Gottfried von Herder (1744–1803), a philosopher who explored how our perception of beauty is shaped by our senses and historical context.</note> called it suspended betwee-

<meta>This page continues the author’s investigation into aesthetic theories. Having previously discussed how different people focus on different facets of an object (such as color versus composition), the author now tests several prominent 19th-century definitions of beauty—such as formalist, idealist, and organic theories—by applying them to practical examples like wigs, hats, and architecture.</meta>

...explained by the relationship between **straight and curved** lines, and how often today does one still hear a figure praised for the "beautiful flow" of its forms? The **wig** seems perfectly suited to satisfy all these requirements at once; and if we also include the **Herbart-Zimmermann principle** <note>A formalist theory by Johann Friedrich Herbart and Robert Zimmermann suggesting beauty is found in the mathematical or proportional relationship between different elements, such as size.</note>, which states that the large is pleasing when placed alongside the small, then we would have to see the most perfect beauty in the largest wig. Why, then, do we nevertheless reject the wig and even prefer the stiff hat <note>Likely referring to the top hat, the standard formal headwear of the 19th century.</note>—despite everything we find to criticize about it—over a wig that is half-wavy, half-elliptical, merging the straight and the curved in its falling curls, and, in short, offering the most beautiful "flow of forms"? —

According to some, beauty is the divine **Idea** <term>Idea: In German Idealist philosophy, this refers to a supreme, spiritual reality that manifests in the physical world.</term> expressing itself in the earthly realm, appearing to our senses; they claim the task of art, as the representer of the beautiful, is to capture **the Ideal**. But toads and spiders are also divine creatures; why, then, do they please us—or why should they please us—less than the lily and the rose? And why should the wig, as a grandiose cloak for the head, be less "ideal" than the cloak draped around the shoulders, which is so much more highly valued in "ideal" artistic depictions? After all, a wig is merely idealized hair. —

Again, according to others, the beautiful is that which arises from a **free play of the imagination** <note>This concept was central to the aesthetics of Immanuel Kant and Friedrich Schiller, who argued that beauty triggers a harmonious balance between our senses and our intellect.</note> and is capable of stimulating such play. But who would deny that a much freer play of the imagination was able to express itself in the old hairstyles—the tower-like and garden-like head-dresses—than in our modern hair arrangements? In this regard, the costume of the era of **Louis XIV and XV** was far ahead of the most tasteful modern dress, and certainly ahead of ancient dress, which restricted the imagination to the very specific paths of **appropriateness** and **utility**. —

According to others, the beautiful should represent the "idea" and the laws of the **organic** only in their purest expression; they claim the shape of columns, the entire proportions of buildings, and their beauty (which transcends their mere utilitarian purpose) owe everything to the memory of organic structures. However, it is a law of all higher organic structures to rest entirely upon a column-like base, and all organic supporting columns <note>The author is likely referring to the legs of animals or humans.</note> are thicker at the top than at the bottom; why, then, do we refuse to accept this in **Bencoolen architecture**? <note>original: *Bencoolen’schen Bauwerke*. This likely refers to a 19th-century perception of "exotic" or "primitive" architectural styles from Bencoolen (modern-day Bengkulu, Indonesia), used here as a counter-example to European classical rules.</note> — We find the sequences of fifths and fourths <note>This refers to *organum*, a primitive form of harmony in medieval music.</note> of **Hucbald** and **Guido of Arezzo**, and especially the music of the Black Africans <note>original: *Musik der Neger*. The author uses the terminology of his time to compare archaic European music with non-Western musical traditions that contemporary Europeans found discordant.</note>...

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of various aesthetic principles. ...and Chinese find it abominable; but can we see these as aberrations of taste, when it is rather we who have only later deviated from a taste that was paved by nature itself? — To us, the most barren glacier regions appear as the most sublime things in existence; to the ancients, they appeared as the most desolate. If one wants even more principles, one could bring up the **perfection of sensory appearance** <note>This refers to Alexander Baumgarten’s definition of beauty: "perfectio cognitionis sensitivae."</note>, **disinterested pleasure** or **purposiveness without purpose** <note>These are key concepts from Immanuel Kant’s *Critique of Judgment*, suggesting beauty is something we enjoy without needing to "use" it for a specific goal.</note>, and probably others; yet with the previous ones, one already has more than enough.

Now, the way I have addressed all these principles is admittedly highly superficial; and it could not be otherwise, because a sharper and deeper investigation would have required a more extensive look back than was appropriate here. Thus, it would not be difficult for a representative of any of these principles to convict me of this superficiality and to turn or interpret their principle so that the <term>perücke</term> <note>wig</note>, the <term>sequences of fourths and fifths</term> <note>In traditional Western music theory, parallel fourths and fifths were often seen as "forbidden" or "ugly" intervals.</note>, the <term>Bencoolen architectural style</term> <note>A reference to the colonial architecture of Bencoolen (modern-day Bengkulu, Indonesia), often used in 19th-century German texts as a shorthand for exotic or "distasteful" building styles.</note>, etc., truly appear reprehensible according to it, and everything that aligns with current taste—and especially the taste of that principle's representative—truly appears beautiful. However, it unfortunately simply depends on a clever turn of phrase and interpretation to justify or reject the taste of any given time, and the interpretation will always be guided much more by existing taste than vice versa. We saw this with Hogarth <note>William Hogarth, the English painter who attempted to define a mathematical "Line of Beauty."</note> and can see it with the critics of taste of all times.

I am now also no <term>aesthetic savior</term> <note>original: "ästhetischer Heiland." A playful term for a theorist who claims to have found the ultimate, "saving" solution to the chaos of art theory.</note> to remedy this state of affairs; rather, I am of the opinion that no principle can be established at all that would enable us to settle the dispute of taste in all cases—yet there is one that changes the point of view from which the dispute...

<meta>This page continues the author's investigation into the principles of aesthetic judgment. ...is to be conducted is designated clearly enough and, in cases that are not too complex, truly leads to a decision—even if only with a greater or lesser degree of certainty. ### 3) Disposition and the Cultivation of Taste.

Human taste is innate according to its **disposition** <note>The German word *Anlage* refers to one's natural aptitude or biological starting point.</note>, but it is acquired according to its **cultivation** <note>The term *Bildung* implies both formal education and the lifelong process of self-formation and cultural refinement.</note>. While the nature of this "becoming" is partly determined by the original disposition, it is by no means determined by that alone. In short, taste is the product of original disposition and educational influences; depending on the variation in both, the resulting taste will differ.

By virtue of our innate makeup, all human beings are affected in a fairly consistent way by the simplest sensory stimuli and the simplest sensory relationships. Almost every child delights in a sweet taste; every primitive people <note>original: "rohen Volke." In the 19th-century context, this referred to cultures the author perceived as being in a "raw" or "unrefined" state of nature.</note> prefers red above all other colors; and the most uneducated eye will find more pleasure in a symmetrical figure than in an irregular tangle of lines. Yet, upon this common foundation, taste already finds—by way of innate nature—the internal conditions for varying degrees of refinement, height, and direction of development. On average, a woman is disposed toward a finer, yet less "lofty" taste than a man; the European toward a finer and loftier taste than the Negro <note>The author reflects the racial hierarchies and "scientific" prejudices common to 19th-century European academic thought.</note>; and the Frenchman or Italian toward a different direction of taste than the German or Englishman. It is true that educational influences generally differ according to gender, race, and nationality; however, insofar as a people "educates itself," these influences have themselves partly emerged from a specific innate disposition. Meanwhile, the influences of the natural environment contain factors that simultaneously affect both disposition and education.

As important as innate disposition is as a starting point for further development, it is easily given too much weight. Taste, much like conscience, is all too easily regarded as something bestowed upon man ready-made from the very beginning, or something that arises without mediation from the...

<summary>The author argues that human taste is a product of both innate biological disposition and external educational influences, noting that while basic sensory preferences are universal, refinement varies by gender, nationality, and culture.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 249 regarding the development of human taste. The previous page ended mid-sentence discussing the "common foundation" of sensory pleasure; this page picks up with how that foundation evolves as a child grows.</meta>

...ion <note>The text here completes the thought from the previous page regarding the "common foundation" of human development.</note> developing out of the child’s unconsciousness, where good taste is merely seen as a particularly lucky inheritance. In truth, however, taste everywhere only becomes fully formed through education <note>The German word *Erziehung* encompasses both formal schooling and the general upbringing or "nurturing" one receives from their environment.</note> and, even with the same innate disposition, can still turn out very differently in terms of quality, level, refinement, and direction, depending on the diversity of educational influences.

The means by which taste is educated are difficult to bring under a single general point of view, but like those of human development in general, they can be considered under the following categories, which can be kept distinct from one another, at least up to certain limits:

1) Transmission from others.
2) Personal reflection.
3) Habituation and desensitization <note>The term *Abstumpfung* (literally "blunting") refers to how our aesthetic reactions can become dulled or less intense through repeated exposure.</note>.
4) Practice.
5) Association.

Let us limit ourselves to considering these from the main perspectives, while psychology may go deeper, and the theories of education, cultural history, and ethnology <note>The study of different peoples and their cultures.</note> may go further into them than will happen here.

**First.** It is a fact that the expressed pleasure or displeasure of others is capable of helping to determine our own pleasure and displeasure, or even of determining it from the outset. This happens all the more easily the less we are already influenced by other factors, and the more "determining power" the other person possesses over us. Thus, taste passes from parents to children until the children's own judgment strengthens; thus, taste in art schools stands under the influence of teachers and peers; and when a taste in a certain respect dominates an entire era or an entire nation, transmission combined with habit will always play the primary role.

This transmission can come about partly through the fact that reasons for pleasure or displeasure are asserted by others—reasons which only need to be highlighted to achieve their effect; in short, through instruction. Partly, it happens because the pleasure or displeasure of others, through its very expression, transplants itself into us by causing a kind of mental contagion <note>original: "geistige Ansteckung." A 19th-century psychological concept suggesting that emotions and preferences can spread between people like a virus, especially in social groups.</note>, to which a passive or still indifferent nature is most easi...

<summary>This page argues that taste is not merely an innate gift but a product of education and social influence, listing five primary ways taste is formed—chiefly through "transmission" from authority figures and social groups.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 253 (noted as 249 in the previous metadata) regarding the external and internal factors that shape human taste. It specifically elaborates on the first three of the five points mentioned previously: transmission from others, personal reflection, and habituation.</meta>

...tested, particularly on the part of those to whom one is otherwise accustomed to subordinate oneself <note>The author suggests that we are most susceptible to the opinions of those we naturally respect or look up to, such as parents, teachers, or experts.</note>. The psychological basis for this transmission <note>original: *Uebertragung*</note> may still require explanation and clarification, but it must, in any case, be recognized as a fact. One might think that an originally innate imitative drive <term>Nachahmungstrieb: The natural human tendency to mimic the behaviors and attitudes of those around us.</term> extends from actions to feelings, and accordingly, the pleasure and displeasure <note>original: *Lust und Unlust*</note> that others express at the sight of this or that elicits our own pleasure and displeasure at the sight of the same thing. Alternatively, one might consider—though I would not see this as the sole reason—that once we know what is *supposed* to please (and here we defer to those we consider wiser as long as we do not find ourselves wise enough), the pleasure easily arises for no other reason than that, in general, what *ought* to be pleases us. Everyone values good taste as a virtue they should possess, and thus the striving to acquire this advantage awakens an involuntary mood in this direction. Furthermore, anyone is free to satisfy the desire for a more thorough explanation.

**Second.** No less than instruction from others, our own repeated reflection can make the pleasing or displeasing significance of things familiar enough to henceforth find immediate pleasure or displeasure in them. Instead of being "set up" or adjusted by others, we can adjust ourselves. Thus, we often see the taste of the art connoisseur <term>Kunstkenner: A person with specialized knowledge and refined judgment in the fine arts.</term> and the philosophical aesthetician determined by their artistic principles <note>original: *Kunstprincipien*</note>, rather than the other way around.

**Third.** By virtue of so-called habituation <note>original: *Gewöhnung*</note>, a person can—after constant or frequently repeated exposure—come to tolerate <note>The German phrase *gefallen lassen* literally means "to let it please you," but functions as an idiom for "to put up with" or "tolerate."</note> what initially displeased them, or even find positive pleasure in it. Conversely, what initially pleased them without being necessary for their well-being may eventually be demanded and needed; one may even feel the loss of something inherently indifferent with displeasure once habituation has set in. This is a kind of internal adaptation of the organism to a stimulus, which is gradually brought about by the effect of the stimulus itself.

However, the laws of habituation are complicated by those of desensitization <note>original: *Abstumpfung*; refers to the dulling of a response through overexposure.</note>, oversaturation, and overstimulation...

It argues that taste is not just innate but is constructed through our interactions with others and our own mental and physical adaptations to stimuli.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the psychological factors that shape human taste. The previous page ended while explaining how personal reflection and familiarity influence our preferences. This page moves into the third factor (habituation) and the fourth factor (the refinement of taste from coarse to subtle impressions).</meta>

...and partly come into conflict with it. In proportion to how an impression is stronger and more frequently recurring, its effect becomes duller; every stimulus can be increased so far and repeated so often that the limits within which an adaptation to it can exist—in the sense mentioned above—are exceeded. Therefore, habituation <term>Gewöhnung: The psychological process of becoming accustomed to a stimulus until it no longer provokes a strong reaction.</term> to pleasurable stimuli is generally not associated with an increase in their effect of pleasure. Instead, it reveals itself more through the displeasure <note>original: *Unlust*</note> felt when they are removed, rather than the pleasure felt during their effect on the dulled senses. For this reason, we cling in a certain way to habits, yet we also wish to be stimulated anew by breaking out of the boundaries of the familiar. Consequently, through impressions that are too strong or too often repeated, weariness, over-saturation, over-stimulation, or even a sense of paralysis can arise. From this, very diverse relationships emerge, the pursuit of which would lead us far afield; however, it is sufficient here to have recalled the most general viewpoints under which they are categorized.

Now, through every time period, every country, every social class, every gender, and every age, other circumstances and conditions prevail, either permanently or in a specific cycle. These give rise to other directions of habituation and, consequently, to other determinations of taste, insofar as taste depends on habituation.

**Fourth.** It was mentioned above that all people are addressed by the simplest sensory stimuli in a manner that is, if not identical, then quite similar by nature. However, in proportion to how the stimulus of lower and coarser impressions becomes duller through repeated engagement with them, a need for engagement with finer and higher impressions arises in those who are generally susceptible to them. Thus, gradually, increasingly finer qualities and higher relationships begin to make an impression—ones that initially made none—while at the same time the aesthetic impression of coarser qualities and lower relationships recedes.

So it is that for the wine connoisseur, the crude taste of spirits <note>original: *Spiritus* (referring to high alcohol content)</note> and sweetness gradually recedes, and he becomes all the more receptive to the finer qualities of the flavor. The gourmand <term>Gourmand: A person who takes great pleasure in consuming good food and drink, often to a sophisticated degree.</term> no longer cares for the dumplings that our— <gloss>Delicious!</gloss> <note>The word "Köstlich!" (Delicious!) appears here as an interlinear annotation, perhaps a humorous commentary by a previous reader on the mention of dumplings.</note>

<summary>This page discusses how habituation can dull our response to pleasure and how repeated exposure to simple stimuli leads a person to seek out more refined and complex aesthetic experiences.</summary>
<keywords>habituation, sensory stimuli, satiety, refinement of taste, aesthetics, wine connoisseurship, gourmandism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the refinement of taste, moving from the impact of habituation to how educated taste evolves from simple sensory pleasure to the appreciation of complex relationships and ideas.</meta>

...public soup kitchens <note>original: *Speiseanstalten*; these were communal eating houses for the poor, often associated with the social reforms of the era.</note> see a doubling of the crowd, and for that reason knows all the better how to appreciate the proper mixture of a **dumpling**. In this way, **Rumohr** <note>Carl Friedrich von Rumohr (1785–1843) was a prominent German art historian who also wrote *Geist der Kochkunst* (The Spirit of the Art of Cookery), famously linking culinary and aesthetic taste.</note> became a judge of **culinary taste**. What applies here to physical taste, however, applies quite similarly to taste in **higher spheres**. This is primarily how the taste of the more highly educated—and of more educated times and nations—differs from the taste of the child, the peasant, or a coarse era and nation. The delight in sharp **contrast**, in bright **red**, in brightly painted **picture sheets** <note>original: *Bilderbogen*; inexpensive, popular printed broadsheets often colored with stencils.</note>, or the colorful **doll** recedes as education grows, and finer and higher relationships, which do not affect undeveloped taste at all, begin to determine the main impression. Finally, the educated person demands of every work that is to please him that all its relationships be linked in a highest relationship—an **idea**—which the child or the savage is completely unable to grasp.

As in the visual arts, so it is in music. The ears of the most primitive peoples are best pleased by the most boisterous music moving in the simplest transitions, which affects their senses most strongly; the child coming home from the fairground enjoys the blaring of his little **trumpet** better than a **Beethoven sonata**; but even the musical experts of past ages preferred simpler melodic and harmonic progressions—presenting pleasure "on a silver platter," so to speak—to those which allow a higher pleasure to be drawn from more widely branching and thus more highly intensified relationships and the resolution of distinct **dissonances**. However, to the extent that these latter begin to please, those simple tonal progressions cease to satisfy; they appear insignificant, boring, no longer occupy the mind, and therefore no longer please. If in earlier times sequences of **octaves**, **fifths**, and **fourths** appeared pleasing, while sequences of **thirds** and **sixths** were avoided, this can likely be explained by the fact that octaves, fifths, and fourths are the simplest possible, most inherently comprehensible tonal relationships, which are the most **consonant** <term>consonance: A combination of notes that sound pleasant and stable together, as opposed to dissonance.</term> in themselves. As long as one was not yet as practiced in the comprehension of musical relationships as we are now, the multiplication of the pleasing impression of an individual consonance produced an intensification of effect, which was not yet—as it is now—thwarted by a displeasure in the monotonous...

He uses historical shifts in musical preference (from simple octaves to complex thirds and sixths) to illustrate this evolution of aesthetic sensibility.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the psychological factors influencing aesthetic pleasure, specifically focusing on the principle of repetition and moving into the "Principle of Association." It explores how external social factors, like royalty and fashion, shape our sense of beauty.</meta>

...recurrence of the same was outweighed. In short, the repetition of what is pleasing still outweighed the displeasure of the repetition itself.

**Fifth.** According to the difference in circumstances under which people live, and the different times in which they live, **experience** associates different things with the same object, or the same thing with different objects. Because of this, something may appear to one person under pleasing associations and to another under displeasing ones. **Habituation** <note>original: *Gewöhnung*; the process of becoming accustomed to a stimulus through repeated exposure.</note> and practice usually go hand in hand with this or take their starting point from it.

**Fashion** <note>original: *Die Mode*.</note> provides the most obvious evidence for this. Let us recall the example of the **wig**. How did the taste of past times for such a thing come to be? The impression it makes through its mere form and color signifies almost nothing; and how should one have become habituated to it without a reason for habituation? It is said that the wig was invented to cover the baldness of a king. Had a peasant covered his bald head with it instead of a king, it would never have become fashion; but now, something royal became **associated** with the wig. Even if the king's entourage initially imitated him merely out of flattery, from then on the impression of nobility, dignity, and the wealth of its wearers began to be linked to the sight of it, radiating outward from the circle of courtiers. Initially, wigs were only of the modest size granted by their first purpose, but then they grew as an outward sign of greatness and dignity—much like a seed, once it has taken a certain direction, continues to grow to certain limits; with this, their aesthetic impression grew simultaneously. And we saw <note>This likely refers to a previously mentioned anecdote in the text about children perceiving powdered wigs as divine or belonging to higher beings.</note> that this impression even intensified in children to the impression of the divine. In itself, the wig has nothing divine about it; it could only owe this impression to **association**. Following this, habituation and **transference** <note>original: *Uebertragung*; the psychological process where feelings toward one thing are moved onto another connected thing.</note> contributed to securing this impression, but without association, they could not have evoked it from the start. And so one can perhaps say in general that most changes in taste ultimately depend on causes that do not belong to the realm of taste at all, but enter into it through the mediation of **association** and then become established and propagate through habituation and transference.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the principle of association in aesthetics, moving from the example of the wig as a symbol of nobility to how cultural and personal associations define our perception of beauty and dignity.</meta>

<meta>Handwritten 'X' in right margin</meta>

In a similar way, among the Chinese, the impression of nobility, wealth, and the dignity of their wearers has become linked to the **bound feet** <note>original: *Klumpfüsse*, literally "clubfeet," referring here to the historical practice of foot binding in China.</note> of their ladies, and the thick bellies and long nails of their mandarins. To the Chinese person, this association has become so familiar that he measures the reverence he pays to the noble partly by the thickness of their belly and even fashions his idols with a thick belly; in short, the thick belly has become an ideal form for him, the sight of which overcomes him with a feeling of power and greatness—and indeed, if the belly exceeds earthly limits, a feeling of divine sublimity. The slenderness of the <term>Apollo Belvedere</term> <note>A famous ancient Roman marble statue of the Greek god Apollo, long considered the pinnacle of aesthetic perfection in Western art.</note> would appear to him only as wretchedness; quite involuntarily, it would evoke the idea that he sees before him someone of a lower class who does not have enough wealth, power, and rank to settle down comfortably and tend to his belly; he would only be able to see a man eagerly running after his livelihood, since the Chinese man himself, for other reasons, is not accustomed to running.

However much an individual’s taste is generally influenced by the transmission of the prevailing taste, it happens often enough that those who stand further from the world of art come into full contradiction with the prevailing artistic taste due to unrelated associations they experience in life. May the <term>Sistine Madonna in Dresden</term> <note>One of Raphael's most famous paintings, housed in the Gemäldegalerie Alte Meister in Dresden, Germany.</note>, this most beautiful painting in the world, provide us with a few examples of this in the following insertion.

A military officer remarked after a visit to the <term>Dresden Gallery</term> that the <term>Madonna</term> only gave him the impression of a drunken peasant girl. Naturally, he had previously only seen peasant girls walking barefoot and with bare heads, and he likely viewed the expression of being sublime above the earthly realm only as a consequence of intoxication. — Before the same painting, Dr. B., known for his popular medical writings, was asked how the image appeared to him. Fixing his gaze on the child, he said: “Dilated pupils! He has worms, must take pills.” His life habits caused him to see in the **Christ Child** only a child sick with worms. — I heard another doctor I know say of the two angels at the bottom edge of the frame: if his children leaned themselves so rudely <note>original: *flegelhaft*, meaning boorish or ill-mannered.</note>, he would knock their arms onto the table; and a little English girl remarked of the same angels that they must not have had a <term>governess</term> <note>original: "governess" (used as an English loanword in the German text); a woman employed to teach and train children in a private household.</note>.

<summary>This page illustrates how the "Principle of Association" shapes aesthetic judgment, showing how cultural ideals (like the Chinese preference for large bellies) and personal professional biases (like a doctor's medical gaze) can lead people to perceive beauty or ugliness in ways that contradict standard artistic tastes.</summary>
