<meta>This page transitions from specific cultural examples of beauty—such as the Chinese ideal of the "thick belly"—to a philosophical investigation into the objective standards that allow us to distinguish "good" taste from "bad" taste.</meta>

### 4) *Principles* of Good or Correct Taste.

Undoubtedly, explanatory reasons for the origin of every taste can be found under the previous *categories*; but naturally, it is not enough to have explained its origin in order to have also justified it, unless we wish to declare everything that has arisen—and thus every taste—as rightfully existing. After all, everything that has ever come into being has reasons for its origin. And what is it, finally, that allows us to approve of one taste and reject another, or to distinguish a better taste from a poorer one at all?

Fundamentally, the viewpoint on this is very simple, almost self-evident; only the application is usually too difficult. The standard for the quality of a taste is simply the general standard of goodness. That is to say, it is not merely a question of whether something immediately pleases or displeases, or whether it provides pleasure or displeasure in the present moment—that is the mere fact of taste—but rather whether it is *good* that it pleases or displeases. In other words, does the well-being, the happiness, or in a higher sense, the salvation <note>original: *Heil*—This term implies a sense of wholeness, prosperity, and spiritual well-being.</note> of humanity as a whole gain more than it loses through such a manner of liking or disliking? For it is by this that the goodness and the value of things are judged. Now, certainly, every instance of pleasure contributes to present well-being and must be weighed when judging taste, because the present must be weighed alongside the consequences in the overall measure of goodness. But how often is present or selfish pleasure outweighed by disadvantageous consequences for the whole, or how often does it appear in a harmful context? Therefore, in judging taste, it is also necessary to take into account the consequences and contexts of its existence and its development; in short, to ask everywhere whether something good results from this or that taste.

Whoever remains dull toward the sources of pleasure <note>original: *Lustquellen*</note> that lie in nature and art, or receives less pleasure from that which is capable of giving more, introduces a "pleasure-gap" or a loss of pleasure into the world, assuming the consequences and contexts remain equal. This is a flaw in his taste. However, this is often reversed when we take the consequences and contexts into account. Whatever a person likes, he seeks to possess, to produce, and to recreate; and whatever his own disposition, he seeks to bring others to that same disposition. Pleasure in certain things is, in general, only [associated] with a worth- <note>The text cuts off mid-word (*werth-*); likely intended to be *werthvoll* (valuable) or *werthwidrig* (contrary to value).</note>

<summary>The author argues that taste is not merely subjective preference but must be judged by its contribution to the collective well-being and "salvation" of humanity. A "good" taste is defined as one where the immediate pleasure of an object aligns with positive long-term consequences for society.</summary>
<keywords>aesthetics, ethics of taste, social utility, pleasure, well-being, standards of beauty, moral consequences</keywords>

--- Page 267 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "principle of goodness" as the objective standard for judging taste. It moves from the theoretical definition of "good" taste to the practical application of this standard when comparing different cultural preferences and moral representations.</meta>

...a fuller, more beneficial organization, cultivation, and mood of the mind is possible than with others, and can lead to a more or less valuable organization of the external world. Whatever the intellect <note>original: *Verstand* — the faculty of reason and logical reflection.</note> recognizes through reflection as the most purposeful and the best overall, should ideally appear that way to our feelings immediately, awakening corresponding impulses and moods.

Whether it is an object of fashion, art, or nature, it can always be viewed from the perspective of whether the pleasure taken in it is "good" or "not good" in the previously mentioned relations. Insofar as we are able to decide this, a specific taste will be approved or rejected, and one taste will be ranked above or below another.

In countless cases, we will find such a weighing <note>original: *Abwägung* — the careful balancing of pros and cons.</note> too difficult to provide a decisive result. In such instances, the principle does nothing more than make us wise enough to remain modest in our judgment. This wisdom and modesty eventually seep into our feelings themselves; often, our feelings do not dare to decide, and we cannot say whether something pleases us or not, even though we know or feel that it is an object of potential pleasure or displeasure. However, in many cases, it is quite easy to judge according to the standard of goodness <note>original: *Massprincipe der Güte* — the ethical or utilitarian yardstick Fechner uses to evaluate aesthetic pleasure.</note>, at least with relative certainty. In any case, every evaluation should be undertaken this way, and every dispute should be conducted on this foundation, should one wish to argue at all.

<margin>X</margin> If the Chinese find pleasure in the "crippled feet" of their ladies, or in the "thick bellies" of their dignitaries and idols, one might indeed doubt whether this taste is not just as immediately pleasure-inducing for them as the opposite is for us. However, their taste must still be called worse than ours—and bad in general—because a taste that allows one to find pleasure in the unhealthy and the detrimental, and which links the idea of dignity and sublimity to sensory heaviness and physical mass, leads to no good consequences and is not connected to any healthy sensibility. Even more so, all immoral representations are of bad taste. They may please this or that person—indeed, they may provide as much immediate pleasure as moral representations provide to a moral person—but it is not *good* that they please him, and for that reason, we call his taste "bad."

Fechner, *Propaedeutic to Aesthetics*. <note>original: *Vorschule d. Aesthetik*</note>
47

<summary>Fechner argues that "good taste" is defined by whether an aesthetic preference leads to beneficial or healthy outcomes for the mind and body. He illustrates this by claiming that tastes favoring unhealthy traits (like bound feet) or immoral content are objectively "bad," regardless of how much immediate pleasure they provide.</summary>
<keywords>aesthetics, ethics, taste, Fechner, Chinese beauty standards, principle of goodness, moral judgment, health</keywords>

--- Page 268 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "principle of goodness" as the objective standard for judging taste. It moves from the theoretical definition of "good" taste to the practical application of this standard when comparing different cultural preferences and moral representations.</meta>

bad ones. A person should not shape their taste in such a way that disadvantages for the healthy and purposeful conduct of their life—and certainly for their morality—arise from it; and they can indeed shape it so that this is not the case. Not only is every taste to be rejected that incurs such a fault, but also every taste that is only made possible through such a fault, because it cannot exist without reinforcing that fault.

With everything immoral and unhealthy, everything unfitting, inauthentic, and internally untrue is to be rejected by good taste. This is from a dual perspective: first, that it is not good for the mind <note>original: *Geist* — can mean spirit, mind, or intellect depending on context.</note> to find pleasure in contradictions of this kind, and second, that it is not good for the world to tolerate such things. For sooner or later—if not in an individual case, then in the general order of the moral and intellectual world—untruth and internal contradiction translate into disadvantages for the internal or external well-being of humanity.

In all such cases, deciding on the preference of taste appears easy; however, it is not always so easy. For example, if I had to decide whether the wig <note>original: *Perücke* — fashionable in the 17th and 18th centuries.</note> or our modern stiff hat, or whether the hair-pigtail <note>original: *Zopf* — a braid of hair worn by men in the 1700s.</note> of the last century or the two "tails" <note>original: *Zöpfe* — here referring to the ornamental tails or buttons on the back of a tailcoat (*Frack*).</note> on the tailcoat of the current century were more tasteful or tasteless, I would not dare to do so. How much more complex and difficult to weigh, however, are the considerations that generally come into play when deciding in the higher realms of taste which way of feeling <note>original: *Empfindens* — the subjective experience of emotion and perception.</note> is the most valuable overall. It is not that our principle leaves us in the lurch in these higher areas; we will still be able to draw much that is authoritative from it; but a primary benefit of the principle will always be to teach us modesty of judgment.

Generally, in all the countless cases where conflicts between different aesthetic considerations assert themselves, it will indeed be easy and simple to reject extreme one-sidedness and the favoring of clearly subordinate considerations over superior ones as being contrary to good taste. However, it will not only be impossible to determine the exact point of the best balance <note>original: *Abwägung* — the act of balancing competing interests.</note> between them, but also necessary to permit a certain breadth or freedom within it as still compatible with good taste, without [crossing] the boundaries of this free...

<summary>Fechner argues that good taste must align with morality and truth, as aesthetic pleasure in "internal contradictions" eventually harms human well-being. He acknowledges that while some fashion choices (like wigs vs. hats) are difficult to judge, the "principle of goodness" should primarily teach us modesty in our aesthetic evaluations.</summary>

--- Page 269 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that while subjective feelings are personal, "good taste" is tied to objective utility and the principle of association. The previous page left off mid-sentence discussing the difficulty of deciding between different historical fashions (like wigs vs. hats).</meta>

[sure]ty to be able to determine exactly. On this point, dispute will always be possible without a certain decision, and it will be useful to exercise caution so as not to consider one's subjective feeling as universally authoritative. 

Such caution, however, becomes an aesthetic duty through the consideration that, on the one hand, everyone's taste could only have been formed under specific conditions of time and place, and has been subject to specific conditions of transfer <note>original: *Übertragungsverhältnisse* — this refers to how feelings or meanings are shifted from one object to another through habit or context.</note> according to their particularity; on the other hand, that truly different things suit different temporal and local conditions, and thus different things can be considered "right" in the sense of a correct taste. Let us allow a previously cited example to speak in this sense.

However strange and absurd the Bencoolen <note>Bencoolen, or Bengkulu, is a region in Sumatra, Indonesia. The author uses its traditional architecture as a case study in functional beauty.</note> architectural taste may appear to us, its origin can be explained just as well by the **principle of association**, as it can be justified by our principle of judging taste by its goodness; indeed, in this case, the justification is almost self-evident once the explanation is given.

<margin>X</margin> The way in which people build in Bencoolen is, as will be shown immediately, the most functional <term>purposeful or expedient; original: *zweckmässigste*</term> for the conditions of Bencoolen, and therefore the best. The feeling for this functionalism has associated itself for the inhabitants of Bencoolen with the sight of their buildings, reinforced through habit and transfer, and thus contributes just as much to making them appear beautiful to them as functionalism contributes to beauty through association for us. If they wanted to build as we do, it would be just as absurd—and their taste, which has adapted to it, would be just as absurd to name—as if we wanted to build like them. Every taste must adapt itself to find pleasing only that which is intended for specific purposes when it fulfills them.

Regarding first the elevation of houses above the ground <note>Referring to houses built on stilts or piles.</note>, in Bencoolen it is justified by more practical motives than we can demonstrate for most of our own house arrangements. First and foremost, for the hot climate of Bencoolen, this arrangement brings the advantage that when one walks beneath the houses, one is always in the shade—an effect that must be achieved in other cities of hot climates through the great narrowness of the streets, causing greater inconvenience for traffic. Furthermore, since most settlements in the country are situated on rivers

<vocab>Bencoolen, Principle of Association, Taste, Style of Construction, Functionalism, Climate</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that aesthetic taste is not purely subjective but is shaped by the "principle of association" and local utility. Using Bencoolen stilt houses as an example, the author explains that architectural styles we find "absurd" are actually beautiful to those for whom they are perfectly suited to the climate and environment.</summary>

<keywords>aesthetics, principle of association, functionalism, Bencoolen, architecture, cultural relativism, stilt houses, climate and design</keywords>

--- Page 270 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the aesthetic "principle of association," using the architecture of Bencoolen (modern Bengkulu, Sumatra) to illustrate how utility and environment shape what a culture considers "good taste." The previous page ended mid-sentence discussing how people in Bencoolen build near water.</meta>

or lakes lie, which often overflow, the houses are protected by their elevation against the disadvantages of flooding. Finally, they are also thereby more secure against attacks from wild animals, of which tigers in particular are said to be so common there that, as I recall reading, being eaten by a tiger is almost considered a natural end to life in Bencoolen. 

Thus, what must displease us as a tasteless whim if it were carried out here—because it would serve no purpose and therefore establish no pleasurable association <note>The "principle of association" suggests that our feelings of beauty are often tied to the useful or pleasant things we associate with an object.</note>, and must even still displease us in Bencoolen if we were not raised there—will take on an entirely different meaning for the inhabitants of Bencoolen themselves. To them, the houses are simultaneously parasols, for which the supports form the sticks, and not just dwellings on the ground, but also refuges through which they are raised above the dangers that threaten them from the earth; and whatever contributes to fulfilling these purposes in the house also contributes to fulfilling them with pleasure, and has a right to so contribute.

Just like the elevation of the houses via supports, the form of the latter has also emerged quite simply as the most straightforward way to fulfill natural functional relationships <term>the relationship between an object's form and its intended use; original: *Zweckverhältnisse*</term>, and the Greek column is no more justified in this regard than the Bencoolen support. In Bencoolen, earthquakes are very frequent, making stone construction generally impossible; the houses are light wooden structures; and so, to put it briefly, the construction there was not about founding heavy masses upon the earth, but rather about pinning light masses *into* the earth, much like one pins a light object to a fixed one with needles so that it is not thrown off by the vibration. The needles are represented here by piles <note>original: *Pfähle* — the author is referring to the wooden stilts of the traditional *Rumah Panggung* architecture.</note> that are driven into the ground; but piles, by their very nature, can only be thinner at the bottom than at the top. 

What we have done here in judging the architectural taste of Bencoolen is what we should actually do everywhere when judging the taste of foreign times and nations: transport ourselves into the conditions of time and place and see whether the taste, which may seem unjustified for our conditions, is not nevertheless justified for the conditions of the other time or place.

<summary>The author argues that Bencoolen's stilt-house architecture, which might seem "tasteless" to Europeans, is actually an aesthetically perfect response to the region's floods, tigers, and earthquakes. This demonstrates that "good taste" is relative to an object's utility and the environment in which it was created.</summary>

<keywords>Bencoolen, architectural taste, principle of association, functionalism, stilt houses, aesthetics, cultural relativism, earthquakes</keywords>

--- Page 271 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation into aesthetic "taste" and the "principle of association." The author previously established that the unusual architecture of Bencoolen (modern Bengkulu, Sumatra)—houses built on stilts to avoid floods and tigers—is "good taste" for that environment because it is functional. This page now explores whether some cultural tastes are objectively "higher" than others, even if both are functionally justified.</meta>

However, a taste that is justified by existing conditions—insofar as it demands what is most appropriate to those conditions—can nevertheless contradict higher demands of taste, insofar as these conditions themselves are not justified; and an often difficult-to-resolve conflict can take place regarding the extent to which the more immediate and thus more urgent, or the higher and more general, demands of taste are to be satisfied.

In any case, above all demands that change according to time, place, and special circumstances, the supreme demand of good taste remains in force: to allow nothing that contradicts the most general principles of human flourishing, and thus nothing that contradicts physical and mental health, religiosity, morality, or logical consistency. And according to this, it may be the case that the taste of entire eras or nations can be declared "bad" in one respect or another; and the popularity of a taste within a time or nation does not yet guarantee its quality. 

One can say this, for example, of the taste of the Orientals <note>In the 19th century, "Oriental" was a broad term used by European scholars to refer to Middle Eastern, Indian, and East Asian cultures.</note> for metaphorical excess <term>florid, bombastic, or over-saturated imagery; original: *Bilderschwulst*</term> in poetry. Indisputably, it would only require other educational influences to allow that which, in this regard, overgrows their sense of proportion and meaning, to grow instead into something rich and yet beautiful.

Furthermore, it can also be the case that not only are the conditions under which a people live justified, but the taste for these conditions is also quite justified—indeed, it could not be better suited for them—and yet the taste of this people is to be valued lower from a certain perspective than the taste of another people. This might be because it provides less opportunity to immediately satisfy the aesthetic feeling, or because the equally justified conditions to which both tastes adapt are nonetheless not equally valuable; for every taste can only be judged in connection with the conditions under which it exists.

Thus, while the inhabitants of Bencoolen <note>Bencoolen refers to the British settlement in Bengkulu, Sumatra, often cited in 19th-century ethnographic texts.</note> can no more be denied the right to live in Bencoolen and to adapt their architectural taste to the conditions of Bencoolen than the Greeks can be denied the right to live in Greece and arrange themselves according to the conditions of their country; it is nonetheless conceivable that the Greek architectural taste not only offers a greater possi—

<summary>The author argues that while "good taste" must be functional for its environment, it must also align with universal human flourishing (morality, health, and logic). He suggests that while cultural tastes like the architecture of Sumatra or "Oriental" poetry are locally justified, they can still be ranked as "lower" than Greek aesthetics if the underlying conditions are less conducive to universal beauty.</summary>
<keywords>aesthetics, taste, human flourishing, Bencoolen, Greek architecture, cultural relativism, Bilderschwulst, poetic imagery, morality in art</keywords>

--- Page 272 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the "goodness of taste." The previous page concluded by comparing the "metaphorical excess" of "Oriental" poetry to a growth that, under different cultural conditions, might have developed into something more balanced.</meta>

...ility than the Bencoolen <note>Referring to the architectural style of Bencoolen (Bengkulu), Sumatra, previously discussed as being functionally appropriate for its environment.</note> affords, to satisfy the aesthetic feeling immediately, but is also rooted in and mutually supports and maintains itself through conditions that allow for a more prosperous development and conduct of life in general. In such a case, even if it has no greater formal justification, it is to be valued more highly. This must apply all the more in relation to the architectural taste of the Fuegian <note>The indigenous people of Tierra del Fuego.</note> and the Greenlander.

That **goodness of taste** does not necessarily coincide with the **refinement** and **height of taste** was already remarked upon in general terms. For it can easily happen that the pleasure found in finer details and higher relationships—insofar as it can only develop at the expense of pleasure in less refined and high things—costs more in this regard than it yields. Furthermore, it places the person in a discordant relationship with those people and things that are not "screwed up" high enough or "fibered" finely enough for them, but with whom they must nonetheless interact. In such cases, we find what is criticized, rather than praised, as **over-refinement** or the **over-education of taste**.

In contrast, one would logically have to call the taste of a child, who finds greater pleasure in their colorful picture sheet <term>a cheap, mass-produced popular print; original: *Bilderbogen*</term> than in a painting by Raphael, a taste of a **lower level** rather than a **bad taste**, although common usage does not always maintain this consistency. Indeed, it would not be beneficial if, conversely, the child liked the Raphael painting better than their picture sheet, because no prosperous development would be compatible with such premature development; one would see here a taste over-educated for the child's stage. Only for an adult who claims to stand at the height of the education of their time and nation would childish taste be regarded as bad. This is because the goodness of taste in someone who, by age, status, and nationality, belongs to a higher and finer level of education, also requires that their taste harmonizes with it in height and refinement. Here, the goodness of taste does indeed grow alongside its height and refinement up to certain limits, whereas it can decrease again beyond those limits through the over-education and over-refinement of taste.

<summary>The author distinguishes between "good taste" (functional appropriateness) and "high taste" (sophistication), arguing that refinement becomes "bad" if it leads to over-education that alienates a person from their environment or developmental stage.</summary>
<keywords>aesthetic theory, goodness of taste, refinement, over-education, cultural comparison, child development, Raphael, Bencoolen</keywords>

--- Page 273 ---
<meta>This page continues the examination of "goodness of taste," transitioning from the discussion of how taste relates to cultural development to how the need for variety (change) justifies different aesthetic styles across time and geography.</meta>

Taste, understood in an objective sense (p. 233), can be justified within a certain period or geographical extent simply because it differs from the taste prevailing in the recently passed time or a neighboring region. This is because humans, in order not to become desensitized to existing sources of pleasure, require variety. Even if one were to generally prefer the taste of antiquity <note>Refers to the classical Greek and Roman styles</note> in the visual arts, architecture, and industrial arts above all others, one would still have to permit temporal and spatial deviations from it. These deviations, though they might be less advantageous if all other factors were equal, become more advantageous precisely because they provide a change from the ancient style. However, the application of this principle requires great caution and is limited by a counteracting principle.

In general, the circumstances with which taste must relate already change so much on their own according to time and place that changes in the demands of taste occur naturally. these changes meet the need for variety without needing to be pursued independently. Thus, the need for variety can only be a deciding factor insofar as other circumstances—those that determine the demands of taste—leave the choice between preservation and change open, or when different directions of taste offer various advantages that can only be realized through alternating between them. 

For instance, architectural taste in the pointed arch style <term>Gothic architecture</term> and the round arch style <term>Romanesque or Renaissance architecture</term> each has its own advantages and merits. One does justice to both and simultaneously fulfills the need for variety by not favoring one of them unilaterally. In this way, even Chinese architectural taste can find its place. However, no need for variety could justify, even temporarily or locally, an architectural style that contradicts the conditions of durability and general utility <term>original: *Zweckmässigkeit*; the fitness of an object for its intended function</term>.

While a very general limitation of the previous principle is that one should not change from the good to the bad, it is limited even more specifically and directly by the following principle—one which sounds directly opposite to it, yet is only seemingly contradictory: a...

<summary>This page discusses the aesthetic necessity of variety, arguing that different styles (like Gothic or Chinese architecture) are justified as they prevent sensory dullness, provided they remain functional and durable.</summary>
<keywords>aesthetics, variety, architectural style, Gothic, Romanesque, utility, durability, objective taste</keywords>

--- Page 274 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "goodness of taste," specifically addressing the tension between the need for variety (introduced on the previous page) and the value of cultural consistency or "preservation." It culminates in a definitive moral-aesthetic principle for judging taste.</meta>

A taste prevailing during a certain time or geographical extent can be justified, within **certain** limits, simply by its agreement with the taste of the period just passed or with a neighboring region. But how does this principle reconcile with the previous one? <note>The "previous principle" refers to the need for variety and change discussed on the preceding page.</note> Firstly, according to the subjective makeup of humans, the need for a change in impressions (which are not in themselves unpleasant) only becomes apparent once a certain measure of preservation <term>Forterhaltung: the persistence or continuation of a specific state or style</term> has been exceeded; secondly, however, certain objective conditions always persist through neighboring times and spaces, whereby common demands are placed upon taste.

How both principles are to be weighed against each other in any particular case depends on the subjective and objective conditions of that case; in the spirit of our most general principle, one can only provide the rule to account for the conflict between both principles by utilizing the advantages of both preservation and change as much as possible—progressing from one to the other only in proportion to the emerging dominance of one over the other.

After all, then, beyond all the principles for judging the goodness of taste that were touched upon briefly earlier (p. 246 f.), there is a single, inherently complete, and universally decisive principle in which all those other principles meet, insofar as they are valid, and which decides their conflict insofar as they do not coincide; yet all of them are valid up to certain limits and do not coincide everywhere. The only disadvantage this shared with so many otherwise valid principles is that it is easier to state than to apply, because it requires a weighing for which we lack precise knowledge of the "weights" involved. This principle is connected to the fundamental relationship of the Beautiful to the Good (cf. pp. 16, 19), and is stated briefly—essentially self-evident, and therefore seemingly trivial:

**The best taste is that which, on the whole, produces the best for humanity; but what is better for humanity is that which is more in the sense of its temporal and presumably eternal well-being.**
